# Epic Games Inc v Apple Inc (No 2) [2026] FCA 1552

## Judgment

### ORDERS

|  | NSD 1236 of 2020 |
|---|---|
| BETWEEN: | EPIC GAMES INC<br>First Applicant<br>EPIC GAMES INTERNATIONAL S.A.R.L<br>Second Applicant<br>EPIC GAMES ENTERTAINMENT INTERNATIONAL GMBH<br>Third Applicant |
| AND: | APPLE INC<br>First Respondent<br>APPLE PTY LIMITED<br>Second Respondent |

| order made by: | BEACH J |
|---|---|
| DATE OF ORDER: | 22 OCTOBER 2026 |

### OTHER MATTERS:

In these orders the following definitions apply:

A. “App Store” means in order 1 Apple’s App Store irrespective of the geographic region or labelling of the storefront, but in orders 2 to 11 means the App Store Australian storefront.

B. “Developers” means developers of native iOS apps who are and remain admitted to the Apple Developer Program, wherever in the world such a developer is incorporated, resides or is otherwise domiciled.

C. “Australian Apple Account” means an Apple Account with the country or region set to “Australia”.

D. “User” means in orders 2 to 11 a user who has an Australian Apple Account.

E. Save as aforesaid, the terms used in these declarations and orders shall have the meaning given to them in the liability reasons (Epic Games Inc v Apple Inc [2025] FCA 900) or these reasons on relief, but to the extent of any inconsistency the meaning given in these latter reasons shall prevail.

### THE COURT DECLARES THAT:

1. Since 6 November 2017, the first respondent has engaged in conduct in contravention of s 46(1) of the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth) (CCA), in that:

the first respondent had a substantial degree of power in the iOS app distribution services market and the iOS in-app payment solutions market at all relevant times;

the first respondent has engaged in conduct (described in sub-paragraph (c)) that had the purpose, and that had or was likely to have the effect, of substantially lessening competition in the iOS app distribution services market by:

requiring that app developers who wish to distribute iOS apps to iOS device users do so only via the App Store;

prohibiting the distribution of iOS apps to iOS devices by any means other than the App Store, save for certain trivial exceptions expressly permitted;

preventing or hindering app developers from developing or updating iOS apps for distribution through distribution channels other than the App Store;

preventing or hindering iOS device users from downloading, installing or updating iOS apps, including alternative app stores, from websites or alternative app stores; and

imposing OS-level restrictions that prevent iOS apps from running on an iOS device unless obtained via the App Store or pre-installed by the first respondent;

the conduct comprised requiring app developers that wish to distribute iOS apps to enter into the developer program licence agreement (DPLA), the terms of which are non-negotiable, and to comply with the App Store Review Guidelines that impose the following terms upon those app developers, and enforcing those terms against developers, including the applicants:

iOS apps may be distributed only if selected by the first respondent in its sole discretion for distribution via the App Store, subject to trivial exceptions;

an iOS app may not provide, unlock or enable additional features or functionality through distribution mechanisms other than the App Store, Custom App Distribution or TestFlight;

app developers must not distribute their iOS apps to third parties via distribution methods other than those expressly permitted by the first respondent;

interpreted code downloaded to an iOS app may not create a store or storefront for other code or iOS apps;

iOS apps may only use Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) which are contained in software provided to app developers by the first respondent in the manner prescribed by the first respondent and must not use or call any private APIs;

app developers must not allow users to access content, subscriptions or features they purchased from the developer’s own website or another app distribution channel without also making it available as an in-app purchase within their iOS apps distributed via the App Store;

app developers seeking to distribute iOS apps must appoint the first and second respondents as their agent for the marketing and delivery of the developer’s app to iOS users located in Australia; and

app developers are prohibited from issuing any refunds to users of their iOS apps;

the first respondent has engaged in conduct (described in sub-paragraph (e)) that had or was likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS in-app payment solutions market by preventing or prohibiting app developers and users from using payment methods other than the first respondent’s payment solution (IAP) for purchasing digital content for use in-app, by:

requiring app developers to use IAP for all in-app purchases of digital content in iOS apps and prohibiting them from using an alternative payment method within that iOS app; and

prohibiting app developers from directing users from within iOS apps to alternative payment methods for purchases of digital content;

the conduct comprised requiring app developers that wish to distribute iOS apps to enter into the DPLA and comply with the App Store Review Guidelines that impose the following terms upon those app developers, and enforcing those terms against developers, including the applicants:

if developers want to unlock features or functionality within their iOS app, they:

must use IAP; and

must not use their own mechanisms to unlock content or functionality;

iOS apps and their metadata may not include buttons, external links, or other calls to action that direct customers to purchasing mechanisms other than IAP;

the first respondent enforced the contractual terms in sub-paragraphs (c) and (e) against the applicants by inter–alia:

refusing to allow the first applicant to offer Epic Direct Pay for any in-app purchases in iOS apps;

removing the Fortnite app from the App Store on 13 August 2020; and

terminating the first applicant’s Team ID ‘84 developer account on 28 August 2020.

### AND THE COURT ORDERS THAT:

#### Fortnite and other iOS apps developed by Epic

2. Upon written application being made by the applicants, the first respondent must do all things necessary (including procuring any act of any related corporation) to permit the Fortnite app and/or other iOS apps developed by the applicants (not including an alternative app store) to be (i) admitted or readmitted to the App Store subject to the applicants complying with the DPLA and App Store Review Guidelines (insofar as the provisions of the DPLA and App Store Review Guidelines are consistent with these orders), and (ii) made available for discovery, downloading, use and updating by iOS device users, in the same manner as other apps available on the App Store by taking the following steps:

processing in accordance with its usual business practices any of the following application forms or documents which are submitted by any of the applicants:

Apple Developer Agreement;

DPLA and any Schedule thereto;

Xcode and Apple SDK Agreement; and

any variation or replacement of these application forms or documents;

accepting such application forms or documents which are in a compliant form;

in the case of tools and APIs (including software development kits) that are generally made available by the respondents to third-party app developers who distribute apps via the App Store, permitting the applicants to use such of the respondents’ tools and APIs (including software development kits) as are required for the development, distribution and updating of the Fortnite app and/or other apps developed by the applicants for macOS and iOS, in accordance with the applicable contractual terms and on substantially the same terms as generally apply to all such third-party app developers (insofar as those terms are consistent with these orders); and

processing and accepting in accordance with its usual business practices any compliant update to the Fortnite app and/or other iOS apps developed by the applicants which are submitted by any of the applicants for distribution via the App Store.

#### Competition in iOS app distribution

3. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must do all things necessary (including procuring any act of any related corporation) to ensure that users of iOS devices can discover, obtain and update iOS apps, including alternative app stores, via distribution channels other than the App Store (including via direct download and alternative app stores) provided those iOS apps have undergone an automated scan for genuine security and/or privacy reasons and been notarised by Apple or a suitably qualified third party (Alternative Distribution Channels), and that app developers can distribute and update such iOS apps to such users via Alternative Distribution Channels, including but not limited to providing access to all tools and APIs (including software development kits):

necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps;

necessary for developers to develop iOS apps that are capable of creating an interface for displaying iOS apps and which may be used by an iOS user to discover, download, install and update iOS apps; and

necessary for iOS users to discover, download, install and update apps via Alternative Distribution Channels,

where such tools and APIs are made available in the same manner and on substantially the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store, and to users of iOS apps distributed via the App Store.

4. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder users of iOS devices from discovering, obtaining and updating iOS apps, including alternative app stores, via Alternative Distribution Channels, or from using iOS apps obtained in that way on iOS devices, or prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder app developers from distributing and updating such iOS apps to such users via Alternative Distribution Channels, including but not limited to:

prohibiting, preventing or taking any action (including imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder access to any tool or API (including software development kits):

necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps and which are available to developers distributing iOS apps via the App Store;

necessary for developers to develop iOS apps that are capable of creating an interface for displaying iOS apps and which may be used by an iOS user to discover, download, install and update iOS apps;

necessary for iOS users to discover, download, install and update apps via Alternative Distribution Channels;

imposing any restrictions that prevent iOS apps from running on an iOS device unless downloaded from the App Store or pre-installed by the first respondent; and

making it a term of the applicants’ or any other app developer’s access to the App Store that they will not make their iOS apps available via an Alternative Distribution Channel.

5. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

impose any fees or conditions for access to, or use of, Alternative Distribution Channels by iOS app developers or iOS users;

impose any fees or conditions for access to, or use of, any tool or API (including software development kits) necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels, that have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels, including by imposing fees higher, or conditions more onerous, than those applied to developers of iOS apps which are monetised other than through in-app purchases (including free apps and apps monetised through advertising) when distributed via the App Store;

require the applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to refrain from offering lower prices, or more favourable discounts or other benefits, to users who make purchases of digital content for use in that app when it is distributed via the Alternative Distribution Channel, as compared to when the app is distributed via the App Store;

require the applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel, and who allows users of those apps to access content, subscriptions or features they purchased from the applicants’ or developer’s own website or another distribution channel to also make that content, subscriptions or features available as an in-app purchase within their iOS apps distributed via the App Store;

prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder the applicants or any other operator of an alternative app store from making and implementing their own choices as to which apps they will distribute via any alternative app store that they operate;

require the applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to appoint any particular person as their agent for the marketing and delivery of the developer’s app to iOS users located in Australia via that distribution channel;

remove an app from, or disable an app installed on, an iOS device which has been obtained by a user via an Alternative Distribution Channel unless the app:

contains illicit, objectionable or harmful content; or

constitutes malware or malicious code which poses a genuine security risk that either requires urgent elimination without prior notice or that has not been remedied following the first respondent (or a related corporation) providing the app developer with reasonable notice of the existence, nature and extent of that security risk; or

is required by the requirements of the Online Safety Act 2021 (Cth) or any subordinate legislation or instrument made thereunder to be removed or disabled;

impose frictions on or impediments to the discovery, downloading, installation, execution or updating of any iOS apps available or distributed via Alternative Distribution Channels, including alternative app stores:

that are disproportionate to the security risk posed by those apps to iOS devices and iOS device users; or

that otherwise have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels or the use of such apps by iOS device users;

require the applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their iOS apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to offer or to use any particular payment method, or require that they must offer IAP as an alternative to the developer’s preferred payment method;

require the applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to enter into any contractual arrangement with the first respondent (or any other person) as a condition of the applicants or the developer distributing their iOS apps via the Alternative Distribution Channel, other than in respect of the licensing of the first respondent’s tools and APIs for the development of iOS apps.

#### Competition in iOS in-app payment solutions

6. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

require that app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store must use IAP for in-app purchases of digital content in those apps;

prohibit app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store from directing users to a payment method other than IAP for purchases of digital content;

prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder iOS app developers from:

using payment methods other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content within iOS apps distributed via the App Store or via an Alternative Distribution Channel;

providing buttons, external links, or otherwise informing, leading or directing iOS device users from within an iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP; or

using their own choice of payment method for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content, provided that, where the conditions referred to in paragraph 7 below have been satisfied, the first respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP;

prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder iOS users from using payment methods other than IAP for purchasing digital content for use in-app, provided that, where the conditions referred to in paragraph 7 below have been satisfied, the first respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP.

7. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent may only impose necessary and proportionate security requirements, as referred to in paragraph 6(c)(iii) or (d), or in paragraph 8(a) if they have first given the affected iOS app developers reasonable prior notice in writing that identifies and explains the nature and causes of that additional security risk, and why the proposed requirements are necessary and appropriate to address that risk. For the purposes of this paragraph, reasonable prior notice in writing can be given by direct communication with developers and where that is not reasonably practicable, by a notice published on a website maintained by the first respondent.

8. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must take all steps necessary to permit the Applicants and any other app developer (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

to use a payment method other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases in apps distributed to iOS device users via the App Store or via an Alternative Distribution Channel, provided that, where the conditions referred to in paragraph 7 above have been satisfied, the first respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP; and

to provide buttons, external links, or otherwise inform, lead or direct iOS device users from within an iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP.

9. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) require that the applicants or any other iOS app developer who uses a payment method other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content for use in-app:

refrain from offering lower prices, or more favourable discounts or other benefits, to users who purchase via a payment method other than IAP than those applicable to in-app purchases effected via IAP;

report the transactions accepted and processed using a payment method other than IAP or grant any audit right in respect of those transactions;

offer IAP in addition to the developer’s preferred payment method or prevent app developers from using payment providers that act as a merchant of record;

in connection with any iOS app distributed via an Alternative Distribution Channel, pay any fee in connection with:

in-app purchases made via a payment method other than IAP, or

purchases of digital content from any website or from any developer’s platform for use in-app;

in connection with any iOS app distributed via the App Store, where:

in-app purchases are made using a payment method other than IAP, or

purchases of digital content are made on any website or from any developer’s platform for use in-app,

pay a fee that exceeds any fee that is imposed on app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store in connection with in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP, plus any additional economic costs that are genuinely and reasonably incurred by Apple in connection with an iOS device user making an in-app purchase of digital content using a payment method other than IAP that are not incurred when a user makes an in-app purchase of physical goods or services using the same payment method.

10. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

impose or require the introduction of any friction or impediment into the process whereby iOS device users can elect to use, or can in fact use, a payment method other than IAP when purchasing digital content for use in-app which is additional to those frictions or impediments that a user encounters when making in-app purchases of physical goods and services using a payment method other than IAP in an app distributed via the App Store; nor

impose any fees or conditions for access to, or use of, any tool or API (including software development kits) that is necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps that allow or enable developers and iOS device users to use a payment method other than IAP, which do not apply in respect of apps distributed via the App Store within which iOS device users can make in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP.

#### Compliance and discrimination

11. Subject to orders 12 and 13, the first respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) reject for distribution, discriminate against, take retaliatory action against, or otherwise disadvantage:

any of the applicants’ or any other app developer’s apps submitted for distribution via the App Store; nor

the applicants or any other app developer,

on the basis that:

the developer’s app is available for discovery, downloading, installing or updating via Alternative Distribution Channels;

the app uses or will use a payment method for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases other than IAP; or

the developer of the app links or otherwise informs, leads or directs iOS device users from within the iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP.

#### Commencement date of certain orders

12. Subject to order 13, orders 3 to 11 shall not commence operation until Noon (Melbourne time) on 1 March 2027.

13. If Apple files and serves an application no earlier than 1 February 2027 but no later than 15 February 2027 with, at the same time, all necessary supporting evidence seeking an extension of the 1 March 2027 commencement date referred to in order 12 to 3 May 2027, then if the Court grants that application such date shall be extended to Noon (Melbourne time) on 3 May 2027.

#### Costs

14. The first respondent pay 85% of the applicants’ costs of and incidental to the proceeding including reserved costs up to and including the trial on liability, on the standard basis.

15. The parties’ costs of and incidental to the hearing on relief be reserved.

16. Liberty to apply.

Note: Entry of orders is dealt with in Rule 39.32 of the Federal Court Rules 2011.

### REASONS FOR JUDGMENT

### BEACH J:

[1] These reasons address the question of the relief sought by Epic Games Inc against the Apple parties consequent upon my findings against Apple Inc after the joint trial on liability where I found that it had contravened s 46 of the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth) (CCA).

[2] It will be recalled that I heard four proceedings concurrently against the Apple and Google parties and delivered three sets of reasons being Epic Games Inc v Apple Inc [2025] FCA 900; (2025) 189 IPR 1; (2025) ATPR ¶42–910; Epic Games Inc v Google LLC [2025] FCA 901; (2025) ATPR ¶42–911; and Anthony v Apple Inc; McDonald v Google LLC [2025] FCA 902; (2025) ATPR ¶42–912. What I am about to say assumes some familiarity with the detail of those reasons and I have adopted my previous definitions of various terms unless indicated to the contrary.

[3] Now there are four preliminary points worth noting before proceeding further.

[4] First, I have today delivered a separate set of reasons in the Apple class action and the Google class action (Anthony v Apple Inc; McDonald v Google LLC (No 2) [2026] FCA 1553).

[5] Second, it has not been necessary for me to address the question of relief in the action that Epic took against Google flowing from my s 46 findings in that proceeding as that action has now settled. On 6 March 2026 I made the following orders in that proceeding:

> 1. The proceeding be dismissed.

> 2. All costs orders previously made in the proceeding be vacated.

> 3. There be no order as to costs.

[6] Third, Epic has requested additional reasons from me concerning my rejection of its s 45 and s 47 cases, but I have declined that invitation. Admittedly, my reasons in Epic v Apple were a little brief on those topics, but nothing more was needed to be said or need be said.

[7] Fourth, on 20 April 2026 I granted leave to the ACCC to intervene in this proceeding to permit it to make written submissions on the question of relief. I made the following orders:

> 1. The Australian Competition and Consumer Commission be granted leave to intervene in the proceeding pursuant to s 87CA(1) of the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth) for the purposes only of making written submissions in relation to relief (the ACCC’s submissions on relief) in the form of annexure A to the ACCC’s submissions dated 10 April 2026 in support of its intervention application.

> 2. The applicants and the respondents file and serve any further submissions in response to the ACCC’s submissions on relief (limited to 5 pages each) by noon on 27 April 2026.

[8] The ACCC filed written submissions that focused on the ambit of the power under s 80(1), questions of extra–territorial effect and questions of international comity.

[9] Now before proceeding further, it is worth recapping my principal conclusions in Epic v Apple concerning finding that Apple had contravened s 46. At [51] to [69] I made the following findings:

> First, I have found in favour of Epic concerning its two posited markets being, first, the iOS app distribution market and, second, the iOS in app payment solutions market. Now I should note that the fact that I have found that various other products and services are not close constraints and therefore are not part of the relevant product dimension of market definition does not entail that the availability of such products and services do not provide some form of competitive constraint. In my view they do provide a competitive constraint to some extent although not such as to deny the market definition that I have found or Apple's substantial market power in such a market.

> Second, I have found in favour of Epic to the effect that at all relevant times Apple has and had a substantial degree of power in each market.

> Third, I have found that Apple has, contrary to s 46 of the CCA, engaged in conduct in either or both markets that had the purpose or had or is likely to have or had the effect of substantially lessening competition in such markets being, specifically, conduct that prevents or prohibits the direct downloading or sideloading of native apps and prevents or prohibits developers and users from using alternative payment methods to IAP for purchasing digital in app content; as to the latter, I have only found in favour of Epic on its effects case.

> Let me elaborate further on some of my key findings.

> In my view Apple's restrictions on direct downloading or sideloading of native iOS apps had the purpose and effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS app distribution services market. Further, in my view such conduct had such a purpose and effect or likely effect in the broader app distribution market, if I am wrong in my primary finding of an iOS app distribution services market.

> Further, in my view Apple's conduct in imposing IAP on app developers in the circumstances indicated had the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the payment solutions market.

> Now I would note in relation to these findings that Apple may in part in imposing these restrictions have sought to address security concerns and risks. But nevertheless, none of that negates my key conclusions. Apple may have had a security purpose in preventing direct downloading or sideloading, but that does not deny other substantial purposes. Moreover, the existence of a security purpose says little about the effect or likely effect of Apple's restrictive conduct in terms of competition questions.

> Now before proceeding further I should say something about Apple's centralised app distribution system. After all, the various contractual constraints and technical restrictions were designed to keep in place and to fortify such a centralised app distribution system.

> First, it may be accepted that a centralised system has some benefits over a decentralised system in terms of security concerns.

> Second, I also accept that the quality of Apple's offerings including its distribution services is in part a function of the premium style security that Apple offers and enshrines in its centralised app distribution system. So, it is a relevant non-price factor. And so, for example, in dealing with any counterfactual commission charges absent the relevant impugned conduct, quality differentials such as concerns security differences need to be considered in terms of Apple's offerings as compared with other entities' offerings.

> Third, the fact that Apple has imposed a centralised app distribution system for the purpose of protecting security does not entail that there is not also a substantial anti-competitive purpose involved.

> Fourth, in terms of anti-competitive effects, any security beneficial effects in maintaining a centralised distribution system do not necessarily outweigh any anti-competitive effects flowing from Apple's conduct.

> Let me say something concerning Apple's ban on rival app stores within the App Store. As I have indicated, Apple does not permit the direct downloading or sideloading of rival iOS app stores. Contrastingly, Google permits this concerning rival Android app stores.

> Now I am not persuaded that the restriction precluding rival app stores within the App Store had an anti-competitive purpose relevant to the iOS app distribution services market. Such a ban is targeted if at all against competitors rather than competition. Further, Apple seems to have had various purposes for such a ban. One purpose was to address security risk as Apple saw it. Another purpose was to prevent free riders. And an associated purpose was to protect the asset that it had invested in and built up.

> But does this restriction have an effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS app distribution services market?

> Now given that direct downloading or sideloading of rival iOS app stores was not permitted, it would seem that the combination of such a restriction together with the ban on rival app stores within the App Store had such a likely effect.

> But of course if I permit the direct downloading or sideloading of rival iOS app stores, then the competition question concerning the ban on rival app stores within the App Store itself is substantially ameliorated. There would then be no effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition. A rival app store could enter the market and compete through the direct downloading or sideloading of an iOS app store, albeit that there are imperfections and disadvantages in such a pathway as distinct from being permitted to do this through the App Store. But that less desirable pathway would not give rise to substantial competition consequences.

> Now there is another way to consider the matter. If I allowed an app store within an app store, that would diminish the quality of Apple's App Store offering. That would not be a pro-competitive step. Further, to the extent that new security risks were injected by permitting such access, that would also not be a pro-competitive step. These last two concerns may have an anti-competitive effect. For reasons I will later address, in my view Apple's ban on rival app stores within the App Store itself does not contravene the CCA.

> In all other respects I have not accepted Epic's case against Apple concerning the other alleged contraventions of Part IV of the CCA or its unconscionable conduct case under s 21 of the ACL.

[10] In summary and in order to give effect to my liability findings, I have reached the following conclusions in terms of the appropriate relief.

[11] First, I have largely accepted Epic’s formulation of the first declaration which embodies my principal liability findings concerning Apple’s contravention of s 46 of the CCA. This declaration is not limited to the location of the user, the app developer or the particular geographic region of the storefront of the App Store.

[12] Second, I have rejected Epic’s proposed order and declaration that was sought under s 87 seeking to have certain contractual provisions declared void.

[13] Third, I have made Epic’s proposed order concerning the admission or readmission of Epic’s Fortnite and other apps to the App Store, but only to the Australian App Store.

[14] Fourth, I have largely made Epic’s proposed prohibitory and mandatory injunctions but with some important qualifications. These injunctions only impose requirements on Apple concerning the Australian App Store and users with an Australian Apple Account. But as for developers, they can be in Australia or anywhere else in the world so long as they are current members of Apple’s Developer Program. Likewise alternative payment solution providers can be in Australia or anywhere else in the world.

[15] Fifth, the prohibitory and mandatory injunctions that I will make have no sunset provision. Apple has the capacity in the future to apply under s 80(3) to vary or discharge any of my injunctions if there are any material changes in circumstances. No doubt my successor will deal with any such application sympathetically.

[16] Sixth, I have provided in my orders that the injunctions that impose technical and commercial changes, whether positive or negative in operation, are not to come into operation until Noon on 1 March 2027, with one shot given to Apple to obtain a further possible extension until 3 May 2027. But such a postponement does not apply to the declaration and the order concerning the re-instatement of Epic’s apps to the Australian App Store.

[17] Seventh, in the light of what I have just said, it will be necessary to discuss with the parties whether if there is any appeal, any stay is necessary for the moment on any of my orders and if so what the terms and duration of such a stay should be.

[18] Eighth, in terms of the costs of the liability trial I will order that Apple pay 85% of Epic’s costs. Now at the moment I have reserved the parties’ costs of the relief hearing, but I would suggest that costs be dealt with in the same way. Epic has been substantially successful in terms of its arguments, save on two aspects. The first aspect is that I have not granted any s 87 relief. The second aspect is that on the substantive mandatory and prohibitory injunctions I have not accepted the geographic scope that Epic sought. On that aspect, although I have largely rejected Apple’s legal arguments on the construction and operation of s 80, I have accepted its discretionary arguments for limiting the geographic reach to the Australian App Store and users who have an Australian Apple Account. On balance Epic should have most of its costs but there should be some discount to reflect these two aspects. In any event I will hear further from the parties on these questions.

[19] Let me now turn to addressing what Epic has sought and at this stage begin with the declarations.

#### Declarations

[20] Epic has sought the following declarations:

> 1. Since 6 November 2017, the First Respondent has engaged in conduct in contravention of s 46(1) of the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth) (CCA), in that:

> (a) the First Respondent had a substantial degree of power in the iOS app distribution services market and the iOS in-app payment solutions market at all relevant times;

> (b) the First Respondent has engaged in conduct (described in sub-paragraph (c)) that had the purpose, and that had or was likely to have the effect, of substantially lessening competition in the iOS app distribution services market by:

> (i) requiring that app developers who wish to distribute iOS apps to iOS device users do so only via the App Store;

> (ii) prohibiting the distribution of iOS apps to iOS devices by any means other than the App Store, save for certain trivial exceptions expressly permitted;

> (iii) preventing or hindering app developers from developing or updating iOS apps for distribution through distribution channels other than the App Store;

> (iv) preventing or hindering iOS device users from downloading, installing or updating iOS apps, including alternative app stores, from websites or alternative app stores;

> (v) imposing OS-level restrictions that prevent iOS apps from running on an iOS device unless obtained via the App Store or pre-installed by the First Respondent; and

> (vi) prohibiting the pre-installation of third-party apps, including alternative app stores, on iOS devices;

> (c) the conduct comprised requiring app developers that wish to distribute iOS apps to enter into the developer program licence agreement (DPLA), the terms of which are non-negotiable, and to comply with the App Store Review Guidelines that impose the following terms upon those app developers, and enforcing those terms against developers, including the Applicants:

> (i) iOS apps may be distributed only if selected by Apple in its sole discretion for distribution via the App Store, subject to trivial exceptions (2023 DPLA (EPIC_AUS_00060297, 5 June 2023), clauses 3.2(g) and 6.9);

> (ii) an iOS app may not provide, unlock or enable additional features or functionality through distribution mechanisms other than the App Store, Custom App Distribution or TestFlight (2023 DPLA, clause 3.3.3);

> (iii) app developers must not distribute their iOS apps to third parties via distribution methods other than those expressly permitted by the First Respondent (2023 DPLA, clause 7.6);

> (iv) interpreted code downloaded to an iOS app may not create a store or storefront for other code or iOS apps (2023 DPLA, clause 3.3.2);

> (v) iOS apps may only use Application Programming Interfaces (APIs) which are contained in software provided to app developers by the First Respondent in the manner prescribed by the First Respondent and must not use or call any private APIs (2023 DPLA, clause 3.3.1);

> (vi) app developers must not allow users to access content, subscriptions or features they purchased from the developer’s own website or another app distribution channel without also making it available as an in-app purchase within their iOS apps distributed via the App Store (App Store Review Guidelines, APL-EG_AUS_00077706, 14 February 2023, clause 3.1.3(b);

> (vii) app developers seeking to distribute iOS apps must appoint the First and Second Respondents as their agent for the marketing and delivery of the developer’s app to iOS users located in Australia (2023 DPLA, EPIC_AUS_00060389, Schedule 2, clause 1.1);

> (viii) app developers are prohibited from issuing any refunds to users of their iOS apps (2023 DPLA, Attachment 2, clause 3.4);

> (d) the First Respondent has engaged in conduct (described in sub-paragraph (e)) that had or was likely to have the effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS in-app payment solutions market by preventing or prohibiting app developers and users from using payment methods other than the First Respondent’s payment solution (IAP) for purchasing digital content for use in-app, by:

> (i) requiring app developers to use IAP for all in-app purchases of digital content in iOS apps and prohibiting them from using an alternative payment method within that iOS app; and

> (ii) prohibiting app developers from directing users from within iOS apps to alternative payment methods for purchases of digital content;

> (e) the conduct comprised requiring app developers that wish to distribute iOS apps to enter into the DPLA and comply with the App Store Review Guidelines that impose the following terms upon those app developers, and enforcing those terms against developers, including the Applicants:

> (i) if developers want to unlock features or functionality within their iOS app, they:

> A. must use IAP (App Store Review Guidelines, clause 3.1.1); and

> B. must not use their own mechanisms to unlock content or functionality (App Store Review Guidelines, clause 3.1.1);

> (ii) iOS apps and their metadata may not include buttons, external links, or other calls to action that direct customers to purchasing mechanisms other than IAP (App Store Review Guidelines, clause 3.1.1);

> (f) the First Respondent enforced the contractual terms in sub-paragraphs (c) and (e) against the Applicants by:

> (i) refusing to allow the First Applicant to offer Epic Direct Pay for any in-app purchases in iOS apps;

> (ii) removing the Fortnite app from the App Store on 13 August 2020; and

> (iii) terminating the First Applicant’s Team ID ‘84 developer account on 28 August 2020.

> 2. Pursuant to s 87 of the CCA, the contractual terms identified in paragraphs 1(c) and 1(e), insofar as they are contained in any contracts between the Applicants and the First Respondent, and any term to substantially the same effect, are void and of no effect from 6 November 2017.

[21] I will set out the other orders that Epic seeks later.

[22] Apple has sought the following declaration:

> 1. On and from 6 November 2017, the First Respondent engaged in conduct in contravention of s 46(1) of the Competition and Consumer Act 2010 (Cth) (CCA) that:

> (a) had the purpose and effect, or likely effect, of substantially lessening competition in the Australian iOS app distribution services market, by preventing or prohibiting the direct downloading or sideloading of native iOS apps, including app marketplaces, to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account, other than from the Australian App Store; and

> (b) had, or was likely to have, the effect of substantially lessening competition in the Australian iOS in-app payment solutions market by preventing or prohibiting:

> (i) developers and users from using payment methods other than IAP for in-app purchases of digital content within native iOS apps downloaded from the Australian App Store to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account; and

> (ii) save in respect of “Reader apps”, developers from directing users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account, within native iOS apps downloaded from the Australian App Store, to purchasing mechanisms for digital in-app content other than IAP.

> …

> Definitions

> In these orders:

> “App Developers” means developers of native iOS apps who are and remain admitted to the Apple Developer Program.

> “Australian App Store” means the App Store Australian storefront.

> “Australian Apple Account”, formerly "Apple ID", means an Apple Account with the country or region set to “Australia” and associated with a payment method with an Australian billing address.

> “Reader app” means magazine, newspaper, book, audio, music and video apps which may allow a user to access previously purchased content or content subscriptions.

[23] I will set out the other orders that Apple seeks later.

[24] As I have said, I will make in substance Epic’s proposed form of declaration 1. But I will not make its proposed form of declaration 2. In other words I will not make any order under s 87. Let me elaborate.

#### Proposed order 1 — declaration

[25] Epic seeks a declaration under s 21 of the Federal Court of Australia Act 1976 (Cth) that embodies my principal findings.

[26] The relevant principles are not in doubt.

[27] First, I have a wide discretionary power to make declarations under s 21.

[28] Second, there is utility in declaring contraventions of a statutory provision to have occurred in order to define and publicise the type of conduct that constitutes a contravention, to record my disapproval of the contravening conduct and to set out clearly the foundation on which consequential orders such as injunctions are grounded.

[29] Third, I must be satisfied that a proposed declaration of contravention identifies the conduct that constituted the contravention.

[30] The public interest supports the making of declarations that confirm the contraventions, convey my disapproval of them, deter the contravener and others from engaging in such conduct, and provide a basis for other orders aimed at fulfilling the statutory objective of promoting competition and protecting the public.

[31] But Apple says that there are several deficiencies in the form of declarations sought by Epic.

[32] First, it says that the declarations go beyond the gist of my findings and seek to summarise non-exhaustively each element of my reasoning that led to the ultimate findings of contravention. It says that the gist of my findings was that Apple had contravened s 46 by preventing or prohibiting the downloading or sideloading of native iOS apps from sources other than the App Store and by preventing or prohibiting payment methods other than Apple’s in-app purchase system (IAP) for in-app purchases of digital content. Any declaration should be limited to these findings. It says that it is unnecessary and inappropriate for me to engage in further explanation of the findings by building into the declaration a list of steps Apple took that constituted this conduct.

[33] Second, it says that Epic’s proposed declarations are imprecise.

[34] Third, it says that aspects of the declarations stray beyond the pleaded case and are not supported by my findings.

[35] As to sub-paragraph 1(b)(iii), it says that there was no pleaded case, and I did not find, that app developers were prevented or hindered from developing or updating iOS apps for distribution through distribution channels other than the App Store.

[36] As to sub-paragraph 1(b)(vi), it says that I did not find that Apple contravened s 46 by prohibiting the pre-installation of third-party apps. Further, it says that Apple manufactures the only devices on which iOS runs and has no need to issue a prohibition to itself. On this aspect I agree with Apple and, accordingly, I have not made this aspect of Epic’s proposed declaration.

[37] As to sub-paragraphs 1(c)(iv) to (viii), it says that I did not find that there would be any contravention or other unlawfulness in Apple imposing terms of the type described in sub-paragraphs 1(c)(iv) to (viii) in a contract governing distribution via the App Store. To the contrary, I found that Apple’s ban on the distribution of third-party app stores via its own App Store (a “store within a store”) did not have an anti-competitive effect.

[38] Further, it says that sub-paragraphs 1(c)(vii) and (viii) suggest that Apple engaged in anti-competitive conduct by including terms in the developer program licence agreement (DPLA) that required app developers to appoint Apple Inc or Apple Pty Ltd as their agent and prohibited refunds.

[39] It says that whilst terms along those lines formed part of Epic’s pleaded case, I did not make any explicit finding that those terms formed part of the contravening conduct. Indeed, the finding referred to above suggests such terms would be licit if their aim was to prevent or control the distribution of third-party app stores through the App Store.

[40] Fourth, it says that sub-paragraph 1(f) is inappropriate for the additional reason that it does not properly encapsulate the totality of the reasons for the removal of the Fortnite app from the App Store on 13 August 2020 and the termination of Epic’s developer account. Those actions have been found to have been lawful in the proceeding that Epic brought against Apple in the United States.

[41] The US District Court for the Northern District of California found that Apple validly terminated Epic’s DPLA following Epic’s breach (Epic Games Inc v Apple Inc, 559 F Supp 3d 898 (ND Cal, 2021) at 1068). That finding was affirmed by the Ninth Circuit (Epic Games Inc v Apple, Inc, 67 F 4th 946 (9th Cir, 2023) at 999). In my reasons I will refer to these decisions as Epic v Apple (US, 2021) and Epic v Apple (US, 2023) respectively. Apple says that Epic was given the opportunity to cure its breach before Apple terminated its DPLA, but Epic chose not to (Epic v Apple (US, 2021) at 940 and 941). So, Apple says that those matters are not properly the subject of declaratory relief in Australia.

[42] Apple says that any declaration should be made in the form proposed by Apple. And it says that its proposed declaration is concise, precise, and adequately captures the gist of my findings that identify the contraventions of s 46.

###### Analysis

[43] In my view the need to record the gist of the findings does not require the terms of a declaration to be framed at such a high level of abstraction or generality that they fail to identify the scope and substance of the contravening conduct. Nor does it require a declaration to be so general that it undermines its own utility. I should say that the parties referred to Rural Press Ltd v Australian Competition and Consumer Commission (2003) 216 CLR 53, but I did not find it to be that useful concerning the present question. Moreover, it does not require me to reject Epic’s form of declaration.

[44] A declaration of contravention must be sufficiently specific to give content to the matters identified as constituting the contravention and to be informative as to the basis on which I declare that a contravention has occurred. The declaration should accurately reflect the contravening conduct in a concise way. It is proper for a declaration to be framed so that it is informative as to the basis on which I declare that a contravention has occurred and contains appropriate and adequate particulars of how and why the impugned conduct is a contravention of the CCA. The level of specificity at which Epic’s proposed declaration is framed is appropriate.

[45] Further, Epic’s declaration does not purport to rehearse my reasoning at large or to summarise every step in the analysis. Given the length and complexity of my reasons, such a task would be neither realistic nor desirable. Rather, in circumstances where I have found that both the architecture of iOS devices and the interlinking of multiple contractual restrictions produce the relevant prohibitory effect, it is reasonable to frame the declaratory relief in a way that reflects those essential facts.

[46] Further, there is significant utility in adopting Epic’s more detailed framing.

[47] Epic’s central claim throughout the proceeding was that Apple engaged, and continues to engage, in anti-competitive conduct by precluding developers from distributing native iOS apps through any channel other than the App Store, and by requiring that IAP be the sole payment solution for in-app purchases of digital content in iOS apps. That conduct was alleged to consist primarily of the imposition of prohibitive contractual terms in non-negotiable agreements between Apple and developers and the enforcement of those terms. That was the principal controversy on which the trial was conducted. Apple denied that the terms of the DPLA and associated agreements had the alleged anti-competitive purpose or effect. With some exceptions, I rejected Apple’s case and accepted Epic’s.

[48] Apple purposefully foreclosed every rival channel for iOS app distribution and installed itself as the sole gateway to iOS users, whilst its IAP tie and anti-steering provision unlawfully entrenched and sustained its immensely profitable monopoly over in-app payments for digital content. Apple achieved this outcome through the architecture of iOS devices, coupled with its imposition and enforcement of a web of interlocking contractual restrictions and prohibitions.

[49] Given both the gravity of the contravening conduct and the deliberate way in which it was implemented, it is appropriate to adopt Epic’s proposed declaration, which sets out the substance of Apple’s conduct and the key mechanisms by which it was implemented, so as to record my disapproval of the contravention and to define and publicise the type of conduct that constitutes a contravention in circumstances of this kind.

[50] Epic’s declaration also has practical utility in clearly identifying the foundation for the consequential relief it seeks. It underpins the injunctive relief sought under s 80.

[51] Now Apple contends that Epic’s proposed declaration is imprecise. But I agree with Epic that its proposed language is appropriate given that multiple actions and restrictive provisions combined to produce Apple’s contravening conduct.

[52] Apple contends that aspects of Epic’s proposed declaration stray beyond the pleaded case and my dispositive findings.

[53] But as to proposed sub-paragraph 1(b)(iii), the necessary updating of iOS apps forms part of the distribution services relating to the apps. Apple imposed various interlinked restrictions on developers that prevented developers distributing or updating apps other than through Apple. But if sub-paragraph 1(b)(iii) were to be omitted, that refinement would not disturb the substance of the contravention found or the foundation for the consequential relief Epic seeks.

[54] As to proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(iv) to (viii), I described the “iOS restrictive terms” as a package of measures which together constituted the conduct that contravened s 46. In that context, proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(iv) to (viii) do not postulate a series of discrete, free-standing contraventions; they particularise the means by which the contravening conduct was carried on.

[55] Proposed sub-paragraph 1(c)(iv) does not seek to relitigate the “store-within-a-store” case that I rejected. Those restrictions were pleaded and found to be part of the iOS restrictive terms that fortified Apple’s prohibition on rival iOS app distribution channels. They are therefore a proper component of a declaration that describes the conduct which I held had the purpose, effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS app distribution market.

[56] Proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(v) to (viii) are supported by my findings. I explained the meaning and scope of the phrase “iOS app distribution conduct”, which included the imposition and enforcement of “the restrictive terms”. The meaning and scope of the restrictive terms was dealt with in my reasons and they include the terms identified in proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(v) to (viii). Further, in respect of proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(vi) to (viii), I found that each of the prohibitions that is the subject of those proposed orders had no cogent connection with any of Apple’s purported justifications and were for an anti–competitive purpose.

[57] As to proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(vii) and (viii) in particular, it is possible that in some other contractual context that does not erect and entrench a monopoly distribution channel, terms requiring a platform operator to be appointed as agent or to control refunds might be unobjectionable, but that is not this case. In the setting of Apple’s closed iOS ecosystem, those provisions are properly understood as elements of the contravening conduct. Proposed sub-paragraphs 1(c)(vii) and (viii) do no more than record that reality by identifying specific terms of the DPLA and related agreements which, in combination with the other iOS restrictive terms, contributed to the substantial lessening of competition found by me.

[58] Further, Apple contends that proposed sub-paragraph 1(f) of Epic’s proposed declarations is inappropriate because it does not reflect the totality of the reasons for the removal of Fortnite and the termination of Epic’s developer account, and because those steps have been held lawful in US proceedings. But proposed sub-paragraph 1(f) does not purport to state the totality of the reasons for those steps. It records that Apple enforced the impugned contractual terms by refusing to allow Epic Direct Pay being Epic’s in-app payment solution, removing Fortnite and terminating Epic’s developer account.

[59] Declaratory relief may properly identify those acts insofar as they constitute enforcement of the contravening conduct that has been found to be unlawful under Australian law, irrespective of any findings in US proceedings.

[60] Further, it is not necessary to confine the declaration by reference to Australian users. But I do propose to impose various limits on the prohibitory and mandatory injunctions which I will discuss in detail later.

[61] Further, a theme of Apple’s complaints about Epic’s relief is that the orders regulate conduct beyond that pleaded and found to contravene the CCA. But my remedial power is not confined to prohibiting the precise conduct pleaded and found to constitute the contravention. I will discuss this in more detail later.

[62] In summary, Epic’s proposed declaration is clear on its face. The relevant conduct engaged in by Apple is defined with precision.

#### Proposed order 2 — declaration that contractual terms void

[63] Sections 87(1) and (2) provide:

> (1) Without limiting the generality of section 80, where, in a proceeding instituted under this Part, or for an offence against section 45AF or 45AG or subsection 56BN(1) or 56BZI(1), the Court finds that a person who is a party to the proceeding has suffered, or is likely to suffer, loss or damage by conduct of another person that was engaged in (whether before or after the commencement of this subsection) in contravention of a provision of Part IV, Division 2 of Part IVB, or Part IVBA or IVE, or of section 55B, subsection 56BO(1) or 56BU(1), section 56BZA, 56BZB or 56BZC, subsection 56BZD(1), section 56BZJ, 60C or 60K, a civil penalty provision of a gas market instrument or a civil penalty provision of the consumer data rules, the Court may, whether or not it grants an injunction under section 80 or makes an order under section 82, 86C, 86D or 86E, make such order or orders as it thinks appropriate against the person who engaged in the conduct or a person who was involved in the contravention (including all or any of the orders mentioned in subsection (2) of this section) if the Court considers that the order or orders concerned will compensate the first-mentioned person in whole or in part for the loss or damage or will prevent or reduce the loss or damage.

> …

> (2) The orders referred to in subsections (1) and (1A) are:

> (a) an order declaring the whole or any part of a contract made between the person who suffered, or is likely to suffer, the loss or damage and the person who engaged in the conduct or a person who was involved in the contravention constituted by the conduct, or of a collateral arrangement relating to such a contract, to be void and, if the Court thinks fit, to have been void ab initio or at all times on and after such date before the date on which the order is made as is specified in the order;

> (b) an order varying such a contract or arrangement in such manner as is specified in the order and, if the Court thinks fit, declaring the contract or arrangement to have had effect as so varied on and after such date before the date on which the order is made as is so specified;

> (ba) an order refusing to enforce any or all of the provisions of such a contract;

> …

> (f) an order directing the person who engaged in the conduct or a person who was involved in the contravention constituted by the conduct, at his or her own expense, to supply specified services to the person who suffered, or is likely to suffer, the loss or damage;

> …

[64] Epic seeks a declaration pursuant to s 87(1) that certain contractual terms contained in any contracts between Epic and Apple, and any substantially similar term, are void and of no effect, being terms whose imposition and enforcement formed part of Apple’s contravening conduct.

[65] Section 87(1) empowers the Court to make any order that it thinks appropriate, including any of the orders mentioned in s 87(2), if it considers that the order will, relevantly, compensate a person for, or prevent or reduce, the loss or damage that a person has suffered, or is likely to suffer, by conduct found to be in contravention of a provision of Part IV.

[66] The relevant principles are not in doubt.

[67] First, the references to loss or damage in s 87 should not be given any narrow meaning. They encompass any form of economic loss due to prejudice or disadvantage occasioned by a respondent’s unlawful conduct.

[68] Second, to establish that an applicant is likely to suffer loss or damage, the applicant must establish at least a real chance or possibility of it being suffered.

[69] Third, for the actual or potential loss or damage to be suffered “by” the contravening conduct, it is sufficient for an applicant to demonstrate that the contravening conduct was a cause rather than the cause of the loss or damage.

[70] Now Epic says that it has suffered and is likely to suffer economic loss by Apple’s conduct in contravention of s 46. It says that but for Apple’s anti-competitive conduct in the distribution and payments markets, Epic could and would have competed in those markets.

[71] Epic says that its actual and potential loss flows directly from Apple’s imposition and enforcement of the contractual terms identified in the declarations in sub-paragraphs 1(c) and (e) by which Apple: (a) prohibited developers including Epic from distributing iOS apps otherwise than via the App Store and prevented them from developing apps capable of distribution via alternative distribution channels; (b) prohibited developers including Epic from using a payment method other than IAP for in-app purchases of digital content; and (c) prohibited developers including Epic from directing users from within iOS apps to alternative payment methods.

[72] Moreover, Epic says that by imposing and enforcing the offending terms on Epic, Apple foreclosed Epic’s ability to compete in iOS app distribution and to benefit from competition in in-app payment solutions for its own apps. It says that that loss was compounded when Apple removed Fortnite from the App Store and terminated Epic’s developer account. It says that Apple has not reinstated Epic’s developer account and Epic’s apps are still excluded from the App Store.

[73] Epic says that those matters are sufficient to satisfy the requirement that Epic has suffered, and is likely to suffer, loss or damage by Apple’s contravening conduct, enlivening the Court’s power under s 87.

[74] Further, Epic says that it is necessary to make the declaration of invalidity sought under s 87(2)(a), removing contractual obstacles to Epic distributing and updating iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, to developing such apps, and to offering Epic Direct Pay or any other alternative payment method for in–app purchases. Epic says that that relief would also facilitate readmission of Epic’s apps to the App Store which is dealt with in its proposed order 3 (order 2 as modified and made by me). It says that Apple could not rely on any alleged breach of the unlawful terms to refuse to deal.

[75] Epic says that removing those obstacles and facilitating readmission will at least reduce the loss Epic would likely suffer if the relevant contractual terms were to remain in effect.

[76] Epic says that the declaration sought is confined to Epic’s legal rights vis-à-vis Apple. It does not determine the rights of non-parties.

[77] Epic says that it has identified loss or damage suffered, or likely to be suffered, by Apple’s contravening conduct which can be grouped under various heads. First, there is the loss of commercial opportunities, including the opportunity to compete and earn revenue in the iOS app distribution market and to distribute the Epic Games Store (EGS) on iOS devices. Second, there is the loss of the opportunity to benefit from lower commissions for Epic’s apps. Third, there is the loss of revenue following the removal of Fortnite and other Epic apps from the App Store.

[78] Epic says that in that context, Apple’s narrow focus on the current profitability of the EGS mischaracterises the relevant inquiry. The question for s 87 is whether, in the counterfactual where Apple did not engage in the iOS app distribution conduct and iOS payment solutions conduct, Epic would have obtained, or been likely to obtain, a greater benefit or avoided a detriment. The current profitability of the EGS on other platforms is beside the point. Epic says that what matters is the nature of the opportunities in the iOS app distribution market that Epic was prevented from pursuing and the potential benefits thereby lost. It says that Epic lost benefits of that kind, including benefits flowing from increased user engagement with the Epic ecosystem and additional revenue from in-app purchases within Fortnite.

[79] Further, Epic says that I treated Apple’s enforcement of its restrictive terms against Epic, including the removal of Fortnite and the termination of Epic’s developer account, as a paradigm example of the contravening conduct. It says that loss flowing from those steps is therefore loss suffered “by” the contraventions for the purposes of s 87. And it is sufficient that the contravening conduct was a cause of the relevant loss. It need not be shown to be the sole cause.

[80] Further, Epic says that the availability of injunctive relief under s 80 does not negate a real and ongoing risk of economic loss or prejudice for the purposes of the “likely to suffer” limb of s 87. It says that unless the impugned contractual terms are declared void, Apple retains the ability to rely on them in its dealings with Epic. It says that that continuing exposure is itself a source of commercial prejudice and disadvantage and constitutes a sufficient risk of future loss or damage to enliven s 87, in addition to the past loss already identified.

[81] Further, Epic says that there is no reason in principle why relief under s 87 cannot be framed in ambulatory terms so as to void identified unlawful terms in any contracts between Epic and Apple in which they appear.

[82] Further, Epic says that an order in the terms sought is apt to prevent or reduce loss or damage because, unless the impugned terms are declared void, Apple remains free to invoke Epic’s alleged breach of those terms as a basis for maintaining the termination of, or refusing to reinstate, Epic’s developer account. Epic says that this would perpetuate the exclusion that I identified at [4130] to [4132], where I said:

> On 28 August 2020, Apple terminated Epic’s DPLA and its developer agreement and informed Epic that it could not reapply to the Apple developer program for at least one year.

> As a result, Epic could no longer submit apps or updates to the App Store and, in addition to Fortnite, other Epic apps associated with its Team ID ‘84 Account were removed from the App Store, including in Australia. This meant that new iOS device users could not download those apps and existing iOS users of those apps could no longer update their iOS versions of those apps.

> In August and September 2021, more than one year after the termination, Epic made several requests for reinstatement of its Team ID ‘84 Account. For some time, Apple had not reinstated Epic’s account, despite Epic’s commitment to complying with the App Store review guidelines.

[83] Further, Epic says that declaring the terms void removes any anti–competitive contractual basis for any future refusal to deal and reduces the real risk of continuing economic prejudice to Epic.

[84] Further, Epic says that its s 87 relief is to facilitate the readmission of Epic’s apps by removing an illegitimate contractual basis for exclusion.

[85] Further, Epic says that clause 6.9 of the DPLA and the stipulated “sole discretion” in the prefatory words to that clause as to which apps are distributed via the App Store does not answer that point. Epic says that any such discretion must be exercised consistently with the CCA and cannot lawfully be exercised by reference to contractual terms that I have declared void under s 87 because they form part of the contravening conduct.

[86] Further, Epic says that s 87(2)(a) is not limited to cases where the impugned provision is already invalid at general law. It says that s 87(2)(a) empowers me to declare a contractual provision void where that is an appropriate means of compensating a person, or of preventing or reducing loss or damage resulting or likely to result from a contravention, even if the provision would otherwise be valid at general law.

[87] Further, Epic says that declaring the impugned provisions void as between Epic and Apple is an orthodox exercise of the power in s 87(2)(a) to prevent or reduce loss arising from that contravening conduct. It says that the fact that the same provisions might also operate in other jurisdictions, and have been held lawful under US antitrust law, does not immunise them from being set aside as a matter of Australian law for the purpose of addressing Australian market contraventions.

[88] Further, Epic says that proposed order 2 is not to be read only as a retrospective declaration aimed at an historical situation. Epic says that s 87(1) confers a broad power to make “such order or orders as [the Court] thinks appropriate” if the Court considers the order will compensate for, or prevent or reduce, the loss or damage, or likely loss or damage. It expressly contemplates orders “including” those in s 87(2). Epic says that the remedial power is not confined to the particular forms in s 87(2) and there is no textual basis to read s 87 as incapable of supporting an ambulatory order that bites on future contracts, provided the s 87(1) criteria are satisfied.

###### Analysis

[89] Now Epic by proposed order 2 seeks an exercise of the power in s 87(1) to make an order of the kind contemplated by s 87(2)(a). But I agree with Apple that there are three requirements for the exercise of that power that are not satisfied here.

[90] First, an order under s 87(1) can only be made if I find that a party has suffered, or is likely to suffer, loss or damage by the contravening conduct.

[91] Second, orders can be made under s 87(1) only in so far as those orders will compensate or will prevent or reduce the loss or damage that is identified. In Marks v GIO Australia Holdings Limited (1998) 196 CLR 494 it was said by McHugh, Hayne and Callinan JJ at [43]:

> If loss or damage is shown to have been suffered or to be likely to be suffered, orders of the kind prescribed by s 87 may be made. Proof of loss or damage (actual or potential) is therefore the gateway to the s 87 remedies. But the identification of loss or damage is important in the operation of s 87 not only for this reason but also because the power to make orders under s 87 is limited to making orders “if the Court considers that the order or orders concerned will compensate … in whole or in part for the loss or damage or will prevent or reduce the loss or damage … ” (s 87(1) and (1A)). That is, the Court can make orders under s 87 only in so far as those orders will compensate (or will prevent or reduce) the loss or damage that is identified.

[92] Third, the power under s 87(2)(a) does not convey a broad discretion to declare provisions of a contract void. The power is usually exercised where a contractual provision is void because of the application of a provision of the CCA or some other legal principle.

[93] Now in Tenji v Henneberry & Associates Pty Ltd (2000) 98 FCR 324, French J found that the breadth of the power under s 87(2)(a) was broader than that suggested in Webb Distributors (Aust.) Pty Ltd v State of Victoria (1993) 179 CLR 15 at 37 and that orders under s 87(2)(a) can have an operational rather than declaratory role. And his Honour expressed the view that the width of operation attributed to s 87 in Marks represented the appropriate position. But I agree with Apple that in any event nothing in Tenji expressly indicates that the power in s 87(2)(a) extends to declaring void individual provisions of a contract that do not form part of the contravening conduct. I should say for completeness here that the considerable breadth that I have given to s 80, which I will discuss in the next section, has no necessary tension with what I have said here concerning s 87(2)(a). Section 80 is a different power and the width of the scope of that power is justified in part by its public purpose together with what is said or necessarily implied in ss 80(4) and (5).

[94] Further, French J’s observation in Tenji at [14] that s 87(2)(a) can have an operational rather than declaratory role was directed to an order declaring that an existing contract was void ab initio on certain terms rather than contemplating that an order could be made declaring a contract not yet entered into to be void.

[95] Further, I have not found that Epic has suffered, or is likely to suffer, loss or damage as a result of Apple’s contraventions of s 46. Now Epic says that such a finding follows from my findings that but for the contravening conduct, Epic would have competed in the distribution market and the payments market.

[96] Now I found that in the counterfactual there is a real commercial likelihood that the EGS would launch on iOS, and in those circumstances, Epic would offer Epic Direct Payment to developers and consumers using the EGS. But I did not find that Epic’s launch of the EGS would have been profitable. To the contrary, I found that the EGS business has operated at a loss since 2018 and will continue to do so until 2027 at least.

[97] Now Epic says that its loss was compounded when Apple removed Fortnite from the App Store and terminated Epic’s developer account. But the US courts have found that Apple acted lawfully in removing the Fortnite app from the App Store and terminating Epic’s developer account, following Epic’s breach of its DPLA and after Epic chose not to remedy the breach. Moreover, I have not separately found that Apple acted unlawfully in taking those steps. Further, as Apple points out, such a finding would have been contrary to the findings of the US courts, by which the parties are bound. So, it cannot be said that any loss that Epic suffered as a result of Fortnite being removed from the App Store is a loss occasioned by the contravening conduct.

[98] Epic also asserts that Apple’s contravening conduct foreclosed Epic’s ability to benefit from competition in in-app payment solutions for its own apps, but no particulars of loss to that effect were given by Epic in its case against Apple. Further, I have not found that Epic suffered loss or damage by these means, for instance, by paying higher commissions for in-app purchases of digital content than it would have done in a relevant counterfactual.

[99] Further, Epic’s proposed order 2 does not identify any specific contract to which it would apply. As drafted, it would apply to “any contracts between the Applicants and the First Respondent”.

[100] Now Epic’s proposed order would encompass the DPLA between Epic and Apple that Apple terminated on 28 August 2020. But as I have said, the US courts have found that termination to be valid. I agree with Apple that an order retrospectively declaring a provision of that contract to have been void during the period from 6 November 2017 until 28 August 2020, cannot properly be described as an order that would compensate, prevent or reduce any loss, and so cannot be made under s 87. As Apple says, Epic does not explain how the order could be said to compensate, prevent or reduce any loss in these circumstances. Further, Epic says that Epic Games Sweden AB now has a developer account, but Epic Games Sweden AB is not a party to this proceeding so Epic’s proposed relief would not extend to its DPLA.

[101] Further, I agree with Apple that an order in the form that Epic proposes would not be limited to declaring void terms that contravene the CCA or are otherwise unlawful. The order would regulate the relationship between the parties globally, including by declaring void terms that the US courts have found to be valid under US antitrust law.

[102] Further, Epic says that the reference to “any contracts” in its proposed orders was intended to capture existing contracts and any future contracts entered into after the date of the order. But so framed and as Apple put it, the order has an ambulatory or springing effect that will operate to automatically void the terms of any future contract that may be entered into between Epic and Apple anywhere in the world. But to suggest that such a forward looking order could be made under s 87 is problematic.

[103] First, the power to make orders under s 87 is not at large. In my view s 87(2) does not contemplate an order voiding the terms of a future contract, and even if it did this is not such a case as would warrant such an order.

[104] Second, the proposed order would void terms that would not necessarily have any anticompetitive purpose or effect, on their own, if included in any future agreement. I agree with Apple that to automatically void any such term when included in any future agreement between Epic and Apple would accordingly be an unwarranted overreach that goes beyond my findings.

[105] For the foregoing reasons, I will not grant any relief under s 87.

[106] Let me now turn to the mandatory and prohibitory injunctions that Epic seeks. But before getting into the detail of the particular orders sought, it is necessary for me to address some questions of principle concerning s 80, which include questions dealing with the scope of s 80, any required geographical nexus and the effect of any orders on intellectual property rights.

#### Injunctive relief: competition in the relevant markets

[107] Sections 80(1) and (3) provide:

> (1) Subject to subsections (1A), (1AAA) and (1B), where, on the application of the Commission or any other person, the Court is satisfied that a person has engaged, or is proposing to engage, in conduct that constitutes or would constitute:

> (a) a contravention of any of the following provisions:

> (i) a provision of Part IV;

> …

> (b) attempting to contravene such a provision; or
