> (c) aiding, abetting, counselling or procuring a person to contravene such a provision; or

> (d) inducing, or attempting to induce, whether by threats, promises or otherwise, a person to contravene such a provision; or

> (e) being in any way, directly or indirectly, knowingly concerned in, or party to, the contravention by a person of such a provision; or

> (f) conspiring with others to contravene such a provision;

> the Court may grant an injunction in such terms as the Court determines to be appropriate.

> …

> (3) The Court may rescind or vary an injunction granted under subsection (1) or (2).

[108] Let me deal with some general points concerning s 80 before proceeding further.

[109] The first and obvious point to make is that there is a distinction to be made about the ambit of the statutory power under s 80 on the one hand, and the exercise of that statutory power on the other hand. Apple at times in its submissions wrongly conflated this distinction.

[110] The second and general point to make concerns whether s 80(1) should be construed so that the Court is only empowered to make an order restraining or dealing with the specific contravening conduct as so found or similar conduct. In other words, s 80(1) does not empower me to restrain conduct in the future that was not found to be unlawful or within a like class. Now as to this aspect a number of points can be made.

[111] First, the text of s 80(1) and the concluding words “the Court may grant an injunction in such terms as the Court determines to be appropriate” do not explicitly or implicitly indicate any such limitation.

[112] Second, to say the least the very text of ss 80(4)(b) and (5)(b) points against any such limitation.

[113] Third, if by reason of any contravening conduct economic harm has been caused, there is no good reason why either a prohibitory or mandatory injunction must be tied in its terms to preventing a repetition of the precise prior infringing conduct or like conduct. If economic harm has been caused to the applicant or more generally participants in the relevant market, assuming for the moment any necessary geographic nexus, by the contravening conduct, there is no good reason why any mandatory or prohibitory injunction cannot be made or tailored to addressing or ameliorating such economic harm that has been caused by the contravening conduct. In such circumstances there is clearly a nexus between the injunction and the contravening conduct in the sense that the injunction is dealing with the effect or likely effect of the contravening conduct. So, the injunction is dealing with the effect of what has been caused by the infringing conduct.

[114] Fourth, if one considers for a moment the text of s 80(1), there is nothing that would suggest that an order could not be made, whether mandatory or prohibitory in its terms, dealing with the harmful economic effects of prior contravening conduct, including ordering the infringer to do something or not do something even if otherwise lawful. Such an order in terms of the power to make it, as distinct from whether or not the order should be made, is consonant with the text, context and purpose of s 80 including the public purpose of that provision.

[115] The third point concerns the question of territorial nexus or the geographic reach of s 80 which I will address in a separate section of my reasons.

[116] Let me descend into more detail concerning the relevant principles.

[117] First, once a contravention or proposed contravention has been established, the Court’s discretion in formulating the terms in which a final injunction may be granted under s 80(1) is as wide as the statutory phrase “as the Court determines to be appropriate”. And that phrase has to be construed and applied conformably with s 2 which provides:

> The object of this Act is to enhance the welfare of Australians through the promotion of competition and fair trading and provision for consumer protection.

[118] Second, s 80 exists to enforce the public interest in upholding the legislative enactments embedded in the various prohibitions under Part IV. As such, s 80 should be construed so as to give the fullest relief which a fair reading of its language permits.

[119] Third, ss 80(4) and (5) provide:

> (4) The power of the Court to grant an injunction restraining a person from engaging in conduct may be exercised:

> (a) whether or not it appears to the Court that the person intends to engage again, or to continue to engage, in conduct of that kind;

> (b) whether or not the person has previously engaged in conduct of that kind; and

> (c) whether or not there is an imminent danger of substantial damage to any person if the first-mentioned person engages in conduct of that kind.

> (5) The power of the Court to grant an injunction requiring a person to do an act or thing may be exercised:

> (a) whether or not it appears to the Court that the person intends to refuse or fail again, or to continue to refuse or fail, to do that act or thing;

> (b) whether or not the person has previously refused or failed to do that act or thing; and

> (c) whether or not there is an imminent danger of substantial damage to any person if the first-mentioned person refuses or fails to do that act or thing.

[120] Broadly, ss 80(4) and (5) empower the Court to restrain or require the performance of certain conduct by a person:

(a) in the case of s 80(4)(a) or s 80(5)(a), whether or not that person intends to engage or not engage in that conduct;

(b) in the case of s 80(4)(b) or s 80(5)(b), whether or not that person previously engaged or did not engage in that conduct; or

(c) in the case of s 80(4)(c) or s 80(5)(c), whether or not there is an imminent danger of substantial damage if that person does or does not engage in that conduct.

[121] The new forms of ss 80(4) and (5) were inserted in 1983 to widen the Court’s power to grant injunctive relief, which was previously limited to restraining a contravention of the then TPA and now the CCA. Parliament’s enactment of the new forms of ss 80(4) and (5) made clear that s 80 permits the grant of both negative and mandatory injunctions over a wide range of conduct.

[122] The explanatory memorandum to the Statute Law (Miscellaneous Provisions) Bill (No 1) 1983 (Cth) said at 112:

> Section 80 provides for the granting of an injunction to restrain a person from engaging in certain conduct.

> Sub-section 80(1) is repealed and replaced by new sub-section 80(1) which provides that, where on the application of the Minister, the Trade Practices Commission or any other person the Court is satisfied that a person has engaged in, or proposes to engage in, conduct that constitutes or would constitute or be in certain ways related to a contravention of a provision of Parts IV or V of the Act the Court may grant an injunction in such terms as it determines to be appropriate having regard to all relevant matters. This sub-section is subject to sub-section 80(1A) (which prevents a person other than the Minister or the Trade Practices Commission seeking an injunction in major cases)

> The effect of the amendment is to widen the Court’s power to grant injunctive relief which is at present generally limited to restraining a contravention of the Act. The amended provision will enable the Court to grant more effective injunctive relief, including mandatory injunctions and other injunctive relief.

> Sub-sections 80(4) and (5) are repealed and replaced. Proposed sub-section 80(4) substantially reproduces the provisions of the present sub-sections 80(4) and (5), subject to amendments to reflect the enhanced power of the Court under new sub-section 80(1).

> Proposed new sub-section 80(5) makes like provision with respect to mandatory injunctions as new sub-section 80(4) makes with respect to injunctions restraining conduct.

> (my emphasis)

[123] Fourth, the power should be read liberally to give effect to the relevant statutory purpose, without making implications or imposing limitations not found in the express words. So, the provision “means what it says” (Truth About Motorways Pty. Ltd. v Macquarie Infrastructure Investment Management Ltd (2000) 200 CLR 591 at [13] per Gleeson CJ and McHugh J).

[124] Fifth, the enhanced power is however confined by the need for the Court to be satisfied that the terms of an injunction are “appropriate”.

[125] The grant of power in s 80(1) is not confined by traditional equitable principles, but the discretion is not at large. There must be a nexus between the contravening conduct and the conduct that is the subject of the injunction. Further, the injunction should not prohibit conduct falling outside the boundaries drawn by s 80.

[126] As was established in ICI Australia Operations Pty Ltd v Trade Practices Commission (1992) 38 FCR 248, there must be a sufficient nexus between the contravening conduct that enlivens the s 80 power and the conduct that is the subject of the injunction made in response to the contravening conduct. The terms of an injunction will not be appropriate if, on its face, it operates upon a range of conduct some of which does, but some of which does not, have the relationship with the found contravention.

[127] Gummow J in ICI said (at 267):

> Finally, there are the provisions of s 80(4) and (5). They provide that the power of the court to grant prohibitory and mandatory injunctions may be exercised in circumstances where, under the general law, there ordinarily would be a good answer to an application for injunctive relief. There may have been no previous infraction of the law, there may be no threat of any infraction or further infraction of the law, and there may be no imminent danger of substantial damage by the act or omission of the defendant.

> …

> Section 80(1) confers upon the court the power to grant an injunction where it is satisfied that a person has engaged or is proposing to engage in conduct that constitutes or would constitute conduct of the description in pars (a)-(f). The power of the court to grant an injunction is controlled by the words “in such terms as the Court determines to be appropriate”.

> Thus, the terms of the injunction will not be “appropriate” if, on its face, it operates upon a range of conduct some of which does, but some of which does not, have the relationship required by s 80 with contravention of the Act. The injunction should not prohibit conduct falling outside the boundaries drawn by s 80: see Thomson Australian Holdings Pty Ltd v Trade Practices Commission (1981) 148 CLR 150 at 161. The same limitation applies to mandatory injunctive relief. It is, in my view, no support for the grant of an injunction which, from the outset, has an operation outside the boundaries of s 80, to say that it is open for the party enjoined to apply under s 80(3) to vary the injunction so as to bring its operation wholly within proper limits. The party in question should not be placed under any such obligation in the first place.

> The succeeding subsections, in particular subss (4) and (5), take subs (1) further by indicating that the power may be exercised whether or not there are present certain circumstances which I have described as ordinarily considered essential by courts of equity exercising their inherent or traditional jurisdiction. But that is not to say that the matters referred to in s 80(4) and (5) are irrelevant to the consideration by the court of the question of whether an injunction is to be granted, and if so, to the determination of the appropriate terms of such an injunction.

[128] Now the fact that a person to be restrained has not engaged and does not intend to engage in conduct of the kind to be enjoined is not irrelevant to the question of whether relief under s 80 is appropriate. But ss 80(4) and (5) make clear that the absence of any one or more of these elements is not fatal to the grant of an injunction under s 80 as a matter of power.

[129] Now I agree with the parties that s 80 has various limits that it is well to identify.

[130] The first limit is that the power is confined by reference to the subject-matter, scope and purpose of s 80. Moreover, s 80 requires a relationship between the contraventions as found and the terms of the injunction; see also Australian Competition and Consumer Commission v Pacific National Pty Ltd (2020) 277 FCR 49 where Middleton and O’Bryan JJ at [343] acknowledged that s 80 had been amended in 1983 and that the effect of those amendments had been to broaden its scope and at [344] cited with approval Merkel J’s judgment in Australian Competition and Consumer Commission v Z-Tek Computer Pty Ltd (1997) 78 FCR 197.

[131] In Australian Competition and Consumer Commission v Real Estate Institute of Western Australia Inc (1999) 161 ALR 79, French J explained (at [22] and [23]):

> Significant elements of the proposed orders have the form of mandatory injunctions. By virtue of s 80(5) the power of the court to grant an injunction requiring a person to do an act or thing may be exercised whether or not it appears to the court that the person intends to refuse or fail to do that act or thing and whether or not the person had previously refused or failed to do that act or thing. The court’s power to grant such injunctions is not conditioned upon it being demonstrated that the person the subject of the order has contravened the Act in a way that is specifically addressed by the order. Nevertheless, the power is conditioned upon there having been a contravention or participation in a contravention in one of the various ways set out in s 80(1) and the terms of the injunction must be such as “the Court determines to be appropriate”. The latter terminology requires an evaluative judgment in forming the terms of the injunction to be granted. To some extent questions of power and discretion merge under the rubric of appropriateness.

> Merkel J has said in Australian Competition and Consumer Commission v Z- Tek Computer Pty Ltd (1997) 148 ALR 339 at 343 that “… there must be a nexus between the conduct alleged or found to constitute the relevant contraventions and the injunctions granted”. See also Australian Competition and Consumer Commission v Office Link (Aust) Pty Ltd (1997) ATPR 41–598. Such a requirement is derived from the evident policy of the Act and the grounding of the power under s 80 upon the fact of a contravention. The question whether there is a sufficient nexus between the order sought and the contravention alleged involves an evaluative judgment. At one level the orders sought may be so remote from the contravention alleged that the question is readily resolved as one of power. Thus an injunction directing implementation of a compliance program covering all of the provisions of Pt V in answer to an alleged contravention of Pt IV of the Act might well be thought so remote from the contravention as to be beyond the purpose of s 80 and thus beyond its power. A fortiori such an order might be thought inappropriate even if strictly within power. I respectfully agree with his Honour in relation to that aspect of his approach in the Z-Tek case.

[132] French J observed that remoteness from the contravention may also a fortiori be reason to refuse to exercise the discretion to grant relief even if strictly within power.

[133] The second limit is that s 80 must be read and applied within the constitutional limits of federal judicial power under Chapter III of the Constitution, which is limited to the resolution of “matters”, in the sense of justiciable controversies.

[134] The third limit to the power in s 80 and any exercise of the discretion is that injunctions should be granted in clear and unambiguous terms. Of course injunctive relief should not be so precisely expressed as to encourage evasion of the spirit but not the letter of the injunction.

[135] Let me return to the ICI case. There were three issues on appeal in that case. First, whether it was correct to construe s 80 as a discretion to refuse to grant an injunction in an appropriate case rather than as a discretionary power to grant an injunction. Second, whether the exercise of the discretion to grant an injunction had miscarried. Third, whether the form of the injunction was inappropriate.

[136] In the course of addressing the first issue, Lockhart J said about the scope of the power under s 80 (at 256):

> ... There is no doubt that subs (4) and (5) depart from the general law in permitting injunctions to be granted in the circumstances therein mentioned; but in my opinion they do not suggest legislative nihilism.

> In my opinion subs (4) and (5) are designed to ensure that once the condition precedent to the exercise of injunctive relief has been satisfied (ie contraventions or proposed contraventions of Pt IV or V of the Act), the court should be given the widest possible injunctive powers, devoid of traditional constraints, though the power must be exercised judicially and sensibly.

[137] Now Apple says that the observation that s 80 confers the widest possible injunctive powers, subject to being exercised judicially and sensibly, did not mean that the Court could restrain any conduct at all, even lawful conduct, so long as there was some connection to contravening conduct that enlivened the power.

[138] And Apple says that no different position emerges from Truth About Motorways. In that case, Gummow J said (at [79] and [80]):

> Section 52 thus is an exercise by the Parliament of its powers to create new norms of conduct and require their observance by specified sections of the community. The legislature may also, in exercise of its powers, adapt remedies known at general law or modify them or create new remedies. It may do so not only to prevent or to compensate for injury done by violation of the new federal norm of conduct, but to enforce or induce compliance with the federal law. An example of the latter was the treble damages provision of s 11 of the Australian Industries Preservation Act 1906 (Cth). The validity of s 11 was upheld in Redfern v Dunlop Rubber Australia Ltd.

> Part VI (which contains s 80) and Pt XII (which contains s 163A) make provisions which effect the attainment of one or more of those ends. In many cases, the remedy sought under s 80 for a prohibitory injunction would have the character of enforcing present compliance or inducing future compliance with the norm of conduct imposed by s 52, and a declaration would provide consequential relief. In the present case, the mandatory injunction sought would be apt to counterbalance the injury to the public interest allegedly sustained by the publication of the Statement.

> [footnotes omitted]

[139] Apple made the following points. The sentence “[i]n the present case, the mandatory injunction sought would be apt to counterbalance the injury to the public interest allegedly sustained by the publication of the Statement” was confined to the facts of that case. Truth About Motorways concerned standing. The underlying conduct was alleged misleading conduct relating to the publication of a prospectus. Until the relevant misrepresentation had been dispelled through a corrective statement, there was a continuing misleading representation. The mandatory injunction to which Gummow J therefore referred (at [80]) was directed to preventing the continuation of the contravening conduct. So, Apple says that the observation was not directed to some notion of redressing injury to the public at large, but rather was consistent with the notion that s 80 is confined to restraining or preventing conduct found to be prohibited. But in my view Apple reads Gummow J’s observations too narrowly. Let me say something more on the public interest question.

[140] Section 80 is a public interest provision that empowers the Court to grant relief where it is “appropriate” to the public interests to which Pt IV is directed, being to protect and to promote competition. And this public interest character remains the same whether s 80 is invoked by the ACCC or a private litigant. Provided the orders remain appropriate to the public interest purpose of the provision, a private litigant may also and incidentally obtain a benefit. The text and context of s 80 does not suggest that it has any different reach, character or purpose when the applicant for relief is a private litigant rather than the ACCC. And part of what informs the broader public interest character of the injunction power is that relief under s 80 can be sought on the application of the ACCC or any other person.

[141] The prohibitions in Part IV are directed at enhancing the welfare of Australians through the protection and promotion of competition (see s 2). It is that public interest to which any relief under s 80 is to be directed in the present case. The protection of competition in Australian mobile app marketplaces is of significant public benefit. Australian consumers rely extensively on mobile apps to provide a range of goods and services in their daily lives. Competition in digital app marketplaces improves the welfare of Australians by increasing choice and efficiency for consumers.

[142] Sometimes that public interest purpose may be served by a narrowly framed negative injunction prohibiting the commission of the specific conduct found to have contravened the CCA. But where the contravening conduct has been implemented through a web of interlocking contractual and technical measures that foreclose markets, it may be both necessary and appropriate to grant an order that remedies the contravening conduct and its effects by prising open the market to competition, and which prevents the recurrence of the same substantial lessening of competition by different means.

[143] Now one of the ways in which s 80 can be seen to protect broader public interests is that it is not limited to simply preventing a repetition of the particular contravening conduct. This sets the power apart from traditional equitable injunctive relief directed to the protection of private interests.

[144] As Lockhart J explained in ICI (at 256 and 257):

> Subsections (4) and (5) of s 80 are novel because they empower the court to grant injunctive relief notwithstanding that the defendant has not previously engaged in the prohibited conduct or does not intend to engage in it again or to continue to engage in it or there is no imminent danger of substantial damage. Yet these are the traditional requirements for equitable injunctive relief.

> These subsections substantially depart from the traditional basis for the grant of injunctions in Chancery …

> …

> Injunctions are traditionally employed to restrain repetition of conduct. A statutory provision that enables an injunction to be granted to prevent the commission of conduct that has never been done before and is not likely to be done again is a statutory enlargement of traditional equitable principles. But this is because traditional doctrine surrounding the grant of injunctive relief was developed primarily for the protection of private proprietary rights. Public interest injunctions are different. Parts IV and V of the Act involve matters of high public policy. … These are legislative enactments of matters vital to the presence of free competition and enterprise and a just society. This does not mean that the traditional equitable doctrines are irrelevant. For example, it must be relevant to consider questions of repetition of conduct or whether it has ever occurred before or whether imminent substantial damage is likely: but the absence of any one or more of these elements is not fatal to the grant of an injunction under s 80. That is the effect of subss (4) and (5) (subs (4) in relation to the prevention of conduct and subs (5) in relation to a mandatory injunction). Their presence is not an indication of a new statutory house, rather an old house with some modern extensions.

[145] The framework set out above is consistent with Foster v Australian Competition and Consumer Commission (2006) 149 FCR 135.

[146] In Foster, the Full Court explained the requisite nexus between the contravention found by the Court and the terms of the injunction in the following terms (at [35]):

> In our view, the need, suggested by the authorities, for a nexus between the contravention of the Act which the Court has found and the terms of the restraint which it then decides to impose is a specific reflection of Lockhart J’s insistence that the power be exercised “judicially and sensibly”. It goes to the appropriateness of the relief contemplated by the concluding words of s 80(1), not to the extent of the power to grant it. If the Court considers that a complete prohibition, whether permanently or for a specified period, on a respondent’s engaging in a particular field of commercial activity or industry is required to protect the public from conduct of the kind which constituted the contravention, s 80 is wide enough to support such a prohibition as a matter of power. …

[147] The Court extracted part of the second reading speech for the Bill for the 1983 amendments to s 80, in which the Attorney-General described the then statutory power as seriously inadequate, including because the Court was not able to make orders corrective of the results of the relevant contravention. The Court said (at [25]):

> To similar effect, the perceived deficiencies in s 80 as it existed before the amending Act were described as follows in the then Attorney-General's Second Reading Speech in support of the amending Bill:

> This power is seriously inadequate. First it merely enables the Federal Court to enjoin a person from doing that which he is already under a legal obligation not to do. Secondly, the Federal Court's power effectively to prevent repetition of the conduct is restricted — in proceedings for breach of an injunction it could be necessary to prove all the elements of a contravention (including, where applicable, the effect on competition). Thirdly, the Federal Court is not able to make orders corrective of the results of the contravention (except in the case of corrective advertising provided for in s 80(A)). [Statute Law (Miscellaneous Provisions) Act (No 1) 1983 (No 39, 1983), Second Reading, 18 May 1983.]

> (my emphasis)

[148] In Medibank Private Ltd v Cassidy (2002) 124 FCR 40, another Full Court said at [26] and [27] in relation to the 1983 amendments:

> … [The] powers of the Court were expanded significantly. That is to say, instead of authorising the Court to grant an injunction restraining a person from engaging in conduct, the Court was empowered to grant an injunction in such terms as the Court determines to be appropriate.

> That change of language clearly empowered the Court to make mandatory orders in addition to restraining orders. That was recognised by the Parliament in the explanatory memorandum that was circulated in connection with the Bill for the Act to amend s 80 …

[149] Let me synthesise some propositions from these authorities and from the terms of s 80 read in context and also in light of s 2.

[150] First, although a traditional application of s 80 may involve restraining conduct that has been found to contravene the CCA or like conduct, s 80 is not so limited. Section 80 can also be used to restrain conduct not found to have contravened the CCA provided that there is a sufficient nexus with the contravening conduct as so found. And that sufficient nexus may be established if the injunction is directed to addressing or ameliorating the harmful effects of the prior contravening conduct. In other words the relevant nexus is established flowing from or by reason of the consequences of the prior contravening conduct. Moreover, it also follows that for the subject matter of the injunction in terms of prohibiting future conduct, s 80 does not require that such future conduct itself needs to be shown to involve a likely contravention of the CCA if not restrained. The future conduct to be restrained could be otherwise lawful if the restraint is necessary to ameliorate or address the harmful effects of prior contravening conduct. And indeed to so accept this point is to accept an attractive symmetry. Section 80 also empowers the Court to grant a mandatory injunction to address the harmful effects of prior contravening conduct. And of course the mandatory injunction could only be in terms of requiring lawful conduct. As a matter of symmetry then and the text, context and purpose of s 80, there is no good reason why s 80 cannot be used to grant a prohibitory injunction concerning what is otherwise lawful conduct provided that it has the relevant nexus with addressing the effects or consequences of prior contravening conduct.

[151] Second, as I have said, s 80 and the relief that can be granted thereunder concerns the public interest and its protection. But this is not to say that the position of a private litigant seeking orders under s 80 is irrelevant. Such a litigant may be expected to seek relief appropriate to advance its own interests. But it is necessary to ensure that the orders are “appropriate” to the public purposes of the protection and promotion of competition for which the power has been conferred. And provided that they are, there is nothing inappropriate about the fact that those orders may also confer a benefit on the private party. Moreover, there is no requirement that any injunctive relief ordered under s 80 to protect and promote competition in Australia be limited to Apple’s conduct towards Epic as opposed to other developers.

[152] Third, Apple’s intellectual property rights are no shield against the scope and application of s 80. I have already held that neither those rights nor licence terms afford immunity from Part IV, and Parliament confirmed as much in s 51(1)(a) following the repeal of the former s 51(3). Coercive supply can be an appropriate remedy for misuse of market power, and I found that access to Apple’s tools is essential for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps. I will elaborate on the intellectual property question later, but before that I need to deal with some matters of geography.

[153] Fourth, in order to avoid an inferno of appellate scrutiny more unforgiving than Dante’s allegorical circles due to any vice in my choice of expression, I need to make one point. The words in s 80(1) “to be appropriate” could be seen to be a condition on the exercise of power, they could be seen to require an evaluative judgment or they could be seen to involve an exercise of discretion. For convenience I will refer from time to time to the exercise of discretion. But if I was to formulate my reasons in the other ways, no different result would arise. So when I say or indicate from time to time that in the exercise of my discretion I will make the particular order, I also intend by such short–hand to indicate that that is my evaluative judgment of what is “appropriate” and also that this stipulated condition for the exercise of the statutory power has been satisfied.

[154] Finally, Epic’s originating application also sought “[s]uch further or other orders as this Honourable Court sees fit”. Contrary to Apple’s assertions, Epic is not precluded from seeking injunctive relief in terms that refine and particularise the relief described in its originating application so as to reflect my findings on liability.

#### Geographic scope

[155] Epic seeks relief that would apply globally, such that third party marketplace operators and developers distributing via third party marketplaces or websites who wished to use alternative distribution and alternative payment solutions could distribute and transact with any user globally, not just with users who have an Australian Apple Account.

[156] Contrastingly, Apple’s proposed relief is limited to users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account and the Australian App Store.

[157] I should say upfront that I am more disposed to Apple’s position on this aspect. But before getting into the legal issues it is worth saying a little more about how Apple’s systems are currently configured.

[158] Now to access the functionality of iOS devices, users must create and sign into an Apple Account. In doing so, a user will set their geographic location, which is generally their country of residence or what has been described in the material before me as the home country. Users can also enter their chosen payment credentials such as a debit or credit card and billing information, which must be associated with the selected country or region, in order for any upfront payments to obtain the app or in-app purchases for digital content to be made.

[159] The identification and location of users by reference to their Apple Account and the exclusive association of users with specific geographical App Store storefronts has been one of the foundations of iOS since the inception of the iPhone. All of Apple’s tools, technologies and systems including the software development kits (SDKs), application programming interfaces (APIs), frameworks and entitlements, which I have described in detail elsewhere, under both its standard business model and in each jurisdiction where a bespoke model permitting alternative distribution or alternative payments, or both, has been implemented, presuppose that each user and device is associated with one App Store storefront exclusively.

[160] There are 175 localised App Store storefronts with over 40 languages and supporting over 40 different currencies, each with unique tax regulations which require adjustments in pricing, and geo-specific local laws and regulations as regards distribution and transactions for digital goods and services. So, for example, in Australia there are newly introduced social media restrictions for under 16 year olds and in Saudi Arabia there are strict content distribution regulations on mobile devices. Apple as the marketplace operator of the App Store assumes responsibility for each of its 175 storefronts and enforces its contractual terms, policies and guidelines in accordance with the local laws and regulations.

[161] The country set within a user’s Apple Account settings determines the storefront associated with the account. So, the Australian App Store storefront is accessible to users whose country is set to Australia, who have an Australian billing address and Australian payment method. The services, content types, features or functionality depends upon the home country selected for the Apple Account.

[162] Further, in jurisdictions where Apple has been required to facilitate alternative app distribution, the user must be located in that jurisdiction and their billing account details must be associated with that jurisdiction. So, if a US user with an US Apple Account tried to download a marketplace app in Japan or Europe, then Apple’s devices would not permit that functionality to be unlocked for the US user, even if they were physically located in Japan or Europe. It is a feature of the entitlements and frameworks which enable developers to use alternative distribution and alternative payments in these jurisdictions that all users and devices, in relation to whom the relevant entitlement permits alternative distribution or payments to be used, must be located in the relevant jurisdiction and have an Apple Account set to the relevant jurisdiction such as Europe or Japan.

[163] Now whilst the subset of iOS users to whom apps can be distributed by alternative means is confined geographically, being currently to users with a Japanese Apple Account or an Apple Account for a country in Europe, there is no equivalent geographical criterion limiting availability of alternative app distribution for developers, in respect of those Japanese and European users. So, developers anywhere in the world who meet and accept Apple’s basic eligibility criteria are able to distribute apps to those Japanese and European users, while they are also physically present in Europe or Japan, via alternative channels.

[164] Now it is possible for a user to change the country or region of their Apple Account. But in order to do this, a user will be required to cancel all their subscriptions made via the account and spend any unspent balances in the App Store Storefront in the country or region in which they are currently located. In order to make payments to obtain the app or in-app purchases for digital content in the country or region to which they move their Apple Account, the user must have payment credentials, including billing information, associated with the selected country or region. If a user cannot supply payment credentials associated with that country or region, they will be able to download free apps only and will not be able to make in-app purchases of digital content within those apps.

[165] Whilst it is technically possible for a user to have more than one Apple Account, each with a different region or country, a user can only be signed into one Apple Account at a time on the same device, that is, a user cannot simultaneously be signed into two accounts on the same device such that they could not access both the US App Store storefront and Australian App Store storefront concurrently.

[166] Further, for completeness, let me make four other points. First, there is presently no technical mechanism to enable an app downloaded whilst signed into one Apple Account to update once the user signs out of that account and into their other Apple Account associated with a different region or country. Second, if the functionality of what is known as family sharing has been invoked, then all family members must share the same home country. Third, devices can only be associated with a different Apple Account once every 90 days. Fourth, Apple has lauded the virtue of its form of orders by saying that even if the relief is limited to users of Australian Apple Accounts, international visitors who are in Australia for any material length of time can benefit from the form of their orders simply by adopting an Australian Apple Account. But that possibility is not as straight-forward as Apple would have it for the reasons that I have indicated.

###### Apple’s principal arguments

[167] Apple says that Epic’s proposed orders do not include any language limiting the scope or effect of the orders to markets in Australia and for that reason should not be made.

[168] Apple says that Epic’s pleaded markets were the Australian iOS app distribution markets and the Australian iOS in-app payment solutions market. Epic pleaded that the geographic scope of the markets is limited to Australia or that the markets in Australia form part of global markets (excluding China). And Apple said that the relief Epic claimed was confined to those markets or else to the Australian App Store or to contractual terms operating in Australia or in relation to iOS device users in Australia.

[169] Further, Apple says that if Epic’s geographically open-ended relief were to be entertained, significant additional evidence would be required in order to address it, including evidence that such relief would prohibit Apple from engaging in conduct deemed lawful in other countries.

[170] Apple refers to the fact that in the US, Apple’s prohibitions on sideloading and alternative app stores and the requirement that iOS apps use IAP for in-app purchases of digital content were challenged by Epic but found to be lawful; see Epic v Apple (US, 2021) and Epic v Apple (US, 2023). Apple points out that the US courts only found a violation of California’s Unfair Competition Law by reason of the anti–steering provision in the DPLA prohibiting developers from directing users to other platforms that was said to involve an unfair practice.

[171] Apple says that it follows that Epic’s geographically unlimited orders should not be entertained. Contrastingly, Apple says that the orders proposed by it achieve the appropriate geographic limitation. First, they make it explicit that the declarations apply to the Australian iOS app distribution market and the Australian iOS in-app payment solutions market. Second, they confine the injunctions to conduct concerning the Australian App Store storefront and iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple ID.

[172] Apple says that Epic’s ambit claim for relief regulating Apple’s iOS app distribution and iOS in-app payment solutions conduct globally should be refused, and Apple’s geographical parameters should be adopted whichever form of relief is otherwise granted.

[173] It says that the activities Epic seeks to bring within the iOS app distribution and iOS in-app payment solutions markets, as “markets in Australia” pursuant to s 4E of the CCA, formed no part of those markets as pleaded or found at trial.

[174] Further, it says that even if I was otherwise empowered to make orders in the terms sought by Epic, I should not make orders regulating Apple’s conduct in the iOS app distribution and iOS in-app payment solutions markets in respect of users outside Australia, except to the extent that such users participate in the relevant Australian markets. It says that if I was to go broader, then this would interfere with the balance struck by various foreign courts and by regulators in other countries in regulating analogous conduct in their own jurisdictions.

[175] Further, Apple says that Epic has not established that the supply of iOS app distribution services or iOS payment solutions services in the identified categories, that is, to app developers and other providers in relation to iOS users outside Australia, which users are not otherwise relevantly participating in the Australian markets by reason of having an Australian Apple Account, form part of the markets analysed in its case in these proceedings.

[176] Apple says that the markets in which its conduct was analysed were markets for the supply of iOS app distribution and iOS payment solutions services in respect of iOS users in Australia.

[177] Apple says that Epic now seeks to widen those markets to encompass activities in relation to apps distributed to iOS users elsewhere, but it says that my liability findings do not support that extension.

[178] Apple says that Epic’s case had been conducted on the footing that the iOS app distribution services market and the iOS in-app payment solutions market are each a market in Australia within the meaning of s 4E of the CCA because and insofar as the users to whom app developers and payment solutions providers were alike geared were iOS users in Australia.

[179] Apple says that Epic’s economic expert, Professor Wright, defined the product market by means of a hypothetical monopolist/small but significant and non-transitory increase in price (HMT/SSNIP) analysis based on an increase in the commission payable on iOS app distribution services in relation to iOS users in Australia only.

[180] Apple says that Professor Wright considered that the supply of services for the distribution of iOS apps in Australia is not constrained by the supply of app distribution services in other countries, that no international firms can enter to supply iOS app distribution services in Australia so as to make a SSNIP on the Australian App Store commission unprofitable and that the App Store in Australia is specifically curated for Australian users, such that in the factual a relevant market to assess the effect or likely effect of Apple’s iOS app distribution conduct on competition in Australia is a market limited to Australia.

[181] Apple says that Professor Wright applied the 7.5% increase in Apple’s commission to iOS app distribution services in the Australian App Store only

[182] Apple says that Professor Wright proceeded on the basis that developers would be insensitive to the cost savings available by delisting from the Australian App Store because they defrayed fixed costs across revenue generated by operating on App Stores globally. So, Professor Wright proceeded on the basis that the costs and benefits of delisting from the Australian App Store storefront could be analysed on the assumption that commissions in other App Store storefronts were unchanged.

[183] Further, Apple says that Professor Wright’s definition of the iOS payment solutions product market was derivative of his analysis of the iOS app distribution product market, and was similarly focused on services in relation to iOS users in Australia, exclusively.

[184] Further, Apple says that Professor Wright did not conduct a SSNIP analysis by reference to a global commission increase. Rather, Professor Wright explained his view that app developers would also be unlikely to stop investing in maintaining and upgrading their apps in response to such a global increase in commissions. This view was advanced in support of Professor Wright’s proposition that, since developers have fixed costs of investing in, maintaining and upgrading apps globally, they are unlikely to be sensitive to an increase in commissions on the Australian App Store.

[185] Apple says that this analysis supplemented Professor Wright’s analysis of a SSNIP on the Australian App Store storefront, not a SSNIP analysis by reference to a global commission increase. Apple says that the relevant appendix to his report did not contain any analysis of the kind Professor Wright considered (at 10.56 to 10.57 of his report) that it would be possible to conduct, namely, of the iOS app distribution services market in its global geographic dimension. And it says that Professor Wright’s report went no further than entertaining the possibility of such analysis.

[186] So, Apple says that Epic’s case was conducted on the basis that the product dimension of the relevant markets was Australia specific. Apple says that the product markets as defined by Professor Wright and accepted by me had a geographical dimension built into them. It is said that they concern the supply of iOS app distribution and payment solutions services to developers in respect of Australian iOS users only.

[187] Further, Apple says that whether or not the geographical dimension of the product market so understood is Australia specific or global is immaterial. It says that the conduct of Apple that has been in issue in these proceedings, and which stands to be regulated by remedies granted by me, is its conduct in relation to iOS users in Australia, rather than Apple’s conduct in relation to iOS users anywhere else.

[188] Further, Apple says that Epic did not lead any evidence or cross-examine any Apple witness concerning the varieties of app developer or the activities of Australian developers abroad.

[189] Further, Apple says that Ms Wong’s evidence concerned her team’s efforts to maximise their Australian developers’ use of Apple’s offerings in the development and distribution of apps on the Australian App Store.

[190] Before proceeding further, it is convenient at this point that I set out some of her evidence given at the liability trial which was as follows:

> At any given time (including as at the date of this affidavit), Apple Australia ordinarily provides direct support and guidance to approximately 30 to 50 Australian app developers, in the development and distribution of their apps on the Australian Storefront, including in their use of Apple Inc.’s tools and services, to maximise consumer engagement, and to market and promote developers’ apps on the Australian Storefront.

> Apple Australia’s engagement with developers occurs independently of Apple Inc. My team, and other Apple Australia teams, decide which developers we work with and how we interact with them.

> For example, my team works with the Australian free to air television networks (Seven, Nine, Ten, ABC, SBS) in relation to the apps they offer through the Australian Storefront. It is important that these apps are available on the Australian Storefront, to meet consumer expectations, and that they work faultlessly on Apple devices. Thus, we work closely with these developers on their apps to optimise the consumer experience, even though these developers pay no commission to Apple.

> In my experience as Business Lead of the Australian Storefront, Apple Australia interacts with developers on an individual basis. Developers may have entirely different needs from each other. For example, I have observed that developers are often at different stages of growth in respect of their business, and have different app styles and genres, number of apps, plans for development of their app(s) on, often, multiple platforms (for example, iOS, PC, console) and plans for expanding distribution to App Store storefronts outside Australia. In my experience, developers may also have cause to use different tools within the suite of tools which Apple Inc. makes available to assist in developers’ initial and progressive development of their app for the App Store. In light of this experience, the teams within Apple Australia tailor their engagement to the particular circumstances, and needs, of each developer at the relevant time.

> From time to time, Apple Australia reports on activity with those developers we manage in Australia to our United States and International counterparts. This is done by communications which include monthly updates on noteworthy success stories based on app downloads and engagement from consumers relating to the Australian Storefront. This can include sharing our learnings and experiences on a piece of curation, an Australian developer’s app(s) worthy of promotion in other regions or a method of promotion we have adopted.

> Otherwise, the majority of my, and my team’s, interaction with Apple Inc. and its related entities is for the purpose of maximising our Australian developers’ use of Apple’s offerings in the development and distribution of apps on the Australian Storefront. For example, my team and I collaborate with other Apple personnel in our equivalent positions globally to introduce Australian developers to other countries for the purpose of facilitating the distribution of their apps in those countries via other country-specific App Stores.

> Internally, my team works with Apple Australia’s Developer Relations team during the development stage of a new app. The role of Developer Relations is to engage with developers on the availability and use of Apple’s APIs from Apple’s Developer Program to optimise their apps and maximise consumer engagement. As apps are updated and are the subject of new releases, we continue to engage with the Developer Relations team to connect with developers and provide assistance or guidance to a particular developer. This forms part of our continuing engagement with developers and their apps post launch on the Australian Storefront.

> If a developer chooses to monetise an app through paid downloads or in-app transactions for digital content, they need to implement StoreKit APIs. My team supports developers during the development phase of an app with StoreKit API implementation for their app. Business Managers will assist with trouble shooting for developers by connecting them with Apple Inc.’s App Store Commerce team, who oversee commerce operations of the App Store. Post launch, and as part of ongoing management, especially with subscriptions, my team will assist developers seeking to maximise the lifetime value of each customer using the tools available in StoreKit.

> We also work with Product Marketing and Publicity teams within Apple Australia to help with the selection of apps and games suited to showcase key features of individual Apple devices.

> All of the activities I have referred to in paragraphs 68 to 70 above, relate to apps and games by local Australian developers. I have observed during my interactions with Apple personnel in other geographic regions that the App Store and Developer Relations teams in respective international markets experience equivalent issues with their own local developers and on occasion we have discussed common approaches to addressing such issues.

[191] Further, in rejecting Epic’s case of knowing involvement of Apple Pty Ltd in Apple Inc’s contraventions, I observed that it had not been put to the relevant witness Ms Wong that the Australian App Store was a monopoly. I said at [6135]:

> So, despite direct evidence from Ms Wong of her understanding that competition exists across multiple platforms, it was not suggested that she knew that the Australia App Store was a monopoly or that Apple had conducted itself in particular ways with a purpose or effect of substantially lessening competition. Nor are there any documents that provide the basis for such a finding.

[192] Further, Apple says that Epic did not lead any evidence or cross-examine any witness to establish any preference of developers to use one payment solutions provider to serve iOS users across all geographical regions. Further, it says that Epic did not lead any evidence or make any submission to the effect that there even exist any actual or likely Australian payment solution providers who wish to provide services in relation to iOS apps distributed outside of Australia.

[193] Further, Apple says that the counterfactual which Epic asked me to analyse was one in which impediments for alternative app stores and direct downloading of apps on iOS devices were removed. It says that given that the relevant markets were framed by references to Australian iOS device users and the Australian App Store storefront, it was necessarily implicit in this that the impediments were to be removed in respect of Australian iOS devices only.

[194] It says that the factual was characterised by the inability of either developers or users to switch to an app store outside of Australia for the distribution of apps to iOS device users in Australia, and the inability of international suppliers of app distribution services to enter and offer iOS app distribution services in Australia.

[195] Now Epic submits that making orders omitting various categories of activity would likely damage the future state of competition in s 4E markets in Australia. It says that the ability of Australian developers, app store and website operators, and payment solution providers to service international customers within a global market that encompasses Australia will directly affect their ability to compete with Apple and others in the s 4E markets, constraining their growth so they can never achieve scale sufficient to enhance the state of competition in Australia through such advancements as quality and price.

[196] But Apple says that that proposition was not foreshadowed in any pleaded allegation, is not tethered to any lay or expert evidence led at trial, and finds no support in my liability findings.

[197] Apple says that it is a theoretical question whether its conduct in restricting the supply of distribution services and payment solutions services to developers in respect of iOS users outside Australia could operate to foreclose entry or expansion by Australian developers in this fashion. And it says that to establish any real commercial possibility of such an anti-competitive effect or purpose would require proper analysis of that conduct, on the identified market participants, in a defined market, but Epic has not undertaken that exercise.

[198] Now before getting into the legal arguments concerning geographic scope, I should address Apple’s arguments concerning how the liability case was pleaded and was run. I would say upfront that the way the case was pleaded and run was not as narrow as Apple would have it.

###### The pleaded markets

[199] At the outset it is to be noted that there are elements in the second further amended statement of claim (2FASOC) that are broader than Australia.

[200] First, there are the pleas concerning the parties and their activities (see for example [1] to [30]). Further, the 2FASOC refers repeatedly to the App Store as distinct from the Australian App Store (see for example [24] to [30], [43] to [47B], [95] and [106] to [109]).

[201] Second, the pleas of the relevant markets at [90] to [94] make clear that Epic’s case was to the effect that the markets were markets in Australia either because their geographic dimension was limited to Australia or because they formed part of a global market but excluding China. I should say that the case as run made little of any such limitation on geographic dimension.

[202] Third, although the s 46 allegations from [114] refer to the definitions of “the Australian iOS App Distribution Markets” and “the Australian iOS In–App Payment Solutions Markets”, they clearly embrace the case of the Australian market being part of a global market but excluding China.

[203] Now Apple joined issue with the pleaded geographic markets, but it did not assert that the geographic dimension of any market defined by reference to the services pleaded by Epic was confined to Australia.

[204] Epic pleaded that there was a market for the supply of services for the distribution of iOS apps to iOS device users. This was the product dimension of the pleaded market; it was geographically unconstrained. The restrictive terms of the DPLA that Epic attacked applied to all developers and all iOS device users, wherever located. Various allegations supporting Epic’s pleaded market definition, were not restricted to activities in Australia.

[205] Further, Epic pleaded that there was a market for the supply of services to app developers for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases within an iOS app. This pleading of the product dimension was also geographically unconstrained. The pleadings that formed the basis of the pleaded in-app payment solutions market were not restricted to activities in Australia.

[206] Further, in the context of the pleaded payments markets, the market concerns the supply of payment processing services to app developers for inclusion in their iOS apps that, in the absence of Apple’s restrictions, could and would be distributed anywhere. In fact, Australian app developers have been seeking, and were being encouraged by Apple, to distribute their apps to countries beyond Australia.

[207] Further, the conduct alleged to give rise to contraventions was similarly not limited to Australia. At its core, Epic’s case in respect of the iOS distribution services market was that Apple ensured that the App Store was the sole means for developers to distribute iOS apps and for iOS device users to obtain iOS apps. The pleading to this effect was not geographically constrained.

[208] Further, Epic’s pleaded allegations as to the lessening of competition caused by Apple’s conduct were similarly not limited to circumstances involving Australian iOS device users.

[209] Epic pleaded that, but for Apple’s conduct, Apple would have faced the threat of entry by competitors and vigorous and effective competition in one or more of the pleaded markets from alternative methods of iOS app distribution, including competing app stores. This was an allegation not constrained by geographic constraints on the location of the firms offering the alternative methods of distribution, the developers who use those methods, or the users who access apps through them. Epic pleaded a substantially similar allegation in relation to the in-app payment solutions market.

[210] Further, the Australian market labels in declarations 1 and 2 in the further amended originating application (the FAOA) import the defined terms in the 2FASOC including the global market alternative. Declarations 1 and 2 allege the s 46 contraventions by incorporating the pleaded conduct via cross-references. Those allegations are not geographically confined. Where, in those paragraphs, the pleading refers to Australian iOS device users it does so inclusively, not by way of geographic limitation on the impugned conduct.

[211] The key relief is [7] of the FAOA, which operates by reference to the declarations, and hence the geographically unconstrained pleaded conduct that was alleged to affect competition in s 4E markets. I do not read down the declarations or [7] by reason of the express Australian limitations elsewhere in the FAOA.

[212] Of course, Epic’s case is directed to protecting competition in s 4E markets bearing upon competition in Australia. But that does not answer the separate question as to what scope of restraint is necessary to do that effectively, and therefore “appropriate” within s 80.

[213] Let me turn to the economic evidence. Again, this covered a broader geographic reach than Apple would have it.

###### Epic’s economic expert evidence

[214] Epic’s expert evidence was to the effect that the markets extend beyond services provided in respect of Australian iOS device users. Now Apple says that Epic’s case was conducted on the footing that the relevant markets are markets in Australia within the meaning of s 4E because the users to whom relevant services were provided were users in Australia.

[215] But Epic’s case was that, in each pleaded distribution market, Apple’s conduct foreclosed competition in relation to the supply of distribution services to all iOS users and the supply of in app payment services to developers.

[216] Apple focuses exclusively on the markets from the perspective of device users and ignores the perspective of developers located in Australia or elsewhere who would supply their apps to users in Australia, or from Australia to other countries, but for Apple’s unlawful conduct. Likewise, the payment solutions market concerns the supply of payment solutions by payment providers, located in Australia or elsewhere, in various ways, including to Australian app developers who may supply their apps to users located in Australia or elsewhere, and to overseas developers who may supply their apps to users located in Australia.

[217] Further, the economic expert evidence was not confined to considering services in respect of iOS users in Australia only.

[218] First, Professor Wright conducted three analyses to determine the relevant markets. He conducted a qualitative analysis which considered what if any close substitutes existed. He conducted a traditional SSNIP on Apple’s commission rates which took into account Australian App Store transactions data. And he conducted a reverse SSNIP which compared Apple’s effective commission rates to PC app stores, and working backwards determined that the candidate market was no broader than the pleaded iOS app distribution market. Professor Wright’s analysis of substitution possibilities and competitive constraints was conducted in a qualitative manner by reference to matters that were not limited to Australia.

[219] Second, Professor Wright’s conclusions about developer responses to a SSNIP were also dependent on matters that were not specific to Australia. So, for example, Professor Wright’s analysis of the likelihood that developers would delist from the App Store was not limited to the Australian App Store. And his detailed analysis was directed to a global increase in commissions, not an increase in commissions on the Australian App Store.

[220] Third, although as to the reverse SSNIP Professor Wright relied on Australian App Store data, the underlying commission rates for each comparator store were global.

[221] Further, Professor Wright’s evidence as to the iOS in-app payment solutions market was similarly not confined to Australia. His assessment of substitution possibilities was not so limited.

[222] More generally, Professor Wright’s evidence was that the product dimension would not change if one adopted a global geographic frame of reference.

[223] Further, it is convenient to note here that because of the presence of Apple’s restrictions, the supply of services for the distribution of apps in Australia was not constrained by the supply of those services outside Australia. And so Professor Wright concluded that the relevant market was one limited to Australia “in the presence of Apple’s restrictions”. Resonating with that theme, in Epic v Apple (US, 2021) at 991 the trial judge found Apple’s factual basis for its assertion of a domestic market “to be weak” on the basis that “Apple’s restrictions appear to be imposed by Apple, rather than by market forces”. And as was also explained (at 1027), “Apple’s engagement in [the defined market] does not change based on national borders. Developers globally access the platform based on the same set of rules and agreements”.

[224] Further, Professor Wright gave evidence that it would be possible to assess the effect of Apple’s conduct on a global basis and observed that this would be particularly relevant in the case of developers who operate globally. His evidence was that the geographic frame of reference would be global, but excluding countries with very different competitive conditions arising from significantly different regulation.

[225] He also made clear that his conclusions as to the product scope of the market, as well as whether Apple had market power and the effect of its conduct on competition in Australia, would be the same whether he adopted the Australian or global frame of reference. His view was that in either case there were no close substitutes for the supply of services for the distribution of iOS apps.

[226] Professor Wright’s evidence in respect of the iOS payment solution market was to similar effect. He also concluded that if one adopted a geographic frame of reference beyond Australia, the product dimension of that market would remain limited to the supply of payment solutions to developers for iOS in-app purchases, and his conclusions on market power and competitive effects in the iOS in-app payment solutions market would be unchanged.

[227] Further, a global frame of reference is also supported by the evidence led in Epic v Google. Ms Edwards–Warren concluded that the geographic scope of the Android mobile app distribution market and the in-app payment solutions market was likely global.

[228] Let me then say something more generally about how the case was run at trial.

###### Epic’s case at trial

[229] Epic’s case at trial was that the markets extended beyond services provided in respect of Australian iOS device users.

[230] And the global focus of Epic’s submissions was consistent with the evidence of Apple’s own witnesses as to the manner in which Apple operates the App Store.

[231] So for example, Ms Wong gave evidence that her team collaborates with other Apple personnel to introduce Australian developers to other countries for the purpose of facilitating the distribution of their apps in those countries via other country specific App Stores. Ms Wong had direct experience of Australian developers distributing their apps overseas and being encouraged to do so by Apple. But in the presence of Apple’s restrictions, that could only be done through other country specific App Stores.

[232] Further, Epic made submissions as to the geographic scope of the iOS in-app payment solutions market, namely that it was at least as wide as Australia and may be global.

[233] Epic led evidence from Mr Owens of Paddle, the developer of payment solutions that could be used in iOS apps both outside Australia and in Australia. It also led evidence from Epic, including about the EGS and Epic Direct Pay. Again, neither of these are limited to apps for distribution to users in Australia. The evidence before me gave a picture of the activities of developers focused on the provision of services and apps both in Australia and globally.

[234] Now Apple contends that Epic did not lead any evidence to establish that developers had a preference to use one payment solutions provider across jurisdictions. But I made findings in Epic v Google to the effect that developers do prefer to have a single payment solution. At GJ[5499] I said:

> Now under the UCB program developers are compelled to offer Google Play Billing alongside their preferred alternative payment solution. As Epic says, this is unattractive for developers. App developers prefer all payments to be facilitated by a single payment solution so that the developers are only dealing with one payment solution provider and can consolidate transaction data from a single source.

[235] Apple also says that Epic did not lead evidence of Australian payment solution providers, which is said to support the conclusion that the case run by Epic was confined to iOS users in Australia. But as Epic says, the fact that Australian developers have to date been foreclosed from participating in the markets by Apple’s unlawful conduct means that there could be no evidence of those providers existing at present. Further, there is a real likelihood that developers will seek to use alternative in-app payment solutions if they are able to.

[236] Generally, I do not agree with Apple’s assertions to the effect that Epic’s case was pleaded and run on a constricted geographic basis. But to say this does not get Epic home in justifying the expansive and ambitious global reach of its proposed orders.

[237] Let me now turn to s 80 and some relevant principles concerning its geographic reach. I will then say something concerning international comity and then turn to the discretionary factors to which comity is also relevant.

#### Section 80 — extra–territorial operation

[238] Section 80 is not to be read as subject to territorial constraints beyond those otherwise present in the structure of the Act. Further, there is nothing in the text of s 80 that contains any express or implied territorial constraint.

[239] Indeed such constraints are expressly denied by s 5 which provides:

> (1) Each of the following provisions:

> (a) Part IV;

> …

> (b) Part XI;

> …

> (f) the remaining provisions of this Act (to the extent to which they relate to any of the provisions covered by paragraph (a), (aa), (ab), (b), (ba) or (c));

> …

> extends to the engaging in conduct outside Australia by:

> (g) bodies corporate incorporated or carrying on business within Australia; or

> (h) Australian citizens; or

> (i) persons ordinarily resident within Australia.

> …

> (2) In addition to the extended operation that sections 47 and 48, and Divisions 3 and 4 of Part IVE, have by virtue of subsection (1), those provisions extend to the engaging in conduct outside Australia by any persons in relation to the supply by those persons of goods or services to persons within Australia.

[240] Sections 5(1)(a) and (g) provide that Pt IV extends to the engaging in conduct outside Australia by a company that carries on business within Australia. Sections 5(1)(f) and (g) then provide that the remaining provisions of the CCA, to the extent to which they relate relevantly to Pt IV, similarly apply to the engaging in conduct outside Australia by a company that carries on business within Australia. Section 80 is a provision which relates to Pt IV insofar as it provides for an injunction to be granted consequent upon a contravention of a provision of that Part.

[241] So, s 5(1) displaces the presumption of statutory interpretation that legislation will not have an extraterritorial effect, which can also be described as the “presumption in favour of international comity”; see BHP Group Ltd v Impiombato (2022) 276 CLR 611 at [23] per Kiefel CJ and Gageler J.

[242] In analogous reasoning in Karpik v Carnival Plc (2023) 280 CLR 640 concerning s 23 of the Australian Consumer Law when read with s 5 of the CCA, it was observed (at [19]):

> The so-called common law “presumption” against extraterritoriality – that subject to a contrary intent, words in a statute describing acts, matters or things in general words are to be read so as not to have extraterritorial effect – is an interpretive principle only. …[T]he application and force of the presumption depends upon the extent to which the provisions of a statute depart from common expectations that Parliament’s concern with the subject matter is limited to matters within its territory. It may have little or no role to play where, as will be seen in relation to s 23 of the ACL read with s 5(1)(c) and (g) of the CC Act, the statute expressly departs from those common expectations. In sum, the application or consideration of the presumption cannot precede the question of interpretation – being whether the statute expressly or impliedly addresses the territorial reach of its subject matter.

> [footnotes omitted]

[243] Further, any necessary geographic nexus is built into the structure of Part IV and s 80. Relief under s 80 is premised on the finding of a contravention of Part IV. But conduct will constitute a contravention of Part IV of the Act only insofar as it has an effect on competition or trading practices in a market in Australia, within the meaning of s 4E of the Act.

[244] So, in Air New Zealand Ltd v Australian Competition and Consumer Commission (2017) 262 CLR 207 it was found that s 45 of the then TPA was contravened by conduct which occurred largely in Hong Kong, Singapore and Indonesia, because the conduct being the entering into of contracts which amounted to price fixing nonetheless occurred within a market in Australia; see at [33] to [35] per Kiefel CJ, Bell and Keane JJ.

[245] Further, s 80 is to be interpreted in light of the object of the CCA, which is (s 2) to “enhance the welfare of Australians through the promotion of competition and fair trading and provision for consumer protection”.

[246] The prohibitions in Part IV are directed at enhancing the welfare of Australians through the promotion of competition. The advancement of the objects of Pt IV extends to the protection of competition for the benefit of all persons physically located in Australia, including those who use international devices, and all app developers physically located in Australia, including those who wish to supply apps to users overseas. But considerations of practicality and comity are not irrelevant to the making of any decision as to the scope of “appropriate” relief in the circumstances of the case.

[247] Further, where an injunction is directed at conduct outside Australia, limitations in enforceability through foreign courts are not a bar to injunctive relief under s 80, but of course are a relevant consideration in the exercise of the Court’s discretion. Of course, limitations on enforceability may be of less significance in the context of statutory public interest injunctions, as compared to traditional equitable relief, because the aims of the public interest injunctive relief extend to the broader regulatory purposes of the CCA.

[248] But regardless of its geographic reach, any injunction imposed will ultimately operate as part of the domestic law of Australia. Section 80 is not capable of and does not purport to affect a person’s rights under foreign law in respect of the same or similar subject matter. Whether Australian judgments will be recognised in other jurisdictions and in what circumstances is a matter for foreign law and not for me in considering the purpose and scope of s 80, although it is not irrelevant to the exercise of discretion.

#### A little on international comity

[249] The presumption against extra–territoriality is a specific application of the general presumption that every statute is to be interpreted and applied as far as its language permits so as not to be inconsistent with the comity of nations or with established rules of international law; see Impiombato at [28] to [32] per Kiefel CJ and Gageler J.

[250] But I should also note what Gordon, Edelman and Steward JJ said (at [59], [61], [63] and [71]):

> In statutes, like the Federal Court of Australia Act, where there is no express provision relevantly addressing the territorial reach of the subject matter of the statute, the task is to identify the hinge (also referred to as the statutory springboard, general subject matter, object of legislative concern, central conception, character or central focus) of the statute and identify its territorial connection, if any. …

> …

> Only after identifying the hinge of the provisions and revealing the territorial connection (if any) of the subject matter, does the question of the application of the common law presumption against extraterritoriality arise. The so-called “presumption” is an interpretive principle whose force depends upon the extent to which the hinge of the provisions departs from common expectations that Parliament's concern with the subject matter is limited to matters within its territory. Put another way, the general common law presumption of territoriality – that an enactment describing acts, events, matters or things in general words, so that, if constrained by no consideration lying outside its expressed meaning, its application would be universal, should not be understood as extending extraterritorially – is a rule of construction only and “it may have little or no place where some other restriction is supplied by context or subject matter [of the statute in issue]”. …

> …

> Section 21(1)(b) of the Acts Interpretation Act 1901 (Cth) does not compel a different approach or give rise to a different conclusion. Section 21(1)(b) provides that “[i]n any Act … references to localities jurisdictions and other matters and things shall be construed as references to such localities jurisdictions and other matters and things in and of the Commonwealth”. Section 21(1)(b) does not answer the question: what is the matter or thing which should be construed, subject to contrary intent, as “in and of” the Commonwealth? …

> …

> It is unnecessary to fully address the question, raised in submissions, of whether the common law presumption only applies where a statute extends to subject matters over which, according to the comity of nations or international law, another sovereign or state properly has jurisdiction. That is not what Pt IVA does. For present purposes, it is sufficient to recognise that the common law presumption has been stated without reference to, and applied without reliance on, the comity of nations or international law. The “presumption” is a rule of statutory construction.

> (footnotes omitted)

[251] And as to the Acts Interpretation Act 1901 (Cth), Kiefel CJ and Gageler J said at [36]:

> The concern of s 21(1)(b) of the Acts Interpretation Act with “references to localities jurisdictions and other matters and things” in a Commonwealth statute is not with the manner of expression of a statutory reference but more substantively with the subject matter to which statutory reference is made. Its instruction that all such references are to be “construed” as “references to such localities jurisdictions and other matters and things in and of the Commonwealth” is a requirement that the statute be construed to ensure that a connection exists between the subject matter to which the statute refers, on the one hand, and the Commonwealth of Australia understood compositely as a geographically bounded polity, on the other hand. The “exact nature” of the requisite connection is not prescribed. That is left by the provision to be determined in the construction of the particular statute: “to be implied or imported upon a consideration of the context and the subject matter”.

> (footnotes omitted)

[252] The content of the general presumption was discussed by Dixon J in Barcelo v Electrolytic Zinc Company of Australasia Ltd (1932) 48 CLR 391 at 423 and 424, quoting what was said by James LJ in Niboyet v Niboyet (1878) 4 P.D. 1 at 7 that:
