> [i]t is always to be understood and implied that the legislature of a country is not intending to deal with persons or matters over which, according to the comity of nations, the jurisdiction properly belongs to some other sovereign or State.

[253] Now in Karpik, it was held that one should start with the text, context and subject matter of the statute before applying the presumption dealing with extraterritoriality. It was said at [22]:

> Princess’ submissions incorrectly invert the inquiry. This Court has recognised that there is a rule “that an enactment describing acts, matters or things in general words, so that, if restrained by no consideration lying outside its expressed meaning, its intended application would be universal, is to be read as confined to what, according to the rules of international law administered or recognized in our Courts, it is within the province of our law to affect or control”. However, that rule is “one of construction only” which “may have little or no place where some other restriction is supplied by context or subject matter”. Put in different terms, determining whether the text, context or subject matter of the statute supplies the territorial connection necessarily precedes the application or consideration of the so-called presumption, not the other way around.

[254] As an interpretative principle, the presumption should inform the proper construction of s 80(1) or the manner in which I should exercise my discretion under that section.

[255] Now Epic says that the presumption has no work to do in this case as s 80 is expressed in unqualified terms and s 5 expressly addresses extraterritorial operation. But the absence of an express territorial limit in s 80 is not determinative. Indeed, the presumption has most commonly been applied to statutes expressed in general words which if “taken literally in their widest sense, would apply to the whole world” (Impiombato at [27]).

[256] Clearly, s 80 is broad enough to permit me to prohibit or mandate acts abroad, but the question in this case is how far I can go in prohibiting or mandating acts abroad, having regard to considerations of comity.

[257] Now in Neilson v Overseas Projects Corporation of Victoria Ltd (2005) 223 CLR 331 Gummow and Hayne JJ at [90] said:

> Once Australian choice of law rules direct attention to the law of a foreign jurisdiction, basic considerations of justice require that, as far as possible, the rights and obligations of the parties should be the same whether the dispute is litigated in the courts of that foreign jurisdiction or is determined in the Australian forum. This is not a consideration which seeks uniformity for the sake of the aesthetic value of symmetry. Nor is it a precept founded in notions of international politeness or comity. As has been said, comity is “either meaningless or misleading”; it is “a matter for sovereigns, not for judges required to decide a case according to the rights of the parties”.

> (footnotes omitted)

[258] But their Honours were there addressing the limited role that comity plays in choice of law rules applicable to private causes.

[259] In CSR Ltd v Cigna Insurance Australia Ltd (1997) 189 CLR 345 at 395 and 396, the majority approved of the definition adopted by the Supreme Court of the United States in Hilton v Guyot (1895) 159 US 113 at 163 and 164, being that:

> “Comity”, in the legal sense, is neither a matter of absolute obligation, on the one hand, nor of mere courtesy and good will, upon the other. But it is the recognition which one nation allows within its territory to the legislative, executive or judicial acts of another nation, having due regard both to international duty and convenience, and to the rights of its own citizens or of other persons who are under the protection of its laws.

[260] In Lipohar v The Queen (1999) 200 CLR 485 at [100], Gaudron, Gummow and Hayne JJ referred to Hilton but added a reference to Story by saying:

> In any event, the common law doctrine of comity is neither “a matter of absolute obligation, on the one hand, nor of mere courtesy and good will, upon the other”. As Story put it, in the nature of things comity cannot “be defined and fixed”, but “must necessarily depend upon a variety of circumstances which cannot be reduced to any certain rule” …

> (footnotes omitted)

[261] Now as to s 5, as I have indicated it extends the provisions of Part IV to the engaging of conduct outside Australia by bodies corporate incorporated or carrying on business within Australia, Australian citizens, and persons ordinarily resident in Australia. So, one can have regard to conduct abroad by Australians and bodies corporate who carry on business in Australia when determining whether there has been a contravention of s 46. But it does not follow that my power to grant a remedy for any contravention arising from conduct abroad is unconstrained. I have to consider what is “appropriate”. And clearly, considerations of international comity and the courts’ traditional respect for those considerations are not irrelevant to that question. It is not irrelevant to consider whether to prohibit or mandate acts that properly fall within the legislative or regulatory competence of other nations, even if those acts have the territorial nexus described by s 5.

[262] Now Epic says that Apple has not proved a positive entitlement under any foreign law to refuse to open its platform. But of course the US courts have affirmed that their antitrust laws do not require Apple to make alternative app distribution available or to allow alternative payment solutions in apps distributed via the App Store, although steering to out-of-app payment methods must be allowed under California unfair competition law.

[263] On one view, the orders proposed by Epic would be in tension with that legislative choice and impose the requirements of Australian law on Apple when dealing with US citizens in relation to the distribution of apps occurring wholly within the US. Moreover, as Apple points out, this is what US courts have declined to do when Epic sought worldwide orders in the US courts, out of respect for the legislative and regulatory processes of other nations (Epic v Apple (US, 2021) at 1058).

[264] Further, in Epic Games Inc. v. Google LLC (ND Cal, No. 20-cv-05671-JD, 2024), Order Re UCL Claim And Injunctive Relief, at Section II, Subsection B, pages 7 to 8, Judge Donato said:

> The initial question for the injunction is where it will apply geographically. The jury found a relevant geographic market of worldwide except for China for both of the product markets, which was a conclusion that was amply supported by the evidence at trial. See JMOL Order at 14-15. Even so, the permanent injunction is limited to the United States.

> This is due mainly to principles of comity and deference to the rights of other countries to address anticompetitive conduct under their own laws and regulations. The record indicates that enforcement agencies around the world are investigating Google's conduct with respect to the Play Store. See, e.g., Dkt. No. 700 at 3 (granting Google's motion in limine to “exclude evidence or argument re foreign proceedings and investigations”); Dkt. No. 644-2 at ECF pp. 61-68 (discussing foreign investigations and regulatory reports); see also Dkt. No. 958 (Google’s Objections) at 87-89 (describing extensive legal, investigative, regulatory, and other informal proceedings underway in foreign jurisdictions). It is neither the right nor the duty of a United States court to preempt the enforcement powers of other nations by imposing an injunction that would operate within their borders. Consequently, the Court elects, as a prudential matter, not to interfere in the administration of antitrust laws outside the United States. See Mujica v. AirScan Inc., 771 F.3d 580, 598 (9th Cir. 2014) (“International comity is a doctrine of prudential abstention, one that ‘counsels voluntary forbearance when a sovereign which has a legitimate claim to jurisdiction concludes that a second sovereign also has a legitimate claim to jurisdiction under principles of international law.’”) (citation omitted).

[265] Further, regulators and legislatures in the EU and Japan have not been silent. They have put in place detailed regulatory regimes which no doubt reflect their best assessments of what promotes the public interest within their jurisdictions. Those assessments may not accord with what I consider best promotes the welfare of Australians, but it is not my prerogative to superimpose my own view over those foreign regulators and legislatures where they have turned their minds to the same or similar questions concerning the players and the platforms operating within or subject to their jurisdictions.

[266] Further, I agree with Apple that the absence of evidence or regulatory silence regarding the position in the remaining countries in which Apple does business does not mean that the entitlement of those jurisdictions to regulate dealings with their citizens or corporations is to be overridden. Those countries are entitled to introduce their own specific regulation of methods of app distribution or in-app payments for digital content. They may also have broader policy interests relevant to the regulation of app distribution for all I know. Clearly, I was not asked to and could not embark on a voyage of discovery to address such questions.

[267] So for example and as Apple put it, I need not enquire into the competition laws of Malaysia or their enforcement to recognise that the regulation of dealings between Apple and Malaysian citizens is a matter properly within the legislative and regulatory competence of Malaysia. The mere fact that some Malaysian app developers may have an attenuated territorial nexus with Australia because they supply apps to Australian consumers or wish to do so, does not mean that the legislature intended to empower me to intervene and regulate Apple’s dealings with those persons in their own home jurisdiction.

[268] Moreover, regulatory silence may equally reflect that a regulator in another jurisdiction has made a conscious choice not to regulate. A foreign regulator that did not bark may be like Conan Doyle’s canine for all I know.

[269] Now Apple goes so far as to say that having found a s 46 contravention, I am not empowered by s 80(1) to grant injunctions that intrude into areas that properly fall within the jurisdiction of other nations, namely, the extent to which those nations consider it necessary or desirable as a matter of local law and policy to require Apple to allow their citizens to access iOS apps through alternative distribution channels or pay for digital in-app content using alternative payment solutions.

[270] Apple says that I must construe the CCA and s 80 in particular having regard to the statutory subject matter and statutory context. And it also says that it is a principle of interpretation that statutory provisions should be interpreted, so far as possible, to be consistent with international law; reference was made to Kingdom of Spain v Infrastructure Services Luxembourg Sàrl (2023) 275 CLR 292 at [16].

[271] But I am empowered to grant relief that I determine to be “appropriate” within the meaning of s 80. And s 80 when read with s 5 displaces the presumption against extra–territorial effect. Further, relief will be “appropriate” if it advances the public interest objectives of the CCA by addressing or remedying the effects of Apple’s unlawful conduct. Moreover, complying with Epic’s proposed relief would not necessarily upend and supplant the legislative and regulatory balances struck by other states’ legislatures and regulators.

[272] In summary then, I do not accept Apple’s absence of power argument in terms. But in any event Apple says that each of the matters that it has relied upon concerning international comity are relevant to the exercise of my discretion under s 80, which if I might say so is a more powerful point in its favour.

#### Epic’s nexus arguments

[273] Now Epic says that there is an appropriate nexus between the relief Epic has proposed, and the supply of distribution services and payment solutions within, into and out of the markets in Australia.

[274] First, it says that the proposed relief applies to any iOS users physically located in Australia, whether or not they have an Australian Apple Account.

[275] Second, it says that the proposed relief applies to any developers that are incorporated in or carry on business in Australia, supplying services anywhere.

[276] Third, it says that the proposed relief applies to Australian citizens or persons normally resident in Australia, supplying services anywhere, or using services anywhere.

[277] Fourth, it says that the proposed relief applies to the supply of payment processing services to an Australian developer, wherever the developer or the payment supplier may be located.

[278] Fifth, it says that the proposed relief applies in the course of or in relation to trade or commerce in Australia or between Australia and another country.

[279] Sixth, it says that the proposed relief applies to the supply of goods and services to the Commonwealth or an instrumentality of the Commonwealth, wherever the Commonwealth officers, entities or instrumentalities may be located.

[280] Let me now turn to some more detailed arguments of Epic.

[281] Epic says that in Epic v Apple (US, 2021), the Court rejected Apple’s contention that its self-imposed business structures involving country specific storefronts dictated that the relevant market was geographically confined to the US. The Court explained at 991 that it:

> finds more persuasive that Apple actually treats app distribution as a global enterprise. Its rules and guidelines apply globally to all storefronts, the business development team engages with developers globally, the DPLA applies globally, and the complexity and justification for the complexity of the IAP system is due in large part because of the global nature of the business.

[282] Further, Epic says that I expressly contemplated that the relevant markets may be global, and that was consistent with a number of my findings about the services Apple supplied to developers, which were not confined to Australia.

[283] Consistently with the expert evidence, Epic says that I conducted an analysis of competitive constraints and substitution possibilities that was not limited to Australia. My findings as to the choices available to developers and the constraints their behaviour may place on Apple were, by and large, not geographically tied. This was consistent with Professor Goldfarb’s conceptual evidence, which was accepted by me and is of global application. Epic says that my findings about the relevant product do not admit of a conclusion that the product has a built in geographic limitation.

[284] Epic says that my analyses of Apple’s market power and the competitive effects of Apple’s conduct were similarly not tied to iOS users in Australia. Further, in Epic v Google, I concluded that rival app stores are concerned with the ability to compete for global, not only Australian, revenues.

[285] Further support for a global frame of reference is found in Epic v Google, where I found that the geographic scope of the payments solution market was likely global, but excluding China.

[286] Epic points out that my reasons consistently use the expressions “iOS app distribution services market” and “iOS in-app payment solutions market” without appending the adjective “Australian”. In the key summary of conclusions, I stated that Apple’s restrictions on direct downloading or sideloading had the purpose and effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS app distribution services market, and that its imposition of IAP had the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the payment solutions market. The same formulations reappear in my closing summary of findings. Those are the markets in which the contraventions are found to have occurred, and the declarations ought to reflect that language.

[287] Further, Epic says that the geographic dimension of those markets is already dealt with in my reasons. In relation to the iOS in-app payment solutions market, I said (at [5981]):

> Finally, let me just make the separate point that the economic experts agreed that the geographic dimension of the market is at least as wide as Australia. It may be global, save for any countries which have different competitive conditions from Australia, for example, due to applicable regulation. For the purposes of this case it does not make a material difference.

[288] Epic says that that observation is equally applicable to the iOS app distribution services market. The economic experts agreed that the geographic dimension of “the relevant market or markets in which to assess the competitive effects of the alleged iOS App Distribution Conduct” was at least Australia.

[289] Epic says that those markets are properly characterised as markets in Australia within the meaning of s 4E, because the relevant rivalrous behaviour and demand include Australia, even if the same economic markets extend beyond Australia. And they are not confined, as a matter of economic analysis, to some Australian-only sub-segment of the App Store or to devices linked to Australian Apple IDs.

[290] Now stopping at this point for a moment, I largely agree with Epic’s detailed description and arguments, but ultimately this does not avail Epic.

[291] As to injunctive relief, Epic says that the orders should not be confined to conduct concerning the Australian App Store storefront and iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple ID. Epic has made the following points.

[292] First, I have already found that Apple Inc carries on business in Australia for the purposes of s 5(1)(g). The relevant provisions of the CCA apply to Apple Inc. Apple Inc carries on business within Australia for the purposes of s 5(1)(g). The relevant provisions therefore apply to its conduct outside Australia.

[293] Second, by s 5(1)(g), Pt IV including s 46 applies to conduct engaged in outside Australia, by a body corporate that carries on business in Australia. Section 46(1) is directed to conduct of a corporation with a substantial degree of power “in a market” that has the purpose, or has or is likely to have the effect, of substantially lessening competition “in that market”. Section 4E then defines “market” as “a market in Australia”. The combined effect is that s 46 can, and in this case does, catch extraterritorial conduct of Apple Inc which has the proscribed purpose, effect or likely effect in markets in Australia.

[294] Third, whether there is a “market in Australia” is a practical, commercial question about where the relevant rivalrous behaviour and demand are located, not a formal question about where contracts are made or services commence. A market may be partly in and partly outside Australia. It is enough that the field of rivalry and the pursuit of customers includes Australia, even if the same economic market extends elsewhere. On my findings, each of the iOS app distribution market and the iOS in-app payment solutions market is a market “in Australia” in that sense, notwithstanding that each may also have a broader geographic dimension.

[295] Again I can agree with these three points of Epic but ultimately they do not persuade me to accept the geographic reach of what Epic seeks.

###### Proposed orders 4 to 6 (iOS app distribution services) — geography

[296] Epic says that in relation to proposed orders 4 to 6 (orders 3 to 5 as modified and made) which concern competition in iOS app distribution, I have identified an iOS app distribution market as a market for the supply of app-distribution services. Those services are supplied on both sides of the platform: to developers, by enabling or facilitating their iOS apps to be discovered, downloaded and updated by iOS device users; and to iOS device users, by enabling them to discover, obtain and update iOS apps.

[297] Epic says that developers, including Australian developers and non-Australian developers whose apps are, or but for Apple’s restrictive conduct would be, supplied in or into Australia, are customers for iOS app distribution services. They acquire those services from Apple in order to have their apps discovered, downloaded and updated by iOS device users, including users in Australia. But for Apple’s restrictive conduct, Apple and alternative app store operators whether based in Australia or elsewhere would compete to supply those iOS app distribution services to those developers. That rivalrous pursuit of developers as customers forms part of the iOS app distribution market and is competition in a “market in Australia” within the meaning of s 4E, even though the same distribution services are or may be used by those developers to reach users outside Australia.

[298] On that footing, Epic says that limiting proposed orders 4 to 6 so that they focus exclusively on iOS device users in Australia, or exclusively on iOS device users associated with an “Australian Apple ID”, rests on flawed assumptions about the relevant markets and would unjustifiably narrow the scope of the injunctive relief.

[299] Further, Epic says that on the user side of the platform, the same logic applies. But for Apple’s conduct, alternative app store operators would compete to distribute iOS apps to iOS device users in Australia. The relevant demand is that of iOS device users who are in Australia, whether as residents or as visitors, not that of a subset of users whose Apple IDs happen to be configured in a particular way.

[300] Epic says that Apple’s proposed definition of Australian Apple ID is tied to the country/region setting and to the presence of a payment method with an Australian billing address. That formulation necessarily excludes iOS device users physically in Australia whose Apple IDs are associated with non-Australian billing addresses. Epic says that those users are part of the demand-side of the iOS app distribution services market in Australia, and Apple competes, or would but for its own restrictions face competition, for their custom.

[301] Epic says that developers and payment solution providers in Australia that supply services in Australia and globally (excluding China), are also part of the supply-side of the iOS app distribution services and iOS payment solutions markets that encompass Australia, where the supply has a relevant impact on competitive rivalry in that market.

[302] Epic says that to confine the injunctions to conduct concerning the Australian App Store storefront and iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple ID is therefore to depart from the markets in which the contravention has been found and from the statutory conception of a “market in Australia”. And it would allow Apple to continue to engage in conduct, outside that artificially defined subset of storefronts and Apple IDs, which has a substantial lessening effect on competition in the iOS app distribution services market in Australia. Epic says that that result would be inconsistent with the operation of s 5 and s 4E, and it is not appropriate as a matter of remedial discretion under s 80.

###### Proposed orders 7 to 9 (iOS in-app payment solutions services) — geography

[303] Epic says that parallel considerations arise in relation to the iOS in-app payment solutions market concerning proposed orders 7 to 9 (orders 6 to 8 as modified and made). I accepted Epic’s posited market as a market for the supply of services to app developers for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases, involving the provision of a payment solution enabling or facilitating app developers to accept and process payments for in-app purchases within an iOS app. Apple’s tying of IAP and its related anti-steering measures have been found to substantially lessen competition in that market.

[304] On that footing, Epic says that the primary customers in reality in the iOS in-app payment solutions market are app developers, including Australian developers and non-Australian developers whose apps are, or but for Apple’s restrictive conduct would be, supplied in or into Australia. They acquire in-app payment solutions services so that payments for in-app purchases of digital content in their iOS apps can be accepted, processed and settled for their users. Epic says that but for Apple’s tying and anti-steering conduct, there would be competition to supply those in-app payment solutions services to developers, including in respect of transactions with users both in and outside Australia.

[305] Epic says that the rivalrous pursuit of Australian developers, and non-Australian developers whose apps serve iOS device users in Australia, is competition in a “market in Australia” within the meaning of s 4E, even if the same payment solutions are or may be used by those developers to serve users in other jurisdictions.

[306] Epic says that it follows that on the developer side, injunctive relief directed to restoring competition in the iOS in-app payment solutions market should not be confined by reference to the Australian App Store storefront or to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple ID.

###### Whether Apple’s proposed orders appropriately limited

[307] Further, Epic says that Apple’s position and proposed relief leave major gaps.

[308] First, Epic says that they do not address the supply of app distribution services to developers participating in the relevant markets in Australia, including Australian-incorporated/domiciled developers and developers whose apps are, or would be but for the contravening conduct, distributed in or into Australia, insofar as those developers distribute apps to users outside Australia, or users not associated with an Australian Apple ID.

[309] Second, Epic says that they do not address the supply of app distribution services by Australian app store operators or website operators, insofar as those operators distribute apps to users outside Australia, or users not associated with an Australian Apple ID.

[310] Third, Epic says that they do not address the supply of payment solution services to Australian developers, insofar as those developers’ apps are used by users outside Australia, or users not associated with an Australian Apple ID.

[311] Fourth, Epic says that they do not address Australian developers that steer users out-of-app to conclude transactions for digital content for use in-app, insofar as those users are outside Australia, or are not associated with an Australian Apple ID.

[312] Fifth, Epic says that they do not address the supply of payment solution services by Australian payment solution providers to developers, insofar as those developers’ apps are distributed to users outside Australia, or users not associated with an Australian Apple ID.

[313] Sixth, Epic says that in limiting its proposed order l(b) to native iOS apps downloaded from the App Store Australian storefront, Apple excludes apps providing for alternative payment solutions that have been obtained outside the App Store by direct downloading or sideloading.

[314] Moreover, Epic says that these omissions would damage, or likely damage, the future state of competition in s 4E markets in Australia. It says that the ability of Australian developers, app store and website operators, and payment solution providers to service international customers within a global market that encompasses Australia will directly affect their ability to compete with Apple, and perhaps others, in the s 4E markets, constraining their growth so they can never achieve scale sufficient to enhance the state of competition in Australia through such advancements as quality and price.

#### Analysis — what is appropriate?

[315] Even if Epic were to establish that making orders in the terms it seeks is within the power enlivened by my findings of contravention, discretionary considerations do not favour relief in the terms sought by Epic. Putting it another way, the geographic reach sought by Epic is not appropriate.

[316] I agree with Apple that to grant relief in the global terms sought would have or may have a tendency to interfere with the balances struck by regulators of other states responding to the same conduct, in ways not shown to be necessary to protect competition in the relevant markets in Australia. Generally, I agree with Apple that the grant of relief in the global terms sought by Epic is not “appropriate” within the meaning of s 80.

[317] As Apple correctly points out, there are at least three different non–standard iOS app distribution and in-app payment regimes under which Apple is now required to operate.

[318] In the US, Apple’s practice of prohibiting distribution of native iOS apps other than through the App Store storefront has been upheld as permissible under US antitrust law, such that alternative app marketplace apps and web distribution are prohibited.

[319] In the EU, Apple is required to permit both alternative app marketplaces and web distribution, but is only required to do so on specified conditions, and is permitted to apply a number of security and privacy protections to alternative distribution which Epic’s proposed orders would forbid.

[320] In Japan, Apple is required to permit alternative app marketplaces, again on specified conditions and on terms that permit Apple to apply various security and privacy protections, but is not required to permit and does not permit web distribution.

[321] In the EU, Japan and South Korea, Apple is required to permit alternatives to IAP, but is permitted to use disclosure screens for users to assess their risk appetite in proceeding with a change to the IAP process in providing their payment details to a source other than Apple, for apps distributed via the App Store.

[322] I agree with Apple that if orders were made in the terms proposed by Epic, Apple would be required to alter, reorganise and reconfigure iOS and the tools and APIs which developers use to create, distribute and maintain iOS apps, not only in respect of iOS users using devices associated with the Australian Apple Account, but in effect globally.

[323] And as Apple points out, the effect of Epic’s orders would be to forcibly restructure Apple’s supply of products and services, not only in Australia or in respect of iOS device users with an Australian Apple Account, but in respect of each of the 175 geographical regions in which Apple currently operates.

[324] I agree with Apple that this would or may inappropriately interfere with the regulatory balances struck and judicial determinations made by other states’ regulators and courts.

[325] As Apple points out, in the US, Fortnite, being among the universe of iOS apps which will become available by alternative distribution in Australia, would become accessible to users associated with the US App Store storefront by means of alternative distribution. That is an outcome which US antitrust law, as clarified through Epic’s suit against Apple in California, has not mandated.

[326] As Apple points out, in Japan, in relation to iOS users associated with the App Store Japan storefront, to whom web distribution is not currently available, Apple would be required by the operation of my orders to make web distribution available to iOS device users, notwithstanding that Japanese regulators in mandating that Apple make alternative marketplace apps available have not required Apple to permit web distribution.

[327] Further, and as Apple points out, in the EU and Japan, in respect of iOS device users associated with App Store storefronts in the EU or Japan, extant EU and Japanese law strictures as to such matters as disclosure screens and fees for alternative payments and distribution would be supplanted by my orders. Conditions on access to alternative app distribution, which Apple has been permitted to apply in these jurisdictions, would be superseded and avoided. If Apple continued to apply those conditions, conformably with the directives of regulators in those jurisdictions, it may violate my orders. Let me be more specific for a moment concerning Japan.

[328] In relation to Japan, the Mobile Software Competition Act (MSCA) Guidelines of July 2025 promulgated by the Japan Fair Trade Commission recognise a range of conditions Apple may apply as “justifiable reasons” under Articles 7 and 8 of the MSCA, including cybersecurity reviews of alternative app stores, parental control features restricting minors’ access to alternative app stores, alternative payment services and link–outs, requirements that alternative payment services properly handle payment information and ensure appropriate refund and cancellation procedures and neutral pop–up warnings informing users of the risks of redirection to deceptive or misleading websites.

[329] The Commission said (at pp 25 and 26):

> D. Understanding Justifiable Reasons

> (A) General Principles

> For Article 7 and Article 8, Items 1 to 3 of this Act (excluding cases where the specified software under Article 8, Item 3 is a browser), actions deemed necessary for ensuring cybersecurity for smartphone use, protecting information related to smartphone users, safeguarding youth who use smartphones, or achieving other objectives stipulated by the Cabinet Order (prevention of significant delays or other abnormal operations of smartphones (hereinafter referred to as “prevention of abnormal operations of smartphones”) and prevention of gambling and other criminal activities conducted using smartphones (hereinafter referred to as "prevention of criminal activities conducted using smartphones”), as defined in Article 2 of the Cabinet Order for the Mobile Software Competition Act (Cabinet Order No. 376 of 2024, hereinafter referred to as "the Order")) (collectively defined as "Ensuring Cybersecurity, etc.") may qualify as "justifiable reasons" for noncompliance if these objectives are difficult to achieve through other less competition-restricting actions. Even if a designated provider’s action appears to fall under Article 7 or Article 8, Items 1 to 3 of this Act, it will not constitute a violation of Articles 7 and 8 as long as there is an accepted justifiable reason.

> (B) Specific Examples of Justifiable Reasons

> Specific examples of the objectives for justifiable reasons listed in (A) above are as follows.

> These examples are illustrative only, and whether a justifiable reason is recognized requires individual, case-by-case examination.

> a. Ensuring cybersecurity for smartphone use refers to ensuring cybersecurity in smartphones as defined in Article 2 of the Cybersecurity Basic Act (Act No. 104 of 2014) (Note 1).

> Specifically, this includes:

> • Measures to prevent OS features from being used in a way that harms the smartphone or smartphone user due to unauthorized access by third parties to OS features.

> • Measures to prevent data stored on smartphone devices (e.g., confidential documents, location history on smartphones, authentication information related to multi-factor authentication) from being leaked, lost, or damaged due to unauthorized access by third parties.

> • Measures to prevent smartphone operation from stopping or smartphone performance from significantly deteriorating due to excessive load on smartphone features through the use of OS features (including cases where unauthorized use of Wi-Fi or other networks by third parties results in the cessation or significant deterioration of network features).

> • Measures to prevent data stored on the smartphone device (e.g., photo data, contact data) from being leaked, lost, or damaged due to defects (e.g., those caused by programming bugs) in individual software that accesses OS features on the smartphone.

> • Measures to prevent alternative application stores that do not implement measures against malicious software (malware, ransomware, etc.) from being provided on the basic operation software of a designated provider of basic operation software.

> • Measures to prevent individual software (excluding browsers) that incorporates browser engines without vulnerability countermeasures from being provided in the application stores of designated providers, etc., of basic operation software.

[330] Epic’s proposed orders may not allow that second last dot point.

[331] The Commission also said (at pp 27 and 28):

> c. Safeguarding youth who use smartphones refers to preventing minors from becoming involved in trouble or problems when using smartphones.

> Specifically, this includes:

> • Regarding in-app purchases in individual software, measures to establish parental control features in the basic operation software to prevent excessive or erroneous charges by minors, and to restrict the use of all payment systems (including alternative payment systems) by minors based on parental consent.

> • Regarding alternative application stores that provide individual software containing harmful content for minors, measures to establish parental control features in the basic operation software to prevent minors from using such alternative application stores, and to restrict their use by minors based on parental consent.

> • Measures to require alternative application stores to implement appropriate age restrictions (ratings) for individual software or content/features provided through individual software, and to restrict the use of such features, from the perspective of appropriate use by minors.

> • Measures to require alternative application stores to set stricter privacy protection standards for information related to minor smartphone users than for non-minors (e.g., not displaying targeted advertisements based on profiling of information related to minor smartphone users).

[332] Epic’s proposed order 7(c)(iii) may not allow for this.

[333] The Commission said (at pp 47 and 48):

> 4. Article 8: Prohibited Conduct by Designated Providers of Application Stores

> (1) Item 1 (Prohibition of Restricting or Hindering the Use of Alternative Payment Management Services, etc.)

> A. Basic Approach

> Article 8, Item 1 of this Act prohibits designated providers of application stores from imposing conditions that prevent individual app providers from using alternative payment management services (i.e., payment management services other than those provided by the designated provider, including its subsidiaries; hereinafter referred to as “designated providers, etc.” in this section) when offering individual software through the application store. It also forbids designated providers from obstructing individual app providers from enabling smartphone users to make payments using other methods without relying on payment management services. By prohibiting actions that restrict the use of alternative payment management services, etc. (meaning either alternative payment management services or other payment methods that individual app providers can offer to smartphone users without utilizing payment management services), this Act aims to enhance competition in individual software by allowing app providers to offer diverse payment services.

[334] The Commission said (at pp 58 and 60):

> (F) Actions that "Prevent" the Provision of Goods or Services Through Related Web Pages, etc.

> Actions that prevent the provision of goods or services through related web pages, etc., refer to conduct that, while not directly prohibiting the display of external promotional information or the provision of link-outs in the terms of use within this individual software, creates a high likelihood of making it difficult to provide goods or services through related web pages, etc., including the display of external promotional information and the provision of link-outs. Such actions include imposing unreasonable technical restrictions on individual app providers, placing excessive financial burdens on them, or steering smartphone users away from obtaining goods or services through related web pages, etc. These actions constitute "preventing" the provision of goods or services through related web pages, etc., if they create a high likelihood of making such provision practically difficult.

> The impact of such actions on the likelihood of making provisions difficult is assessed comprehensively based on various factors, including the nature of the designated provider's conduct, the duration of the conduct, the degree to which the conduct affects individual app providers offering goods or services through related web pages, etc., and the extent of impact on smartphone users utilizing the individual software.

> C. Hypothetical Scenarios

> …

> • Hypothetical Scenario 87: A designated provider, without reasonable grounds, limits the number of displayed link-out destinations or does not allow payment web pages as link-out destinations (excluding the use of pop-up displays that provide explanations, using neutral expressions, about transitions to external websites when tapping the link).

[335] The Commission said (at pp 61 and 62):

> D. Hypothetical Scenarios of Justifiable Reasons

> Based on the basic understanding of the applicability of justifiable reasons in 3 (1) D above, hypothetical scenarios where justifiable reasons are typically recognized and do not constitute violations, and hypothetical scenarios where justifiable reasons are typically not recognized and constitute violations, are as follows:

> (A) Hypothetical Scenario Where Justifiable Reasons are Accepted and Not Considered Violations

> Hypothetical scenarios of actions that are typically recognized as having justifiable reasons and are not considered violations of Article 8 include the following:

> Hypothetical Scenarios:

> • Hypothetical Scenario 93: A designated provider, as a provider of basic operation software and an application store, displays a pop-up using neutral expressions warning smartphone users that clicking on a link-out may redirect them to external websites that mimic legitimate ones in an attempt to deceive users or cause misunderstandings, and informs users that once redirected, the designated provider no longer controls the external website, because the designated provider cannot control the risk of smartphone users being redirected to such deceptive or misleading websites via link-outs. This action, while potentially causing smartphone users to abandon transitioning to external websites via link-outs and thus hindering the provision of goods or services on external websites, contributes to the prevention of criminal activities conducted using smartphones. Furthermore, if the content of the pop-up warning users, using neutral expressions, about the risk of being involved in fraudulent activities if they transition to an external website via a link-out is factual, is not discriminatory towards individual app providers, is limited to the necessary scope in light of its purpose, and if the designated provider has no way to control such risks or no more effective way to convey the content to users, it is considered that there are no other means to achieve that purpose. Therefore, a justifiable reason is recognized, and it does not violate Article 8 of the Act.

[336] Epic’s proposed orders may be against hypothetical scenario 93. Epic’s proposed orders 6(a), (e), (h), (i) and 11(a) (orders 5(a), (e), (h), (i) and 10(a) respectively as made and modified) may preclude these protections.

[337] So in summary and in terms of what I consider to be “appropriate” and/or in the exercise of my discretion, it seems to me that the appropriate balance is struck by limiting my orders to the Australian App Store and users with an Australian Apple Account. And in that respect let me make the following points.

[338] First, this limitation is consistent with focusing on markets in Australia and competition in such markets.

[339] Second, my orders will specifically allow for the participation of developers wherever they are in the world and payment solution providers wherever they are in the world, with consequential benefits to Australian users.

[340] Third, Epic poses the question: what about visitors to Australia who do not have an Australian Apple Account? But this is down in the weeds. If they are here long enough they can get one. It is their choice if this is significant enough to them. Further, Epic asks the question: what about Australian users abroad? But again, the fact that they may have to use an international Apple Account and so may not get similar access is a matter for them.

[341] Fourth, Epic makes some fair points about Australian developers, app store operators, website operators and payment solution providers who may want to extend their reach beyond Australia to international users which, if achieved, may strengthen their position in the Australian markets. That is all true, but a line needs to be drawn which is appropriate and which I have drawn.

[342] Let me now turn to the question of intellectual property rights.

#### The relevance of Apple’s intellectual property rights

[343] I made it clear in my liability judgment that intellectual property rights are not a shield against the operation of the CCA in the sense of their exercise being used as a basis for immunising a party from the operation of s 46 or their existence being used as a basis to deny an appropriate remedy for such a contravention.

[344] In NT Power Generation Pty Ltd v Power and Water Authority (2004) 219 CLR 90 it was accepted that coercive supply can be an appropriate remedy for misuse of market power. And in context it was recognised that s 46 can be attracted by two distinct types of conduct in relation to intellectual property being either the refusal to supply intellectual property, or the supply of it on particular conditions. It was said by the majority (at [85]):

> Indeed, the supposed disharmony would weigh against s 46 being used to create an access regime of any kind, and Lee J, who was of the opinion that s 46 “does not purport to interfere with the due exercise of rights of property per se”, gave various examples of the supposed inability of one competitor to obtain access to the real or personal property of another. However, private traders could be obliged to supply goods or services against their will before s 2B was enacted, provided the preconditions to s 46 liability were satisfied. Lee J accepted that this was so for intellectual property rights. The exclusion by s 51 of various types of conduct from Pt IV is limited in relation to intellectual property rights. In deciding whether Pt IV has been contravened, anything specified in, or specifically authorised by, a Commonwealth Act must be disregarded — but not an Act relating to patents, trademarks, designs or copyrights: s 51(1)(a). A contravention of a provision of Pt IV is not to be taken to have been committed by various licences and other contracts, arrangements or understandings relating to patents, registered designs, copyright and other rights, and trademarks — but this does not apply to ss 46, 46A and 48: s 51(3). The legislative scheme contemplates that whether the conduct is refusal to supply intellectual property, or the supply of it on particular conditions, s 46 can be attracted. The fact that s 46 can apply to intellectual property rights, and hence to the market power which they can give, suggests that it can apply to the use of market power derived from other property rights not specifically mentioned in the Act. It follows that, provided the notoriously difficult task of satisfying the criteria of liability can be carried out, s 46 can be used to create access regimes, and that s 2B is not to be read down as if it could not.

> (footnotes omitted)

[345] Now in the present case I found that Apple contravened s 46 in prohibiting the sideloading of native apps from sources other than the App Store and prohibiting payment methods for in app-purchases other than IAP.

[346] But Apple says that I did not find that Apple contravened s 46 by refusing to licence the software that developers use to develop apps, which is currently licensed by the DPLA. In the language of NT Power, Apple says that the contravention occurred by the supply of Apple’s software on particular conditions, not because of a refusal to supply that software. So, I said at [5305]:

> I agree with Epic that on the evidence, in a counterfactual that removes the impugned conduct, there is at least a realistic chance that Apple would license its intellectual property in a way that would permit alternative modes of iOS app distribution.

[347] Apple says that my findings justify relief directed to Apple’s prohibition on sideloading and alternatives to IAP. But it says that my findings do not give rise to a right on the part of Epic or developers generally to a compulsory licence of Apple’s software. Further, Apple says that my findings do not give rise to a right to a licence on any particular terms, so long as those terms do not prohibit sideloading or alternatives to IAP.

[348] Further, Apple says that there is no statutory regime generally providing for the compulsory licensing of software in the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth); as Apple says, one can compare this with ss 133 to 136A of the Patents Act 1990 (Cth) which deal with the compulsory licensing of patented inventions. I should say here that I accept that the regime dealt with under Part VI of the Copyright Act dealing with collective licensing organizations (see the s 136(1) definition of “licensor”), licensing schemes and the powers given to the Copyright Tribunal under s 157 is not of any present relevance; for a summary of this regime see Copyright Agency Ltd v Isentia Pty. Ltd. (2022) 405 ALR 619 at [5] to [12] per Jagot, Beach and Burley JJ.

[349] Now Apple characterises Epic’s proposed orders in the following terms.

[350] Apple says that proposed order 3(c) (order 2(c) as modified and made) is a compulsory licence for Epic of Apple’s tools and APIs including SDKs required for the development, distribution and updating of the Fortnite app and/or other apps developed by Epic for macOS and iOS.

[351] It says that proposed orders 4(a) and (b) (orders 3(a) and (b) as modified and made) would provide a compulsory licence for all developers of all tools and APIs including software development kits necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps, including alternative app stores, on the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to developers who distribute apps via the App Store.

[352] It says that proposed order 4(c) (order 3(c) as modified and made) is a compulsory licence for iOS device users of software necessary to discover, download, install or update apps via alternative distribution channels on the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to users of apps distributed via the App Store.

[353] It says that proposed order 5(a)(i) (order 4(a)(i) as modified and made) would in effect impose a compulsory licence for developers by enjoining Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action, including imposing a fee or condition, which is likely to hinder access to any tool or API including software development kits necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps and which are available to developers distributing iOS apps via the App Store.

[354] It says that proposed order 5(a)(ii) (order 4(a)(ii) as modified and made) would in effect impose a compulsory licence by enjoining Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action, including imposing a fee or condition, which is likely to hinder access to any tool or API including software development kits necessary for developers to develop iOS apps that are capable of creating an interface for displaying iOS apps and which may be used by an iOS user to discover, download, install and update iOS apps.

[355] It says that proposed order 5(a)(iii) (order 4(a)(iii) as modified and made) would in effect impose a compulsory licence for iOS users by enjoining Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action, including imposing a fee or condition, which is likely to hinder access to any tool or API including software development kits necessary for iOS users to discover, download, install and update apps via alternative distribution channels.

[356] It says that whilst not compulsory licences, proposed orders 6(b) and 11(b) (orders 5(b) and 10(b) as modified and made) would regulate the fees that Apple can charge for licensing its software to developers using alternative distribution channels, including by preventing the imposition of fees that are higher than those charged to developers of other types of apps, who are currently charged only the annual developer fee.

[357] It says that proposed order 6(b) would prevent Apple from imposing any fee or condition on a licence of any software tool, API or SDK necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps via alternative distribution channels that have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via those channels. It says that this prohibition extends to imposing any fees higher, or conditions more onerous, than those applied to free apps and apps monetised through advertising, distributed via the App Store, which are currently charged only the annual developer fee. In other words, Apple says that unless it decides to increase the fee that it charges to developers of apps that are free or monetised through advertising and distributed via the App Store, proposed order 6(b) would prevent Apple from charging developers using alternative distribution channels any fee higher than the current annual developer fee. Generally, Apple says that there is no basis in the judgment for imposing such a restriction on Apple’s licensing of its copyright works. It says that it would conflict with Apple’s normal exploitation of its copyright works including licensing for a fee and prejudice Apple’s legitimate interests as the owner of those works. And it says that proposed order 6(b) would also be anti-competitive and adverse to the interests of consumers and developers of free apps.

[358] Further, Apple says that proposed order 11(b) would prevent Apple from imposing any fees or conditions on the licence of any software tool, API or software development kit necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps that enable payment methods other than IAP, if such fees and conditions do not apply with respect to apps distributed via the App Store which enable in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP. Now at present, Apple does not impose any fees or particular conditions on apps that allow the purchase of physical goods which may not use IAP. As such, Apple says that proposed order 11(b) would appear to prevent the imposition of any fees or particular conditions on the licence of any software tool, API or SDK necessary for app developers to develop or distribute iOS apps that enable payment methods other than IAP, unless Apple were to increase fees payable by developers of apps that allow the purchase of physical goods.

[359] Generally, Apple says that there is no basis in my judgment for any of the above orders to be made.

[360] Further, Apple says that the orders proposed by it are preferable to those proposed by Epic because they preserve Apple’s freedom to deal with its own intellectual property in terms of its protected tools and technology, whilst preventing any continuation or repetition of the contraventions that I have found. In this respect Apple points to some evidence concerning alternative app distribution given by Mr Zachary Friedland, whose factual description I largely accept. I will say something more about Mr Friedland’s position and his detailed technical evidence much later.

[361] Apple was required to develop updated versions of iOS so that iOS could access the new APIs and other technologies that enable alternative app distribution. It was necessary for iOS users in the EU and Japan to download an updated version of iOS in order to use alternative app distribution, and it was not technically possible to provide alternative app distribution to users who did not update the version of iOS on their device.

[362] Now in updating the version of iOS on their devices, users make a copy of a new copyright work. That new copyright work, as well as the new APIs and other new or modified SDKs and software programs, are licensed to developers under the DPLA for use in developing and testing apps.

[363] Mr Friedland also explained that Apple makes pre-release versions of iOS updates available to developers in order to test the new functionalities and APIs. Those pre-release versions are similarly copyright works licensed to developers under the DPLA for use in developing and testing apps.

[364] Further, the updated iOS SDK now comprises the MarketplaceKit Framework. SDKs typically comprise a number of frameworks, which are a structured bundle or directory comprising a code library, image files, header files, reference documentation and APIs that are published within the framework. The MarketplaceKit Framework enables developers to create an alternative app marketplace that can function on iOS devices and facilitates installations, downloads, and updates of apps made available on alternative app marketplaces. In order to use the MarketplaceKit Framework, developers must download the updated version of the iOS SDK, making a copy of the SDK on their computer and reproducing the copyright works that comprise the SDK and the MarketplaceKit Framework specifically, such as the code in the code library, image files, header files, reference documentation and API documentation. Developers’ use of code from a code library or image files involves making further copies of those copyright works. In at least some instances, developers’ use of header files and APIs also involves reproducing Apple code. I will return to discuss the MarketplaceKit Framework in more detail later.

[365] The App Licence Delivery software tool is an example of software that Apple developed and licensed to developers, in order to facilitate alternative app distribution. The App Licence Delivery software tool works with Apple’s digital rights management and content protection system known as Fairplay. In order for an app that has been installed on an iOS device to run, Fairplay uses cryptography to confirm that the user is presently entitled to use the app. That was an existing system used by the App Store that was extended and packaged into the App Licence Delivery tool to make equivalent functionality available to developers using alternative app distribution. The App Licence Delivery software tool has an associated SDK. To use the App Licence Delivery software tool and the associated SDK, developers download them from the Apple developer website, so making a copy of the copyright works comprising the software tool and the SDK.

[366] Further, in order to enable alternative app distribution, Apple developed alternative distribution packages (ADPs). Apple’s systems generate an IPA file for each specific operating system and device. An IPA is an electronic package file which comprises everything that is needed for an Apple device to install an app. I should note that an “IPA” is more jargon than acronym. IPA files are used for App Store distribution such that the developer does not require a licence of Apple’s code that has been incorporated into the IPA file, because it is Apple that makes the IPA files available for downloading. Developers download ADPs, and the IPAs packaged in them to their own servers to make them available to users to download via alternative distribution. In doing so, developers use the copyright in Apple’s code that has been incorporated into the IPAs and the ADPs.

[367] Mr Friedland’s evidence also discussed numerous new APIs that have been developed in order to facilitate alternative app distribution and in-app payment solutions, including the MarketplaceAppExtension APIs, the External Purchase APIs, and others. I will elaborate on the concept of MarketplaceAppExtension later. I have found that Apple’s APIs are a valuable resource and their use may involve reproducing Apple code and so a copyright work and likely also using Apple’s confidential information.

[368] It is convenient now to address another topic concerning Apple’s reference to some international treaties. Let me identify at this point some relevant provisions before addressing the parties’ arguments.

###### Some international treaties

[369] Australia is a party to several treaties that require it to respect the exclusive rights of intellectual property owners, including the Berne Convention for the Protection of Literary and Artistic Works of 9 September 1886, as revised [1978] ATS 5, the Agreement on Trade–Related Aspects of Intellectual Property Rights (TRIPS) being annexure 1C to the Marrakesh Agreement establishing the World Trade Organisation [1995] ATS 8, and the World Intellectual Property Organisation Copyright Treaty (Geneva, 20 December 1996) [2007] ATS 17.

[370] Membership of the Berne Convention and TRIPS is near–universal and membership of the WIPO Copyright Treaty is also widespread. Australia, the United States, Japan and all countries in the EU are parties to all three treaties.

[371] Articles 9(1) and (2) of the Berne Convention provide:

> (1) Authors of literary and artistic works protected by this Convention shall have the exclusive right of authorizing the reproduction of these works, in any manner or form.

> (2) It shall be a matter for legislation in the countries of the Union to permit the reproduction of such works in certain special cases, provided that such reproduction does not conflict with a normal exploitation of the work and does not unreasonably prejudice the legitimate interests of the author.

[372] By article 2(6) the protections of the Berne Convention extend to authors and their successors in title. Article 4 of the WIPO Copyright Treaty and article 10(1) of TRIPS both provide that computer programs will be protected as literary works for the purposes of the Berne Convention.

[373] Article 9(2) of the Berne Convention provides that member countries’ legislation may permit the reproduction of literary works in “certain special cases”, provided that the reproduction “does not conflict with a normal exploitation of the work and does not unreasonably prejudice the legitimate interests of the author”. Equivalent provisions are found in article 10 of the WIPO Copyright Treaty and article 13 of TRIPS.

[374] Articles 10(1) and (2) of the WIPO Copyright Treaty provide:

> (1) Contracting Parties may, in their national legislation, provide for limitations of or exceptions to the rights granted to authors of literary and artistic works under this Treaty in certain special cases that do not conflict with a normal exploitation of the work and do not unreasonably prejudice the legitimate interests of the author.

> (2) Contracting Parties shall, when applying the Berne Convention, confine any limitations of or exceptions to rights provided for therein to certain special cases that do not conflict with a normal exploitation of the work and do not unreasonably prejudice the legitimate interests of the author.

[375] In article 13 of TRIPS, limitations are expressly limited to those that do not prejudice the legitimate interests “of the right holder”. Article 13 provides:

> Members shall confine limitations or exceptions to exclusive rights to certain special cases which do not conflict with a normal exploitation of the work and do not unreasonably prejudice the legitimate interests of the right holder.

[376] Further, article 40 of TRIPS specifically allows for legislation concerning intellectual property “licensing practices or conditions” that may have an “adverse effect on competition in the relevant market”, provided that such measures are adopted “consistently with the other provisions of this Agreement”. Articles 40(1) to (4) provide:

> (1) Members agree that some licensing practices or conditions pertaining to intellectual property rights which restrain competition may have adverse effects on trade and may impede the transfer and dissemination of technology.

> (2) Nothing in this Agreement shall prevent Members from specifying in their legislation licensing practices or conditions that may in particular cases constitute an abuse of intellectual property rights having an adverse effect on competition in the relevant market. As provided above, a Member may adopt, consistently with the other provisions of this Agreement, appropriate measures to prevent or control such practices, which may include for example exclusive grantback conditions, conditions preventing challenges to validity and coercive package licensing, in the light of the relevant laws and regulations of that Member.

> (3) Each Member shall enter, upon request, into consultations with any other Member which has cause to believe that an intellectual property right owner that is a national or domiciliary of the Member to which the request for consultations has been addressed is undertaking practices in violation of the requesting Member's laws and regulations on the subject matter of this Section, and which wishes to secure compliance with such legislation, without prejudice to any action under the law and to the full freedom of an ultimate decision of either Member. The Member addressed shall accord full and sympathetic consideration to, and shall afford adequate opportunity for, consultations with the requesting Member, and shall cooperate through supply of publicly available non-confidential information of relevance to the matter in question and of other information available to the Member, subject to domestic law and to the conclusion of mutually satisfactory agreements concerning the safeguarding of its confidentiality by the requesting Member.

> (4) A Member whose nationals or domiciliaries are subject to proceedings in another Member concerning alleged violation of that other Member's laws and regulations on the subject matter of this Section shall, upon request, be granted an opportunity for consultations by the other Member under the same conditions as those foreseen in paragraph 3.

[377] Further, I should also note article 1(1) of TRIPS which provides:

> (1) Members shall give effect to the provisions of this Agreement. Members may, but shall not be obliged to, implement in their law more extensive protection than is required by this Agreement, provided that such protection does not contravene the provisions of this Agreement. Members shall be free to determine the appropriate method of implementing the provisions of this Agreement within their own legal system and practice.

[378] And I should also note that articles 8(1) and (2) of TRIPS provide:

> (1) Members may, in formulating or amending their laws and regulations, adopt measures necessary to protect public health and nutrition, and to promote the public interest in sectors of vital importance to their socio-economic and technological development, provided that such measures are consistent with the provisions of this Agreement.

> (2) Appropriate measures, provided that they are consistent with the provisions of this Agreement, may be needed to prevent the abuse of intellectual property rights by right holders or the resort to practices which unreasonably restrain trade or adversely affect the international transfer of technology.

[379] I will say something about the Australia–US Free Trade Agreement (Washington, 18 May 2004) later.

###### Apple’s arguments based on these treaties

[380] Now because I found that Apple’s prohibitions on alternative app distribution via direct downloading and alternative app stores contravene s 46, that conduct can be enjoined in Australia, consistent with Australia’s obligations under the Berne Convention, the WIPO Copyright Treaty and TRIPS.

[381] But Apple submits that other aspects of Epic’s proposed injunctions would, if made, put Australia in breach of its obligations under the Berne Convention, TRIPS and the WIPO Copyright Treaty.

[382] Apple says that Epic’s proposed orders seek to impose terms upon Apple’s licensing of its IP-protected software, tools and technologies that go far beyond enjoining the prohibition of alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions. In doing so, Apple says that they seek to prohibit Apple from licensing its proprietary software on terms that have not been found to contravene the CCA. Apple says that that would conflict with its normal exploitation of the copyright in its software and other copyright works and prejudice its legitimate interests as the owner of that copyright. So, Apple says that Epic’s proposed injunctions are not consistent with Australia’s obligations under the Berne Convention, TRIPS and the WIPO Copyright Treaty.

[383] Further, Apple says that the lack of any geographic limitation in Epic’s proposed injunctions also offends Australia’s obligations under the Berne Convention, TRIPS and the WIPO Copyright Treaty. It says that those treaties allow for members to include exceptions to copyright owners’ rights in their national legislation, in “certain special cases” that do not conflict with the normal exploitation of the work or unreasonably prejudice the copyright owner’s legitimate interests. And Apple had to accept that one particular type of exception recognised in article 40 of TRIPS is members’ legislation regarding licensing practices that have an “adverse effect on competition in the relevant market”. But Apple says that that exception, and the more general exceptions for “certain special cases” in article 9 of the Berne Convention and article 10 of the WIPO Copyright Treaty, should not be understood to extend to Australia regulating the terms on which Apple licenses its copyright so that iOS apps may be developed and then distributed in markets outside Australia. It says that for the purposes of article 40, for example, the “relevant market”, which state parties may regulate, is plainly their own national market, not a global or international market.

[384] Further, Apple says that the same licence conditions that I have found to contravene the CCA have been found not to contravene US antitrust law. Apple says that this underscores why a contravention of one State party’s competition law would not be understood to give rise to an entitlement under article 40 of TRIPS to regulate Apple’s copyright licensing on an international or global basis.

[385] Generally, Apple says that Epic’s proposed orders would inappropriately interfere with other States’ intellectual property laws by imposing compulsory licences of global territorial scope in conflict with other States’ laws that enshrine Apple’s exclusive rights as a copyright owner. And in particular, Apple says that Epic’s global compulsory licences would conflict with US copyright law, which grants Apple exclusive rights in those copyright works, as well as the copyright laws of other states that also provide for those exclusive rights in their own intellectual property laws consistent with their obligations under the Berne Convention, TRIPS and the WIPO Copyright Treaty.

###### Analysis

[386] Now as I have already indicated, Apple in the present case seeks to characterise the contravening conduct as the supply of Apple’s intellectual property on particular conditions, rather than as any refusal to supply that intellectual property. Apple says that the contraventions found by me consist only in a prohibition on the sideloading native iOS apps from sources other than the App Store and a prohibition on the use of payment methods other than IAP for in-app purchases of digital content.

[387] Given the contraventions so framed, Apple says that my findings justify only relief directed to removing those prohibitions, and do not support what it describes as any compulsory licence or right to a licence on particular terms, because the contravening conduct did not involve a refusal to supply IP.

[388] Now as Epic correctly says, underpinning this position is the enthymematic fallacy that for the purposes of my remedial powers Apple’s conduct must be divided into two mutually exclusive categories. The first category is conduct that prohibits the sideloading of native iOS apps and the use of third party payment solutions for in-app purchases of digital content. The second category is ancillary conduct that could or did assist those prohibitions, including Apple’s ability to withhold the tools, technologies and supporting IP necessary for those alternatives to function.

[389] Now Apple contends that my findings justify relief directed only to the first category, but not the second category. But I would reject Apple’s position.

[390] First, dividing Apple’s contravening conduct into direct prohibitions and supposedly ancillary measures is inconsistent with my findings as to the practical manner in which Apple has foreclosed competition in the relevant markets. I accepted that the use of Apple’s tools and technology, supported by Apple’s IP, is essential in order for a developer to distribute native apps and digital in-app content on iOS devices. I also found that unless legally compelled to do so, Apple has never licensed those tools, technologies or supporting IP to enable the distribution of apps or the conduct of in-app transactions for digital content outside its walled garden. So, the prohibitions on sideloading and on third party payment solutions did not operate in isolation. They were reinforced and made effective by terms which enabled Apple to withhold the tools, technologies and supporting IP necessary for sideloading and third party payment solutions to operate in practice. It is the way in which this intricate web of terms and technical measures operate together to foreclose competition in the relevant markets that I found contravenes s 46.

[391] Apple seeks to constrain, at a practical level, my power to remedy the contravening conduct. But I am not confined to granting relief which merely removes specific prohibitions. Section 80 permits me to formulate the appropriate injunctive relief to suit the needs of the case. Apple’s narrow form of relief would leave it free to engage in conduct that would render my orders ineffective. Contrastingly, the orders proposed by Epic respond directly to the way the contravening conduct operates in practice, including Apple’s use of its IP, tools and technologies as part of the overall foreclosure mechanism.

[392] Second, Apple relies on NT Power at [85] to suggest that a refusal to supply is a distinct category of conduct from supply on conditions, and that it necessarily follows that distinct remedial consequences attach to each refusal to supply may, on Apple’s case, support an order in the nature of coercive supply, whereas supply of IP on particular conditions may only be remedied by injunctions directed at those conditions. And that artificial dichotomy underpins Apple’s contention that I cannot grant relief in the nature of coercive supply.

[393] But s 46 can be attracted by either conduct consisting of a refusal to supply IP or conduct consisting of supply on particular conditions. And I agree with Epic that what was said in NT Power at [85] does not purport to classify those as distinct species of contravention to which different and limited remedies attach. As I have already held, if the conduct of a corporation in respect of a market otherwise satisfies the terms of s 46, the corporation thereby commits a contravention, a consequence of which might include an order requiring the contravener to give competitors access to its property.

[394] Once a contravention of s 46 is established my remedial powers are relevantly governed by s 80, which is concerned with whether the proposed orders are appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy the contraventions and their effects. Section 80 is not confined to orders that mirror, in form, the precise way in which the contravention was committed.

[395] I agree with Epic that nothing in NT Power prevents me from making orders in the nature of coercive supply in an appropriate case, even if one were to accept Apple’s characterisation of the conduct as the supply of IP on particular conditions, and not a refusal to supply IP.

[396] Third, Apple’s submission also mischaracterises my findings about the ways in which Apple licenses its IP. I found that Apple contravened s 46 by imposing and enforcing contractual conditions that prevent app distribution other than through the App Store, and equivalent conditions. Those terms are contained in instruments such as the DPLA and its schedules. By contrast, the intellectual property on which Apple now relies is licensed through separate contractual arrangements which I found were exogenous to the DPLA.

[397] The Xcode agreement authorises developers to substantially reproduce “Apple Software”, comprising Xcode, Apple SDKs and Apple APIs including any SDKs or APIs that may be necessary for a developer to use in the development of an iOS app, in the executable code of apps developed for inter-alia iOS. Apple’s Xcode is the default integrated development environment being a software application for developing apps for Apple devices.

[398] The iOS Software Licence Agreement authorises iOS device users to reproduce Apple Software by downloading and installing apps on an iOS device, thereby creating a copy of the apps’ executable code.

[399] I concluded that Apple’s existing IP licensing arrangements are exogenous to the DPLA and likely would, absent the impugned conduct, permit alternative modes of iOS app distribution. Further, as I have indicated in my liability reasons, schedule 2 to the DPLA imposes conditions on developers who choose to offer in-app purchases of digital goods. But schedule 2 does not apply to developers who monetise their apps in other ways, even though those developers use the same Apple software in the same manner. And as I found, the 30% service fee required by schedule 2 is not a licence fee in respect of any Apple IP. For the same reason, the conditions in schedule 2 are not conditions in respect of any IP licence. Developers who monetise through other models can continue to use the same IP in an identical way without accepting those conditions.

[400] Now Apple’s position appears to be that whilst its contravention of s 46 justifies injunctions restraining Apple from preventing or prohibiting the direct downloading of native iOS apps and from preventing or prohibiting developers and users from using payment methods other than IAP, my liability findings otherwise leave Apple free to exploit its IP rights as it sees fit, including by withholding the tools, technology and supporting IP that I have found are essential to iOS app development and distribution, and also essential to the incorporation of software features in an iOS app to facilitate the use of alternative payment methods.

[401] But Apple could reproduce the same substantial lessening of competition by shifting from what it appears to characterise as a supply on exclusionary conditions, to outright refusal to supply the necessary IP and tools.
