But I have already rejected Apple’s case theory that because it holds exclusive rights over certain broadly asserted intellectual property it is thereby free to exploit those rights as it sees fit.

[402] In my view Epic’s proposed relief explicitly preserves Apple’s ability to continue to license its tools and APIs for the development of iOS apps and to impose lawful fees or conditions for the use of that IP.

[403] What the orders would prevent is Apple using its IP licensing arrangements as a means of recreating the circumstances or conditions that I have found to contravene s 46. Given Apple’s foreshadowed threat to replicate the impugned conduct through a complete or constructive refusal to license its IP, it is appropriate to frame the injunctions so that Apple must provide access to the relevant IP in a manner that does not have the exclusionary purpose or effect identified in the reasons. Failing to do so would leave Apple at liberty to avoid my remedies by declining to license that IP at all, or by licensing it only on terms that would perpetuate the same substantial lessening of competition.

[404] Fourth, Apple contends that in determining relief I should have regard to the absence in the Copyright Act of a general compulsory licensing regime of software copyright, beyond specified collective licensing regimes. But Epic’s proposed orders are not a compulsory licence as such and nor do they confer on Epic or any other developers any free-standing proprietary rights in Apple’s copyright. And to the extent that certain orders require Apple to continue making available its tools and APIs, they are orders in the nature of coercive supply of the kind recognised as permissible in NT Power and in my reasons. The orders Epic seeks do not establish a free-standing compulsory licensing regime for software. They enjoin Apple from deploying or restructuring its IP licensing arrangements in a way that would allow the recurrence of, or continuing effects from, the contravening conduct.

[405] Now as I have discussed previously in my liability reasons, s 51(1)(a) together with the repeal of former s 51(3) make clear that Parliament did not intend intellectual property rights to provide a broad shield to contraventions of Pt IV. That result is beyond doubt following the repeal of s 51(3). But in any event s 51(3) never applied to contraventions of s 46.

[406] Once Apple’s use of its IP including copyright has been found to form part of its misuse of market power in markets in Australia, the remedial response is governed by the CCA.

[407] Now I have found that the use of Apple’s property is essential for a developer to create native iOS apps, and that in order to distribute such apps and digital in-app content on iOS devices, third parties require permission to access and use a suite of tools and technologies offered by Apple, supported by Apple’s IP.

[408] To grant appropriate remedies for Apple’s misuse of market power, it is therefore necessary to make orders ensuring that the necessary tools and technologies are licensed in a way that permits the development and distribution of iOS apps and in-app content via alternative modes of distribution. I should not now make orders that would leave scope for Apple to refuse to license the IP necessary to remedy its contraventions of s 46, or to license it on terms that would have the same or similar effect as the impugned conduct.

[409] Further, Apple wrongly submits that Epic’s proposed orders would have the effect of eliminating or severely curtailing Apple’s ability to seek compensation for tools, technologies and services it provides in the Australian market. The true position is that Epic’s proposed orders only prevent Apple from using fees, conditions and technical dependencies to hinder developers’ practical freedom to use alternative app distribution channels or payment methods.

[410] Epic’s proposed relief does not eliminate Apple’s ability to seek compensation for the use of its intellectual property. I have already found that Apple’s intellectual property licensing terms are exogenous to the restrictive terms, and likely would, absent the impugned conduct, permit alternative modes of iOS app distribution.

[411] Epic’s proposed orders preserve Apple’s ability to license its tools and APIs: proposed orders 6(b), 6(k), 10(e), 11(b) (orders 5(b), 5(k), 9(e) and 10(b) respectively as modified and made). What they curtail is Apple’s ability to impose fees or conditions in ways that would, in practice, perpetuate the impediments to competition already found by me. This is not a hypothetical concern. As I found, in the European Union, Apple’s core technology fee and the restrictions implemented more generally in response to the Digital Markets Act (Regulation (EU) 2022/1925) (DMA) were designed to dissuade developers from taking up alternative payment solutions. I will deal further with the DMA later in my reasons.

[412] Further, as to Epic’s proposed orders 10(b) and 10(d) (orders 9(b) and 9(d) as modified and made). The former relates to reporting and audit rights in respect of transactions processed using an alternate payment method, while the latter relates to the payment of fees to Apple in connection with transactions for digital content for use in apps distributed via an alternative channel. Apple submits that those orders would effectively prohibit Apple from seeking compensation for the value of its tools, technologies and services provided to developers in respect of apps and payments made via alternative channels. But proposed orders 10(b) and 10(d) do not prevent Apple from licensing its tools and APIs, or from obtaining lawful remuneration for use of those tools and APIs, nor for services which Apple actually provides. The order only relates to specific commercial transactions, between parties that do not include Apple, in circumstances where Apple is not providing the relevant payment service.

[413] My findings support the conclusion that imposing fees on those transactions deters the uptake of alternative payment solutions, rather than compensates Apple for any service it provides.

[414] In that limited way, Epic’s proposed orders rule out one anti-competitive method by which Apple might choose to monetise its intellectual property. But that limitation does not prevent Apple from monetising its intellectual property otherwise than by “taxing” transactions that take place between developers and users for the purchase of digital content, but not physical goods or services, via a payment method which is not supplied by Apple. The existence of that narrow limitation is not a sound objection to the relief sought. It is necessary to prevent Apple from relocating its anti-competitive restrictions to the conditions that govern the licensing of and access to tools, APIs and functionality that are essential if competition is to emerge in the payments market.

[415] Contrastingly, Apple’s proposed orders would leave it free to impose “reasonable terms” on access to the tools and APIs necessary to enable alternative iOS app distribution and payment methods: Apple’s proposed orders 2(b) and 3(b). Apple would thereby be able to use its control over access to its intellectual property and developer tools to continue hindering or preventing competition in practice. I do not propose to grant relief which leaves Apple with a very broad latitude to determine what “reasonable” terms it will impose for access to the tools and APIs necessary to distribute iOS apps via alternative channels or to use alternative payment methods for the purchase of digital content.

[416] Further, Epic’s proposed orders would not put Australia in breach of treaty obligations. Article 40 of TRIPS is titled “Control of Anti-competitive Practices in Contractual Licences”. In article 40(1), Members agree that licensing practices or conditions that restrain competition “may have adverse effects on trade”. Article 40(2) then preserves members’ power to adopt “appropriate measures to prevent or control” practices that have an “adverse effect on competition in the relevant market”.

[417] Section 80 is such a measure and it empowers me to make orders remedying serious and prolonged contraventions of s 46 and preventing Apple from achieving the same anti-competitive outcomes via alternative means. In this regard, it is also to be recalled that I held (at [1431]) that s 46 “can be used to create access regimes, including with respect to intellectual property rights, and so to the market power which they can give”.

[418] Finally, article 8(2) of TRIPS affirms that “[a]ppropriate measures ... may be needed to prevent the abuse of intellectual property rights by right holders”. The word “abuse” maps onto my finding that Apple has misused its market power, much of which derives from its IP rights.

[419] I said at [1362], [1363] and [1385]:

> Now Apple says that the point is particularly acute where the property in question is intellectual property. It says that this is so because intellectual property arises from statutory norms which offer limited monopoly rights to incentivise investment and innovation. It says that the good or service is, because of the intellectual property rights which define it, incapable of separation from the identity of the supplier.

> Firms compete to innovate and acquire those statutory rights. So, when one asks of an intellectual property holder who might have a degree of market power by virtue of its IP rights, whether it would be able to restrict supply in competitive conditions, one cannot take a temporal perspective that excludes the anterior competition for the IP rights.

> …

> As Epic points out, to posit that Apple’s exclusive control over any relevant market arises by reason of Apple’s lawful control over its intellectual property begs the question as to whether, on the facts of this case, Apple’s control has been lawfully exercised. Neither intellectual property rights nor the terms of an intellectual property licence can provide any shield against the operation of the CCA in the absence of a relevant statutory exemption or exception.

[420] The three–step test in article 13 of TRIPS, article 9(2) of the Berne Convention, and article 10 of the WIPO Copyright Treaty governs legislated limitations and exceptions to exclusive rights. An order under s 80 is not such a limitation or exception. In any event, the test is satisfied.

[421] The proposed orders address a certain special case being identified contravening conduct in identified markets. They do not conflict with any normal exploitation of Apple’s copyright. The concept of “normal exploitation” does not include modes of exploitation that a court has determined must be addressed to remedy the effects of a contravention of competition law, nor modes of exploitation that, in my assessment of Apple’s conduct, would risk being deployed to reproduce the anti-competitive effects of the conduct that I have found to contravene s 46.

[422] And nor do they unreasonably prejudice Apple’s legitimate interests. Any order will, ex hypothesi, be one that I have found to be appropriate, and therefore reasonable, to remedy Apple’s contravening conduct and its effects and to prevent its continuation, whether in the same or a different guise. Apple has no such legitimate interest.

[423] Further, Apple says that the absence of a geographic limitation in Epic’s proposed orders offends Australia’s treaty obligations. But article 40(2) of TRIPS does not exclude legislation that may operate on or in respect of a market that includes, but which is not confined to, Australia. Apple’s contrary reading is inconsistent with the object and purpose of article 40(1), which recognises that anti-competitive licensing practices “may have adverse effects on trade”, that is, on commerce across borders. Further, the fact that Apple’s restrictive terms have not been found to contravene US antitrust law is irrelevant. Article 1(1) preserves members’ freedom to implement TRIPS “within their own legal system and practice”. And as Epic points out, differences in competition enforcement across member states are the premise of article 40, not an objection to its operation. But I do not need to linger further on the geographic limitation question given that, as against Epic’s position, I am going to impose a geographic limitation on the users and the App Store in the orders that I make.

[424] Further, even if I was satisfied that Epic’s proposed orders would put Australia in breach of the TRIPS, the Berne Convention or the WIPO Copyright Treaty, Apple identifies no mechanism by which that conclusion would operate as a constraint on my power under s 80. Despite some commentary both vain and in vain from top down academic types who would wish or pretend otherwise, treaties unincorporated by legislation do not form part of Australian domestic law. Although statutes should be construed consistently with such obligations where a constructional choice is available, Parliament may legislate inconsistently with international obligations. Section 80 confers a broad discretion in terms that admit of no constructional choice to be made by reference to the treaties that Apple identifies. Moreover and as I have said, the repeal of s 51(3) removed the only IP-specific carve-out from Part IV, and s 51(3) never applied to s 46 in any event. Apple’s tendentious treaty entreaties go nowhere.

[425] Further, let it be accepted that Australia’s international obligations are to be weighed as part of my discretionary considerations, which I have done.

[426] Take chapter 14 of the Australia–US Free Trade Agreement (Washington, 18 May 2004) [2005] ATS 1, articles 14.2(1) to (3) of which provide:

> (1) Each Party shall maintain or adopt measures to proscribe anticompetitive business conduct and take appropriate action with respect thereto, recognizing that such measures will help realise the objectives of this Agreement. To this end, the Parties shall consult from time to time about the effectiveness of measures that a Party has undertaken. Each Party shall ensure that a person subject to the imposition of a sanction or remedy for violation of such measures is provided with the opportunity to be heard and to present evidence, and to seek review of such sanction or remedy in a court or independent tribunal of that Party.

> (2) Each Party shall maintain an authority or authorities responsible for the enforcement of its national competition laws. The enforcement policy of each Party’s central government authorities responsible for the enforcement of such laws includes treating non-nationals no less favourably than nationals in like circumstances, and each Party’s authorities intend to maintain this policy, in that regard.

> (3) The Parties recognize the importance of cooperation and coordination between their respective authorities to further effective competition law enforcement in the free trade area. The Parties shall cooperate in relation to the enforcement of competition laws and policy, including through mutual assistance, notification, consultation, and exchange of information.

> (a) The Parties recognize their existing mechanisms for cooperation in relation to competition law enforcement, specifically:

> (i) The Agreement between the Government of Australia and the Government of the United States of America relating to Cooperation on Antitrust Matters of 1982; and

> (ii) The Agreement between the Government of Australia and the Government of the United States of America on Mutual Antitrust Enforcement Assistance of 1999.

> (b) The Parties shall work to further strengthen their cooperation in these areas. Such cooperation shall include consideration by a Party’s central government authorities responsible for the enforcement of its competition laws, where feasible and appropriate, of a request by the other Party’s central government authorities responsible for the enforcement of its competition laws to initiate or expand enforcement activities.

[427] An order under s 80, being an effective remedial response to my findings of contravention of s 46 is “appropriate action” of the kind article 14.2(1) contemplates.

[428] Further, Apple has invoked Australia’s obligations under the Berne Convention, TRIPS and the WIPO Copyright Treaty to seek to narrow the scope of my orders, but there is an evidentiary gap that underlies them.

[429] I have already found that Apple failed to prove the value of any intellectual property rights that Apple said would inevitably need to be licensed by the developer of an iOS app, and that consequently I could not speculate as to what a commercial return might be if Apple were to monetise its investments differently in the counterfactual. I said at [5338]:

> Now Apple says that there is no evidence that any licence fee it might lawfully charge would be so low, or the other terms it might offer would be sufficiently attractive, as to permit viable third-party stores or direct downloading. But Apple has failed to prove the value of any intellectual property rights that Apple says would inevitably need to be licensed by the developer of an iOS app. Accordingly, I cannot speculate as to what a commercial return might be, if Apple were in the counterfactual to seek to monetise its investments in some different way.

[430] That finding sits alongside my rejection of Apple’s s 51(3)(a) defence on the basis that Apple had not identified any specific inventions or works of the relevant kind, with the result that it could not discharge its burden of establishing the factual elements of that defence.

[431] The relief stage has given Apple a further opportunity to address that gap. It has not done so. Mr Friedland’s evidence identifies categories of technology said to facilitate alternative distribution and payments.

[432] That evidence does not engage with my findings on the evidence at trial. I said at [6183] to [6185]:

> There is no evidence that any code provided by Apple to developers is executed by an iOS app when the app is being displayed within the iOS App Store, downloaded by a user from the iOS App Store or subsequently updated. That is, there is no evidence that any Apple code is exploited by developers in relation to iOS app distribution.

> Accordingly, the iOS restrictive terms, to the extent that they preclude developers from distributing their native iOS apps through alternative channels to the iOS App Store, are not conditions which impose restrictions on the use of any intellectual property rights of Apple in relation to app distribution. They are not conditions “of” any licence to use the intellectual property rights in any Apple software or tools to develop native iOS apps.

> Further, there is no evidence that, absent the requirement in the DPLA that developers must use IAP for in-app purchases of digital content within iOS apps, Apple’s iOS SDK and APIs would be exploited by a developer implementing an alternative payment solution for their iOS app.

[433] That evidence does not provide a value for any Apple intellectual property it identifies.

[434] That evidence does not clearly distinguish between intellectual property developed specifically to enable alternative distribution and payments, and pre-existing intellectual property that is already deployed in the App Store and in connection with IAP.

[435] That evidence does not identify what promotion of that intellectual property is already compensated through the USD 99 annual developer program fee, which I found is the consideration for the licence of Apple Software under the DPLA.

[436] That evidence says comparatively little about the intellectual property nexus for in-app steering and linked-out payments, as distinct from alternative app distribution.

[437] To the extent Apple’s complaint is that Epic’s proposed orders cut down its freedom to monetise its intellectual property differently in the future, that submission carries no weight. Apple’s freedom is a freedom to monetise lawfully, which Epic’s proposed orders preserve. What the orders prevent is Apple deploying its IP–licensing arrangements as a mechanism of foreclosure of the kind that I have already condemned.

[438] If Apple in the future wishes to restructure its monetisation in a way that is consistent with my findings, it remains free to do so. And it can seek any necessary variation of the orders at that point, on a properly developed evidentiary basis. What Apple cannot do is resist the making of remedial orders now by appeal to future monetisation strategies whose contours and justification it has not articulated.

[439] The position is supported by a related observation. At no point has Apple identified the business model, including the IP-licensing model, that it would propose to adopt under either its own proposed orders or Epic’s. Nor has it chosen to call a witness who could give that evidence. In such circumstances, I discount any weight to be given to any complaint that Epic’s proposed orders interfere with a lawful commercial model Apple has, or might have, in mind.

[440] Apple’s IP–based complaints, including those resting on Australia’s international obligations, ultimately depend upon the proposition that Epic’s proposed orders deprive Apple of a commercial return on identifiable, valuable intellectual property that is in some material way distinct from what Apple already monetises through existing arrangements. Apple bore the burden of establishing that proposition. It has not done so.

[441] Further, s 80 operates as part of the domestic law of Australia. It is not capable of affecting, and does not purport to affect, Apple’s rights under foreign law. Whatever exclusive rights US copyright law or elsewhere confers on Apple are unaffected.

[442] Let me now turn to a different topic concerning Apple’s position in other jurisdictions. The conduct of Apple in other jurisdictions that I am about to discuss is not irrelevant context that in part informs my exercise of discretion when dealing with the need for the precise orders that I intend to make, the terms of which I will discuss in more detail in later sections.

[443] Now I have previously discussed questions of international comity, but what I am here discussing is more focused on Apple’s behaviour in other jurisdictions and how this has been dealt with.

#### Apple’s behaviour in other jurisdictions

[444] In my liability findings I discussed the practices that Apple has adopted in other jurisdictions to circumvent the effect of regulatory obligations in connection with the App Store.

[445] In response to regulatory developments in South Korea and the Netherlands and Apple’s decision to impose a 26% or 27% commission on developers who opted to use a third party payment solution for in-app purchases in those jurisdictions, I found that those commissions were uneconomic, providing a choice that was illusory, and that Apple had “set the commission rates to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions” with the effect that Apple had replaced a contractual tie with an economic tie: [5881], [5884] and [5995].

[446] In response to the DMA, Apple’s response to being forced to permit developers to use alternative payment solutions for in-app purchases was to impose “a series of restrictions designed to dissuade developers from taking up that option, including an obligation to pay a core technology fee”: [5830].

[447] Epic has filed further evidence addressing developments concerning Apple in California and the European Union that have occurred since the trial concluded on 4 July 2024.

[448] In response to regulatory or judicial constraints imposed in connection with the App Store that are anything other than detailed, prescriptive and precise, Apple has systematically implemented practices that seek to circumvent the practical effects of those constraints.

[449] In combination with my earlier findings, these matters are relevant to informing the type of relief to be ordered in these proceedings so as to ensure that the distribution and payments markets are opened to competition effectively.

###### Developments in the United States

[450] In the California proceeding, Apple’s anti-steering restrictions were found to contravene California’s unfair competition laws. As a consequence, Judge Gonzalez-Rogers granted an injunction in the California proceeding ordering Apple to permit developers to link-out to alternative payment solutions from within iOS apps, to inform users that purchases outside of apps were available, and to communicate with customers through information obtained in those apps (Epic v Apple (US, 2021) at 1058).

[451] The injunction came into effect on 17 January 2024. In response to the injunction, Apple published alternative payment guidelines for developers seeking to avail themselves of the benefit of the injunction. Those guidelines required developers who wished to use external purchase links in their apps to enter into the following terms: (a) developers must pay a 27% commission to Apple on in-app purchases made via the developer’s website; (b) developers must report transactions taking place outside the app to Apple; and (c) developers must display a “disclosure sheet” in a form prescribed by Apple, warning of privacy and security risks associated with using external purchase links.

[452] On 13 March 2024 Epic filed a motion to enforce the terms of the injunction. On 30 April 2025 Judge Gonzalez–Rogers granted Epic’s motion and made findings that Apple had the goal of “maintaining [Apple’s] anticompetitive revenue stream”, that Apple “wilfully selected a commission rate that in practice made all alternatives to IAP economically unviable” and that Apple imposed a range of restrictions on external purchase links that were in contravention of the terms of the injunctions (Epic Games, Inc v. Apple Inc, 781 F Supp 3d 943 (ND Cal, 30 April 2025)).

[453] Judge Gonzalez-Rogers made two findings of contempt against Apple, and permanently enjoined Apple from imposing any commission or any fee on purchases that consumers make outside an app, from restricting or conditioning inter–alia developers’ style, language, flow or placement of links for purchases outside an app, from prohibiting or limiting inter–alia the use of buttons or other calls to action, from excluding certain categories of apps and developers from obtaining link access, from interfering with consumers’ choice to proceed in or out of an app by using anything other than a “neutral message” apprising users that they are going to a third party site, and from restricting a developer’s use of dynamic links of a certain type.

[454] Further, on 16 May 2025 Epic filed a further motion to enforce the injunction after Apple refused to accept Epic’s submission of a version of Fortnite to the US App Store which included a link-out to make purchases via Epic’s website.

[455] On 4 June 2025, the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit denied Apple’s motion for a partial stay of the injunction. On 11 December 2025, the Ninth Circuit affirmed Judge Gonzales-Rogers’ finding that Apple was in contempt and affirmed in part and reversed in part the civil contempt sanctions (Epic Games, Inc v Apple Inc, 161 F 4th 1162 (Cal, 11 December 2025) 1180 to 1194).

###### Continental developments

[456] On 25 March 2024, the European Commission opened an investigation into Apple for noncompliance with the DMA.

[457] On 24 June 2024, the Commission announced its preliminary view that Apple’s App Store steering rules were in breach of the DMA, including the fees imposed on developers who steered users to their own payment solution outside the App Store. It announced that it was opening a further investigation into Apple’s updated alternative terms addendum including in relation to the core technology fee, the multi-step journey to directly download apps, and the eligibility requirements for developers to offer alternative app stores or directly distribute apps on iOS.

[458] On 23 April 2025, the Commission announced its finding that Apple had breached its anti-steering obligation under the DMA and stated in a press release that:

> Due to a number of restrictions imposed by Apple, app developers cannot fully benefit from the advantages of alternative distribution channels outside the App Store. Similarly, consumers cannot fully benefit from alternative and cheaper offers as Apple prevents app developers from directly informing consumers of such offers. The company has failed to demonstrate that these restrictions are objectively necessary and proportionate.

[459] The Commission made the following findings in relation to Apple’s implementation of its DMA obligations based on the alternative terms addendum: (a) Apple failed to show that requiring developers to submit external links to it for approval was objectively necessary and proportionate to protect end user security; (b) Apple had not justified why a disclosure sheet was necessary every time an end user links-out, nor why the disclosure sheet displayed by Apple at link-out should be displayed with the design and wording chosen by Apple; (c) Apple’s core technology fee prevented developers steering users and concluding transactions outside the app free of charge, as required by the DMA.

[460] The Commission fined Apple 500 million Euros and ordered it to remove technical and commercial restrictions on steering. It also ordered Apple to: (a) allow developers to steer users to any destination or channel of their choice; (b) allow developers to steer users towards all types of offers including alternative app marketplaces and other apps; (c) use neutral prompts or disclosure sheets only where essential to inform users they are transacting with the app developer rather than Apple; (d) allow developers to communicate and promote offers and steer users free of charge; (e) only charge fees related to facilitating initial user acquisition, that is, not charge developers fees in relation to already-acquired users; (f) limit these fees to an amount commensurate to the value of that initial acquisition; and (g) not impose any fee to cover services linked to the acquisition of end users.

[461] On 26 June 2025, Apple announced additional changes in the EU in response to the Commission’s decision. The revised alternative terms addendum that Apple has issued propose a revised fee structure for purchases of digital in-app content on apps distributed other than through the App Store.

[462] Let me now say something about Apple’s practices in Japan.

###### Japan

[463] Apple now permits alternative distribution channels in Japan. But it has not just removed pre-existing restraints. Rather, Apple now requires developers who wish to distribute an “alternative app marketplace” to obtain authorisation to do so from Apple, through an “entitlement request”. Such requests will only be granted if developers satisfy a suite of eligibility criteria and agree to abide by various ongoing requirements imposed by Apple, including publication of marketplace terms, acceptance of apps that meet those terms, monitoring and anti-fraud obligations, and intellectual property review mechanisms.

[464] To qualify for an app marketplace entitlement, the developer must either provide Apple with a USD 1 million standby letter of credit and maintain it for at least 6 months, or be a member of good standing in the Developer Program for at least 2 years and have an app that had more than 1 million first annual installs worldwide on iOS in the prior calendar year.

[465] So it would seem that, absent legal restraint, Apple is likely to impose additional conditions on alternative distribution channels that are apt to hinder new entry into the iOS app distribution market, and will enable Apple to exercise control or influence over, and/or to impose restrictions on, the nature and operation of those alternative app distribution channels that do enter.

[466] I agree with Epic that this affirms both the utility and desirability of the restraints sought by it in proposed orders 5, 6(a), 6(c) and 6(k) (orders 4, 5(a), 5(c) and 5(k) respectively as modified and made).

[467] Further, the evidence relating to alternative payment methods in Japan shows that, where a developer offers an alternative payment method for digital goods or services within an app, or directs users to a website to purchase digital goods or services, Apple requires the developer to also present IAP as a payment option at the same time. Apple seeks to justify this by saying that this, apparently, is to “provide a consistent and transparent user experience when offering alternative payment methods for digital goods and services within your app”.

[468] Apple further requires that IAP “must be provided as an option on every screen where your app merchandises a digital good or service for purchase and offers a payment option” and that the display of IAP “must be at least as prominent as any other payment option shown”.

[469] I agree with Epic that this conduct supports Epic’s proposed orders 6(j) and 10(c) (orders 5(j) and 9(c) respectively as modified and made). It seems to me that absent appropriate restraint, Apple is likely to require developers to offer IAP as an alternative to the developer’s preferred payment method.

[470] Moreover, this also supports proposed order 7(c) (order 6(c) as modified and made). Because many developers prefer as Epic described it a “one stop shop”, Apple’s conduct in compelling them to offer IAP in addition to any alternative payment method will, in practice, prevent many developers from offering alternative payment methods at all.

[471] Apple also requires that apps using alternative payment methods must present users with an in-app “disclosure sheet” that lets users know “they’ll be transacting with the developer and not Apple”. Apple has said that “a future iOS release” will give users the ability to opt out of showing the screen for future transactions in the app, but it appears that this option is not currently available.

[472] In my view this conduct demonstrates the need for proposed order 11(a) (order 10(a) as modified and made), since it clearly signals that Apple is otherwise likely to impose additional frictions or impediments into the user’s payment experience.

[473] Let me now turn to the specific orders sought, which I will address in detail under a number of heads of subject matter.

#### Fortnite and other iOS apps developed by Epic

[474] Epic has sought the following orders on this topic:

> 3. Upon written application being made by the Applicants, the First Respondent must do all things necessary (including procuring any act of any related corporation) to permit the Fortnite app and/or other iOS apps developed by the Applicants (not including an alternative app store) to be (i) admitted or readmitted to the App Store subject to the Applicants’ complying with the DPLA and App Store Review Guidelines (insofar as the provisions of the DPLA and App Store Review Guidelines have not been declared void under paragraph 2 and are consistent with the balance of the orders made by the Court), and (ii) made available for discovery, downloading, use and updating by iOS device users, in the same manner as other apps available on the App Store by taking the following steps:

> (a) processing in accordance with its usual business practices any of the following application forms or documents which are submitted by any of the Applicants:

> (i) Apple Developer Agreement;

> (ii) DPLA and any Schedule thereto;

> (iii) Xcode and Apple SDK Agreement; and

> (iv) any variation or replacement of these application forms or documents;

> (b) accepting such application forms or documents which are in a compliant form;

> (c) in the case of tools and APIs (including software development kits) that are generally made available by the Respondents to third-party app developers who distribute apps via the App Store, permitting the Applicants to use such of the Respondents’ tools and APIs (including software development kits) as are required for the development, distribution and updating of the Fortnite app and/or other apps developed by the Applicants for macOS and iOS, in accordance with the applicable contractual terms and on substantially the same terms as generally apply to all such third-party app developers (insofar as those terms have not been declared void under paragraph 2 and are consistent with the balance of the orders made by the Court); and

> (d) processing and accepting in accordance with its usual business practices any compliant update to the Fortnite app and/or other iOS apps developed by the Applicants which are submitted by any of the Applicants for distribution via the App Store.

#### Competition in iOS app distribution

[475] Epic has sought the following orders on this topic:

> 4. The First Respondent must do all things necessary (including procuring any act of any related corporation) to ensure that users of iOS devices can discover, obtain and update iOS apps, including alternative app stores, via distribution channels other than the App Store (including via direct download and alternative app stores) provided those iOS apps have undergone an automated scan for genuine security and/or privacy reasons and been notarised by Apple or a suitably qualified third party (Alternative Distribution Channels), and that app developers can distribute and update such iOS apps to such users via Alternative Distribution Channels, including but not limited to providing access to all tools and APIs (including software development kits):

> (a) necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps;

> (b) necessary for developers to develop iOS apps that are capable of creating an interface for displaying iOS apps and which may be used by an iOS user to discover, download, install and update iOS apps; and

> (c) necessary for iOS users to discover, download, install and update apps via Alternative Distribution Channels,

> where such tools and APIs are made available in the same manner and on substantially the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store, and to users of iOS apps distributed via the App Store.

> 5. The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder users of iOS devices from discovering, obtaining and updating iOS apps, including alternative app stores, via Alternative Distribution Channels, or from using iOS apps obtained in that way on iOS devices, or prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder app developers from distributing and updating such iOS apps to such users via Alternative Distribution Channels, including but not limited to:

> (a) prohibiting, preventing or taking any action (including imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder access to any tool or API (including software development kits):

> (i) necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps and which are available to developers distributing iOS apps via the App Store;

> (ii) necessary for developers to develop iOS apps that are capable of creating an interface for displaying iOS apps and which may be used by an iOS user to discover, download, install and update iOS apps;

> (iii) necessary for iOS users to discover, download, install and update apps via Alternative Distribution Channels;

> (b) imposing any restrictions that prevent iOS apps from running on an iOS device unless downloaded from the App Store or pre-installed by the First Respondent; and

> (c) making it a term of the Applicants’ or any other app developer’s access to the App Store that they will not make their iOS apps available via an Alternative Distribution Channel.

> 6. The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> (a) impose any fees or conditions for access to, or use of, Alternative Distribution Channels by iOS app developers or iOS users;

> (b) impose any fees or conditions for access to, or use of, any tool or API (including software development kits) necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels, that have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels, including by imposing fees higher, or conditions more onerous, than those applied to developers of iOS apps which are monetised other than through in-app purchases (including free apps and apps monetised through advertising) when distributed via the App Store;

> (c) require the Applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to refrain from offering lower prices, or more favourable discounts or other benefits, to users who make purchases of digital content for use in that app when it is distributed via the Alternative Distribution Channel, as compared to when the app is distributed via the App Store;

> (d) require the Applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel, and who allows users of those apps to access content, subscriptions or features they purchased from the Applicants’ or developer’s own website or another distribution channel to also make that content, subscriptions or features available as an in-app purchase within their iOS apps distributed via the App Store;

> (e) prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder the Applicants or any other operator of an alternative app store from making and implementing their own choices as to which apps they will distribute via any alternative app store that they operate;

> (f) require the Applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to appoint any particular person as their agent for the marketing and delivery of the developer’s app to iOS users located in Australia via that distribution channel;

> (g) prohibit the pre-installation of third-party apps, including alternative app stores, on iOS devices;

> (h) remove an app from, or disable an app installed on, an iOS device which has been obtained by a user via an Alternative Distribution Channel unless the app:

> (i) contains illicit, objectionable or harmful content; or

> (ii) constitutes malware or malicious code which poses a genuine security risk that either requires urgent elimination, or that has not been remedied following the First Respondent (or a related corporation) providing the app developer with reasonable notice of the existence, nature and extent of that security risk;

> (i) impose frictions on or impediments to the discovery, downloading, installation, execution or updating of any iOS apps available or distributed via Alternative Distribution Channels, including alternative app stores:

> (i) that are disproportionate to the security risk posed by those apps to iOS devices and iOS device users; or

> (ii) that otherwise have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels or the use of such apps by iOS device users;

> (j) require the Applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their iOS apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to offer or to use any particular payment method, or require that they must offer IAP as an alternative to the developer’s preferred payment method;

> (k) require the Applicants or any other app developer who wishes to make, or makes, their apps available for distribution via an Alternative Distribution Channel to enter into any contractual arrangement with the First Respondent (or any other person) as a condition of the Applicants or the developer distributing their iOS apps via the Alternative Distribution Channel, other than in respect of the licensing of the First Respondent’s tools and APIs for the development of iOS apps.

#### Apple – order

[476] Contrastingly, Apple has sought the following order:

> 2. Pursuant to s 80(1) of the CCA, the First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> (a) prohibit:

> (i) users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account from discovering, obtaining and updating native iOS apps, including alternative app stores, via direct downloading or alternative app stores, or from using iOS apps obtained in that way on such iOS devices; or

> (ii) App Developers from distributing and updating native iOS apps to such users via direct downloading or alternative app stores; or

> (b) unreasonably prevent or hinder users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account or App Developers from engaging in the conduct described in subparas (a)(i) or (a)(ii), and without affecting the generality of the foregoing this will permit the First Respondent (and any related corporation) to impose reasonable terms on any licence or other access of its tools and APIs (including software development kits) necessary for such users of iOS devices or App Developers to engage in that conduct.

> …

> 4. Orders 2 and 3 are to have effect for a period of 5 years with effect from the date that is 6 months from the date of these orders.

> Definitions

> In these orders:

> “App Developers” means developers of native iOS apps who are and remain admitted to the Apple Developer Program.

> “Australian App Store” means the App Store Australian storefront.

> “Australian Apple Account”, formerly "Apple ID", means an Apple Account with the country or region set to “Australia” and associated with a payment method with an Australian billing address.

> “Reader app” means magazine, newspaper, book, audio, music and video apps which may allow a user to access previously purchased content or content subscriptions.

[477] Before proceeding further, it is convenient to make the following points concerning Apple’s proposed orders.

[478] I agree with Epic that if I was to make Apple’s proposed orders, it would not ensure that the necessary tools, APIs and functionality were provided on terms that did not stifle competition in the relevant markets.

[479] Further, Apple’s proposed orders are only prohibitory in nature, and turn on whether Apple has unreasonably prevented or hindered the relevant conduct. Accordingly, I am not satisfied that if Apple’s proposed orders were made, Apple would take the positive steps necessary for competition to emerge in the relevant markets. As Epic points out, limited prohibitory injunctions like those proposed by Apple would leave Apple with considerable scope to preserve the substance of the restraints that I have previously condemned.

[480] Contrastingly, Epic’s proposed orders will require Apple to take positive steps to ensure that users can discover and developers can distribute iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, including by providing developers with access to all necessary tools and APIs on substantially the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store.

[481] Further, Epic’s orders will require Apple to take positive steps to permit developers to use payment systems other than IAP and to provide external links. I will discuss this topic in a separate section of my reasons.

[482] Let me turn to the specific orders and address Epic’s proposed order 3 first. But before doing so I should make clear that in the discussion of this proposed order and the following orders, “user” refers to a user with an Australian Apple Account and “App Store” refers to the Australian App Store.

#### Proposed order 3 (order 2 as modified and made)

[483] Now Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 3, which is in the form of a mandatory injunction directed to Epic’s apps being admitted or readmitted to the App Store, should not be made. I would note that this corresponds, with modification, to order 2 that I have made.

[484] Apple says that Epic did not bring a case alleging a refusal to deal with it on the part of Apple generally or in any specific respect. There was no case or controversy brought and it says that I made no finding to the effect that Apple contravened s 46 by refusing to deal with Epic.

[485] Further, Apple says that Epic’s order impinges upon the operative effect of existing terms of Apple’s DPLA that are not related to the conduct that has been found to have contravened s 46 and which, in some cases, have been unsuccessfully challenged by Epic in this proceeding.

[486] Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 3 would operate to re-enliven and vindicate aspects of the case Epic did bring which have been rejected. So, it said that by clause 6.9 of the DPLA, Apple reserved the right to select at its sole discretion which iOS apps will be distributed on the App Store and will be available by that means to iOS device users. And under schedule 2 to the DPLA, Apple reserved the right to cease offering an iOS app for download at any time without cause.

[487] Apple says that Epic included these terms among its catalogue of restrictive terms, but it says that no finding has been made that imposing or giving effect to these terms contravened s 46.

[488] In addition, Apple says that clause 11.2 of the DPLA also provided that either party “may terminate this Agreement for its convenience, for any reason or no reason, effective 30 days after providing the other party with written notice of its intent to terminate”. But it says that that part of clause 11.2 was challenged by Epic.

[489] Further, Apple says that the US courts have held that Apple acted lawfully in removing the Fortnite app from the App Store and terminating Epic’s developer account and says that there is no proper basis for me to order Apple to reverse an exercise of its contractual rights which the US courts have upheld.

[490] Further, Apple says that proposed orders 3(a), (b) and (d) would effectively force it to deal with Epic. But it says that I have not made findings to the effect that Apple has contravened, or would contravene s 46 or any other provision, by refusing to deal with Epic. And it says that nothing determined by me warrants any compulsion of Apple to deal with Epic irrespective of the specific contractual discretions which the DPLA contains.

[491] Apple says that proposed order 3(c) would force it to give Epic access to such of Apple’s intellectual property as Epic considers desirable. But it says that nothing in my findings warrants forcing Apple to give Epic the run of its intellectual property. It says that those findings went no further than identifying that some unspecified portion of Apple’s SDKs and APIs are essential to developers in the sense that it is not practical for a developer to develop native apps for iOS without them.

[492] Further, Apple says that proposed order 3(a) purports to require processing of the identified forms or variations of them, whereas proposed order 3(b) requires Apple to accept such application forms or documents as are in a compliant form. It says that the order is ambiguous in that the forms referred to are actually standard form contracts that govern Apple’s relationship with all developers. In any event, whether or not applications or forms as Epic may submit are in a compliant form is not a matter which Apple can reasonably be required to assess on pain of being held in contempt to the extent of any error in that assessment.

[493] Apple says that proposed order 3(c) would compel it to permit Epic to use such of the respondents’ tools and APIs including SDKs as are required or desirable for the development, distribution and updating of Fortnite and/or other apps developed by Epic for macOS and iOS; I note that what is proposed does not include “or desirable”. Apple says that the tools and APIs that may be required by or desirable to Epic is indeterminate and the order does not in terms place any limit on Epic’s desires. Further, it says that the findings in this matter do not give rise to any basis for relief relating to apps developed for macOS.

[494] Further, Apple says that proposed orders 3(a) and (d) require Apple to deploy usual business practices in implementing a regime which will be, in the context of Apple’s global operations, highly unusual and specific to Epic and the Australian App Store. It says that this is a criterion which Apple cannot be expected to meet on pain of being found to be in contempt of court.

[495] Further, Apple says that the effect of the order would be to override Apple’s right under the DPLA terms to select, at its sole discretion, which iOS apps will be distributed in the App Store, which right has not been found to contravene s 46 or any other relevant provision.

[496] Further, Apple says that the proposed orders where Apple is only required, upon written application by Epic, to permit the Fortnite app and other iOS apps developed by Epic to be admitted or readmitted to the App Store if Epic complies with the DPLA and the App Review Guidelines, and uses Apple’s tools and APIs in accordance with applicable contractual terms, do not address these issues.

[497] Apple says that if the injunctions proposed at paragraphs 2 and 3 of Apple’s proposed orders are made, then Epic can apply for a new DPLA and seek to have Fortnite and any other iOS apps it develops added to the App Store like any other developer.

[498] Apple says that injunctive relief in the form proposed by it would enjoin Apple from preventing or prohibiting sideloading or direct download of apps including app stores or from preventing or prohibiting use of payment methods other than IAP for in-app purchases of digital content in relation to iOS devices associated with an Apple ID registered in Australia. And it says that that is all the injunctive relief that my liability findings support.

###### Analysis

[499] As is apparent, Epic’s proposed order 3 requires Apple to do all things necessary to ensure that iOS apps developed by Epic are admitted or re-admitted to the App Store, and made available for discovery, downloading, use and updating by iOS device users, in the same manner as other apps available on the App Store.

[500] Moreover, as Epic correctly points out, the specific matters identified in sub-paragraphs (a) to (d) of order 3 reflect my recognition of the developers’ need to access Apple’s tools and APIs, including SDKs, in developing apps, as well as the importance of apps being able to be updated as required.

[501] In my view this mandatory injunction but limited to the Australian App Store is warranted given my finding that Apple’s removal of Fortnite from the App Store and termination of Epic’s developer account was a paradigm example of Apple’s enforcement of its restrictive terms, in contravention of s 46.

[502] But for Apple’s contravening conduct in imposing and enforcing those terms on and against Epic, Epic’s apps, including Fortnite, would be available for discovery, downloading, use and updating via the App Store. As Epic says, the mandatory injunction proposed prevents Apple from continuing to contravene s 46 by enforcing, or purporting to enforce, its restrictive terms against Epic.

[503] Moreover, proposed order 3 is a targeted exercise of s 80 that remedies Epic’s ongoing exclusion from the Australian App Store. Epic’s proposed orders are targeted at requiring Apple to do what is necessary to admit or readmit Epic’s apps to the Australian App Store. Of course these orders do not prevent Apple from exercising its contractual rights in the future for legitimate reasons.

[504] Further, I am not required to make separate findings that, in every but for scenario, Epic’s apps would necessarily remain available via the Australian App Store irrespective of any legitimate exercise of Apple’s contractual discretions.

[505] As I found at [5227]:

> Further, Epic also makes the good point that there is an inherent difficulty in the notion that Epic is required to prove contractual terms which would be acceptable to Apple in the future. Any litigation could be defeated by Apple simply stating that such terms would not be acceptable, which would provide Apple with a form of non-justiciable fiat such that it could always escape the reach of ss 45, 46 and 47.

[506] I agree with Epic that it would not be feasible to require it to prove that in the counterfactual its apps would remain available in every conceivable scenario.

[507] Further, my power to grant relief under s 80 is not limited by the operation of United States law. And the US courts’ conclusions about the contractual validity of Apple’s conduct under US law do not limit my power to grant relief under the CCA to address the competition law consequences of Apple’s conduct in Australia, particularly as the order that I propose to make will be limited to the Australian App Store.

[508] Further, proposed order 3(c) will grant Epic the tools necessary to remedy Apple’s contraventions of s 46 and does not give Epic “the run” of Apple’s IP. I found that decentralised distribution of apps cannot occur without access to Apple property. Apple itself identified the software that it considered necessary for developing an iOS app or alternative app store that is capable of distribution on iOS.

[509] I agree with Epic that Apple should not be permitted to remain free to withhold the tools that I have found are essential to iOS distribution and development.

[510] Further, and contrary to what Apple has said, in my view Apple should not be permitted to repeat the same impugned conduct in respect of macOS. My findings give rise to a clear basis for relief relating to apps developed for macOS. I found that macOS and iOS are based on the same core technologies and source code and that a developer’s access to that technology is governed, or largely governed, by the same contractual provisions. It would be an illogical result if Epic were only allowed access to the necessary tools for iOS while simultaneously being denied access to those very same tools for macOS.

[511] Finally, there should be no difficulty with proposed order 3 in circumstances where the mandatory injunction proposed is subject to Epic complying with the DPLA and App Review Guidelines. The order is necessary for the reasons given elsewhere. Apple’s complaints about the geographic scope of order 3 have been addressed elsewhere.

[512] Let me now turn to Epic’s proposed orders 4 to 6 dealing with injunctions directed at competition in the distribution market.

#### Proposed order 4 (order 3 as modified and made)

[513] Apple says that the further mandatory injunctive relief sought by Epic’s proposed order 4 should also not be made. I would note that this corresponds, with modification, to order 3 that I have made. Apple says that proposed order 4 does not respond to any aspect of the controversy or case that has been contested between the parties, and so is not within my power to grant by way of relief in these proceedings.

[514] Apple says that no case has been brought alleging that it is or should be required by the CCA to positively facilitate distribution by developers of iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, much less discovery by users of apps so distributed. And it says that Epic has claimed no form of mandatory injunctive relief analogous to proposed order 4.

[515] Further, Apple says that insofar as this order purports to require Apple to provide access to all tools and APIs including SDKs that are necessary for developers to develop and distribute iOS apps or necessary for developers to develop iOS apps that are capable of creating an interface for displaying iOS apps, nothing in my liability findings supports the grant of that relief.

[516] Indeed, Apple says that my findings positively disfavour the grant of such relief, since they posit and presuppose that in a world without the impugned conduct, that is, the world which the making of orders enjoining the impugned conduct will create, Apple will likely license its intellectual property to developers of its own volition and in its own interests. Apple says that it was an integer of the finding of contravention that in the counterfactual Apple “could and likely would license its intellectual property” as I said at [5237]. And it says that Epic chose not to seek to establish how or to what extent or on what terms Apple would likely do so.

[517] So, Apple says that there was no case or controversy concerning the terms upon which Apple would licence its intellectual property in a world without the contravening conduct. And it says that my findings do not give rise to a right on the part of Epic or developers generally to a compulsory licence of Apple’s software.

[518] Apple says that in circumstances where the “with and without” analysis was integral to determining whether impugned conduct contravenes Pt IV, it was incumbent upon Epic to articulate any parameters it says should apply to Apple’s conduct in the counterfactual in the liability hearing.

[519] Apple says that Epic, having made the forensic decision that a finding in the terms of [5237] was sufficient for its purposes, should not be permitted to now ask me to further prescribe the shape of the world without the contravening conduct in granting relief.

[520] Further, Apple says that making available tools and APIs in the same manner and on substantially the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to developers and users of apps distributed via the App Store would limit Apple’s commercial freedom to set the terms on which it licenses its intellectual property in a manner that my liability findings do not justify.

[521] Apple says that I accepted that it should have such freedom. In substance I observed that Apple could take lawful action in restructuring the way it recovers compensation or adopting alternative monetisation strategies, if it were restrained from imposing and enforcing the iOS restrictive terms.

[522] I said at [5375]:

> In summary, I am satisfied that if I were to restrain Apple from imposing and enforcing the iOS restrictive terms, there is a commercially realistic chance that any lawful response taken by Apple in restructuring the way in which it recovers compensation, or adopting an alternative monetisation strategy, would not have the same or similar effect as the impugned conduct.

[523] Further, Apple says that it is particularly necessary that that should be so given that the tools and APIs addressed by proposed order 4, being for the development of alternative app stores and sideloading, are not tools and APIs made available to developers and users of apps distributed via the App Store.

[524] Further, Apple says that it would be inappropriate to order that it do all things necessary to ensure that iOS device users and app developers can utilise alternative app distribution. It says that such an injunction would be of uncertain scope. Indeed it goes so far as to say that such an order could be understood as requiring Apple to subsidise app developers wishing to use alternative app distribution, but lacking the financial or technical resources to do so.

[525] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 4 would require that existing and new software tools, APIs and SDKs be licensed and made available to developers and iOS users in the same manner and on substantially the same terms as tools and APIs are made available to developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store and to users of iOS apps distributed via the App Store.

[526] But it says that nothing in my liability findings justifies an order requiring this. And it says that such an order would unreasonably prejudice Apple’s interests as an intellectual property owner.

[527] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 4 is inconsistent with my judgment, which pre-supposed that with the exception of terms found to contravene s 46 Apple would retain its commercial freedom to deal with its intellectual property. It says that there is no competition analysis demonstrating that it would be anti-competitive for Apple to licence its intellectual property, including tools that specifically facilitate alternative distribution, other than on substantially the same terms as other tools and APIs are made available to developers who distribute apps via the App Store. And in those circumstances, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order would interfere with normal processes of innovation, intellectual property licensing and competition.

[528] Further, Apple says that such an order would be an unjustified practical impediment to appropriate licence conditions for specific tools. Apple points out that it presently attaches detailed conditions to specific APIs, tools and technologies. It says that there is no basis for an order that would restrict Apple from similarly imposing specific conditions on APIs, tools and technologies that facilitate alternative app distribution. It says that those tools ensure the security of alternative app distribution via mechanisms such as making cryptographically signed IPA files available to developers in ADPs and allowing cryptographic communication between the App Licence Delivery software tool and Fairplay. It says that it may be appropriate for Apple to impose specific conditions on the use of those tools and that my orders should not prevent that.

[529] Now in contrast to its criticisms of Epic’s proposed order, Apple has extolled the virtues of its proposed order 2.

[530] Apple’s proposed order 2 is an injunction that restrains Apple from prohibiting users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account from discovering, obtaining and updating native iOS apps, including alternative app stores, or from using iOS apps obtained in that way. The injunction also restrains Apple from prohibiting app developers from distributing and updating native iOS apps to such users via direct downloading or alternative app stores. In addition, order 2 restrains Apple from unreasonably preventing or hindering users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account, or app developers, from engaging in that conduct, but provides that Apple may impose reasonable terms on any licence or other access of its tools and APIs including SDKs necessary for such users of iOS devices or app developers to engage in that conduct.

[531] Apple says that its proposed injunction is appropriately focused on the conduct that I have found to have contravened s 46 and notes five features.

[532] First, Apple’s proposed injunction restrains Apple from prohibiting the distribution of apps via direct downloading and alternative app stores, but not sideloading. During the hearing on relief, Apple used the expression “direct downloading” to refer to the downloading of apps from alternative distribution channels that are subject to review and notarization by Apple, whereas it used the terminology of “sideloading” to refer to the downloading of apps from alternative distribution channels without any process of notarization or review by Apple. I will use that terminological distinction for present purposes. Apple says that an injunction extending to direct downloading and alternative app stores, but not sideloading, is appropriate because I proceeded on the basis that if alternative app distribution was ordered in Australia, Apple could, and would, adopt a system of app review and notarization, consistent with Australian law. It is convenient to say at this point that I agree and a proviso about security scanning and notarization is now included.

[533] Second, via the definition of app developers, Apple’s proposed order 2 clarifies that developers of native iOS apps will have the benefit of the injunction so long as they are and remain admitted to the Developer Program. Apple says that that is appropriate given that I proceeded on the basis that Apple could, and would, continue to conduct significant security procedures in relation to the iOS ecosystem. It says that I did not find that Apple’s requirement that developers be members of the Developer Program in order to distribute iOS apps was unlawful or problematic. And it says that that requirement is appropriate in order to ensure that Apple can continue to protect iOS users from fraudulent and malicious developers.

[534] Third, Apple’s proposed injunction applies to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account, which will allow Australian iOS users to download and update apps on their iOS devices via alternative distribution channels. Apple says that the injunction allows for developers globally to distribute native iOS apps to Australian iOS users via alternative distribution channels. It says that that is the appropriate geographical scope of the orders that I should make in this case. Further, Apple says that it is also consistent with the technical manner in which Apple makes apps available to iOS users globally. Each iOS user globally has an Apple Account associated with one of the 175 geographic-specific App Store storefronts. In jurisdictions where alternative app distribution is available, it is facilitated via that system, which pre-supposes that every device is exclusively associated with a single geographic-specific App Store storefront, but allows developers globally to distribute to users in each geographic location. Now as I have already indicated I have modified Epic’s proposed orders as a whole to reflect such a geographic limitation.

[535] Fourth, Apple says that its proposed order 2 restrains it from unreasonably preventing or hindering iOS users with an Australian Apple Account, or app developers, from distributing and downloading apps via alternative distribution channels. It says that that addresses the spectre Epic raises of Apple seeking to avoid the operation of my orders. Apple says that because of the range of conduct which might be construed as preventing or hindering iOS users or developers from using alternative distribution channels, including the adoption of necessary and appropriate security measures, measures to protect users’ privacy, measures to protect users who are minors, measures to adapt to unforeseen and presently unforeseeable technological changes, measures to respond to developer or user fraud, and the imposition of fees and other conditions on access to Apple’s IP-protected tools, technologies and services, my orders must permit Apple to act reasonably.

[536] Fifth and more specifically, Apple says that its proposed order 2 recognises that an injunction restraining it from unreasonably preventing or hindering alternative app distribution will require it to license or otherwise grant access to its software tools and APIs which are necessary for that form of distribution to occur, and makes it clear that Apple can impose reasonable terms in doing so.

[537] Apple says that I proceeded on the basis that if alternative app distribution was ordered, Apple would be entitled to licence its intellectual property necessary for app development and distribution on reasonable terms. Indeed, it points out that I found at [6298]:

> Further, Apple’s terms protect legitimate commercial interests. It is entitled to seek a commercial return on its highly valuable intellectual property-protected assets, which it generated by taking the risk of vast expenditure on research and development, and which developers like Epic take the benefit of.

[538] Apple says that the terms of its proposed order 2 is necessary because in its absence a prohibition on hindering alternative app distribution could be interpreted as prohibiting any limitation on a licence of Apple’s software tools and APIs, including reasonable fees and conditions.

###### Analysis

[539] In my view, proposed order 4 reflects my finding that Apple contravened s 46(1) by prohibiting the distribution of iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, and prevents Apple from continuing to contravene s 46 by requiring it to take positive steps to ensure the viability of app distribution via those channels.

[540] Further, the specific matters identified in orders 4(a) to (c) reflect the recognition of the need to access Apple’s tools and APIs in connection with the development of iOS apps, and the fact that Apple makes such tools and APIs available in connection with the distribution of iOS apps via the App Store.

[541] Now Apple has advanced four criticisms against proposed order 4 which I reject.

[542] First, Apple argues that Epic has not explained why proposed order 4 is necessary to undo or prevent the anti-competitive foreclosure that I have found.

[543] But I found that Apple contravened s 46(1) by deliberately foreclosing every rival channel for iOS app distribution, that is, banning direct downloading and alternative app stores, and thereby installing itself as the sole gateway to iOS users. To remedy that situation, it follows that Apple must no longer be free to foreclose rival channels for iOS app distribution.

[544] As I found, every alternative form of iOS app distribution envisaged in this proceeding cannot occur without access to Apple property. The various SDKs/APIs that Apple supplies are essential in the sense that developers cannot practically develop iOS apps without them.

[545] So, if Apple were free to withhold access to those tools and APIs that are necessary to develop and distribute iOS apps, simply because the developer chose to distribute its iOS apps through an alternative method of app distribution, then developers would have no viable way of distributing apps outside the App Store. This would defeat my findings and the utility of injunctive relief, and perpetuate the anti-competitive conduct and the damage to the public interest.

[546] I should exercise my injunctive power under s 80 in a way that offers practical relief. Proposed order 4 does that, responding directly to the way the contravening conduct actually operates. Its scope is limited and proportionate, in that it is confined to those tools and APIs that are necessary for the viability of alternative app distribution and which Apple already makes available to developers for the App Store. As such, it merely asks that alternative channels of app distribution be provided the minimum necessary playing ground to compete.

[547] The mandatory operation of proposed order 4 is warranted as a corollary of the corresponding prohibitive obligation in proposed order 5, and to safeguard against Apple’s persistent pattern of resistance to external constraint. I said at [6005]:

> In these instances, even regulatory obligations have not prevented Apple from devising ways by which it can continue to foreclose competition. The same applies to Apple’s response to the enactment of the Digital Markets Act.

[548] Second, Apple argues that proposed order 4 does not respond to any aspect of the controversy or case that has been contested between the parties, and that nothing in the liability judgment supports the grant of that relief.

[549] But consonant with the broad and public interest discretion under s 80, the requirement that there be a sufficient nexus between the order sought and the contravention found is not to be inflexibly or mechanically applied. It is an evaluative judgment. Nor is it to be artificially limited. Section 80(5)(b) makes clear that the Court may grant an injunction requiring a person to do an act or thing whether or not the person previously refused or failed to do that act or thing. I agree with Epic that the freeing of the Court’s injunctive power under s 80 from this constraint is necessary to uphold the public interest purpose of s 80.

[550] Accordingly, in circumstances where I found that Apple foreclosed rival channels for iOS app distribution and that developers cannot practically develop iOS apps for such channels unless they have access to certain SDKs/APIs that Apple supplies, an order requiring Apple to take positive steps to ensure the viability of alternative channels of app distribution bears a sufficient nexus to the contravening conduct.

[551] Third, Apple argues that my counterfactual findings positively disfavour mandatory relief involving access to its intellectual property, on the basis that I assumed, in the counterfactual, that Apple could and would likely license its intellectual property of its own volition. Apple claims Epic chose not to prove how or on what terms that would occur, and there was therefore no case or controversy about Apple’s licence terms. Given that, Apple says there is no basis now to dictate those terms by way of relief, and in any event I should in my discretion refuse to allow Epic to run its case in that way at a separate relief hearing.

[552] Now the assumptions that I made concerning the likelihood of Apple licensing its intellectual property in a world without the impugned conduct were made for the purpose of facilitating my counterfactual inquiry. They were directed to the liability question, being whether removing the impugned conduct would result in increased competition.

[553] They were not directed to confining my remedial powers and nor could they be. As I noted at [3791] and [3792]:

> What must be proved on the balance of probabilities is that there is real commercial likelihood of a substantial lessening of competition. It is not necessary to prove each predicted fact on the balance of probabilities. The counterfactual is no more than an element of the calculus aimed at detecting the existence and extent of a change in the competitive process. It is not a matter that needs to be separately proved as if it were a primary fact.

> Conduct which lessens competition in a part of the market can constitute a substantial lessening of competition in that market, even if the conduct is not market-wide or does not otherwise affect the whole market. This is because “it is the degree to which competition has been lessened which is critical, not the proportion of that lessening to the whole of the competition which exists in the total market” (Dandy Power Equipment Pty Ltd v Mercury Marine Pty Ltd (1982) 44 ALR 173 at 192; 64 FLR 238 at 260 per Smithers J).

[554] It is a tool that operates by way of a hypothetical comparison between two projected states of competition. It is not a matter that needs to be separately proved as if it were a primary fact.

[555] As such, to read into the terms of s 80 an implied limitation that the relief granted must mirror the bare assumptions used in the counterfactual inquiry misunderstands the nature and purpose of that inquiry. I am free under s 80 to grant such injunctions as I determine to be appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy the contravening conduct and its effects.

[556] As I have said, I assumed for the purpose of the counterfactual inquiry that there is at least a realistic chance that Apple would license its intellectual property in a way that would permit alternative modes of iOS app distribution. I did not make the finding that Apple should be free not to do so, or to do so on the terms it sees fit. I said (at [1384] to [1387]):
