> Now I have no difficulty in rejecting Apple’s self-centred argument that there can be no field of rivalry between Apple and any potential competitor for the provision of services for the distribution of iOS apps or for the provision of in-app payment solutions services, in circumstances where Apple’s intellectual property rights give it exclusive control over any market in which Apple might compete with such competitors.

> As Epic points out, to posit that Apple’s exclusive control over any relevant market arises by reason of Apple’s lawful control over its intellectual property begs the question as to whether, on the facts of this case, Apple’s control has been lawfully exercised. Neither intellectual property rights nor the terms of an intellectual property licence can provide any shield against the operation of the CCA in the absence of a relevant statutory exemption or exception.

> Furthermore, even if access to a corporation’s property is a prerequisite to the functioning of a market, that does not mean that that market does not exist for the purposes of competition law. And if the conduct of that corporation in respect of such a market otherwise satisfies the terms of s 45, s 46 or s 47, the corporation will thereby commit a contravention, a consequence of which might include an order requiring the contravener to give competitors access to its property.

> As Epic correctly characterised the matter, Apple’s argument amounts to a contention that the market definition exercise depends wholly on Apple’s commercial choices and that the iOS app distribution market should be rejected because competition in that market depends on Apple granting access to its property, which Apple does not do and which is no more than theoretical. I have no difficulty in rejecting such an Apple-centric perspective.

[557] Yet Apple now in effect asks me to treat that assumption as necessitating the grant of some permanent entitlement to complete commercial freedom.

[558] In any case, there is no unfairness to Apple in settling the operational detail of mandatory relief at this stage of the proceedings. I bifurcated the questions of liability and relief, to which the parties agreed. Moreover, I specifically held that Epic was not required to prove the form of contract that Apple is likely to accept in the counterfactual. Apple cannot now complain that Epic should have done so in more detail. Apple has had notice of the orders Epic proposes and an opportunity to respond.

[559] Further, Apple appears to suggest that the relevant counterfactual is the world which the making of orders enjoining the impugned conduct will create. But in populating the counterfactual, I did not take into account the remedial orders that I would grant in the event that I made a finding of liability. To do so would be a speculative and unworkable exercise.

[560] Fourth, Apple contends that the requirement in proposed order 4 to make tools and APIs available in the same manner and on substantially the same terms as for App Store distribution would radically limit its ability to set the terms on which it licenses its intellectual property as it pleases, and is contrary to my finding at [5375]. In that paragraph I said:

> In summary, I am satisfied that if I were to restrain Apple from imposing and enforcing the iOS restrictive terms, there is a commercially realistic chance that any lawful response taken by Apple in restructuring the way in which it recovers compensation, or adopting an alternative monetisation strategy, would not have the same or similar effect as the impugned conduct.

[561] But that paragraph forms part of the counterfactual analysis and recognises that there is a commercially realistic chance that any lawful response Apple may adopt in restructuring its monetisation would not have the same or similar effect as the impugned conduct. It is not a finding that Apple must be left free of any obligation at the relief stage that is appropriate to prevent the recurrence of the contravening conduct or its effects.

[562] I agree with Epic that there is no relevant uncertainty in a mandatory injunction requiring Apple to do all things necessary, and this is a standard formulation of words. The purpose of the mandatory injunction is to require Apple to do what is necessary to ensure iOS device users and app developers can utilise alternative app distribution.

[563] Further, order 4 does not amount to a compulsory licence of Apple’s intellectual property. As Epic points out, order 4 operates in personam, does not compel any transfer of Apple’s property, and does not confer on Epic or any other developer any free-standing proprietary rights in Apple’s copyright. To the extent that the order requires Apple to continue making available its tools and API, they are orders that are permissible.

[564] Proposed order 4 does not prohibit Apple from imposing conditions on the use of APIs, tools and technologies to ensure the security of app distribution. The order provides that Apple’s APIs, tools and technologies are to be made available in the same manner and on substantially the same terms as those tools are made available to developers who distribute apps via the App Store, and to users of such apps. So, to the extent that Apple attaches conditions to specific APIs, tools and technologies made available to such developers and users, the same conditions are permissible under proposed order 4.

[565] Finally, and for the reasons that I have previously addressed, I have limited the geographic reach of proposed order 4.

#### Proposed order 5 (order 4 as modified and made)

[566] Apple says that insofar as Epic’s proposed order 5 purports to enjoin Apple from preventing or prohibiting sideloading including of alternative app stores, the prohibitive injunction proposed by Apple to that effect is sufficient. I would note that proposed order 5 corresponds, with modification, to order 4 that I have made.

[567] Apple says that to the extent that proposed order 5 goes further than Apple’s proposed injunction, it too goes beyond Epic’s pleaded case and is not supported by and is, in respects, contrary to my findings.

[568] Apple says that Epic did not allege that Apple would, in a future without prohibitions on sideloading of iOS apps, hinder or deter users or developers from using alternative distribution channels, or that any such conduct would contravene s 46. It only brought a case, relevantly, to the effect that Apple should be enjoined from prohibiting the development and use of alternative app stores and direct downloading.

[569] Moreover, to the extent that proposed order 5, particularly order 5(b), purports to enjoin Apple from imposing frictions on the use of alternative distribution channels, Apple says that it is not supported by, and is countermanded by, the liability findings. It points to the following matters.

[570] First, I did not accept that evidence concerning the EU showed that Apple was imposing frictions that were wholly disproportionate to the risks involved.

[571] Second, I accepted that it may be appropriate to have some degree of friction, in terms, before setting an alternative marketplace as a default, but implicitly before permitting a user to engage with an alternative marketplace, given the security risks involved. So I said at [4631] to [4634]:

> Now I do agree that it may be appropriate to have some degree of friction before setting an alternative marketplace as a default. Generally speaking I accept the following three points.

> First, disclosure screens and other user interactions can yield security benefits because they require users to understand what they are about to do on their device. Such screens ask users to engage with more information before acting without understanding the implications of their actions.

> Second, the screens and requirements depicted in publicly available materials concerning third party marketplace installation in the EU on iOS devices provide meaningful context and content for the actions that the user is attempting to take, and can assist the user in making a more informed decision.

> Third, it may be appropriate to have multiple screens, and therefore multiple interactions, when different types of information are being presented and multiple consents are required.

[572] Third, I was not then able to draw any specific adverse conclusions as to Apple’s warnings or installation processes for third party marketplaces and found that it had not been shown that Apple’s imposition of frictions on directly downloaded apps and alternative app stores in the EU was wholly disproportionate to the risks involved.

[573] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 5 could also inhibit the discharge by Apple of its regulatory obligations under the Online Safety Act 2021 (Cth). In particular, under the Online Safety Codes made pursuant to that Act, Apple is required to implement tools and take steps to help reduce the risk of harm to children when using interactive devices including mobile phones and tablets. Apple says that any relief granted in these proceedings should preserve its ability to meet those obligations. I should say now that I have modified proposed order 5 to reflect this.

[574] Further, Apple says that proposed order 5(a) would in effect impose a series of compulsory intellectual property licences on Apple that Epic did not seek in the FAOA and which my findings do not support.

[575] Further, it says that proposed order 5 would apply to any and all would be app developers. In that way, it would deprive Apple of the ability, for example, to limit access to its tools and APIs for app development and alternative app distribution to developers who have been admitted to its Developer Program and, in the case of alternative app marketplace operators, to those who meet such additional requirements as Apple might reasonably impose. Depriving Apple of that ability is inappropriate.

[576] Further, Apple says that an order restraining any action including by imposing a fee or condition which is likely to hinder alternative app distribution is not appropriate. That restraint would at least arguably prevent Apple from imposing reasonable fees and conditions, indeed any fees, when licensing the IP–protected tools and technologies that facilitate app development and alternative distribution. It would at least arguably prevent Apple from imposing reasonable conditions on alternative distribution, to protect the security of the iOS ecosystem. Apple says that that is not consistent with my liability findings, with Apple’s legitimate interests as an intellectual property owner, or the imperative of preserving the security for iOS devices and users.

[577] Further, Apple says that a difficulty with the restraint on any action including by imposing a fee or condition which is likely to hinder alternative app distribution is that Epic’s proposed order 5 operates indiscriminately in respect of all developers. Apple says that even relatively benign fees and conditions might be likely to hinder at least some of the millions of developers in the Developer Program. And it says that the prohibition on taking any action including imposing any fee or condition which is likely to hinder access to certain tools and APIs including software development kits is repeated in order 5(a) and is inappropriate.

[578] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 5(c) would restrain Apple from making it a term of any developer’s access to the App Store that they will not make their iOS apps available via an alternative distribution channel. It says that I have made no finding and there is no expert economic evidence or other economic analysis establishing that such a term would be anti-competitive or, indeed, other than pro-competitive. It says that I found that platforms engage in competition for developers’ resources which can include exclusive availability of games, which the platforms can then use to attract users. Further, it says that although there is no evidence that Apple intends to engage in exclusive agreements with developers, restraining it from doing so would be to inappropriately restrain a form of competition that Epic and other platforms engage in, particularly in circumstances where this is not relief sought in the FAOA. Moreover, it says that whilst such relief might be appropriate in response to a contravention of s 47, no such contravention has been established.

[579] Finally, Apple says that its proposed order 2 renders Epic’s proposed order 5, insofar as aspects of it might otherwise be appropriate, unnecessary.

###### Analysis

[580] Proposed order 5 is framed as a negative injunction that prohibits Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder: (a) users of iOS devices from discovering, obtaining and updating iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, or from using iOS apps obtained in that way on iOS devices; or (b) app developers from distributing and updating such iOS apps to such users via alternative distribution channels. Before proceeding further I would again note that what follows is to be read with the geographic limitation on “user” and “App Store” that I have previously indicated.

[581] Now proposed order 5(a) reflects my finding that Apple imposes technical restrictions, encoded in iOS, that mean only native apps from the App Store maybe installed on an iOS device, apart from certain trivial exceptions.

[582] As to proposed order 5(b), the App Store is currently the sole, and therefore the dominant, channel for the distribution of iOS apps. This order restrains Apple from using that position to leverage its control over the App Store to hinder the growth or viability of alternative distribution channels.

[583] Now Apple says that proposed order 5 exceeds Epic’s pleaded case and is not supported by my findings. And it says that Epic never ran a case that Apple would, in a future without prohibitions on the sideloading of iOS apps, prohibit, prevent or take any action which is likely to hinder users or developers from using alternative distribution channels or that any such conduct would contravene s 46.

[584] But my remedial power is not confined to prohibiting the precise conduct pleaded and found to constitute the contravention as I have discussed earlier in my reasons.

[585] And as I have indicated earlier, if the object of the injunction could stretch no further than proscribing the specific contravening conduct, then the offending party would be free to exert the exact same anti-competitive effect but through a modified form of conduct. Not only would this risk compromising the utility of my order and exposing the public to further harm, but it would also require the applicant to bring a fresh round of proceedings each time the offending party so varied its conduct.

[586] As such, contrary to Apple’s nostalgic attempt to take me back to the dawn of the golden age of the 1980s, s 80 today permits me to grant injunctions that are appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy that contravening conduct and its effects. Specifically, that includes restraining apprehended modes of conduct which, although not themselves the historical contravention or its centrepiece, would re-create or perpetuate the same preclusion or lessening of competition.

[587] This approach is particularly apt here where the contravening conduct was based on interlocking restrictions that operated at multiple levels and targeted both developers and users, and included iOS level restrictions that prevented apps running unless they were obtained via the App Store or Apple’s pre-installation of the app. The range of restrictions that Apple relied upon to deploy, implement and enforce its contravening conduct supports the making of proposed order 5.

[588] A contravention of s 46 is achieved only where the commission of certain conduct is accompanied by either a prohibited purpose or effect. Where conduct has been found to contravene s 46 because of its particular effect on competition, it follows that the successful remedy will be the one that ends not just the offending conduct itself, but also the perpetuation of its associated anti-competitive effect. In this way, the remedial purpose of s 80 is brought in line with the purpose of the CCA more generally, being to protect and enforce the public’s interest in upholding the high matters of public policy embodied in the legislative enactments of Pt IV, namely, the procurement and maintenance of competition in trade and commerce.

[589] In short, I agree with Epic that it was not necessary for it to have run a case that, in a future without direct prohibitions, the specific conduct enjoined under proposed order 4 would have contravened the CCA. I found that the contravening conduct has the effect that no alternative source of supply of app distribution services is available to developers or iOS device users other than the App Store, which precludes competition.

[590] Given that finding, alongside the finding that access to certain of Apple’s SDK/APIs are necessary for the viability of alternative channels of app distribution and the findings outlined, proposed order 5 contains a sufficient nexus to the relevant contravention.

[591] Further, given my finding concerning Apple’s history of deploying deterrence tactics to protect its preferred arrangements, even in the face of legal compulsion proposed order 5 is an appropriate exercise of my discretion. I said at [4092]:

> I agree with Epic that Apple’s ability to deploy such deterrence tactics to protect its preferred arrangements, even in the face of legal compulsion, manifests its substantial degree of market power.

[592] Further, Apple contends that in so far as proposed order 5(b) enjoins Apple from imposing frictions on the use of alternative distribution channels, it is unsupported by and contrary to the liability findings. I accepted that some degree of friction may be appropriate before setting an alternative marketplace as a default, and was not able to draw any adverse conclusions about Apple’s warnings or installation processes for third-party marketplaces, finding that it had not been shown that Apple’s EU frictions are wholly disproportionate to the risks involved. I said at [4631], [4635] and [4639] the following:

> Now I do agree that it may be appropriate to have some degree of friction before setting an alternative marketplace as a default. Generally speaking I accept the following three points.

> …

> I am not presently able to draw any specific adverse conclusions as to Apple’s warnings or installation processes for third party marketplaces in the EU.

> …

> Further, Apple has imposed friction on directly downloaded apps and alternative app stores in the EU, requiring a multi-screen installation process for directly downloaded apps and distribution and operational restrictions on directly downloaded apps and alternative app stores. But in my view it has not been shown that this is wholly disproportionate to the risks involved.

[593] But proposed order 5(b) is not intended to prohibit frictions altogether. So, proposed order 6(i), which addresses the topic directly, reads:

> The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> …

> (i) impose frictions on or impediments to the discovery, downloading, installation, execution or updating of any iOS apps available or distributed via Alternative Distribution Channels, including alternative app stores:

> (i) that are disproportionate to the security risk posed by those apps to iOS devices and iOS device users; or

> (ii) that otherwise have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via Alternative Distribution Channels or the use of such apps by iOS device users;

> …

[594] Proposed order 5 is consistent with my findings that some degree of friction may be appropriate and that the EU evidence did not establish that Apple’s existing frictions are wholly disproportionate.

[595] It is appropriate that I grant an order directed at preventing Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder users and developers from using alternative distribution channels. It is foreseeable that Apple could diminish the viability of alternative distribution channels. I will further discuss the question of frictions in dealing with proposed order 6(i).

[596] Further, proposed order 5(b) is directed at a more fundamental problem. It responds to my findings that, by Apple’s policies, contractual restrictions and technical restrictions encoded in iOS, only native apps from the App Store may be installed on an iOS device, apart from certain trivial exceptions, and that Apple pre-installs a few native Apple-developed iOS apps on iOS devices supplied in Australia. That absolute technical barrier is a core mechanism by which the App Store is made the sole distribution channel. Removing it is necessary to remedy the contravening conduct and its effects in the iOS app distribution market. It does not conflict with my acceptance of proportionate security related frictions.

[597] Finally, Apple contends that proposed order 5(c) might be appropriate if there had been a contravention of s 47, but there was no such finding. But given Apple’s market power, if it could make access to the App Store conditional on developers not using alternative distribution channels, it could restore the position of the App Store as the sole source of iOS apps by contractual means, even though formal prohibitions on sideloading and alternative app stores were removed. Again, s 80 does not confine injunctive relief to prohibiting only the precise legal form of the contravening conduct, nor to conduct characterised under the same Pt IV provision.

[598] Proposed order 5 does not prevent Apple from imposing conditions when licensing IP-protected tools and technologies that are necessary for app development and alternative distribution. However, given Apple’s pattern of conduct in using fees to suppress the emergence of competition, any fees must adhere to guardrails of the kind imposed on Apple by the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit discussed earlier. Such fees and conditions would not objectively operate to hinder users or app developers in the manner prohibited by the order. Order 5 is framed by reference to an objective standard and will not be contravened by reason of unintended and objectively unlikely effects on individual developers with idiosyncratic characteristics.

[599] Now Apple advances the contention that it should now be free to make it a term of any developer’s access to the App Store that they will not make iOS available via an alternative distribution channel. This suggests that unless restrained, Apple could continue to take steps to suppress competition. Now I agree with Epic that it is irrelevant that there is no finding in Epic v Apple that such a term, in isolation, would be anti-competitive because, of course, it did not exist. But there is evidence and findings in Epic v Google that supports Epic’s position by analogy of such a realistic possibility or risk; the utility of my findings in Epic v Google I accept can be put no higher than that.

[600] In that proceeding at GJ[4154] I said:

> Further, Google’s own assessment was that “[f]or any alternative mobile store to be successful at scale … it would need at least a couple of titles from our top ~25 developers identified by Hug”.

[601] So, if Google could prevent even some of its top 25 developers from launching their titles exclusively on an alternative app store, that alternative app store would not be able to compete at scale. This of course is the strategy that Google pursued through Project Hug. This conduct limited the ability of rival app stores to compete on quality with the Play Store by differentiating themselves by attracting exclusive or early content. And I found that Google’s agreements with certain developers preventing those developers from releasing titles exclusively on alternative app stores had an anti-competitive purpose, and likely had an anti­competitive effect. Now Google argued that neither the GVP agreements nor the AVP agreements required Play Store exclusivity, but as I found at GJ[4276]:

> Google also says that neither the GVP agreements nor the AVP agreements require Play Store exclusivity. But in circumstances where the Play Store is the incumbent, it does not require exclusive content in order to maintain its user base. Rather, as Epic points out, it is prospective rivals who require exclusive or differentiated content, to draw users away from the Play Store. Project Hug denied them that content.

[602] The inability of rival app stores to offer exclusive or differentiated content, or even early access to content, from major developers, was found by me to be a real impediment to the success of a rival Android app store, since, without access to exclusive or differentiated content from key app developers, it will be even more difficult to entice users and developers away from the Play Store. I said (at GJ [2844]):

> Seventh, during the relevant period, by reason of the GVP and AVP agreements, rival Android app stores were unable to offer exclusive or differentiated content, or even early access to content, from the major developers who were party to those agreements with Google. This is a real impediment to the success of a rival Android app store, since, without access to exclusive or differentiated content from key app developers, it will be even more difficult to entice users and developers away from the Play Store.

[603] It would seem that Apple may require developers who wish to distribute apps via the App Store to agree to terms with more far-reaching anti-competitive effect than the terms of the impugned Project Hug agreements. Whilst the GVP agreements did not technically require developers to release content exclusively on the Play Store, the fact that those agreements prevented exclusive releases off–Play was enough to render the terms anti­competitive in likely effect in circumstances where the Play Store is the incumbent. So, the same would be true of any terms imposed by Apple requiring developers to distribute apps exclusively through the App Store if they wish to utilise that distribution channel at all, given the App Store’s status as the incumbent app distribution channel in the distribution market.

[604] My findings in Epic v Google support the proposition that a restraint on the imposition of such terms as proposed by order 5(c) is justified.

[605] Finally, and as I have indicated, proposed order 5 would not prevent Apple performing its Australian statutory obligations. I have added a necessary carve-out to ensure that proposed order 5 does not inhibit Apple’s compliance with its obligations under the Online Safety Act and associated Codes.

#### Proposed order 6 (order 5 as modified and made)

[606] Apple says that Epic’s further prohibitive injunction in proposed order 6 is also unwarranted. I would note that this corresponds, with modification, to order 5 that I have made.

[607] It points out that proposed order 6 stipulates 11 categories of conduct and that the common thread running between these appears to be that each category of conduct represents means by which an operator of an ecosystem in Apple’s position could seek to disfavour direct downloading of native apps.

[608] Apple says that whether it could or would engage in such conduct, and whether if and to the extent that Apple did so that conduct would contravene the CCA, was not the subject of any pleading by Epic, and was not explored at trial and in my liability findings. It says that I have made no findings and there is no evidence that any of those 11 categories of conduct would contravene the CCA.

[609] Apple says that it follows that the conduct the subject of proposed order 6 lies outside the case or controversy brought before me in these proceedings.

[610] Further, Apple says that a further reason why proposed order 6 should not be made can be found in the assumptions that justified my liability findings.

[611] It says that I found as an aspect of the counterfactual that Apple’s conduct in the counterfactual would be lawful, that is, Apple would not impose other anti-competitive restrictions that would have the same or similar effect as the iOS app distribution conduct. I also declined to draw an inference that, in the counterfactual, Apple likely would not license alternative app stores.

[612] And it says that Epic declined to specify how or on what terms it said Apple’s offering would change in the counterfactual, and asked me only to find that in the counterfactual Apple could and likely would licence its intellectual property.

[613] But it says that Epic’s proposed order 6 now seeks in effect to populate what I said in [5220] with negative content. In that paragraph I said:

> In the counterfactual, the impediments that Apple imposes on alternative app stores and direct downloading on iOS devices would be removed. It must also be assumed that Apple’s conduct in the counterfactual would be lawful, that is, Apple would not impose other anti-competitive restrictions that would have the same or similar effect as the iOS app distribution conduct.

[614] Apple says that proposed order 6 seeks to convert my finding that in the counterfactual Apple would not impose other anti-competitive restrictions into a long series of restrictions on Apple’s conduct in the world without the impugned prohibitions, presumably on the footing that if Apple were to take any of the steps proposed order 6 would proscribe, it would contravene s 46.

[615] Apple says that in circumstances where Epic’s case was that it was not required to specify how Apple’s offering would alter in the counterfactual and the liability findings accepted that the counterfactual was sufficiently clearly delineated by the proposition that Apple could and would license its intellectual property, there is no warrant or occasion for Epic now to seek to confine and limit, as proposed order 6 seeks to do, the ways in and terms on which Apple may license its intellectual property.

[616] Apple says that if it was to be alleged and found that any of the categories of conduct itemised in proposed order 6 would, if engaged in, contravene s 46, the time for that was in and through the competition analysis integral to my liability findings.

[617] Apple says that Epic, having refused to specify in any way how it said Apple would restructure its offerings if its prohibitions on alternative app stores and sideloading were found to be unlawful it cannot now be heard to allege and I should not enter into any analysis or make any findings about what avenues are open to Apple in a world without those prohibitions.

[618] Further, Apple says that contrary to proposed order 6(a), there is no basis either in principle or in my liability findings for restraining Apple from imposing any fees or conditions on access to, or use of, alternative distribution channels. Epic seeks to justify this order on the basis that, where an app is distributed via an alternative distribution channel, Apple provides no distribution service such that it can be inferred that any fee or condition that Apple might elect to impose would, more likely than not, be designed to maintain Apple’s dominance in one or both of the relevant markets. But Apple says that this overlooks the fact that the development and use of such channels requires access to Apple’s intellectual property and that transactions via alternative distribution channels take place in an ecosystem Apple has built and maintains, and must be permitted to earn revenue in and through. Accordingly, there is no basis for inferring that any fee Apple might charge would have an illicit purpose.

[619] Further, Apple says that there is no basis in my reasons or otherwise to restrain Apple from imposing any fees or conditions on access to alternative distribution channels by iOS app developers and iOS users.

[620] As I found, it is appropriate that Apple be able to recover a commercial return on its highly valuable IP-protected assets, including the tools and services that facilitate alternative app distribution. Moreover, Apple must be able to impose appropriate conditions on the use of technologies that facilitate alternative distribution. Apple’s proposed order 2 would prevent Apple from imposing terms that unreasonably prevent or hinder the use of alternative app distribution. Beyond that, no order in the nature of Epic’s proposed order 6(a) is appropriate.

[621] Further, Apple says that proposed order 6(b), if it is said to do something other than enjoin the imposition of any fees at all, would require Apple to assess whether any fee had an effect or likely effect of lessening competition before imposing it. But it says that analysis of that kind cannot be required of a party in order to ascertain whether or not a given step would place them in contempt of court.

[622] Further, Apple says that Epic is wrong to say that Apple would necessarily be acting with a substantial proscribed purpose if it were to act contrary to such a prohibition.

[623] Further, one part of order 6(b) is a prohibition on the imposition of fees or conditions on access to, or use of, tools or APIs including SDKs that have the purpose, effect, or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring alternative app distribution. But Apple says that Epic’s proposed limitation, which adopts statutory language of purpose, effect, or likely effect but couples it with a different concept of preventing, hindering or deterring, creates a standard too complex and subjective to be properly used in an injunction. Moreover, that prohibition would extend to conduct with the likely effect of deterring alternative distribution.

[624] Apple says that there is no justification in the judgment or otherwise for such an onerous limitation on Apple’s licensing of intellectual property and it is not required to address or prevent the conduct that I found to be in contravention of s 46 of the CCA. Apple has millions of developers and almost any condition, no matter how benign, would be likely to deter at least some of them from taking up alternative distribution, thus putting Apple at risk of contempt of this proposed injunction.

[625] Further, the other part of order 6(b) is a prohibition on imposing fees higher, or conditions more onerous, than those applied to developers of iOS apps which are monetised other than through in-app purchases, including free apps and apps monetised through advertising, when distributed via the App Store. Apple says that this would either preclude Apple charging any fee beyond the annual USD 99 developer program fee or require it to impose fees not presently charged on such developers who have not been heard. But Apple says that it is not appropriate for me to impose any limits on the fees that Apple can charge by reference to the fees that it charges to particular categories of developers, being in this case developers of free apps and those monetised through advertising. Those developers have not had an opportunity to be heard in this proceeding.

[626] Further, Apple says that there was no allegation and no finding by me that charging different categories of developers different fees, in the licensing of Apple’s intellectual property, would have anti-competitive effects. The ability to charge different licensees different prices is an ordinary incident of intellectual property rights and a common practice where different licensees exploit the intellectual property in different ways or earn different revenues (or no revenue) from that exploitation.

[627] Specifically, I found that in 2021 more than 85% of apps on the Australian App Store storefront were free to download and did not have any in-app purchases. Apple facilitates the creation and distribution of those apps by licensing its software and other tools used to develop the apps and charging those developers only the annual program fee of USD 99.

[628] Epic’s proposed orders would require that, unless Apple is willing to license its software to Epic and other developers who use alternative distribution for the same USD 99 fee, it must charge developers of free apps more. Apple says that that is not reasonable, and is not shown to be pro-competitive, in circumstances where Epic unlike developers of free apps would be expected to earn very substantial revenues from the iOS version of Fortnite.

[629] Further, Apple says that ultimately, order 6(b) would undermine the viability of the business model that Apple has adopted in relation to free apps, adversely impacting upon the majority of developers whose apps are free, and also consumers who benefit from the availability of free apps.

[630] Further, Apple says that proposed orders 6(c) and (d) would prevent Apple from competing with alternative distribution channels by requiring that app developers offer App Store users no less favourable terms and conditions than they offer users of alternative distribution channels. It says that there is no foundation in my findings against Apple or in the evidence for preventing Apple from competing by such means, a fact which Epic’s reliance on findings in Epic v Google underscores.

[631] It says that no part of [4822] and [4823] records a finding that each and every one of the many provisions of the DPLA and the App Review Guidelines Epic sought to impugn was imposed and enforced in contravention of s 46.

[632] Further, Epic’s proposed order 6(c) seeks to limit the manner in which the App Store can compete with rival app stores, by prohibiting Apple from making it a condition of App Store distribution that an app, or digital content for use in app, not be made available on more favourable terms, or with other benefits, elsewhere. Apple says that such a limitation would be market–distorting in circumstances where it would not equally apply to the EGS, the Play Store, the Samsung Galaxy Store, or any other app store or platform that the App Store competes with.

[633] Further, proposed order 6(d) would restrain Apple from entering into “most favoured nations” arrangements with developers, by requiring them to make content, subscriptions or features available via their apps distributed on the App Store if that content, subscription or feature is made available to users of the same app on other platforms. Apple says that such a restraint would also be market–distorting.

[634] Further, it says that most favoured nation clauses are not inherently anti-competitive and they can be legitimate competitive responses to exclusive arrangements that are common in this industry. Again, Apple says that no economic analysis demonstrates that it would be anticompetitive for Apple to engage in such conduct, and there is no basis for me to enjoin Apple from engaging in it.

[635] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 6(e) would restrain it from prohibiting, preventing, or taking any action, including imposing any fee or condition which is likely to hinder an alternative app store operator from making and implementing their own choices as to which apps they will distribute via their app store, contrary to [4736] to [4739]. I said at [4736] to [4739]:

> As to the third matter, post-distribution security would be maintained irrespective of decentralised distribution.

> Under a decentralised app distribution model, Apple would be able to maintain its ability to safeguard the security of iOS devices after apps are distributed, regardless of their distribution channel.

> First, Apple would have the ability to remove and disable bad apps distributed outside the App Store, given that those apps would have passed through iOS notarization or its equivalent. Apple’s digital signing of all iOS apps means that Apple can at any time revoke the signature of an iOS app, such that it ceases to be capable of being installed or run on an iOS device. It follows that Apple would be able to enforce any decision it might make about the threat posed by an arbitrary iOS app to the security of iOS devices. Apple would thereby act as a single point of enforcement, regardless of the app’s distribution channel.

> Second, given that each and every iOS app would be centrally reviewed by Apple, Apple would at any time be capable of re-scanning or re-analysing a previously submitted app, based on any new information, intelligence, or progress in scanning technology. And if an app previously thought to be innocuous were to be identified as malicious in this way, Apple could disable it by revoking its digital signature.

[636] Apple says that that order would prevent Apple from engaging in notarization or other forms of app review and so it is inappropriate and inconsistent with my judgment. Further, Apple says that whatever other operation it might have, the order is unnecessary in circumstances where Apple proposes an injunction in its proposed order 2 that would restrain it from unreasonably preventing or hindering alternative app distribution.

[637] Epic says that proposed order 6(e) addresses the risk that Apple may attempt to limit the choice of alternative app store operators as to which apps they distribute, but Apple says that this asserted risk is incidental to Apple’s legitimate, pro-competitive interest in maintaining the safety and security of its ecosystem.

[638] As to proposed order 6(f), Apple says that my liability findings provide no warrant for limiting Apple’s freedom to regulate its ecosystem in the manner proposed. Proposed order 6(f) would prevent Apple from requiring app developers using alternative distribution channels only to use as agents, for example, persons who meet certain standards of technical and financial security. Moreover, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 6(f) is unnecessary in circumstances where Apple’s proposed order 2 would restrain it from unreasonably preventing or hindering alternative app distribution.

[639] As to proposed order 6(g), Apple says that a restraint on it prohibiting the pre-installation of third–party apps on iOS devices is unwarranted, for the reasons already given in respect of the declaratory relief sought in order 1(b)(vi). Further, proposed order 6(g) remains inappropriate in circumstances where Apple is the only manufacturer of iOS devices.

[640] Further, Apple says that proposed order 6(h) goes beyond the pleaded case, by reference to steps which I found Apple would be permitted to take in the counterfactual and employs matters of controversy and judgment (“poses a genuine security risk”; “requires urgent elimination”) which cannot practicably form part of any workable regime of injunctive relief.

[641] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 6(h) would require it to give developers reasonable notice of the existence, nature and extent of security risks before disabling or removing apps. It says that this is inappropriate because it cannot be assumed that all of the developers in its Developer Program are acting in good faith. It says that order 6(h) is inappropriate because it would prevent Apple from acting immediately to protect iOS users from fraud and other security risks. It says that in the case of a bad actor, disclosing any security vulnerability which Apple has identified to that developer could aid in the bad actor reverse engineering Apple’s malware detection capabilities. Moreover, it says that Professor Somayaji agreed that there are circumstances in which apps should be disabled or deleted from end-user devices without notice to developers, including, at least, in the case of malicious apps and apps that compromise sandboxing. I will say more about Professor Somayaji’s evidence later.

[642] Further, Apple says that proposed order 6(i) has the same problems as proposed order 5 insofar as the latter order would limit Apple’s ability to impose frictions on the use of alternative distribution channels. Further, it says that the imprecise nature of the drafting makes it unclear whether any default safety settings and tools implemented by Apple pursuant to its Online Safety Act obligations could be characterised as impermissible frictions or impediments that are prohibited by proposed order 6(i).

[643] Further, Apple says that I declined to find that Apple imposed disproportionate frictions or impediments on alternative app distribution in the EU and accepted that some such restrictions are appropriate.

[644] Further, Apple says that the evidence from Mr Grant does not justify revisiting my finding that it has not been shown that Apple’s installation process in the EU is disproportionate to the security risks involved. Nor does it justify a finding that Apple is likely to impose disproportionate frictions in the future.

[645] Further, Apple says that proposed order 6(j) is unsupported by the liability findings against Apple and unduly limits, in a manner that is contrary to my disposition of arguments concerning the pro-competitive aspects of the impugned conduct, Apple’s ability to regulate its ecosystem. This restraint would prevent Apple from requiring the app developers who use alternative distribution channels only to use payment methods which meet certain standards of technical and financial security.

[646] Further, Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 6(j) would prohibit it from requiring that developers offer or use any particular payment method in apps distributed via alternative distribution channels. Apple says that to the extent that that order is intended to, or could be interpreted to, prevent Apple from requiring that developers use payment methods that adhere to technical and financial security standards, it is inappropriate. It says that I have found, relevantly, that Apple has a legitimate interest in maintaining the overall standards of the ecosystem.

[647] Further, Apple says that proposed order 6(k) seeks to repurpose reasons on aspects of power and market to justify restraints targeting asserted or apprehended anti-competitive effects. It would fetter Apple’s commercial freedom in a manner and to an extent which none of the liability findings justify.

[648] Apple says that proposed order 6(k) would prevent it from requiring that developers enter into any contractual arrangement other than a licence of Apple’s tools and APIs for the development of iOS apps as a condition of distributing apps via alternative distribution. But Apple says that that prohibition is inappropriate and not justified by my findings.

###### Analysis

[649] Proposed order 6 is a negative injunction, prohibiting Apple from engaging in specific categories of conduct that would lessen or prevent competition in the distribution market. It does not prevent Apple from imposing fees or conditions on the distribution of iOS apps via the App Store, or for the use of IAP. Nor does it prevent Apple from charging app developers a fee for their use of Apple’s tools or APIs to develop and distribute iOS apps.

[650] Now Apple has raised a number of general objections in relation to proposed order 6, but none have any real merit.

[651] First, Apple contends that proposed order 6 is outside the case or controversy. But proposed order 6 responds to my findings. I found that Apple’s iOS app distribution conduct had both the purpose, and the effect and likely effect, of substantially lessening competition. Further, I found that Apple’s conduct was engaged in for the substantial purpose of maintaining the App Store as the only store-like interface on iOS devices, with a view to protecting Apple’s revenue from any threat of competition. Further, I found that the practical effect was that no alternative source of supply of iOS app distribution services was available to developers or iOS device users other than the App Store, which precludes competition.

[652] Proposed order 6 responds to those findings by enjoining Apple from engaging in 11 specific types of conduct. That approach is warranted by findings which establish that Apple devised and pursued a deliberate and comprehensive strategy of foreclosure in relation to iOS app distribution. I found at [4108]:

> Second, it could not seriously be doubted that Apple imposes and enforces its restrictive terms with impunity. The fact that Apple is not constrained by competition is apparent from its uninhibited ability to impose and enforce its restrictive terms concerning iOS app distribution. Apple enforces those terms inflexibly and refuses to accommodate the needs of even the largest developers. The instances in which Apple has enforced its restrictive terms to foreclose competition are too numerous to set out in full.

[653] I also found that Apple devises ways by which it can continue to foreclose competition even in the face of regulatory obligations, and that its response to the DMA in Europe was designed to dissuade developers from taking up alternative payment solutions. I said at [5830] and [6005]:

> As I have explained elsewhere, in Europe, where Apple has been forced to allow developers to offer alternative payment solutions for in-app purchases, Apple imposes a series of restrictions designed to dissuade developers from taking up that option, including an obligation to pay a core technology fee. But even under those conditions, Apple accepts that some large developers may value having their own alternate payments arrangement.

> …

> In these instances, even regulatory obligations have not prevented Apple from devising ways by which it can continue to foreclose competition. The same applies to Apple’s response to the enactment of the Digital Markets Act.

[654] If one was not to enjoin Apple from engaging in specific conduct that would undermine the viability of alternative distribution channels, this would leave the door open for Apple to continue its pattern of imposing further terms, conditions and charges upon developers designed to prevent the emergence of competition. Apple has repeatedly implemented practices of purported compliance that seek to circumvent the practical effects of regulatory and judicial constraints. So, absent appropriate restraint, it is likely that Apple would employ similar circumvention techniques here.

[655] Second, Apple contends that whether it could or would engage in conduct the subject of proposed order 6 and whether that conduct would contravene s 46 was not pleaded by Epic, and was unexplored at trial and in the liability findings. But s 80 permits injunctions that are appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy Apple’s contravening conduct and its effects, including orders that restrain apprehended modes of conduct which re-create or perpetuate the same foreclosure and anti-competitive purpose. But in any event, many of the sub-paragraphs of proposed order 6 are directed at conduct which I condemned and were the subject of detailed evidence and reasoning.

[656] Third, Apple has emphasised its freedom to regulate its ecosystem and the threat that proposed order 6 poses in denying or severely limiting the means by which Apple can monetise and regulate its ecosystem.

[657] Apple’s asserted freedom to regulate its ecosystem is no shield. It is not a freestanding interest that I am required to safeguard when exercising my powers under s 80. That provision authorises me to fashion orders that are appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy the contravening conduct and its effects. In this case, I am authorised to grant relief that safeguards competition in the iOS app distribution market that has hitherto been completely foreclosed, even if in doing so the orders fundamentally alter the way Apple currently operates.

[658] Further, Apple does not own any such ecosystem. It holds copyright in the iOS operating system which it has fully licensed to users. And it needs and licenses developers to create iOS apps for users. All of Apple’s licensing decisions and arrangements are subject to the CCA. Further, s 80 exists to restrain the misuse of commercial power. The fact that an injunction may fetter Apple’s power or freedom where Apple has misused it is not an objection.

[659] Fourth, Apple claims that proposed order 6 impermissibly seeks to populate the counterfactual assumption with a list of negative prescriptions, but that Epic declined at trial to specify how Apple’s offering would change beyond a finding that Apple could and would license its IP; see what I said at [5220] and [5237].

[660] But Apple has confused the relevant inquiry. Assumptions and findings about Apple’s licensing of its IP were for the purposes of my counterfactual inquiry and directed to the liability question. They do not confine my remedial powers. As I have said elsewhere, I am free under s 80 to grant such injunctions as I determine to be appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy the contravening conduct and its effects and to prevent any attempt to circumvent the relief ordered. An implied limitation that the relief granted must mirror the bare assumptions used in the counterfactual inquiry cannot be read into the terms of s 80.

[661] Let me now go through the sub-paragraphs of the proposed order 6.

###### Proposed order 6(a)

[662] As to proposed order 6(a), I found that in the European Union in response to the DMA, Apple requires developers who wish to distribute iOS apps via alternative distribution channels to pay Apple a so-called core technology fee based on downloads (CTF). I found that the CTF, and restrictions implemented by Apple more generally in response to the DMA, were designed by Apple to dissuade developers from taking up alternative payment solutions. Order 6(a) responds to those findings. Where an app is distributed via an alternative distribution channel, Apple provides no distribution service. Accordingly, it is reasonable to infer that any fee or condition that Apple might elect to impose for access to, or use of, an alternative distribution channel would, more likely than not, be designed to maintain Apple’s dominance in one or both of the relevant markets. Apple should therefore be restrained from doing so. To be clear, this order would not prevent Apple from charging a fee in connection with the use by developers of its technology.

[663] Apple claims that there is no basis in principle or in the findings to restrain it from imposing any fees or conditions on access to alternative distribution channels. It contends that the development and use of such channels requires access to its IP, and that transactions occur within an ecosystem Apple has built and maintains, so it cannot be inferred that any fee would have an illicit purpose.

[664] But proposed order 6(a) is directed at the kind of charges that Apple has imposed in response to the DMA, which I have found were designed to dissuade developers from using alternative payment solutions. In light of those findings and my conclusions as to Apple’s substantial anticompetitive purpose, there is a sound basis for relief that prevents Apple from imposing additional, channel-specific fees or conditions targeted at alternative distribution channels. Proposed order 6(a) leaves intact Apple’s ability to charge for legitimate use of its IP and tools.

[665] Proposed order 6(a) proscribes additional, channel-specific fees or conditions, including core technology type fees structured by reference to downloads via alternative distribution channels, that have the purpose, effect or likely effect of deterring the use of those channels.

[666] In continuing to protest Epic’s proposed prohibition on the imposition of fees or conditions on access to alternative distribution channels, Apple ignores my finding that the charges Apple imposed in response to the DMA were designed to dissuade developers from using alternative payment solutions. A prohibition on equivalent fees in respect of alternative distribution channels is appropriate in light of the conduct Apple has engaged in to date, and my findings as to Apple’s anti-competitive purpose. The same reasoning requires a prohibition on conditions; see proposed order 6(a).

[667] Further, Apple has led no evidence that any Apple IP is used when a developer’s app is downloaded by an iOS user. Nor is there any evidence as to the costs associated with it offering the necessary tools to developers and users to permit app distribution via alternative channels.

[668] I said at [5319], [5325] to [5331] and [5338]:

> So, I infer that any SDKs or APIs that may be necessary for a developer to use in the development of an iOS app are the subject of the Xcode agreement.

> …

> As to Apple’s suggestion that additional Apple software would be necessary to develop alternative app stores or to facilitate direct downloads, Apple has led no evidence to this effect. It was in a position to do so. I would not draw inferences favourable to Apple in the absence of such evidence. Apple’s contention is inconsistent with the evidence before me as to the development of alternative app stores or apps available for direct download on macOS.

> Further, as Epic points out, there are additional reasons that justify me in rejecting any suggestion that Apple is willing to license its software for use in developing iOS apps only on the condition that they be distributed solely through the App Store.

> First, Apple permits developers to distribute their iOS apps to individuals within their organisation on a limited number of devices. Developers enrolled in the Apple developer enterprise program can distribute internal-use-only apps to employees of large organisations.

> Second, Apple permits distribution by developers of “Custom Apps” through “Custom App Distribution”, being the store functionality that enables users to obtain apps through the use of Apple business manager, Apple school manager, or as otherwise permitted by Apple.

> Third, in Europe Apple permits the distribution of iOS apps outside of the App Store. This demonstrates the possibility of Apple licensing any intellectual property necessary for the distribution of iOS apps through alternative methods of distribution when its preferred monopoly position is disturbed.

> Now Apple says, without evidence, that there is no reason to think that Apple would extend access to its SDKs and APIs to assist in the development of alternative app stores for iOS to developers beyond Europe, or that developers could create such apps without Apple’s SDKs and APIs.

> Even if I accepted the premise that such software would be necessary, which is unsupported by the evidence, these are not inferences that I would draw given Apple’s failure to lead evidence on the topic.

> …

> Now Apple says that there is no evidence that any licence fee it might lawfully charge would be so low, or the other terms it might offer would be sufficiently attractive, as to permit viable third-party stores or direct downloading. But Apple has failed to prove the value of any intellectual property rights that Apple says would inevitably need to be licensed by the developer of an iOS app. Accordingly, I cannot speculate as to what a commercial return might be, if Apple were in the counterfactual to seek to monetise its investments in some different way.

[669] Apple’s complaint that it ought not be prohibited from imposing fees or conditions that have the likely effect of prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder the distribution or updating of iOS apps via alternative distribution channels on the basis that such a prohibition is overly onerous should be rejected. Such a prohibition is appropriate having regard to my findings as to Apple’s substantial anti-competitive purpose in prohibiting alternative distribution channels, and its conduct in other jurisdictions where it has sought to maintain its existing business model and to raise obstacles to competition to the greatest extent possible in the face of regulatory intervention.

###### Proposed order 6(b)

[670] As to proposed order 6(b), I found that the decentralised distribution of iOS apps cannot occur without access to Apple’s intellectual property. Further, I accepted that Apple ought to be able to charge developers for the use of its intellectual property, provided that the imposition of any such charge was lawful and not itself contrary to s 46. Order 6(b) reflects these matters, by restraining Apple from imposing any fees or conditions for access to Apple’s tools or APIs necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps via alternative distribution channels that have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the distribution or updating of iOS apps via alternative distribution channels.

[671] Proposed order 6(b) identifies a specific example of a proscribed fee or condition, being a fee that is higher, or a condition more onerous, than those applied to developers of iOS apps which are monetised other than through in-app purchases when distributed via the App Store. I found that developers of iOS apps currently access the same tools or APIs to develop and distribute iOS apps as part of the Developer Program, irrespective of monetisation method, and that developers pay Apple a fixed annual program fee, which is currently USD 99, to gain access to Apple’s technology and tools for, and associated with, the development of apps, whether for iOS, iPadOS, macOS or other Apple operating system.

[672] In all the circumstances, including my finding of Apple’s long-held and unlawful purpose in foreclosing competition in the distribution market, if Apple were now to impose selectively disadvantageous fees or conditions for access to its tools and APIs on developers of apps monetised through in-app purchases, at least a substantial purpose for engaging in that conduct would be a proscribed purpose. In addition to evidencing a proscribed purpose, the imposition of deterrent fees or conditions of that kind would likely reproduce the exclusionary effects that I found to contravene s 46.

[673] Now Apple contends that if proposed order 6(b) is not read as a blanket ban on any fees, it would require Apple, before imposing any fee, to analyse whether it has the effect or likely effect of lessening competition before imposing it.

[674] The “purpose, effect or likely effect” formulation is not an impermissibly uncertain standard. It uses familiar language, and is directed to a concrete question, being whether the fee or condition would materially obstruct distribution via alternative distribution channels, not whether it has some abstract “effect on competition”. ICI at 261 endorsed the expression “to the like effect” as an example of a permissible injunction.

[675] Apple further contends that the parity requirement would either preclude Apple charging any fee beyond the USD 99 annual developer fee or require it to impose new fees on those other developers. But this mischaracterises the nature of proposed order 6(b), which employs a non-discrimination safeguard, not a fee cap nor a compulsory fee on developers. Proposed order 6(b) uses the fixed annual program fee as a comparator to prevent Apple imposing selectively disadvantageous fees or conditions on developers whose apps are distributed via alternative distribution channels. It prevents Apple from imposing additional or more onerous fees on alternative distribution channels by reason of their use of those channels, and in so doing, prevents Apple from preferencing distribution through the App Store.

[676] Against the findings as to Apple’s substantial anti-competitive purpose and pattern of devising anticompetitive strategies set out above, it is appropriate under s 80 to frame relief which constrains the use of fees as a mechanism of foreclosure, including by building in a non-discrimination standard so as to prevent the inappropriate preferencing of the App Store.

[677] Contrary to Apple’s contention, the non-discrimination safeguard imposed by order 6(b) is appropriate having regard to my findings. I found that developers of iOS apps currently access the same tools or APIs to develop and distribute iOS apps as part of the Apple Developer Program irrespective of monetisation method. Further, I found that developers pay Apple a fixed program fee to gain access to Apple’s technology and tools for, and associated with, the development of apps, whether for iOS, iPadOS, macOS or other Apple operating systems.

[678] In those circumstances, having regard to my findings as to Apple’s unlawful purpose in foreclosing competition in the distribution market, if Apple were now able to discriminate between developers who monetise their apps through in-app purchases and those who do not, that would obstruct the emergence of competition. And a substantial purpose of such discrimination would be a proscribed purpose.

[679] Against the background of my findings as to Apple’s anti-competitive purpose and pattern of devising anti-competitive strategies, it is appropriate in the public interest to use the wide power conferred by s 80 to constrain Apple’s use of fees as a mechanism of foreclosure through imposing a non-discrimination standard as proposed by order 6(b).

[680] Apple’s attempt to oppose order 6(b) on the basis that developers who do not monetise their apps through in-app purchases have not had an opportunity to be heard should be rejected. Epic is a developer of, inter alia, free games. Further, order 6(b) is not inherently adverse to such developers’ interests. Whether or not that is so depends on whether Apple makes a business decision to increase fees imposed on such developers in response to my orders. Apple has led no evidence that it will. It should not be assumed that Apple will take that course, given that Apple benefits from the creation, availability and usage of iOS apps by reason of network effects and so is incentivised to provide services to encourage developers to offer iOS apps, including for free.

[681] Nor is there any evidence to support the assertion that order 6(b) would undermine the viability of the business model that Apple has adopted in relation to free apps. The objective of s 80 is to advance the public interest, and Apple’s business choices cannot stand in the way of that objective.

###### Proposed orders 6(c) and (d)

[682] As to proposed order 6(c), in Epic v Google I found that Google prohibits price competition in the context of the user choice billing (UCB) program, other than in South Korea (GJ[638], [5374], [5379]). Conduct of a similar kind is addressed by order 6(c), in the context of the distribution market.

[683] I agree with Epic that the fact that Apple does not currently require price parity between platforms is beside the point. Until now, Apple has eliminated any opportunity for price competition altogether by foreclosing all alternative distribution channels for iOS apps.

[684] As to proposed order 6(d), I found that one of the terms of the DPLA that Apple imposed and enforced on developers in contravention of s 46 was a term prohibiting app developers from allowing users to access content, subscriptions or features they purchased from the developer’s own website, or another app distribution channel, without also making it available as an in-app purchase within their iOS apps distributed via the App Store, subject to limited exceptions. In Epic v Google, I found that Google had entered into agreements with certain developers which required them to ensure that the core content, features and functionality of mobile titles available on the Play Store were the same as on comparable platforms and through other mobile distribution channels, which clauses were found to be directed at hindering rivals from competing by enticing users away from the Play Store. Proposed order 6(d) responds to those findings by restraining Apple from hindering competition in the distribution market by limiting product differentiation as between the App Store and rival distribution channels.

[685] Apple contends that proposed orders 6(c) and (d) would prevent it from competing with alternative distribution channels by requiring that developers offer App Store users no less favourable terms and conditions than users of alternative distribution channels. Apple claims there is no foundation in my findings for restraining Apple in this way, and that no part of my judgment records a finding that each of the relevant provisions of the DPLA and the App Review Guidelines that Epic impugned was imposed and enforced in contravention of s 46.

[686] Proposed orders 6(c) and (d) prevent Apple from using its market power to suppress price and content differentiation on rival channels and, in doing so, neutralising that source of competition. They do not prevent Apple from competing with alternative distribution channels on the merits.

[687] As I have said, Apple has until now completely foreclosed competition in iOS app distribution by eliminating alternative distribution channels altogether. Given Apple’s substantial anti-competitive purpose and pattern of devising anti-competitive strategies, it is appropriate under s 80 to restrain Apple from continuing to insulate the App Store from competition by undermining the effectiveness of alternative channels.

[688] The order prohibits Apple from requiring developers who use alternative distribution channels to refrain from offering their apps at lower prices or with favourable discounts or benefits, compared to when the same app is distributed via the App Store. Any such requirement imposed by Apple, a company with continuing market power, would be likely to suppress competition between the alternative channel and the App Store. The order does not prevent Apple responding to such discounts or benefits by a competitor such as the EGS by offering its own discounts or benefits.

[689] Proposed order 6(d) restrains Apple from hindering competition in the distribution market by limiting product differentiation as between the App Store, and rival distribution channels. As I have said, it responds directly to my finding that the contravening conduct included a term prohibiting app developers from allowing users to access content, subscriptions or features they purchased from the developer’s own website, or another app distribution channel, without also making it available as an in-app purchase within their iOS apps distributed via the App Store. Contrary to Apple’s assertions, proposed order 6(d) does not depend on a finding that every provision of the DPLA and the App Review Guidelines that Epic challenged was individually held to contravene s 46.

[690] Proposed order 6(d) provides that Apple must not require developers who make app contents, subscriptions or features available via alternative distribution channels to also make that same content available when the app is distributed via the App Store. In a competitive market, there is no reason why a developer should not be able to offer differentiated content that preferences one distribution channel over another, including where it is cheaper or commercially beneficial for it to do so.

[691] Further, Apple’s submission that proposed orders 6(c) and (d) are unsupported by competition analysis and would be market-distorting ignores the evidence adduced and findings made in the Epic v Google proceeding.

[692] Certain of the AVP agreements included price parity clauses of the kind prohibited by order 6(c) (GJ[4175]), and the GVP agreements included “content parity” clauses of the kind prohibited by order 6(d) (GJ[4129]). I found that these terms of the AVP agreements had an anti-competitive purpose, and the terms of the GVP agreements were both anti-competitive in purpose and likely effect. The primary reason I did not consider the AVP agreements to be anti-competitive in likely effect was because AVP agreements were only signed with four developers.

[693] If an incumbent with substantial market power such as Apple were permitted to impose price parity and content parity clauses on all developers, as Apple seeks to do, self-evidently the effect would be to substantially lessen competition in the app distribution market. The evidence and my findings in Epic v Google suggest this to say the least.

###### Proposed order 6(e)

[694] As to proposed order 6(e), this order addresses the risk that Apple may attempt to limit the choice of app store operators as to which apps they distribute via their alternative app stores, much as Apple has done in respect of developer choice of payment methods for purchases of digital content for use in-app.

[695] Apple’s contention that proposed order 6(e) would prevent it from requiring apps offered by alternative app stores to be notarised by Apple is not correct. Proposed order 6(e) is not directed at neutral security measures such as notarisation or malware scanning. It is directed at preventing Apple from using its market power to dictate which apps an alternative app store operator may carry, for anti-competitive reasons.

###### Proposed order 6(f)

[696] As to proposed order 6(f), I found that Apple’s current arrangements under the DPLA, which include a requirement that developers appoint it as the developer’s agent for the marketing and downloading of the developer’s iOS apps, gives rise to a relationship of forced agency. That is an aspect of Apple’s conduct that I found has the purpose and effect of substantially lessening of competition in the distribution market. There can be no warrant for Apple requiring the appointment of any particular person as a developer’s agent in respect of the distribution of iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, and proposed order 6(f) restrains Apple from doing so.

[697] Apple contends that the forced agency findings were part of a broader complex of conduct and do not justify a specific restraint of the kind in proposed order 6(f). It also claims that the order would prevent Apple from requiring developers using alternative distribution channels to use only agents who meet certain technical or financial security standards.

[698] I treated the forced agency structure as one aspect of the iOS restrictive terms imposed and enforced by Apple with a substantial anti-competitive purpose and effect. That forced agency was expressed to apply generally, and prevented a developer independently managing the distribution of its own apps, the supply of its own or chosen payment solution, and its relationship with its own customers. It was part and parcel of Apple’s contravening conduct. Section 80 permits me to target particular features of that complex of conduct, where doing so is appropriate to prevent or remedy the contravention and its effects. Proposed order 6(f) is a narrowly-focused restraint on replicating that forced-agency model in relation to alternative distribution channels.

[699] Given that Apple’s forced-agency arrangements formed part of the contravening conduct and that Apple has used its asserted power over its so-called ecosystem control to foreclose competition, there is no warrant for preserving a freedom to insist on particular agents in respect of alternative distribution channels.

###### Proposed order 6(g)

[700] As to proposed order 6(g), Epic says that Apple prohibits the pre-installation of third-party apps, including app stores, on iOS devices, and that this prohibition is an aspect of Apple’s contravening conduct. Order 6(g) responds to this finding.

[701] Apple contends that a restraint on prohibiting pre-installation of third-party apps including alternative app stores is unwarranted for the same reasons it has given in relation to declaratory relief for preinstallation (proposed order 1(b)(vi)). But as I say, the prohibition on pre-installation was not regarded by me as an aspect of the contravening iOS app distribution conduct.

###### Proposed order 6(h)

[702] As to proposed order 6(h), I made findings to the effect that under a decentralised app distribution model Apple could implement a notarization or equivalent process, which would permit Apple to be able to enforce any decision it might make about the threat posed by an arbitrary iOS app to the security of iOS devices, including by revoking its digital signature. Order 6(h) enjoins Apple from exercising any such power, other than in the limited circumstances identified in sub-paragraphs (i) and (ii).

[703] As I have said, Apple contends that proposed order 6(h) goes beyond the pleaded case because it regulates steps which I treated as measures Apple could legitimately take in the counterfactual. Apple also claims that the concepts “poses a genuine security risk” and “requires urgent elimination” are matters of controversy and judgment which cannot practicably form part of a workable regime of injunctive relief.

[704] Proposed order 6(h) is directed at the same kind of power Apple has already been found to have unlawfully used as part of the contravening conduct: the ability to remove apps and cut off developers, exemplified by the removal of Fortnite and termination of Epic’s developer account, which I treated as a paradigm instance of Apple enforcing its restrictive terms to shield itself from competition.

[705] In a world of decentralised app distribution where I have found Apple will likely implement notarisation and the ability to revoke signatures, it is appropriate under s 80 to constrain Apple from using that ability as a renewed mechanism of foreclosure in respect of apps obtained via alternative distribution channels.

[706] The “genuine security risk” and “requires urgent elimination” language is not out of step with ordinary injunctive standards, which may use expressions such as “material risk” and “serious danger”.

[707] Contrary to Apple’s contention, proposed order 6(h) strikes an appropriate balance between the need to restrain Apple from taking steps to stifle competition under the guise of security measures, and the need for Apple to disable or delete malware without notice to developers in certain circumstances. Now Epic says that this is achieved by the use of the disjunctive in proposed order 6(h)(ii), but I will make this clearer in the order that I will make. The order does not prohibit Apple from removing or disabling apps that pose a genuine security risk that requires urgent elimination without notice.

###### Proposed order 6(i)

[708] As to proposed order 6(i), I found that frictions in the downloading and installation process for an app discourage users from obtaining the app (GJ[2459], [4766], [4821]). Installation friction comprises both the warning presented to users as well as the total number of steps required for installation (GJ[4819]).

[709] Proposed order 6(i) restrains Apple from imposing frictions or impediments on the discovery, downloading, installation, execution or updating of iOS apps available or distributed via alternative distribution channels that are disproportionate to the security risk posed by those apps.

[710] I was clear as to the meaning of disproportionate in this context, so an injunction hinging on the imposition of disproportionate frictions or impediments is appropriately precise. I said at GJ[5031] to [5033]:

> In Professor Somayaji’s opinion, the key factor in determining whether installation friction is applied proportionately in relation to security risk is whether the installation process helps a user discern between safe apps and malicious ones.
