> If friction is applied proportionately, users are less likely to install risky apps and will be informed about risks associated with the app which may help them to exercise prudence when using the app after choosing to install it. By contrast, friction applied to apps that are known to be safe is disproportionate, does not serve a security purpose and can be detrimental to security, particularly where its overuse leads to users becoming desensitised to actual risks.

> I have accepted Professor Somayaji’s views given his expertise and reliability.

[711] Now Apple contends that proposed order 6(i) impermissibly limits Apple’s ability to impose frictions on the use of alternative distribution channels, contrary to the liability findings on EU frictions. It also contends that the drafting is imprecise and creates uncertainty as to whether default safety settings and tools implemented under the Online Safety Act could be characterised as impermissible frictions or impediments.

[712] But proposed order 6(i) does not restrain Apple from imposing any frictions and impediments. It restrains only those frictions and impediments that are disproportionate to the security risk or that otherwise have the purpose, effect or likely effect of preventing, hindering or deterring the use of alternative distribution channels.

[713] And I agree with Epic that the importance of ensuring that Apple does not impose disproportionate frictions is underscored by the further evidence of Mr Grant which addresses iOS user behaviour in the direct download flow for the EGS in Europe on iOS devices after Apple reduced installation frictions in that flow.

[714] The evidence shows that iOS users who attempted to directly download the EGS app onto iOS devices dropped off at a significantly lower rate, in other words proceeded to complete their download of EGS at a significantly higher rate, after Apple reduced friction in its direct downloading install flow on iOS devices in the EU. I agree with Epic that the practical importance of orders which preclude or at least minimise unnecessary frictions in the app installation or payment processes is supported by the evidence of Mr Grant.

[715] Mr Grant explained that from May 2024, in response to ongoing enforcement of the DMA, Apple began allowing users in the EU to directly download apps, including the EGS, onto iOS devices. Since then, Apple has updated iOS such that the installation flow has improved for users in the EU who attempt to install EGS from the Epic Games website. In short, for users with iOS version 18.5 or earlier installed on their device, the installation flow comprised several more steps than it did for users with iOS version 18.6 or later.

[716] Data pertaining to mobile installation of EGS before and after 29 July 2025 when iOS 18.6 was released shows a marked reduction in the drop off rate after the number of steps in the installation flow was reduced. Specifically, from 1 June to 15 July 2025, when the old installation flow applied, the drop-off rate between the commencement and conclusion of the installation process was 63%. Contrastingly, from 30 July 2025 to 15 September 2025, when the new installation flow applied, the overall drop off rate across all recorded EGS installations from iOS devices was 47%. When considering EGS installation attempts exclusively from devices running iOS 18.6, the average drop off rate in the same period was only 24%.

[717] This data illustrates two things. First, it shows that minimising unnecessary frictions in the installation flow is important for any developer who seeks to distribute their iOS app outside the App Store. Second, it shows the anti-competitive effect that such restrictions can have if they are allowed to persist. Moreover, it is convenient to note here that the same applies to alternative payment methods. As Epic points out, if users face more steps and frictions to complete transactions for the same good using a third-party payment solution when compared to using IAP, then developers using those alternative solutions are disproportionately affected by the implementation of disclosure sheets. This could lead to lower user spending and increased issues like cart abandonment.

[718] Now Mr Friedland says that disclosure sheets or information sheets can be beneficial from a security perspective when they provide relevant information that is proportional to the risk posed by an app during installation. But even if they are proportional to risk, as Professor Somayaji said such disclosure sheets are only appropriate if present for all apps at a similar level of risk, irrespective of the source of installation.

[719] Professor Somayaji said that implementing disclosure sheets for all app distribution channels except for App Store apps on iOS devices, by default, is a policy decision that is not based on any security or technical constraints and goes beyond Apple’s responsibilities as a platform owner. This differentiated implementation of disclosure sheets in iOS default settings privileges an app distribution method that is not inherently more secure or trusted than other third-party app stores. In other jurisdictions where Apple has enabled alternative app distribution, apps that are available for distribution through direct downloads or via alternative app stores are already subject to Apple-controlled notarization and review processes, which include automated scanning for functionality, safety, security, and privacy prior to certification. Moreover, because iOS apps are sandboxed, as implemented in other jurisdictions, apps available through alternative distribution will be sandboxed, which further strengthens security and reduces the likelihood that any app will compromise other apps. Under these conditions, apps installed on iOS devices from alternative sources would offer a similar level of protection to iOS devices as the App Store.

[720] App installation could require just a single informative screen, and alternative app stores require no additional restrictions for security so long as they must abide by iOS notarization requirements. But such disclosure sheets are only effective when the overall installation friction is proportional to security risks. Otherwise legitimate, well-vetted apps offered via direct downloads and alternative app stores can end up stigmatized relative to potentially less safe App Store downloads.

[721] The refreshed version of disclosure sheets implemented by Apple in the EU do not provide information to the user that is proportional to the security risks of app installation from alternative sources compared to the security risks of app installation from the App Store.

[722] Now Apple opposes proposed order 6(i) on the basis that I declined to find that Apple imposed disproportionate impediments on alternative app distribution in the EU. But as I have previously made clear, my liability reasons were based only on an historic position in the EU. It was always my expectation that I would be taken to further evidence at the relief hearing to be brought up to date on developments in the EU, and I am not precluded from now considering whether what Apple might propose in terms of screen warnings or frictions is disproportionate.

[723] Further, given Apple’s breach of s 46 and its conduct, as Epic correctly described it, in seeking to obstruct the emergence of competition where it has been left free to do so in other jurisdictions, it is in the public interest that I should prohibit Apple from imposing disproportionate frictions on, or impediments to, the discovery, downloading, installation, execution or updating of iOS apps available or distributed via alternative distribution channels.

[724] I will further discuss the topic of disclosure sheets later in the context of discussing proposed orders 7 to 11.

###### Proposed order 6(j)

[725] As to proposed order 6(j), the effect of Apple’s conduct to date has been that any app developer who wishes to make their iOS app available for distribution on iOS must use IAP for all in-app purchases of digital content. Order 6(j) seeks to ensure that Apple does not require developers who make their iOS apps available for distribution via an alternative distribution channel to offer or to use any particular payment solution, or require that they must offer IAP as an alternative to the developer’s preferred payment solution, being conduct which would deter developers from distributing their apps via alternative distribution channels.

[726] Apple contends that proposed order 6(j) is unsupported by the liability findings and unduly limits its ability to regulate its alleged ecosystem. It says the order would prevent Apple from requiring developers using alternative distribution channels to use payment methods that meet certain technical and financial security standards.

[727] But a narrow restraint preventing Apple from tying access to alternative distribution channels to IAP, or to any particular payment method, is a necessary and warranted measure aimed at ensuring that Apple cannot deter developers from using those channels.

[728] Proposed order 6(j) would not prevent Apple in a genuine case from requiring that developers cease using payment methods that do not comply with generally accepted technical and financial security standards. But as Epic points out, Apple has never considered it necessary to examine or review the technical and financial security standards of payment systems that developers use to facilitate the purchase of physical goods or services. As Epic not unfairly described it, Apple’s new found desire to require that developers use only payment solutions that adhere to technical and financial security standards, and then only for payment solutions facilitating digital purchases, suggests that its real objective is to generate obstacles to the use of payment methods other than IAP when deployed for digital purchases.

[729] Further, there is no good reason at all why Apple should be permitted to force on developers the requirement that IAP be offered as an option; I would make a similar point concerning proposed order 10(c).

###### Proposed order 6(k)

[730] As to proposed order 6(k), I found that Apple was compelled to make various changes in connection with iOS app distribution by force of the DMA, and that under that regime, developers were only able to obtain the benefit of those changes by agreeing to the alternative terms addendum for apps in the EU. Moreover, I found that restrictions imposed on developers under the alternative terms addendum were designed by Apple to dissuade developers from taking up alternative payment solutions. Further, I found that the decentralised distribution of iOS apps cannot occur without access to Apple’s intellectual property. Proposed order 6(k) responds to those findings, by restraining Apple from requiring developers who make their apps available for distribution via an alternative distribution channel to enter into any contractual arrangement with Apple, or any other person, as a condition of the developer distributing their iOS apps via the alternative distribution channel, other than in respect of the licensing of Apple’s tools and APIs for the development of iOS apps.

[731] Apple contends that proposed order 6(k) improperly repurposes reasons on power and market structure into restraints aimed at asserted or apprehended anti-competitive effects, and would fetter Apple’s commercial freedom to an extent not justified by the liability findings.

[732] Against the backdrop of my findings regarding Apple’s DMA response, there is a real and stark risk that Apple would, in a world of decentralised app distribution, seek to replicate its DMA model by forcing developers to accept a bundled contractual arrangement as the price of using alternative distribution channels.

[733] Apple identifies no legitimate reason why developers who use alternative distribution channels should be compelled to contract with Apple or its nominee beyond what is necessary to license Apple’s IP for the development of iOS apps.

#### Competition in iOS in-app payment solutions

[734] Epic has sought the following orders on this topic:

> 7. The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> (a) require that app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store must use IAP for in-app purchases of digital content in those apps;

> (b) prohibit app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store from directing users to a payment method other than IAP for purchases of digital content;

> (c) prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder iOS app developers from:

> (i) using payment methods other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content within iOS apps distributed via the App Store or via an Alternative Distribution Channel;

> (ii) providing buttons, external links, or otherwise informing, leading or directing iOS device users from within an iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP; or

> (iii) using their own choice of payment method(s) for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content, provided that, where the conditions referred to in paragraph 8 below have been satisfied, the First Respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP;

> (d) prohibit, prevent or take any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder iOS users from using payment methods other than IAP for purchasing digital content for use in-app, provided that, where the conditions referred to in paragraph 8 below have been satisfied, the First Respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP.

> 8. The First Respondent may only impose necessary and proportionate security requirements, as referred to in paragraph 7(c)(iii) or (d), or in paragraph 9(a) if they have first given the affected iOS app developers reasonable prior notice in writing that identifies and explains the nature and causes of that additional security risk, and why the proposed requirements are necessary and appropriate to address that risk. For the purposes of this paragraph, reasonable prior notice in writing can be given by direct communication with developers and where that is not reasonably practicable, by a notice published on a website maintained by the First Respondent.

> 9. The First Respondent must take all steps necessary to permit the Applicants and any other app developer (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> (a) to use a payment method other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases in apps distributed to iOS device users via the App Store or via an Alternative Distribution Channel, provided that, where the conditions referred to in paragraph 8 above have been satisfied, the First Respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP; and

> (b) to provide buttons, external links, or otherwise inform, lead or direct iOS device users from within an iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP.

> 10. The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) require that the Applicants or any other iOS app developer who uses a payment method other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content for use in-app:

> (a) refrain from offering lower prices, or more favourable discounts or other benefits, to users who purchase via a payment method other than IAP than those applicable to in-app purchases effected via IAP;

> (b) report the transactions accepted and processed using a payment method other than IAP or grant any audit right in respect of those transactions;

> (c) offer IAP in addition to the developer’s preferred payment method or prevent app developers from using payment providers that act as a merchant of record;

> (d) in connection with any iOS app distributed via an Alternative Distribution Channel, pay any fee in connection with:

> (i) in-app purchases made via a payment method other than IAP, or

> (ii) purchases of digital content from any website or from any developer’s platform for use in-app;

> (e) in connection with any iOS app distributed via the App Store, where:

> (i) in-app purchases are made using a payment method other than IAP, or

> (ii) purchases of digital content are made on any website or from any developer’s platform for use in-app,

> pay a fee that exceeds any fee that is imposed on app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store in connection with in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP, plus any additional economic costs that are genuinely and reasonably incurred by Apple in connection with an iOS device user making an in-app purchase of digital content using a payment method other than IAP that are not incurred when a user makes an in-app purchase of physical goods or services using the same payment method.

> 11. The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> (a) impose or require the introduction of any friction or impediment into the process whereby iOS device users can elect to use, or can in fact use, a payment method other than IAP when purchasing digital content for use in-app which is additional to those frictions or impediments that a user encounters when making in-app purchases of physical goods and services using a payment method other than IAP in an app distributed via the App Store; nor

> (b) impose any fees or conditions for access to, or use of, any tool or API (including software development kits) that is necessary for app developers to develop and distribute iOS apps that allow or enable developers and iOS device users to use a payment method other than IAP, which do not apply in respect of apps distributed via the App Store within which iOS device users can make in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP.

#### Compliance and discrimination

[735] Epic has sought the following order on this topic:

> 12. The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) reject for distribution, discriminate against, take retaliatory action against, or otherwise disadvantage:

> (a) any of the Applicants’ or any other app developer’s apps submitted for distribution via the App Store; nor

> (b) the Applicants or any other app developer,

> on the basis that:

> (c) the developer’s app is available for discovery, downloading, installing or updating via Alternative Distribution Channels;

> (d) the app uses or will use a payment method for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases other than IAP; or

> (e) the developer of the app links or otherwise informs, leads or directs iOS device users from within the iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP.

#### Apple – order

[736] Apple has sought the following orders:

> 3. Pursuant to s 80(1) of the CCA, the First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation):

> (a) prohibit App Developers who distribute native iOS apps to users of iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account from both:

> (i) using any payment methods other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases by such users of digital content in those apps; and

> (ii) directing such users from within those apps to out-of-app payment methods for purchases of digital content for use in-app; or

> (b) unreasonably prevent or hinder App Developers from engaging in the conduct described in both subparas (a)(i) and (a)(ii), and without affecting the generality of the foregoing this will permit the First Respondent (and any related corporation) to impose reasonable technical and security requirements on the use of payment methods other than IAP.

> 4. Orders 2 and 3 are to have effect for a period of 5 years with effect from the date that is 6 months from the date of these orders.

> …

> Definitions

> In these orders:

> “App Developers” means developers of native iOS apps who are and remain admitted to the Apple Developer Program.

> “Australian App Store” means the App Store Australian storefront.

> “Australian Apple Account”, formerly "Apple ID", means an Apple Account with the country or region set to “Australia” and associated with a payment method with an Australian billing address.

> “Reader app” means magazine, newspaper, book, audio, music and video apps which may allow a user to access previously purchased content or content subscriptions.

[737] Let me now turn to a detailed discussion of Epic’s proposed orders 7 to 11 dealing with competition in the payments market. Again, the proposed orders that I am discussing and those that I will make are to be read with the geographical limitation on “users” and the “App Store” that I have indicated.

#### Proposed order 7 (order 6 as modified and made)

[738] Epic’s proposed order 7 seeks to enjoin Apple from preventing or prohibiting the use of alternative payment solutions to IAP for in-app purchases of digital content. And it prevents Apple from requiring app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store to use IAP for in–app purchases of digital content in those apps. I would note that proposed order 7 corresponds, with modification, to order 6 that I have made.

[739] Now Apple says that the prohibitive injunction in its proposed order 3 is sufficient, but it says that Epic’s proposed order 7 goes further than this.

[740] Proposed order 7(b) enjoins Apple from prohibiting developers from directing users of apps distributed through the App Store to a payment method other than IAP for purchases of digital content.

[741] Proposed order 7(c)(i) enjoins Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder iOS app developers from using payment methods other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases within apps distributed via the App Store.

[742] Proposed order 7(c)(ii) enjoins Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder iOS app developers from providing buttons, external links, or otherwise informing, leading or directing iOS device users from within an iOS app to a payment method for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content other than IAP.

[743] Proposed order 7(c)(iii) enjoins Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder iOS app developers from using their own choice of payment method(s) for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content, provided that where the conditions referred to in proposed order 8 have been satisfied, Apple may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP.

[744] Proposed order 7(d) enjoins Apple from prohibiting, preventing or taking any action which is likely to hinder iOS users from using payment methods other than IAP for purchasing digital content for use in-app, provided that, where the conditions referred to in proposed order 8 have been satisfied, Apple may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP.

[745] Apple says that save insofar as it purports to restrain Apple from prohibiting or preventing the use of alternative payment solutions to IAP, proposed order 7 is not supported by my liability findings.

[746] First, Apple says that proposed orders 7(b) and (c)(ii) are directed at the maintenance of the anti-steering rule. But Apple says that it has not been demonstrated or found that that rule would, in the absence of the IAP exclusivity requirement, have the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in any relevant market. So, once maintenance of the IAP exclusivity requirement is removed, there is no need for any injunction against the anti-steering rule.

[747] In that regard, the relevant liability findings were to the effect that Apple’s iOS payment solution conduct, comprising the IAP exclusivity requirement and the anti-steering rule, had the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition. Whilst the findings deal with the IAP exclusivity requirement and the anti-steering rule concurrently in places ([432], [433], [5652] and [5653]), the relevant finding is in terms that the IAP exclusivity requirement and the anti-steering rule together contravene s 46.

[748] I said at [5654], [6111] and [6113]:

> In my view by this conduct, Apple prevents developers from acquiring services for facilitating payment for digital content in iOS apps other than from IAP, with the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition. In doing so, Apple forecloses alternative payment solutions from participating in the relevant market.

> …

> For these reasons, the iOS payment solutions conduct has the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the iOS in-app payment solutions market and also I might add the iOS app distribution market.

> …

> Further, in terms of the IAP restrictions being the payments tie provision and the anti-steering provision, I have found that they were imposed or have been maintained at the start of the relevant period with the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the payment solutions market. But I have rejected Epic’s purpose case in this context.

[749] Apple says that Epic did not seek any finding, and no finding has been made, that Apple’s anti-steering rule of itself and in isolation from the IAP exclusivity requirement had an effect of substantially lessening competition. No analysis of the discrete effect of the anti-steering rule forms part of the competition analysis. There is no discrete finding to the effect that the anti-steering rule of itself contravened s 46.

[750] The only discussion of the anti-steering rule per se in the competition analysis of the iOS payment solution conduct concerns the effect of removal of the anti-steering rule on competition in the iOS app distribution market. In this regard I said (at [6110]):

> Generally, if Apple ceased engaging in the impugned conduct, it is likely that alternative app stores on iOS would seek to differentiate themselves by offering developers alternative in-app payment solutions. Moreover, the anti-steering provisions hinder the ability of alternative app stores to compete.

[751] But Apple says that that discussion is supplementary to a finding about the effect on competition of removal of the iOS payment solution conduct as a whole, and is not in terms that the anti-steering rule has an effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition.

[752] Apple says that the effect of Apple’s proposed relief will be to remove the IAP exclusivity requirement. In circumstances where, as a result, alternatives to IAP will be able to be used in-app, it is difficult to see that maintenance of the anti-steering rule, that is, a prohibition on steering users to alternative payment solutions outside an app, would be likely to have any anti-competitive effect. And developers who do wish to steer users to alternative payment solutions may already do so by email and other means of communication outside an app.

[753] Second, Apple says that proposed order 7(d) is not only unnecessary, but also uncertain in its scope and so inappropriate to be made.

[754] Apple says that it is unclear what conduct would constitute preventing, hindering or deterring iOS users or app developers from behaving in the ways described. It says that conduct the subject of order 7(d) might, for example, include informing users that certain benefits, like the ability to manage their subscriptions in one place, use parental controls, or access Apple customer support, will not be available if they use an alternative to IAP. But Apple says that there could be no reasonable objection to Apple informing users of such matters.

[755] More generally, Apple says that Epic also did not allege or submit that any of the steps Apple has taken in response to specific regulatory regimes implemented in other jurisdictions and markets would, if adopted here, contravene s 46. Yet Apple says that Epic now seeks to repurpose and rely upon reasons concerning other topics to justify restraints targeting apprehended anti-competitive effects which were not the subject of any sustained analysis or argument in the liability hearing.

[756] Further, Apple says that my finding on liability was that Apple contravened s 46 by requiring developers to use IAP exclusively, that is, Apple contravened s 46 by requiring that developers use IAP for in-app payments for digital services to the exclusion of any other payment solution. But Apple says that Epic did not bring any case alleging that Apple contravened or would contravene s 46 by requiring developers to include IAP among the payment solutions available to users in purchasing digital content in app.

[757] Further, Apple says that if Epic’s proposed order 7(a) were made, Apple would be prohibited from requiring developers distributing via the App Store to offer IAP as a means to purchase digital content in app. Apple says that such a prohibition would enjoin a form of legitimate commercial activity, being Apple’s insistence at its option that its own payment solution be among the solutions developers make available non-exclusively to users for digital purchases, in apps built using Apple software and distributed through the App Store. Apple says that this was never alleged or found to contravene s 46.

[758] Likewise, Apple says that order 7(b) would enjoin Apple from prohibiting developers distributing apps via the App Store from directing users to a payment method other than IAP. Apple says that this is problematically in absolute terms, and unqualified by any condition to the effect that the prohibition only applies to the extent that Apple also requires the exclusive use of IAP.

[759] Apple says that the conduct found to contravene s 46 was Apple’s prohibition on developers steering users from within iOS apps to alternative payment solutions for in-app purchases of digital content (the anti–steering rule) in the context of Apple’s enforcement of the requirement that developers which supply iOS apps use IAP for all in-app purchases of digital content (the IAP tie). To be clear, the concept of steering means directing users from within an iOS app, including by means of linking out, to out of app payment methods for accepting and processing purchases of digital content for use in–app.

[760] I said at [5651] to [5653]:

> Let me now say something about the relevant conduct at issue being the iOS payment solution conduct. And in this respect there are two aspects.

> First, one has the existence and Apple’s enforcement of the requirement that developers which supply iOS apps use IAP for all in-app purchases of digital content, which the parties have referred to as the IAP tie.

> Second, one has Apple’s prohibition on developers steering users from within iOS apps to alternative payment solutions for in-app purchases of digital content, which the parties have referred to as the anti-steering rule.

[761] Apple says that Epic did not bring any case seeking to restrict Apple’s ability to prevent developers from directing users to non-IAP payment methods. Its case concerned Apple’s prevention of steering to external payment solutions, as applied in the context of Apple’s requirement of the exclusive use of IAP for in-app payments for digital services.

[762] As I have indicated, Apple says that it should be enjoined from applying the combination of a requirement that developers use IAP for in-app payments exclusively, and a prohibition on steering users to out-of-app payment systems. It says that that specific combination of conduct is what I found contravened s 46.

[763] And as I have indicated, Apple says that I made no finding that Apple’s requiring the use of IAP non-exclusively for the purchase of digital content on apps distributed through the App Store would contravene s 46. Apple also says that I made no finding that Apple’s preventing developers from directing users of apps distributed through the App Store out of app to accept and process payment for digital services would lessen competition, in the absence of any requirement that IAP be used for in-app payment to the exclusion of all other payment solutions.

[764] Apple says that both of these steps which it takes or may take in respect of apps operating on its hardware using its proprietary software distributed through its own store front would be enjoined by Epic’s orders. Apple says that nothing in my findings justifies the grant of that relief.

[765] Epic’s proposed orders then seek to apply the equivalent restrictions to all developers irrespective of distribution channel and in terms so broad that they would practically prevent Apple from taking any step to regularise the acceptance and processing of payments for digital content.

[766] Epic’s proposed order 7(c) would enjoin Apple from prohibiting, preventing, or taking any action which is likely to hinder iOS developers from: (i) using payment methods other than IAP for taking payments for digital content within apps; (ii) steering users out of app to take such payments; or (iii) using their own choice of payment method, subject to the imposition of what Epic calls necessary and proportionate security requirements.

[767] Proposed orders 7(c)(ii) and (iii) would enjoin Apple from engaging in conduct which Epic has not alleged and I have not found to contravene s 46. The iOS payment solutions conduct consisted of a requirement to use IAP exclusively coupled with a prohibition on developers steering users to alternative payment solutions.

[768] Apple says that Epic did not bring any case alleging that it would be anti-competitive for Apple to prevent developers from directing users out of app to external payment service providers or platforms. Apple says that Epic’s proposed order would restrain conduct not shown to be anti-competitive, such that to restrict it would not be an appropriate exercise of the power in s 80.

[769] Apple also says that the language of this proposed order is broad and indeterminate.

[770] Apple says that if any step taken were to prevent even one developer from doing any of the things identified in proposed order 7(c)(i) to (iii), Apple would be in contempt of an order so framed. It poses the question: how many iOS developers must an action taken by Apple be “likely to hinder” before the order is infringed? It says that a step which would be likely to hinder any app developer, or perhaps any two app developers, would transgress this order. And it says that the question on any contempt application would be not whether any two developers were in fact hindered, but whether those developers were likely to be hindered, a threshold which would be lower and which would be even more uncertain, so rendering Apple’s bridge over the chasm on contempt more precarious still.

[771] Apple says that the consequence of this uncertainty would be practically to disable it from taking any step to regularise the acceptance or processing of payments for in-app content.

[772] Now to support payment solutions other than IAP in foreign jurisdictions where Apple is required to permit such payment, Apple has created a set of APIs which can display disclosure sheets to the user for transactions made other than via IAP through apps distributed via the App Store; as I have touched on earlier and as I will return to later, these are described as External Purchase APIs.

[773] Apple says that the use of disclosure sheets, being a means of educating users of a change in the standard process, that is, IAP, so that the user is provided with the opportunity to assess their risk appetite in proceeding with providing their payment details to sources outside of Apple, may prevent or be likely to hinder some developers somewhere from using alternative payment methods.

[774] Apple says that it would risk contempt of my orders by using any equivalent mechanism in facilitating alternative payments in respect of users affected by my orders.

[775] Now Apple accepts that Epic has sought to mitigate some part of this uncertainty by introducing a proviso in its proposed order 7(c) to the effect that “where the conditions referred to in paragraph 8 below have been satisfied, the First Respondent may impose requirements that are necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP”.

[776] Epic’s proposed order 8 provides that Apple may impose necessary and proportionate security requirements if they have first given the affected iOS app developers reasonable prior notice in writing to the developer directly, or by public notice that identifies and explains the nature and causes of that additional security risk, and why the proposed requirements are necessary and appropriate to address that risk.

[777] But Apple says that this proviso does not address or overcome the inability to take pro-competitive steps to regulate processing of payments for digital content which Epic’s proposed order 7(c) otherwise creates. Apple complains that Epic’s notice requirement is unworkable and counterproductive and has made the following points.

[778] First, it says that some security requirements are urgent. It says that Epic’s requirement of notice before action is taken against security threats would reduce the efficacy of any eventual response by Apple to that threat.

[779] Second, it says that for some security threats, publicity is antithetical to mitigation. Apple says that if it were required to disclose to affected developers or to make public generally the nature and causes of any additional security risk to iOS users, that disclosure is likely to reduce the efficacy of Apple’s response to that risk and to other security risks.

[780] Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 7(d) has the same problems.

[781] Epic’s proposed order 7(d) would enjoin Apple from prohibiting or preventing or taking any action (including by imposing a fee or condition) which is likely to hinder iOS users from using payment methods other than IAP for digital purchases. Apple says that this order has the same vice as order 7(c), in that any step which poses a likely hindrance to any two users, at any time and anywhere, would contravene the order, putting Apple in contempt. Apple says that because the class of users is so much wider than the class of developers, even taking iOS users with an Australian Apple Account only, the jeopardy in which this would place Apple in hazarding any step to regulate the acceptance and processing of payment for app users’ purchases of digital content is all the more problematic.

[782] Further, Apple says that the proposed orders, by creating uncertainty, would operate to disable Apple from earning any competitive return on its investments in iOS by any version of the monetisation strategy it has previously implemented. Apple says that the External Purchase APIs facilitate the reporting of transactions for the purposes of Apple collecting its commission on transactions made through apps distributed via the App Store and alternative distribution channels. It says that charging any commission on payments accepted or processed by alternative means is a step which could prevent or be likely to hinder some developers somewhere from taking such payments.

[783] So, Apple would risk contempt in charging any commission in respect of any payment taken by alternative means from an iOS user affected by my orders.

[784] Now Apple says that its proposed relief avoids the problems which Epic’s revised orders pose.

[785] Apple’s proposed order 3(b) would enjoin Apple from unreasonably preventing or hindering app developers from both using any payment methods other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content and directing such users from within those apps to out-of-app payment methods for purchases of digital content for use in-app.

[786] It says that an order in that form would attain the anti–avoidance remedial objective which Epic advances in support of its proposed relief, namely, forestalling any attempt Apple may make to impose some equivalent of the contravening conduct by other means. But unlike Epic’s order, Apple says that it would attain this objective without restraining conduct which has not been shown to be anti–competitive, in the manner that Epic’s proposed order 7(c) will.

###### Analysis

[787] Proposed order 7 is a negative injunction, prohibiting Apple from imposing and enforcing the IAP tie and the anti-steering provision, which conduct I found to contravene s 46(1).

[788] Proposed orders 7(a) to (b) will prevent the continuation and repetition of Apple’s contravention of s 46(1) in requiring app developers that wish to distribute iOS apps via the App Store to use IAP for in-app purchases of digital content in those apps, and in prohibiting such developers from leading users to a payment method other than IAP for purchases of digital content.

[789] Proposed orders 7(c) and (d) will prevent Apple from continuing to substantially lessen competition by conduct which has substantially the same or similar effect as the IAP tie and the anti-steering provision.

[790] These orders are necessary given my findings that, even when compelled by law in other jurisdictions to permit developers that wish to distribute iOS apps via the App Store to use payment methods other than IAP, Apple has restructured its commission to make it economically unviable for most developers to use an alternative payment method.

[791] As I put it, the “choice” Apple has presented to developers in these jurisdictions is illusory. Proposed orders 7(c) and (d) are directed to prohibiting such conduct, which I found was engaged in to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions. I said at [5881]:

> Now in my view the low take-up rate of alternative payment solutions demonstrates that the “choice” Apple has presented to developers in these jurisdictions is illusory. Apple has set the commission rates to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions.

[792] I found that Apple contravened s 46 by imposing its payments tie and the anti-steering rule. In other words, I was of the view that both the payments tie and the anti-steering rule amounted to material contravening conduct. Indeed, Apple rejected thousands of apps for distribution on iOS for violating its payments tie or for violating the anti-steering rule. It is therefore appropriate that Apple be enjoined from maintaining each of the payments tie and the anti-steering rule.

[793] Now it is unsurprising that Epic did not bring a case alleging that Apple contravened s 46 by requiring developers to include IAP among the payment solutions available to users in purchasing digital content in app in circumstances where, at the time Epic brought its claim, Apple had never allowed the use of any payment solution other than IAP for in-app purchases in iOS apps. Such a case would have been hypothetical.

[794] The same is true of the complaint that Epic’s case only attacked Apple’s prevention of steering to external payment solutions, as applied in the context of Apple’s requirement of the exclusive use of IAP for in­app payments for digital services. Once again, the fact that Epic’s case was tailored to Apple’s conduct in the factual, rather than hypotheticals, was appropriate.

[795] Apple’s contention that this ought result in me confining the relief to be ordered to a prohibition on the precise conduct found to be anti­competitive ignores the fact that my remedial power is not confined to prohibiting the precise conduct pleaded and found to constitute the contravention.

[796] In any event, the need for proposed orders 7 and 8 is made plain by the evidence led and my findings in the Epic v Google proceeding.

[797] Under the UCB program, Google required developers to make Google Play Billing available alongside any alternative payment solution the developer chose to offer. That is, the UCB program involved the very conduct in which Apple seeks to engage, whereby developers are required to include IAP among the payment solutions made available to users.

[798] Ms Edwards-Warren’s evidence was that a requirement that developers offer Google Play Billing alongside an alternative payment solution did not reflect a competitive market.

[799] I found that app developers entitled to access the UCB program remained subject to the payments tie, albeit in a varied form as they were not permitted to only offer an alternative payment solution: GJ[637]. The choice offered to developers to use an alternative payment solution under the UCB program was found to be not commercially real as app developers remained constrained by the payments tie, even when they had access to UCB: GJ[642]. This was so even putting pricing to one side: GJ[5509]. I also found (GJ[5866]) that the scope for competition within the confines of the UCB and developer own billing (DOB) programs was very limited, and neither program provided developers with a real choice of alternative payment solutions.

[800] This evidence and these findings make plain that any continued insistence that developers offer IAP as a means to purchase digital content in app, even if alternative payment solutions are offered alongside IAP, constitutes an impermissible tie that is likely to stifle competition in the payment solutions market.

[801] My findings in the Epic v Google proceeding similarly demonstrate why it is appropriate that Apple be prohibited from preventing developers from directing users out of app to accept and process payment for digital services, even if Apple does not mandate the use of IAP for in–app payments.

[802] Google’s anti-steering rule prohibited apps required to use Google Play Billing from leading users to a payment method other than the Play Store’s billing system: GJ[5484]. This rule was found to have an anti-competitive effect: GJ[6051], [6053]. Importantly, these conclusions were not confined to those jurisdictions where UCB and DOB were not available.

[803] Accordingly, the anti-steering rule was found to be anti-competitive even where developers were able to offer an alternative payment solution to Google Play Billing for in-app purchases.

[804] The prohibition on preventing steering in proposed order 7(b) is appropriate in those circumstances.

[805] Further, as Epic points out, Apple has not led any specific evidence that shows any need to collect commission on transactions made through alternative payment solutions so as to earn a commercial return on its investments.

[806] There is no evidence that such transactions involve any use of Apple’s copyrighted SDKs or tools, or that they involve any additional service being supplied by Apple: [5313] to [5338]. And despite being on notice, Apple has again chosen not to adduce any such evidence.

[807] In circumstances where I found that, even when Apple has been compelled in other jurisdictions to permit developers of iOS apps to offer payment methods other than IAP, Apple has restructured its commission to make it economically unviable for most developers to use an alternative payment method so as to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions, a prohibition on the imposition of fees that are likely to hinder iOS app developers from using payment methods other than IAP is appropriate.

[808] I said at [5859], [5860], [5871], [5872] and [5880] to [5882]:

> But I agree with Epic that the low take-up of that opportunity can be explained by the fact that, if developers do pursue it, Apple still charges a commission of 27% in the Netherlands or 26% in South Korea.

> As Epic points out, using an alternative payment solution in those circumstances would make no sense for the vast majority of developers. To be worthwhile, they would need to find an alternative payment solution that they consider to be equal to or better than IAP and that charges less than 3% in the Netherlands or 4% in South Korea, or which offers other benefits that can outweigh the cost increase that would need to be incurred to make use of the alternative.

> …

> In other words, Apple did not structure the commission to make it viable for developers to choose an alternative payments provider to IAP. Rather it set its commissions at a level that would make it economically unviable for most developers to use an alternative payment system in South Korea and the Netherlands. Mr Schiller conceded as much during cross-examination.

> I agree with Epic that this supports the inference that the revised commissions charged by Apple in South Korea and the Netherlands understate the true costs to developers of adopting an alternative payments system for in-app purchases. Moreover, that inference is fortified by the fact that Apple’s costs of providing in-app payment services are unlikely to be vastly different to Google’s costs, which also exceed 3% or 4%.

> …

> Further, the regulatory requirements in South Korea and the Netherlands were directed at opening up the in-app payments market, yet Apple’s changes in purported compliance with these requirements were based on an estimate of costs that it knew was unrealistic and out of reach for developers. The reality is that developers would pay commissions that exceed 30% if they used an alternative payment system. The vast majority of developers are therefore unlikely to do so, as they would suffer a loss.

> Now in my view the low take-up rate of alternative payment solutions demonstrates that the “choice” Apple has presented to developers in these jurisdictions is illusory. Apple has set the commission rates to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions.

> In all the circumstances, I would infer that the practical effect of Apple charging a service fee that is only 3 or 4% less than 30% is to offer developers the option of using an alternative payment system at an economically unviable cost.

[809] Further, I agree with Epic that s 80 does not require a separate, standalone finding that the anti-steering rule, in isolation, contravened s 46 before that aspect of the contravening conduct can be restrained. Once a course of conduct has been found to have the effect or likely effect of substantially lessening competition, it is orthodox to enjoin each of its components. As Epic points out, there is no basis on which Apple should be permitted to select the aspects of the contravening conduct it should be enjoined from engaging in.

[810] An injunction that removes the payments tie but leaves the anti-steering rule intact would leave in place a material and foreseeable obstacle to the take-up of rival payment solutions and the ability of alternative channels on iOS to compete with the App Store. This would fall well short of what is appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy the contravening conduct and its effects for the purposes of s 80.

[811] An anti-steering injunction is not only called for by my findings of liability. It is also necessary to create the conditions for competition from rival payment solution providers that Apple has comprehensively foreclosed for many years.

[812] As to proposed order 7(d), Apple argues that the language of the order is unclear, and could capture benign conduct such as informing users that certain benefits, being centralised subscription management, parental controls or Apple support, will not be available if they use non-IAP payments, or setting minimum security/fraud standards for third party payment methods.

[813] Proposed order 7(d) is necessary to prevent Apple from continuing to substantially lessen competition by conduct that has the same or similar effect as the IAP tie and the anti-steering provisions. Such an order is necessary because, as found by me, even when Apple has been compelled by law in other jurisdictions to permit developers that wish to distribute iOS apps via the App Store to use payment methods other than IAP, Apple has restructured its commission to make it economically unviable for most developers to use an alternative payment method.

[814] As I said, this was conduct engaged in by Apple to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions. The language in proposed order 7(d) is an appropriate method by which to prevent Apple from engaging in such conduct again.

[815] Let me now say something more specifically about proposed order 8.

#### Proposed order 8 (order 7 as modified and made)

[816] Proposed order 8 is directed at giving developers reasonable notice of any new security requirement which Apple proposes to introduce in connection with the use of alternative payment methods. But it only operates in the confined circumstances that are the subject of proposed orders 7(c)(iii), 7(d) and 9(a), which are concerned with additional security risks posed by a payment method other than IAP. I would note that proposed order 8 corresponds, with modification, to order 7 that I have made.

[817] Proposed order 8 of Epic’s proposed relief outlines the requirement that Apple may only impose necessary and proportionate security requirements after it has provided a written notice to the affected developers that identifies and explains the nature of the risk. Specifically, proposed order 8 applies to the use of alternative payment solutions other than IAP. Under this provision, Apple may not take any action that prevents iOS app developers from offering third-party payment solutions for accepting and processing payments for in-app purchases of digital content for use in-app, unless there is a security risk associated with the use of third-party payment solutions and Apple has provided a written notice of the security risk to developers in advance.

[818] Now Mr Friedland gave evidence that if a malware or security risk was identified, Apple would want to take action immediately to protect its users, rather than waiting for a notice period.

[819] Further, Mr Friedland also raises concerns with respect to Apple’s prior notice as it could aid in the bad actor reverse engineering Apple’s malware detection capabilities.

[820] Proposed order 8 requires Apple to give appropriate notice to developers regarding necessary and appropriate security requirements, but there is no requirement that Apple discloses the detailed nature of the underlying security issue in a way that would assist exploitation. Further, to the extent Apple is already aware of circumstances to which order 8 of Epic’s proposed relief would apply in which Apple would want to take action immediately to protect its users, Apple should notify developers in advance. Such notice would allow Apple to take reasonable actions such as removing discovered malware without any further notice to developers.

[821] Further, proposed order 6(h)(ii) addresses the conditions under which Apple is allowed to remove or disable apps, including when the app “constitutes malware or malicious code which poses a genuine security risk that…requires urgent elimination” or when a security risk has not been remedied following “reasonable notice of the existence, nature and extent of that security risk.” Proposed order 8 does not prevent Apple from taking action immediately under proposed order 6(h)(ii), which latter proposed order I have tweaked to make this clear.

[822] Further, proposed order 8 only applies to security requirements that are “necessary and proportionate to address any additional security risk posed by a payment method other than IAP” (proposed orders 7(c)(iii), 7(d), and 9(a)).

[823] Now in order to determine the extent to which proposed order 8 impairs Apple’s ability to manage or prevent security risks to iOS devices and iOS device users, Professor Somayaji discussed the scenarios in which it is reasonable for Apple to take security-related actions without providing a written notice to developers.

[824] The scenarios Professor Somayaji considered were the following: (a) a malicious app has been distributed to end-user devices; (b) a vulnerability in a distributed app can be used to exploit users but the app is otherwise legitimate; (c) a vulnerability in a distributed app can be used to exploit devices but the app is otherwise legitimate; and (d) a vulnerability in system software can be used to exploit users. Let me discuss his analysis of those scenarios.

[825] In scenario (a), what is considered is the possibility that a malicious app was distributed and installed on a user’s device. And since the app is malicious, it provides no meaningful utility to the user and jeopardizes the user’s safety and their personal data. In such a case, the response is the same no matter how the app has been distributed. The app needs to be disabled or deleted from end-user devices. This response is drastic but is otherwise straightforward, as malicious apps do not have any legitimate purpose and so removing them is in the end user’s interests, and such removal can proceed without prior notification of the app author or the parties that help distribute the app. Further communication may be needed after the immediate situation is resolved, but such communication should not delay the situation from being addressed. Now this scenario is already covered under Apple’s policies. Apple’s systems on iOS devices regularly check installed third-party apps against a list of known malware identified by Apple. Where an app contains malware, the user’s device prevents the user from opening it and displays an alert recommending that the user delete it.

[826] In scenario (b), one is considering the possibility that an otherwise legitimate app, that is, an app that provides certain functionality to the user and is safe to run on a user’s device, has a newly discovered security vulnerability. Whilst Apple can feasibly disable the app from running on the user’s device, this would neither solve the security risk nor allow the user to access the functionality offered by the app. As this scenario is the only one where there is an explicit vulnerability in the app itself, this scenario is the one that is most likely to apply to apps with payment related vulnerabilities. The other scenarios deal with outright malicious apps or security vulnerabilities in the operating system’s code rather than application code. In this case, because the app is legitimate, disabling or removing it from devices could have a negative effect on users. Further, because device–level security remains intact where only the user of the app is at risk, other apps are not affected, and the security risk can potentially be mitigated though changes in user behaviour, including by potentially receiving instructions from app developers on actions to take or avoid. In this scenario, then, no matter how the app is distributed, in almost all circumstances the security issue should be communicated to the app developer, and that developer would be expected to issue an update to resolve it.

[827] In the case of alternative payment solutions, communications from Apple can help the developer identify problems with code related to the third-party payment solutions and rectify those issues. Additionally, such communication from Apple does not affect Apple’s ability to manage security risks to iOS devices as communications regarding vulnerabilities in specific payment solutions only affect users of specific apps, and those users are otherwise still secure.

[828] In scenario (c), what is considered is a legitimate app that has been distributed and installed on a user’s device. The security vulnerability in this case can have widespread consequences by compromising the iOS device itself. So, a legitimate app could be used to compromise app sandboxing and so enable malicious access to all data on a device. The security vulnerabilities in this case pose significantly higher risk to users and the platform in general, as the vulnerable app can be used to compromise the security of the devices on which it is installed. Further, because the security mechanisms of the device are also at risk, the platform owner may need to take steps unilaterally to preserve the integrity of the platform. So, in these circumstances it is possible that a platform owner such as Apple could have reason to act unilaterally and without prior notification of the app developer, potentially disabling vulnerable apps until the app developer can release an updated version and Apple has had time to update iOS itself.

[829] And under scenario (d), let it be assumed that the platform owner identifies a systemic security vulnerability in the underlying operating system. Sometimes fixing such systemic vulnerabilities impacts the functionality of some legitimate apps until the apps are updated. This scenario is potentially the most serious violation of security as it can affect all devices no matter which apps are installed on the user’s device. If system security updates can be distributed without impacting legitimate app functionality, no coordination with app developers is required. But in this case, the system-level security update impacts the functionality of legitimate apps, which requires them to be updated. Unless the affected apps have small user populations and/or use cases that can tolerate disruptions, any unilateral action by the platform owner could cause harm to end users. There is no good option in this scenario, as disclosing the vulnerability to developers could also disclose it to attackers, potentially facilitating wide-scale attacks before mitigations have been put in place.

[830] So, in summary the proposed requirement for Apple to inform third-party app stores and other developers using alternative app distribution methods is not relevant to scenario (a), as the app is malicious. In scenario (b), which is the most likely scenario where security vulnerabilities with specific third-party payment solutions may be identified, Apple should provide developers a written notice because the impacts of the security issues are limited to users of specific apps, and the overall security of the platform is not affected. In scenario (c), because disclosure can be made just to affected app developers who have a vested interest in retaining app functionality, it is reasonable for Apple to provide a notice to developers in advance, before taking any unilateral action. In scenario (d), confidentiality related to operating system security could be much harder to maintain and potentially all devices could be at risk. So, Apple here might be justified in taking unilateral actions to protect platform security, but app developer cooperation is also essential.

[831] But in any event, proposed order 8 does not relate to scenario (d) as proposed order 8 applies to security updates related to alternative payment solutions on iOS devices, and alternative payments are not directly facilitated by the operating system. So, when specific third-party payment solutions have security vulnerabilities, Apple should inform developers before imposing security requirements that could impact the functionality of legitimate apps that may use those payment solutions.

[832] Further, neither security considerations nor Apple’s technical frameworks make disclosure sheets necessary when app developers use alternative payment methods. I will return to the topic of disclosure sheets later when I discuss proposed order 11.

#### Proposed order 9 (order 8 as modified and made)

[833] Epic’s proposed order 9 is a mandatory injunction requiring Apple to permit the use of payment methods other than IAP and the steering of users to alternative payment mechanisms outside an iOS app. I would note that proposed order 9 corresponds, with modification, to order 8 that I have made.

[834] Apple says that no such order was sought in the FAOA and it says that nothing in Epic’s pleaded case or my liability findings warrants the grant of such a mandatory injunction. Apple says that it is not open to Epic to seek or for me to make proposed order 9.

[835] Apple says that so far as the case that was run and has been determined goes, proposed order 9 is superfluous, achieving no more of that which is open to Epic than is effected by Apple’s proposed order 3.

[836] Apple says that to the extent that order 9 seeks to replicate in the form of a mandatory injunction the strictures which Epic’s proposed order 7 would impose in negative terms, it also has the deficiencies identified above, including by reason of its preclusion of the anti-steering rule.

###### Analysis

[837] Proposed order 9 is a mandatory injunction, requiring Apple to permit Epic and other app developers to use payment methods other than IAP for accepting and processing in-app purchases in iOS apps including but not limited to apps distributed via the App Store, and further to permit app developers to steer iOS device users from within an iOS app to a payment method other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content.

[838] Proposed order 9 thereby requires Apple to permit conduct that, until now, has been prohibited by the IAP tie and the anti-steering provision, being provisions that Apple has hitherto enforced against Epic and other app developers in contravention of s 46.

[839] Order 9 is warranted, since Apple’s proven conduct in other jurisdictions demonstrates that prohibitive injunctions alone may not be sufficient to ensure Apple ceases to stifle competition in the payments market.

[840] Order 9 requires Apple to permit pro-competitive conduct and choice in the payments market, and will operate together with the negative injunction in order 7 to restore competition to that market.

[841] Now although proposed order 9 was not sought in the FAOA, Epic is not precluded from seeking injunctive relief beyond that specifically claimed in its originating application for the reasons above, including because Epic’s originating application included injunctions expressed in general terms given it preceded my findings, I directed that questions of relief be dealt with after its determination of contraventions and Apple has had a full opportunity to answer the case on relief, including by evidence.

[842] Apple contends that proposed order 9 is superfluous because prohibitory relief would prevent Apple from maintaining IAP exclusivity. But s 80 empowers me to grant both prohibitory and mandatory injunctions where appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy the contravening conduct and its effects.

[843] Apple also submits that proposed order 9 merely replicates, in mandatory form, what Epic seeks to achieve by proposed order 7 and inherits the same alleged defects, including as to anti–steering. But I have rejected those alleged defects.

#### Proposed order 10 (order 9 as modified and made)

[844] The further prohibitive injunction which is Epic’s proposed order 10 is analogous to its proposed order 6, but in respect of payments rather than app distribution. I would note that proposed order 10 corresponds, with modification, to order 9 that I have made. Apple says that for similar reasons to those that it advanced concerning proposed order 6, order 10 is also unwarranted.

[845] Apple says that proposed order 10 would dramatically limit its commercial options, truncating the choices as to monetisation strategy which remain open to it. Apple says that proposed orders 10(a) to (d) would render unavailable to it a series of means of monetising its ecosystem and promoting pro-competitive outcomes on the basis of findings in other proceedings and in respect of steps taken in other jurisdictions. And it says that none of this was specifically analysed or argued for in Epic’s case against it.

[846] Apple also says that Epic seeks to justify these and further limitations on Apple’s commercial freedom and ability to commercialise its intellectual property not by allegations and findings of current or apprehended contraventions of the CCA made against Apple, but by findings made against Google in respect of conduct in which it was not alleged Apple had or would engage.

[847] Apple says that Epic seeks to draw support by reference to observations that I made about Apple’s response to regulations imposed in the EU, the Netherlands and South Korea, in my reasons explaining that in-app payment solutions constitutes a separate product market. But Apple says that Epic is redeploying observations made elsewhere in my liability reasons to suit extraneous purposes here.

[848] Further, Apple says that proposed order 10(d) would enjoin it from levying any fees in connection with in-app purchases made by a method other than IAP within any iOS app distributed by an alternative distribution channel. And it says that proposed order 10(e) would do likewise in connection with any purchase of digital content for use in-app, in connection with any iOS app distributed via the App Store.

[849] Now Epic seeks to justify these proposed orders on the basis that Apple provides no distribution or payment services to developers in such scenarios, such that it can be inferred that the purpose and likely effect of the charge is to deter developers from using alternative payment methods and/or alternative distribution channels. But Apple says that the implicit premise is that Apple provides no service to developers in respect of such transactions, but it says that that premise is unavailable. It says that the transactions take place in an ecosystem that Apple has built and maintains. It says that the inference that any fee must have an illicit purpose therefore does not follow.

[850] In any event, Apple says that none of Epic’s factual assertions and logical inferences in support of proposed order 10(d) or (e) have been made or drawn by me. Further, Apple says that such proposed orders rely upon false premises. One premise is that the only explanation for such price discrimination is that it is a means of imposing the anti-competitive payments tie on those developers who offer in-app purchases of digital content, by financially dissuading them from offering a payment solution other than IAP. And it says that the other premise is that any higher or other fees could not be explained or justified other than, once again, as a means of imposing the anti-competitive IAP tie on developers, and so ought to be prohibited by me. But Apple says that as the creator and custodian of the ecosystem patronised by the relevant developers and users alike, it should be permitted to charge fees for providing that service. And it says that both premises are false.

[851] Now proposed order 10(e) allows Apple to supplement the amount of any fee or commission it may charge in respect of app users’ purchases of digital content, which the order otherwise caps at the amount Apple charges by way of commission on physical purchases being currently nil, so as to recoup any additional economic costs that are genuinely and reasonably incurred by Apple in connection with an iOS device user making an in-app purchase of digital content using a payment method other than IAP that are not incurred when a user makes an in-app purchase of physical goods or services using the same payment method.

[852] But Apple says that even if there was a basis for some form of restraint upon Apple’s conduct contemplated by proposed order 10(e), an order in this form does not meet the requirements of clarity and certainty which it says should attend the exercise of the discretion in s 80. But even if these problems of uncertainty could be overcome, Apple says that proposed order 10(e) casts this Court in the role of market administrator, a role which is incompatible with a Chapter III judge and incompatible with the exercise of the power conferred on me by s 80.

[853] Generally, Apple says that proposed order 10 would risk denying it the flexibility to regulate its own ecosystem in order to protect and promote the pro-competitive benefits that its ecosystem brings, preventing it from earning a fair return for its investments in and the services it provides through that ecosystem, and subjecting it to the risk of liability for contempt. And Apple says that none of those outcomes was weighed in my competition analysis constituent of my liability findings.

###### Analysis

[854] As Epic points out, proposed orders 10(a) to (d) are based on my findings that Apple’s iOS payment solutions conduct had the effect and likely effect of substantially lessening competition in the payments market, and that when compelled to remove equivalent restrictions in other jurisdictions, Apple adopted monetisation strategies designed to make the choice of alternatives illusory and to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions.

[855] Moreover, there can be no serious objection to drawing on my findings in Epic v Google when fashioning relief. Evidence in one proceeding was evidence in the other. And as Epic correctly points out, proposed order 10 seeks to deal with techniques that I have already recognised in Epic v Google as devices used by a dominant platform to neutralise developer choice and suppress effective competition in in-app payment solutions once exclusivity requirements are removed. Contrary to what Apple says, those findings can be used to frame relief directed at Apple. And absent such an order, I can and do infer that Apple may take similar steps as at least a realistic possibility.

[856] Proposed orders 10(b) and (c) enjoin a narrow set of conditions that Apple has enacted overseas. Contrary to Apple’s assertions, this was the subject of considerable analysis and Apple’s witnesses were cross-examined about the purpose, commercial reality and effect of the measures implemented overseas.

[857] As to proposed order 10(a), in the context of its UCB program other than in South Korea, Google requires app developers to offer users the same price, irrespective of the payment solution they select. I found that this effectively prohibited price competition (GJ[638], [5374], [5379]). Conduct of that kind is restrained by order 10(a).

[858] As to proposed order 10(b), in both the United States and EU, Apple has required developers who seek to use an alternative payment method for purchases of digital content for use in-app, whether by way of an in-app payment solution or by way of out-of-app payments, to report such transactions to Apple and to grant Apple extensive audit rights. Conduct of that kind is restrained by order 10(b)

[859] As to proposed order 10(c), in the EU, Apple requires developers that wish to use an alternative payment method to contract directly with end users in relation to all transactions. I found in Epic v Google that equivalent conduct prevents the developer from using a payment solution provider that acts as a merchant of record. Let me elaborate on what I said in Epic v Google.

[860] I found that under UCB Google does not permit app developers to offer an alternative payment solution without also offering Google Play Billing (GJ[637], [5374], [5379], [5499], [5509]). This is unattractive for developers, as they prefer all payments to be facilitated by a single payment solution so that the developers are only dealing with one provider and can consolidate transaction data from a single source (GJ[5499]). I further found that Google prevents app developers from using payment providers that act as a merchant of record in the context of UCB and DOB (GJ[640], [641], [5375], [5379]). Using such a provider is a way to take administrative and operational burdens away from the app developer, such that restrictions of that kind would preclude app developers from obtaining a desirable service, prevent alternative payment providers from competing with Apple on the merits, and prevent those payment providers who do act as a merchant of record from competing in the payments market (GJ[641], [673], [5376]). These forms of conduct are addressed by proposed order 10(c).

[861] Further, as to proposed order 10(c) I repeat what I said concerning proposed order 6(j) concerning requiring the option of IAP.

[862] As to proposed order 10(d)(i), it restrains Apple from requiring developers from paying any fee in connection with in-app purchases made via a payment method other than IAP within any app distributed via an alternative distribution channel.

[863] Proposed order 10(d)(ii) restrains Apple from requiring developers to pay any fee in connection with purchases of digital content from any website, or from any developer’s platform, for use in-app, where the app was distributed via an alternative distribution channel.

[864] Now as Epic correctly says, proposed order 10 is directed at predictable strategies that Apple has devised and employed overseas to dissuade or block alternative payment solutions where it has been compelled to open up the payments solutions market. And the justification for this order draws on my findings in relation to Apple’s introduction of transaction-linked fees, including the CTF, where developers make use of alternatives, and which I found to be designed to dissuade developers from taking up the option of using alternative payment solutions, and deterrence tactics employed to protect its preferred arrangements.

[865] So, in the EU, Apple has required developers that wish to use alternative payment solutions or that lead users to payment methods outside an app to agree to the alternative terms addendum, under which developers must pay a new fee referable to the sale of digital goods or services in apps distributed via the App Store as well as a new fee referable to the number of app downloads being the CTF. The alternative terms addendum also required developers to pay an additional fee on the proceeds of sales of digital goods or services for developers who also use IAP.

[866] I said at [4081] to [4083]:

> First, there is a default commission of 17% on the proceeds of sale of digital goods or services for apps in the EU storefronts of the iOS App Store.

> Second, there is an additional 3% commission on the proceeds of sales of digital goods or services for developers who also use Apple’s payment processing service.

> Third, there is a Core Technology Fee (CTF) of 50 euro cents in respect of each first annual install per year. The CTF has the following characteristics.

[867] I found that these fees are designed to dissuade developers from taking up the option of using alternative payment solutions for in-app purchases.
