The CTF in particular discourages the uptake of alternative payment methods because an app which achieves some degree of popularity will cause its developer to incur a substantial debt to Apple irrespective of whether the developer has generated any financial benefit from that app, including in circumstances where the developer would not have owed Apple any commission under the prior regime.

[868] I said at [4086] and [4087]:

> Further, the CTF is dependent upon a single variable, being the number of app downloads. It is conceivable, therefore, that an app which achieves some degree of popularity will cause its developer to incur a substantial debt to Apple irrespective of whether the developer has generated any financial benefit from that app.

> So, if a game developer releases a free game which attracts two million annual downloads, that developer will incur an annual CTF of half a million euros, in circumstances where it would not have owed Apple any commission under the prior regime. If the developer subsequently publishes yearly app updates to its two million users, that fee will be incurred on an annual basis.

[869] I found that these deterrence tactics, which include the CTF, were employed by Apple, in the face of legal compulsion, to protect its preferred arrangements.

[870] I said at [4092]:

> I agree with Epic that Apple’s ability to deploy such deterrence tactics to protect its preferred arrangements, even in the face of legal compulsion, manifests its substantial degree of market power.

[871] In the context of developers who distribute iOS apps via alternative distribution channels, or that distribute apps via channels including the App Store, but who happen to sell digital content on a website or through their own platform for use in-app, the imposition of such fees could only be directed to dissuading such developers from using alternative payment methods, given that Apple would not be providing them with any distribution or payment service.

[872] Let me now address proposed order 10(e) in more detail which seeks to enjoin Apple as follows:

> The First Respondent must not (including by procuring any act of any related corporation) require that the Applicants or any other iOS app developer who uses a payment method other than IAP for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content for use in-app:

> …

> (e) in connection with any iOS app distributed via the App Store, where:

> (i) in-app purchases are made using a payment method other than IAP, or

> (ii) purchases of digital content are made on any website or from any developer’s platform for use in-app,

> pay a fee that exceeds any fee that is imposed on app developers who distribute iOS apps via the App Store in connection with in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP, plus any additional economic costs that are genuinely and reasonably incurred by Apple in connection with an iOS device user making an in-app purchase of digital content using a payment method other than IAP that are not incurred when a user makes an in-app purchase of physical goods or services using the same payment method.

[873] Proposed order 10(e) addresses fees that Apple ought to be restrained from charging developers who use a payment method other than IAP in connection with in-app purchases made within any iOS apps distributed via the App Store. I found that the fees Apple charges developers in South Korea and the Netherlands, where it is required to permit developers to use an alternative payment method to IAP for in-app purchases, were based on an estimate of costs that Apple knew was unrealistic and out of reach for developers, and which ensured that the choice presented to developers was illusory.

[874] I said at [5880] to [5884]:

> Further, the regulatory requirements in South Korea and the Netherlands were directed at opening up the in-app payments market, yet Apple’s changes in purported compliance with these requirements were based on an estimate of costs that it knew was unrealistic and out of reach for developers. The reality is that developers would pay commissions that exceed 30% if they used an alternative payment system. The vast majority of developers are therefore unlikely to do so, as they would suffer a loss.

> Now in my view the low take-up rate of alternative payment solutions demonstrates that the “choice” Apple has presented to developers in these jurisdictions is illusory. Apple has set the commission rates to block any genuine prospect of competition from suppliers of alternative payment solutions.

> In all the circumstances, I would infer that the practical effect of Apple charging a service fee that is only 3 or 4% less than 30% is to offer developers the option of using an alternative payment system at an economically unviable cost.

> Moreover, the fact that any developers in the Netherlands and South Korea have chosen to use an alternative payment solution to IAP is strong evidence of independent demand, given that it is demand to use a third-party payment solution even when it is likely to be more costly to do so than using IAP.

> Further, the “implied” price at which Apple offers IAP to these developers is only 3 or 4% and is not a real or competitive price. It is an artificial construct, designed to ensure limited take-up of alternative payment solutions. Its effect is simply to replace a contractual tie with an equivalent economic tie.

[875] The “implied” price at which Apple offered IAP to developers in those jurisdictions was found to be not a real or competitive price. Apple’s fee structures were found to be an artificial construct, designed to ensure limited take-up of alternative payment solutions and had the effect simply to replace a contractual tie with an equivalent economic tie.

[876] Now Apple’s proven conduct in offering developers an illusory choice of that kind is addressed by proposed order 10(e) via two routes.

[877] First, Apple would be prohibited from charging developers who use an alternative payment method to IAP, in connection with in-app purchases made within an app distributed via the App Store, a fee that exceeds fees imposed on developers who distribute apps via the App Store which allow users to make in-app purchases of physical goods or services using a payment method other than IAP. Clearly, the application of the IAP tie to digital goods and services only is a choice about Apple’s monetisation strategy, which presumably is for its own benefit.

[878] In circumstances where Apple provides the same app distribution services to iOS app developers, regardless of how their iOS apps are monetised, and regardless of the nature of any in-app purchases, no justification can be offered for Apple imposing selectively disadvantageous fees in relation to digital goods and services as compared to physical goods and services, in circumstances where IAP is not used for either form of in-app purchase. I said at [2000] to [2010]:

> Now given the assertions by Apple to the effect that in-app purchases are part of the same relevant product as downloads and updates, there are further reasons why it is inappropriate to treat the payment service that Apple supplies to facilitate in-app purchases of digital content as part of the same product as app downloads and updates.

> First, Apple plays a different role in respect of in-app purchases. Whereas Apple takes responsibility for hosting, marketing and delivering all apps to users as agent for developers, it plays no role in effecting in-app purchases if one leaves aside its payment service.

> Second, Apple refuses to play any role in relation to in-app purchases of physical goods or services.

> Third, clause 3.1 of the schedule 2 agreement provides that developers, rather than Apple, are responsible for hosting and delivering content or services sold by the developer using IAP, that is, in-app purchases of digital content. This is in contrast with Apple’s role in respect of app downloads.

> This difference alone suggests that it is a mistake to treat in-app purchases as part of the same product supplied by Apple as app distribution services.

> Fourth, the inclusion of in-app purchases of digital content within the product description is at odds with the exclusion of in-app purchases of physical goods or services. The substantive difference between the two is that Apple compels the use of IAP for the former but prohibits it for the latter. This is a choice about Apple’s monetisation strategy.

> Now the suggestion put to Professor Wright in cross-examination, and said to be supported by evidence referred to in an aide memoire handed to me at trial, that Apple is involved in in-app purchases through the app review process is a distraction.

> All that evidence shows is that Apple reserves the right to review additional content that a developer wishes to add to the functionality of its app in a way that will work with Apple’s in-app purchase API when a later purchase transaction arises. That is, in substance, the same as Apple’s role in reviewing app updates.

> Importantly, it is a step that precedes, and is disconnected from, any actual in-app purchase by a user.

> Apple’s involvement in any subsequent purchase that a user might initiate is limited to the compelled use of IAP.

> Apple’s present strategy of monetising app distribution services through commissions on certain in-app purchases should not drive the selection of the relevant product. Moreover, Apple’s conception of app transactions as comprising app downloads, app updates, and in-app purchases is problematic because in-app purchases are a fundamentally different type of transaction when compared to downloads and updates.

[879] The only explanation for such price discrimination is that it is a means of imposing the anti-competitive payments tie on those developers who offer in-app purchases of digital content, by financially dissuading them from offering a payment solution other than IAP. It is appropriate that I enjoin Apple from such conduct.

[880] Second, Apple would be prohibited from charging developers who offer an alternative payment method to IAP, in connection with in-app purchases made within an app distributed via the App Store, a fee that exceeds X plus any additional economic costs.

[881] Now I said at [5859] to [5862]:

> But I agree with Epic that the low take-up of that opportunity can be explained by the fact that, if developers do pursue it, Apple still charges a commission of 27% in the Netherlands or 26% in South Korea.

> As Epic points out, using an alternative payment solution in those circumstances would make no sense for the vast majority of developers. To be worthwhile, they would need to find an alternative payment solution that they consider to be equal to or better than IAP and that charges less than 3% in the Netherlands or 4% in South Korea, or which offers other benefits that can outweigh the cost increase that would need to be incurred to make use of the alternative.

> I agree with Epic that the cost structure imposed by Apple makes this a relatively unlikely proposition. Apple’s own costs of IAP are likely to be greater than 4%.

> Payment processing costs vary substantially depending on the FOPs that are offered. Google estimated that they are around 4% when credit cards, PayPal, eWallets and bank transfers are offered but 6% when direct carrier billing and gift cards are included. IAP offers nearly 200 payment methods in total, including credit cards, PayPal, eWallets and, importantly, direct carrier billing and gift cards. Even accepting that Apple’s payment processing costs may not be the same as Google’s costs, they are likely to be far higher than 4%, and certainly higher than 3%.

[882] Now proposed order 10(e) does not deny Apple the ability to obtain remuneration for services it in fact supplies. It is directed at fees imposed in connection with in-app purchases made within any iOS app distributed via the App Store that function as a proxy for the payments tie, that is, charges structured so as to deter developers from using non-IAP payment methods.

[883] As Epic points out, this is not a hypothetical threat. I have already found that in response to regulatory obligations in the EU, Apple introduced a 17% commission and the CTF in a way that was designed to dissuade developers from taking up the option of using alternative payment solutions and that those deterrence tactics were employed to protect its preferred arrangements. I agree with Epic that there is a proper basis to infer that differential or supra-competitive charges imposed on non-IAP transactions would be used as a mechanism of foreclosure rather than as neutral remuneration for App Store distribution.

[884] Now in-app purchases of physical goods and services have some different elements when compared to purchases of digital content, but their relevance is that Apple’s approach to apps in that class demonstrates that Apple can and does provide App Store distribution without levying a transaction-linked fee on those in-app purchases as I recognised in my liability reasons.

[885] Apple’s main complaint in respect of proposed order 10(e), leaving aside its global objection to relief that extends to conduct not specifically found by me to contravene the CCA, is that the order lacks clarity and certainty. But the phrase “economic costs that are genuinely and reasonably incurred” is readily understood. It refers to costs that Apple genuinely and reasonably incurs when iOS apps are distributed via the App Store using payment methods other than IAP, that is, distribution costs. Apple is a sophisticated company. What is required of Apple to comply with proposed order 10(e)(ii) is clear. Let me address a related topic.

[886] On 11 December 2025, the US Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit delivered its reasons for judgment in Apple’s appeal from the first instance decision of Judge Gonzalez-Rogers, in which Apple was held in contempt of orders consequential on Apple’s anti-steering restrictions, having been found to contravene Californian competition laws. I have referred to this decision (Epic Games, Inc v Apple 161 F.4th 1162) earlier.

[887] Now her Honour at first instance permanently enjoined Apple from inter-alia imposing “any commission or any fee on purchases that consumers make outside an app”. The Ninth Circuit said (at 1185) that “[t]he biggest problem with the commission prohibition is that it permanently prohibits the compensation that Apple can receive for linked-out purchases of digital products, regardless of whether the commission is itself prohibitive”. The Court went on to say (at 1186) that “[t]he district court’s commission prohibition goes too far by denying Apple any way to purge its contempt by, for example, imposing a non-prohibitive, reasonable commission or fee”; query the additional words here in light of its later statement.

[888] The Court offered some guidance for the remittal of the matter on what a permissible commission or fee regime could look like for purchases made via external links from iOS apps distributed via the App Store, that is, for charges associated with Apple’s coordination of linked-out transactions rather than for App Store distribution generally. The Court said that Apple should be able to charge a commission on linked-out purchases based on the costs that are genuinely and reasonably necessary for its coordination of external links for linked-out purchases, but no more. And the Court said that Apple should receive no commission for the security and privacy features it offers in connection with external links, and its calculation of its necessary costs for external links should not include the cost associated with the security and privacy features it offers with its IAP.

[889] They said the following (at 1187 and 1188):

> We recommend some possible courses of action to the district court regarding an appropriate commission or fee limitation on remand. Apple should be able to charge a commission on linked-out purchases with the following in mind: (a) Apple should be able to charge a commission on linked-out purchases based on the costs that are genuinely and reasonably necessary for its coordination of external links for linked-out purchases, but no more. We refer to these costs as “necessary costs.”; (b) In making a determination of Apple’s necessary costs, Apple is entitled to some compensation for the use of its intellectual property that is directly used in permitting Epic and others to consummate linked-out purchases. In deciding how much that should be, the district court should consider the fact that most of the intellectual property at issue is already used to facilitate IAP, and costs attributed to linked-out purchases should be reduced equitably and proportionately; (c) Apple should receive no commission for the security and privacy features it offers to external links, and its calculation of its necessary costs for external links should not include the cost associated with the security and privacy features it offers with its IAP; (d) Apple should not be able to charge any commission for linked-out purchases until such time as the district court has approved an appropriate fee, but both parties should be encouraged to reach agreement and/or seek the court’s approval of its proposed fee expeditiously; and (e) The district court may determine how best to make the referenced determination but one possibility includes inviting the parties to provide expert testimony based upon which it would determine the appropriate fee or commission to be chargeable for Apple’s actual costs of providing services for linked-out transactions. The district court might also consider whether to establish a “Technical Committee” somewhat like what was done in In re Google, 147 F.4th 917, to aid it in determining a reasonable fee and/or commission that Apple can charge for linked-out purchases. See id. at 954-55.

> [footnote omitted]

[890] That approach is not inconsistent with the concept underpinning proposed order 10(e).

#### Proposed order 11 (order 10 as modified and made)

[891] By its proposed order 11(a), Epic seeks to prohibit Apple from imposing frictions or impediments into the process by which iOS device users can elect to use, or can in fact use, a payment method other than IAP when purchasing digital content for in-app use, which are additional to the frictions or impediments encountered when making in-app purchases of physical goods or services. I would note that proposed order 11 corresponds, with modification, to order 10 that I have made.

[892] Apple points out that in part Epic seeks to justify this order by reference to findings that I have made in relation to Google. It says that the making of this order would align with my finding that the purported choice Google offered to developers to use an alternative payment solution to Google Play Billing in the context of the UCB and DOB programs is not a commercially real choice, where Google imposes frictions or impediments on users where an alternative payment solution is used.

[893] Now I would note here that in GJ[642], [5491]–[5497] and [5500] I said the following:

> Again, the choice being presented to developers by Google is not commercially real. App developers remain constrained by the payments tie, even when they have access to UCB.

> …

> It must comply with user experience requirements prescribed by Google. Initially, those requirements were set out in the interim guidelines. Presently, they are set out in an alternative billing APIs document.

> The interim guidelines stipulated requirements that included the following matters.

> First, the first time the user initiated a purchase, they had to be shown an information screen whose text and context had to be exactly as prescribed by Google, including a statement that “Only purchases through Google Play are secured by Google”.

> Second, a billing choice screen had to be shown to the user before every purchase.

> Third, the purchase price had to be shown to the user before they were shown either the information screen or the billing choice screen.

> Fourth, the buttons for the alternative payment solution and Google Play Billing had to be represented in a fair and equal manner on the billing choice screen, and all components of that screen had to contain the exact text and elements prescribed by Google.

> The alternative billing APIs document explained that once a developer integrates with Google’s alternative billing APIs, as participating developers are now required to do, then the Play Store renders and manages the applicable information and user choice screens, such that the user experience is dictated by Google. That user experience is not materially different from that required under the interim UX guidelines.

> …

> Further, the billing choice screen introduces additional friction into the payment flow.

[894] Epic also says that this conduct, if not enjoined, would likely dissuade developers from adopting payment methods other than IAP, and would in substance continue the IAP tie.

[895] But Apple says that no order should be made against it on that basis.

[896] First, it says that I did not find either in the Apple or Google judgments that Google’s conduct in respect of the UCB or DOB programmes contravened s 46.

[897] Second, it says that even if such a finding had been made in respect of Google’s conduct vis-a-vis its users, that finding would not justify or support the imposition of any restraint upon Apple, which was not alleged to have engaged, or to be likely to engage, in any cognate conduct and which conduct, accordingly, has not been the subject of evidence or submissions.

[898] Third, it says that it is notable that the gloss Epic has sought to apply to proposed order 11(a) departs from the terms in which the order is cast. Epic described the conduct the subject of proposed order 10 as only display warnings proportionate to security risk. Proposed order 11(a) does not in terms permit Apple to display warnings proportionate to security risk.

[899] Apple says that Epic’s proposed order 11(b) is analogous to its proposed orders 6(b) and 10(e)(i), in that it seeks to enjoin Apple from imposing fees or conditions for access to or use of its intellectual property which do not apply to developers selling physical goods or services through App Store apps. In that way, like those other orders, proposed order 11(b) would prevent Apple for charging any fee for its intellectual property, beyond the annual USD 99 Developer Program fee, or else require it to impose additional fees on developers of apps through which physical goods or services are purchased.

[900] Apple says that not only is such relief outside that claimed in the FAOA and its pleaded case, but it also suffers from the other vices identified above in respect of those analogous orders. It says that there is no basis for finding that, if Apple were to impose fees or conditions for access to or use of its intellectual property beyond those permitted by proposed order 11(b), such conduct would necessarily contravene s 46. In those circumstances, the proposed order should, like its analogues, be rejected in limine.

###### Analysis

[901] Proposed order 11 imposes additional negative injunctions that are directed to ensuring that Apple does not engage in specified conduct to limit the take-up or use of alternative payment solutions and thereby lessen competition in the payments market. Again, in terms of users and the App Store, I will apply the geographic limitations that I have previously indicated.

[902] First, proposed order 11(a) prohibits Apple from imposing frictions or impediments into the process by which iOS device users can elect to use, or can in fact use, a payment method other than IAP when purchasing digital content for in-app use, which are additional to the frictions or impediments encountered when making in-app purchases of physical goods or services. This conduct, if not enjoined, would likely dissuade developers from adopting payment methods other than IAP, and would in substance continue the IAP tie.

[903] Such an order is warranted in circumstances where I found that Apple used deterrence tactics to protect its preferred arrangements ([4092]) and I accepted that “clicks are costly”, meaning as I said in my main reasons that the more friction is imposed in respect of a particular transaction, the less likely it is that a user will complete that transaction.

[904] This aligns with my finding that the purported choice Google offered to developers to use an alternative payment solution to Google Play Billing in the context of the UCB and DOB programs is not a “commercially real” choice, where Google imposes frictions or impediments on users where an alternative payment solution is used (GJ[642], [5491] to [5497], [5500]).

[905] Second, proposed order 11(b) prohibits Apple from imposing fees or conditions for access to, or use of, any tool or API or SDK that is necessary to develop and distribute iOS apps that allow or enable developers and iOS device users to use a payment method other than IAP. As discussed above, in the European Union, where Apple has been prohibited from requiring developers to use IAP for in-app purchases of digital content, it has taken steps to impose fees, including the CTF that I found was designed to dissuade developers from taking up alternative payment solutions ([5830]). Conduct of a similar kind is addressed by order 11(b).

[906] Apple contends that proposed order 11 would prohibit or impede its “implementation in Australia of means to protect user security and privacy”. I agree with Epic that that is code for introducing disclosure screens of the kind that Apple uses in other jurisdictions.

[907] Moreover, Apple has failed to explain why security considerations require frictions of this kind when alternative payment methods are used to purchase digital content, but not when alternative payment methods are used to purchase physical goods and services within iOS apps distributed via the App Store.

[908] Further, I found (at [2030]):

> In my view there is no significant additional security risk associated with the use of alternative payment solutions for in-app purchases of digital goods and services that does not already exist in the case of in-app purchases of physical goods and services.

[909] That finding is consistent with Professor Somayaji’s evidence that third-party payment methods are not inherently riskier than IAP, and that the use of disclosure screens serves only to make a distinction between IAP and third-party payments that has no firm basis in security.

[910] And as Epic says, if disclosure screens were needed to protect user security and privacy, one would expect Apple to impose them equally in other contexts, including when alternative payment methods are used to purchase physical goods and services. Let me deal in more detail with disclosure sheets, drawing on Professor Somayaji’s evidence in part.

###### Disclosure sheets

[911] Now Mr Friedland has stated that “External Purchase APIs […] when called upon: […] present appropriate disclosures to the user” and that the disclosure sheets “are a means of educating users of a change in the standard process (that is, IAP) so that the user is provided with the opportunity to assess their risk appetite in proceeding with providing their payment details to sources outside of Apple.”

[912] But there are a significant number of transactions that a user may be conducting in iOS apps that do not involve Apple. For instance, Apple already allows some video streaming apps to use third-party frameworks for payment processing, rather than being restricted to using IAP. On the other hand, Mr Friedland states that disclosure sheets give users the opportunity to assess their risk appetite when using non-Apple payment methods. But this distinction is not justified.

[913] Furthermore, the ability to use third-party digital payment solutions is only available to a select few subscription services that have negotiated special terms with Apple, that are not required to implement disclosure sheets for payments. As a result, there is no need for Apple to add additional disclosure sheets that simply inform users that they are transacting with the developer and not Apple only for in-app transactions for digital goods.

[914] Further, if users face more steps and friction to complete transactions for the same good using a third-party payment solution, compared to IAP, then developers using those alternative solutions are disproportionately affected by implementation of disclosure sheets, which could lead to lower user spending and increased issues like cart abandonment. Instead, if disclosure sheets are implemented in a uniform fashion, a single payment solution like Apple’s IAP is not advantaged as it is offered with the same number of steps as required for third-party payment solutions. If Apple were to implement the current set of disclosure sheets for alternative payment solutions adopted in other jurisdictions, in response to my orders, such disclosure sheets for alternative payment solutions would only serve to make a distinction between Apple’s in-app payments and third-party payments for purchasing in-app digital content.

[915] Further, some implementations of alternative payments on iOS devices include disclosure sheets, whilst other implementations do not. This demonstrates that the implementation of disclosure sheets for payments processed using alternative payment solutions is a policy decision by Apple. Apple uses disclosure sheets for alternative payments in Japan. But Apple does not require disclosure sheets when developers use non-Apple payment methods for certain types of purchases, including in-app purchases of physical goods or other goods and services consumed outside an app. Similarly, Apple does not require disclosure sheets for purchases of digital goods made via external purchase links in the United States.

[916] Mr Friedland states that the StoreKit framework APIs that facilitate external purchases within an app, when called upon present appropriate disclosures to the user. Mr Friedland further contends that order 11 of Epic’s proposed relief restricts Apple’s use of disclosure screens, if they are considered to be a form of friction. Mr Friedland maintains that if orders 9, 10, and 11 were made concurrently, then certain of the sub-orders in Epic's proposed orders 10 and 11 could not practicably be implemented technically.

[917] But the use of disclosure sheets is not a technical necessity and only serves to make a distinction between Apple’s IAP and third-party payments for purchasing in-app digital content. In fact, the orders that Mr Friedland contends could not practically be implemented, are already implemented in a similar fashion in the US.

[918] Apple implements a handful of policy decisions related to disclosure sheets for alternative payment solutions on iOS. Policy variations are currently based on parameters such as jurisdiction, app type and category of item purchased. Apple does not require disclosure sheets for all alternative payment solutions. In addition to not requiring the use of IAP for physical goods and certain digital goods, Apple also does not require disclosure sheets for alternative payments for physical goods and services. In fact, developers cannot use IAP for the purchase of physical goods and services and items otherwise consumed only outside the app. Goods and services that are consumed outside the app can only be paid for using other purchase methods, and there is no associated disclosure sheet.

[919] In most jurisdictions for which Apple allows alternatives to IAP for processing payments for in-app purchases of digital goods consumed in the app, developers must include in-app disclosure sheets that appear to a user before the app opens an external link or opens an in-app alternative payment.

[920] In Japan, for example, these disclosure sheets that are shown below explain that the user will be “transacting with the developer and not Apple,” that features like payment methods stored in Apple and Apple-managed refunds are unavailable and that the transactions, “including their privacy and security, will be managed by the developer”.

Alternative Payment Disclosure Sheets in Japan in March 2026

[921] Whilst Apple has announced plans to allow users to eventually turn off the disclosure sheet for future transactions in Japan, currently Japanese App Store users see these disclosure sheets for every transaction made using an alternative payment method for in-app purchases.

[922] In contrast to Japan and other jurisdictions where Apple requires disclosure sheets for use of alternative payment solutions, app developers in the United States are no longer required to use disclosure sheets or entitlements for alternative payments. Between 16 January 2024 and 30 April 2025, app developers in the United States had been allowed to use links to external payment methods for their iOS apps. However, Apple implemented a 27% commission on such payments and instituted strict requirements on external payment links, including requiring use of the StoreKit External Purchase Link Entitlement and StoreKit APIs. I will elaborate on StoreKit External Purchase Link Entitlement later. The use of these entitlements also included restrictions that limited link formatting and placement and mandated in-app disclosure sheets, which were shown as follows:

External Purchase Disclosure Sheet in the United States in January 2024 and April 2025

[923] On 30 April 2025, and as I have previously indicated, Judge Gonzalez–Rogers took Apple to task for, inter–alia, its dodgy disclosure sheets.

[924] On 1 May 2025, Apple implemented new external app payment guidelines for the US that allowed app developers to use buttons and links to external payment methods within their apps without entitlements or disclosure sheets with non-neutral messaging and allowed app developers to encourage app users to use payment methods other than IAP. Some app developers including Spotify and Patreon rapidly submitted new versions of their apps that included external links and made use of alternative payment methods. These new app experiences did not include large disclosure sheets, and as of March 2026, app-based purchases in the United States on apps such as Spotify and Patreon continue to offer external payment links that immediately send the user to the app developer’s website to complete the transaction without a disclosure sheet as shown below:

Patreon External Purchase in the United States in May 2025

External Purchase Link Examples in the United States in March 2026

[925] Not only does the removal of this restriction highlight that implementation of alternative payment systems is feasible on iOS devices, but it also showcases how the use of disclosure sheets is a policy decision made by Apple, rather than a decision based on any security or technical constraints. The disclosure sheets currently implemented for processing in-app purchase of digital goods only seek to notify the user that, for example, “This app does not support the App Store’s private and secure payment system.” Such an intermediate step does not contribute to user security, especially given that users regularly purchase physical and, in some cases, digital goods within apps, without attributing the transaction to Apple. Moreover, Apple’s IAP, which is a centralized means of payment processing, does not provide any security advantages over a decentralized means of payment processing.

[926] In summary, the use of disclosure sheets for alternative payment solutions only serves to make a distinction between Apple’s in-app payments and third-party payments for purchasing in-app digital content and does not contribute significantly to user security.

###### General matters

[927] Now as I have said, Apple contends that no such order should be made. Now true it is that Epic partly justifies the order by reference to my findings in Epic v Google about the UCB/DOB programs, but Apple says that I did not find that Google’s conduct in relation to those programs contravened the CCA. And it says that Apple itself was not alleged to have engaged in any cognate frictions conduct, nor to be likely to do so in future, and so there is no foundation for such a restraint.

[928] But as I indicated earlier in my reasons, the question under s 80 is whether the orders are appropriate to remedy the contraventions and their effects. The provision is not confined to enjoining specific acts that have already been found to contravene. And I can formulate relief that addresses the mechanisms by which the contraventions operated and that prevents their re-emergence in a different guise.

[929] Proposed order 11(a) is supported by my findings that Apple has misused its market power by tying IAP to in-app payments for digital content and foreclosing rival payment solutions.

[930] Further, as set out earlier, I have already found that when obliged in other jurisdictions to permit third-party payment solutions or alternative distribution channels, Apple has responded by imposing various commissions, fees and other restrictions. I described those choices as uneconomic, illusory and designed to block any genuine prospect of competition.

[931] In summary there is a real risk, supported by findings and further evidence, that absent proposed order 11(a), Apple will respond to any opening of the in-app payments market by imposing frictions on non-IAP transactions in ways that perpetuate the substantial lessening of competition that I have found.

[932] Further, Apple’s criticisms of proposed order 11(b) regurgitate its earlier complaints about the scope of the originating application, the treatment of Apple’s IP and the requirement that enjoined conduct itself constitute a contravention, all of which have been addressed in relation to proposed orders 6(b) and 10(e).

#### Proposed order 12 (order 11 as modified and made)

[933] Proposed order 12 would impose a broad and imprecise prohibition on Apple taking any steps to reject for distribution, discriminate against, take retaliatory action against, or otherwise disadvantage Epic or its apps, or any other developer or their apps, on the basis that their app is available through alternative distribution channels or uses or links to third party payment services. I would note that proposed order 12 corresponds, with modification, to order 11 that I have made.

[934] Apple says that relief in this form has not been claimed in the FAOA, and is not supported by any aspect of its pleaded case or my liability findings.

[935] It says that the core of Epic’s case attacked Apple’s prohibitions on alternative app stores, direct downloading of iOS apps, and use of alternatives to IAP for in-app purchases of digital content, as well as Apple’s anti-steering rule. It did not identify, much less attack, any conduct in which it was alleged Apple was likely to engage against developers or their apps which took advantage of the removal of those prohibitions or the anti-steering rule should that occur. No such conduct has been explored in these proceedings.

[936] It says that the purpose of the grant of relief is not to restore competition to the distribution or payments market generally, but to respond to the case or controversy brought before the Court by Epic concerning specific practices said to lessen competition in those markets, and to give effect to the findings of contravention which have been made.

[937] Further, Apple says that the grant of relief in these proceedings provides no occasion to seek to anticipate and forestall all possibilities of conduct lessening competition in the future by putting Apple at risk of contempt.

[938] Further, it says that the proposed order would also operate to fetter contractual discretions which Apple presently maintains and which have not been the subject of any adverse finding in the liability judgment.

[939] Apple under the terms of the DPLA reserves wide discretions to itself to reject for distribution or to cease distributing any app through the App Store including by reason of non-compliance with published App Review Guidelines.

[940] Apple says that proposed order 12 attacks and undermines those discretions. Any exclusion of an app or developer who uses alternative distribution channels or third-party payment services might be characterised as discrimination or retaliation in breach of the order, with the result that Apple could not exercise its contractual discretions without running the gauntlet of contempt proceedings. Relief which operated in such a manner would be inappropriate.

###### Analysis

[941] Let me say something concerning the injunctive relief dealing with compliance and anti-discrimination.

[942] Proposed order 12 is a negative injunction directed to prohibiting Apple from rejecting for distribution, discriminating against, taking retaliatory action against or otherwise disadvantaging Epic or any other app developer both specifically in submitting apps for distribution via the App Store, or more generally, on the basis that the developer’s app is available for distribution outside the App Store, the app uses or will use a payment method other than IAP, or the developer links or otherwise informs, leads or directs users from within their app to a payment method other than IAP.

[943] Proposed order 12 is the corollary of the prohibitive injunctions proposed by orders 5, 6, 7, 10 and 11 (orders 4, 5, 6, 9 and 10 respectively as modified and made).

[944] If Apple were permitted to comply with those orders while at the same time taking retaliatory action against developers who took advantage of the opportunity to distribute apps outside the App Store, to use payment methods other than IAP, and/or to steer users to payment methods other than IAP, Apple could subvert the intended effect of those orders.

[945] In such circumstances, proposed orders 5, 6, 7, 10 and 11 would be deficient in achieving their objective, and would be unlikely to restore competition to the distribution market or the payments market, as Apple’s retaliatory action would likely stifle competition in both those markets.

[946] The injunction proposed by order 12 ensures that proposed orders 5, 6, 7, 10 and 11 have their intended effect of restoring competition in the affected markets.

[947] Apple contends that proposed order 12 is impermissibly broad and imprecise, falls outside the relief sought in the FAOA and pleaded case, is unsupported by the liability findings, seeks to restore competition inappropriately and would improperly fetter Apple’s contractual discretions.

[948] But as Epic points out, it is incorrect to say there is no basis in my findings for a restraint on discriminatory or retaliatory action against developers who use alternative distribution channels or payment methods.

[949] I have already found that Apple has foreclosed competition by enforcing the requirement to use IAP and its anti-steering rule against developers even when IAP is not suitable for the developer ([4118]). I found that Apple rejected the Spotify app when it sought to communicate with customers about subscriptions ([4121]). I found that Apple rejected apps/games which use 3rd party payments (including WePay) to bypass IAP ([4122]). I found that Apple terminated more than 2,000 developer accounts since 2017 for failing to use IAP ([4123]). Further, I found that Apple’s conduct towards Epic is a paradigm example of its enforcement of the restrictive terms: [4124], [4130] to [4132].

[950] More broadly, I made findings about Apple’s use of other mechanisms to undermine the practical effect of regulatory and judicial constraints in foreign jurisdictions. Proposed order 12 is directed to preventing that same pattern of retaliatory enforcement from being deployed against developers who take up the opportunities created by the other orders.

[951] Further, s 80 is essentially a public interest provision directed to upholding the competition norms in Pt IV. It confers a broad power to make orders that are appropriate to prevent, restrain or remedy contraventions and their effects. Opening the distribution and payments markets to effective competition in the wake of a s 46 contravention is not some free-floating policy objective. It is the direct way in which the contravention and its effects are prevented, restrained and remedied, and as such it is fully authorised by s 80.

[952] Apple remains free to enforce legitimate App Review Guidelines and to exercise its contractual discretions on other, lawful grounds. The fact that Apple’s discretions would be constrained so that they cannot be exercised for exclusionary purposes is a feature, not a vice. The liability findings show that Apple has previously used those wide discretions to enforce the IAP tie and anti-steering rule in a way that substantially lessened competition.

[953] Finally, let me put a match to yet another straw man argument of Apple. Apple suggests that it could not exercise its discretion without running the risk of being in contempt. But if Apple acts for reasons unrelated to the matters in sub-paragraphs (c) to (e), it will not infringe the proposed order. And as Epic says, Apple can readily record those reasons (if legitimate) in a way that practically protects it from any such liability.

#### The question of timing – technical evidence

[954] During the hearing on relief and as I have already indicated, I heard evidence on some technical questions from Epic’s expert, Professor Somayaji, who had given evidence before me at the trial on liability, and from a technical management witness called by Apple being Mr Friedland, who had not given evidence at the trial on liability. Let me begin with his evidence.

[955] Mr Friedland, the Senior Director of Software Engineering at Apple, was cross-examined. But it is not necessary to descend into the detail of the cross-examination. Much of his technical evidence properly so called was not challenged. Rather what was challenged concerned speculative timing questions as to how long it would take and what would be involved in technically and commercially implementing my orders, which of course at the time of his evidence were uncertain. Mr Friedland expressed views on these questions which in my view were not wholly unreasonable although they at times bordered on taking a counsel of perfection approach. I will address my judgment call on the timing questions later, of course taking into account his evidence.

[956] Mr Friedland leads the Digital Supply Chain Engineering team (DSCE) which is responsible for acquiring and managing the content for Apple’s media businesses. Within DSCE there are approximately 480 engineers structured into either line of business teams or horizontal functions; these latter functions include encoding media, rights and pricing engineering, processing images, quality engineering and the like.

[957] Within the App Store line of business within DSCE there are approximately 110 engineers. There is a dedicated App Store Connect engineering team working on the web portal and APIs for developers to manage iPhone and iPad apps. App Store Connect is the portal interface through which developers can upload, submit and manage their apps.

[958] There is an app processing engineering team that is responsible for processing what are described as binaries from developers and converting them to a final form that is deliverable on an iPhone or iPad. The description of “a binary” or its plural form is short-hand for in essence the object code of an app, which is of course in binary form, being the compiled and executable file of the software that can be run directly on the relevant device. There is a TestFlight engineering team, responsible for running a beta testing service that is agnostic to final distribution. There is an editorial engineering team responsible for how apps are featured on the Today tab on the App Store. And there is an app review engineering team responsible for building tools, workflows and automations utilised by Apple’s App Review team to detect content in apps submitted for review that violates the App Review Guidelines and other Apple policies.

[959] The DSCE team designs, engineers and deploys the workflows, tools and controls through which developers interact with Apple in the process of designing, developing, distributing, operating and updating native iOS apps. This has been described as the technical plumbing. For that purpose, the DSCE team works with dozens of peer teams across Apple including the App Store client team, various server engineering teams, the product teams, and the operating systems teams under Mr Craig Federighi, who was a witness for Apple in the liability trial before me.

#### Alternative app distribution

[960] Mr Friedland gave evidence that there are seven discrete technical processes and workflows that must be formulated, designed and implemented in order to enable alternative app distribution on an iOS device in a jurisdiction where Apple is required to allow alternative app distribution and payment including Australia. First, there is the process of developing or modifying and making available a framework comprising a large number of APIs, and other software tools to facilitate different components of alternative app distribution. Second, there is the process of creating and conferring entitlements to access the APIs that facilitate alternative app distribution. Third, there is the process of setting parameters and creating workflows for review and notarisation of apps as applicable. Fourth, there is the setting of parameters and creating workflows for installation of apps for alternative distribution and marketplace apps. Fifth, there is the setting of parameters and creating workflows for updates to alternative distribution apps. Sixth, there is the process of tracking, calculating and raising fees. Seventh, there is the process of managing the transition and roll-out period to developers and consumers. Further, underpinning each of these seven workflows is a set of legal terms, and a mechanism for tracking which marketplaces and developers have signed which versions of Apple's terms in order to unlock distribution in the particular jurisdiction. Let me identify his specific evidence on these workflows, which evidence I largely accept.

###### Developing tools, technology and services for creating and configuring an alternative distribution app: frameworks, SDKs, APIs and entitlements

[961] Developers can use the same tools and technologies including Xcode, the iOS SDK and other SDKs and APIs to build a native iOS app regardless of whether the app will be distributed through the App Store or an alternative distribution channel. In that sense, iOS native apps, in terms of their creation by developers using these tools, are distribution channel agnostic. Apple SDKs include a collection of source code and object code, libraries, APIs and header files that enable developers to build apps that can run on Apple branded products.

[962] But building and operating an alternative app marketplace, or enabling distribution of a native iOS app through such a marketplace or via direct download from a developer’s website, requires additional SDK features and frameworks and access to those features through new or modified APIs. Developers cannot create that functionality for themselves because, in order to enable such functionality, Apple must develop and/or make available private APIs that are interoperable with iOS and the many features of iOS devices, which are not required by and are therefore not available to developers who choose to distribute through the App Store.

[963] Not all features of iOS and iOS devices are automatically available to all developers. The private APIs that are necessary to facilitate alternative app distribution operate at permission levels which are too privileged for developers to access without Apple's involvement, as they permit access to the operating system of users’ devices that would allow, for example, developers to read other apps' data and personal user data.

[964] In jurisdictions where legal or regulatory requirements do not permit Apple to operate according to its standard business model for app distribution, Apple has developed additional features within iOS in order to conform to the specific requirements of each jurisdiction.

[965] In order to enable alternative forms of app distribution initially in Europe and later also in Japan, Apple was required to make changes to the iOS SDK. Apple developed a new framework, called the MarketplaceKit Framework, which was made available to developers via an update to the iOS SDK and is now part of that SDK. To use the MarketplaceKit Framework, developers must download the updated version of the iOS SDK. I should note here that a framework means a structured bundle or directory comprising shared resources, including a dynamic shared code library, image files, header files and reference documentation in a single package. An application accesses the capabilities of a framework via APIs, which are published within the framework.

[966] I have touched on the MarketplaceKit Framework earlier, which comprises a large number of APIs that enable developers to create, test, distribute, install and update an alternative app marketplace on iOS devices and facilitate web distribution of a native iOS app from a developer website to an iOS device. An alternative app marketplace is a marketplace operated by a third party developer from which apps are available for distribution once the marketplace is installed on the user’s iOS device from a developer’s website. Some of those APIs were newly developed for the purpose of facilitating alternative app distribution. Others are essentially APIs that were previously private APIs used by Apple and not previously available to developers. Creating public APIs based on the private APIs to permit developers to use them for the purpose of facilitating alternative app distribution requires extensive analysis by Apple of the relevant private API because the permission levels to call upon these private APIs affect operating system functionality and have historically been private to Apple.

[967] Apple uses a technical mechanism called “entitlements” to control access to certain APIs and, through them, to features and information in iOS and on user devices. Entitlements are granted upon the developer meeting certain criteria, and in some instances, commitments to ongoing requirements, to protect users and developers, as defined by the applicable policy and legal documentation. Once granted, the entitlement is the technical mechanism which is recognised by iOS as an indicator to either lock or unlock access to the relevant APIs. The term “entitlement” is also used more generally to describe a permission that Apple grants to certain developers that allows the developer’s apps access to specific APIs functionality and services.

[968] Apple builds end-to-end workflows across App Store Connect, the developer portal, the server APIs being Apple APIs which are run on Apple’s servers, and the client APIs being Apple APIs which are built into an SDK that is made available to a developer and run directly on a user’s device, to support the technical implementation of entitlements. Entitlements are the means Apple has deployed to control access to aspects of the MarketplaceKit Framework that enable non-standard functionality within iOS. The criteria and commitments to ongoing requirements which grant access to these entitlements have differed depending on the unique circumstances in each jurisdiction. Whilst marketplace apps are supported in both Europe and Japan, different criteria define developer eligibility to distribute marketplace apps in Europe and Japan. As to web distribution, developers have to satisfy specified criteria in Europe, but Apple does not enable web distribution in Japan.

[969] Once a developer has been granted an entitlement to access certain APIs, the entitlement is cryptographically embedded into the code signature of their apps’ binary. Those code signatures are recognised and verified by iOS and signal the permissions that are unlocked by that entitlement, that is, the APIs which the app is permitted to use or call. This enables the developer to access features in the SDK to which they would otherwise not have access. It also prompts iOS, and the device upon which an app runs, to grant that developer’s apps access to call APIs and to use features of iOS to which the app and device would otherwise not have access.

[970] There is an entitlement called the Alternative App Marketplace Entitlement that grants access to the MarketplaceKit Framework that facilitates alternative app marketplaces in Europe and Japan. Although the entitlement is the same for each jurisdiction, there are distinct commercial terms, that is, terms which a developer must meet in order to gain access to the entitlement, that govern in each jurisdiction.

[971] Different aspects of the MarketplaceKit Framework are used to implement alternative app distribution in Europe and Japan, consistent with the regulatory requirements in those jurisdictions. For example, Apple is required to facilitate alternative app marketplaces in Japan, but not web distribution. In order to facilitate alternative distribution in Australia, the MarketplaceKit Framework will need to be updated in order to provide functionality that is specific to Australia. Any such changes will depend on the detail of my final orders. Let me elaborate further.

[972] Professor Somayaji said that Apple’s MarketplaceKit framework and its underlying functionality is included in the latest version of the iOS SDK.

[973] Furthermore, a comparison of alternative app distribution in the European Union and Japan illustrates how the MarketplaceKit framework can support different features and offerings depending on jurisdiction. The table below summarizes the current availability of alternative app marketplace distribution on iOS devices in comparison to the rest of the world.

Availability of Alternative App Distribution Channels Across Jurisdictions

| Jurisdiction | Installation of Third-Party App Marketplaces | Installation of Apps from Third-Party App Marketplaces | Installation of App Marketplaces from Websites | Installation of Apps from Websites |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| European Union | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| Japan | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✕ |
| Rest of the world | ✕ | ✕ | ✕ | ✕ |

[974] Note that in the EU, app developers are allowed to offer their app for installation through their own websites, in addition to offering app installation from alternative marketplace apps. But in Japan, Apple only allows installation of apps from alternative marketplaces and does not allow installation from a developer’s website. In both Japan and the EU, alternative marketplaces can be downloaded from the marketplace developer’s website. Technically, there is no significant difference between the installation of a marketplace app from the developer’s website as is allowed in both Japan and the EU, compared to any other native app made available through a developer’s website on iOS, as both rely on the same domain-authorized web distribution mechanism designed to ensure authenticity of the original app binary.

[975] Whilst both types of app installation use the same entitlements, Apple conducts policy checks to restrict certain functionality in Japan that is available in the EU.

[976] iOS verifies device eligibility for apps from alternative distribution using on-device processing. In order to maintain eligibility, users must physically be located in the allowed jurisdiction. Further, the document indicates that iOS devices conduct additional checks to enforce the policy that users must be in the eligible country or region to install alternative app marketplaces and new apps through alternative app distribution.

[977] Apple infrastructure thus supports significant variation in alternative app distribution policies.

[978] In addition to the Alternative App Marketplace Entitlement, there are other specific entitlements in both Europe and Japan, which grant developers the ability to call different APIs in the MarketplaceKit Framework, for example, “com.apple.developer.marketplace.app-installation”. This entitlement enables an app to sell other apps as an alternative app marketplace.

[979] Given entitlements are granted at the time of creating / approving the binary code of the app, with the passage of time, legal documents can lapse, terms can change and certain vulnerabilities in the app can be identified at a later stage by Apple. Therefore, while the entitlement unlocks the APIs on the user’s device, Apple conducts a series of server checks along the way, for example at app installation time, to ensure that Apple can monitor and manage the Apple ecosystem over time.

[980] In addition to the MarketplaceKit Framework, Apple has developed other tools to facilitate alternative app distribution, for example, the App License Delivery software tool and associated App License Delivery SDK which developers can download from Apple’s developer website.

[981] The App License Delivery software tool works with FairPlay. In order for an app that has been installed on an iOS device to run, FairPlay uses cryptography to confirm that the user is presently entitled to use the app. This was an existing system used by the App Store that was extended and packaged into the App License Delivery software tool to make equivalent functionality available to developers using alternative app distribution.

[982] Developers use the App License Delivery software tool to generate digital keys, using cryptography, that indicate that the user is entitled to use an app that the user has downloaded via an alternative distribution channel. That permission or licence may be time-limited. As well as being a security and digital-rights management measure, this enables developers to use monetisation strategies such as selling time-limited access to an app, which might then be renewed like a subscription.

[983] A further example of plumbing that needs to be developed and implemented to support alternative app distribution is a mechanism for developers to identify an authorised web domain from which users can download and install a web distribution app or marketplace app. Apple provides a mechanism for the developer to add the domain to their App Store Connect account, via the App Store Connect API, ensuring that the web distribution app and marketplace app only install from the developer's chosen website. This mitigates a risk of a bad actor impersonating the developer and, for example, offering malicious apps or apps that infringe the intellectual property rights of the developer or third parties.

[984] A further example of the plumbing necessary to facilitate alternative distribution is the creation of ADPs, which are the packages that contain the actual binaries that run on each version of iOS. As I have already touched on, ADPs are electronic packages of IPA files, including an index to the IPA files, used to facilitate alternative app distribution.

[985] To the extent that Apple is able to re-use SDKs, APIs, frameworks and entitlements, which are already used to facilitate alternative distribution in Europe or Japan, the time which will be required to implement alternative app distribution in Australia will be reduced, relative to what will be required if it is necessary for Apple to develop technologies that are specific to Australia. The extent to which SDKs, APIs, frameworks and entitlements can be reused depends on the extent of the differences in the technical and business requirements between the jurisdictions. The more specific the requirements of any order that I might make, the more additional engineering is likely to be required and the more time will be required.

[986] In order to enable alternative distribution in Europe, and later Japan, it was necessary for Apple to update iOS in order for iOS to implement the newly created APIs and other technologies that are used for alternative distribution. It was necessary for users in those jurisdictions to install the newly released version of iOS on their devices in order to use alternative app distribution. It was not technically possible to provide alternative app distribution to users who did not update the version of iOS on a compatible model of the device. A further iOS update may be necessary to facilitate alternative distribution in Australia. If that is the case, alternative distribution will only be available to users once they update their device by installing an updated version of iOS. Generally, devices that are more than about eight years old will not be compatible with a new iOS update.

###### App Review for alternative app distribution

[987] Let me say something about alternative app distribution being of course a channel of distribution other than the App Store for native iOS apps on iOS devices, being directly from developers’ websites or from alternative app marketplaces.

[988] Once an app is created and configured by the developer and supplied to Apple, Apple would seek to review that app for security, privacy, and other risks and considerations against the set of guidelines Apple creates to inform the review of apps which are to be available for the target distribution in that jurisdiction.

[989] During this second stage of the process of designing and developing a bespoke business model to respond to my orders, Apple would determine the applicable guidelines, including formulating the criteria to be assessed, and the mechanism of app review, based on the legal order, and then design tools and workflows to support the same.

[990] In order to build the supporting tools and workflows, Mr Friedland’s team would consider the scope of the review based on the criteria including data to be provided by the developer, the type of review and whether the review is the same or different from other places in which alternative distribution has been enabled, that is, Europe and Japan. His team would generally not commence building, reconfiguring or adjusting tools and workflows to support the app review process until any order that I made had been analysed by other teams in Apple and the criteria and form of review settled.

[991] There are two sub-workflows when it comes to a developer submission and app review.

[992] One workflow looks at the app binary which would need to be submitted by the developer regardless of the elected distribution channel. Apple would develop tools that can "x-ray" the binary and interrogate its components. Generally, these tools are storefront agnostic, but there would need to be some technical work to enable Apple's systems to extract the relevant entitlements, cross reference what is permitted against the legal terms and policies, and present the findings to the reviewers in the course of reviewing apps. To the extent that new entitlements or new business logic combinations need to be created for Australia alone, Apple would work on extending the existing tools to match the requirements of the entitlement or business logic.

[993] The other workflow looks at the metadata. This is this second workflow where one has a fork in the process if the developer indicates the app is exclusively for alternative distribution as opposed to App Store distribution. There are fewer requirements for exclusively alternative distribution apps since some of the metadata is for App Store display. Any app that is reviewed for App Store distribution is also eligible for alternative distribution. The degree to which the workflow built for Japan and/or Europe could be re-deployed in Australia will depend on the degree of similarity in requirements between those jurisdictions and what is ultimately ordered in Australia, and secondary to that, Apple's commercial decisions on the approach to app review for alternative distribution in Australia, which impacts the scope of metadata collected and reviewed. Examples of metadata that might be collected in a different format for Australia, compared to other jurisdictions, would be the age rating regional regulatory identifiers, or disclosures. For this workflow, there may need to be technical adjustments in the App Store Connect experience and as part of the review process. It is also necessary to build a loop in the workflow to collect and assess those additional metadata fields.

[994] To the extent the app review policies for alternative distribution are the same between Australia and Europe or Japan, then Apple would seek to reuse as much of the existing solution as possible. However, where there are deviations then there would need to be a fork somewhere in the workflows to collect and assess what is necessary for Australia.

[995] Any app review process implemented in Australia will have to reflect the set of App Review Guidelines to be enforced there. If full app review against all of these guidelines which apply to the App Store were to be undertaken, it would be necessary to build a bespoke technical solution to facilitate this type of review for alternative distribution of apps through a third party marketplace or by direct downloading from a developer website, in Australia.

[996] If notarisation were to be deployed, it would be necessary to build into the workflow a code-signing mechanism at the end of the review process. This would create and attach the digital certificate signalling that the app has been through the requisite review channels, and is the mechanism which ensures at the time of installation that the binary has not been changed since Apple’s review.

[997] The term “notarisation” for iOS apps under the Europe and Japan solution describes a different process to the app notarisation process which occurs for macOS apps. For macOS apps, notarisation entails a specific flow with an automated scanning process for malicious content and malware before certification. For iOS apps in Europe and Japan where the developer elects to distribute their apps solely through alternative distribution channels, those apps go through a baseline app review, with automated and human review steps checking for accuracy, functionality, safety, security and privacy. Where the developer of iOS apps in Europe and Japan elects both App Store distribution and alternative distribution, there is no baseline app review as those apps are subject to Apple's app review process for App Store distribution, such that upon meeting the App Review Guidelines, the apps are also certified for alternative distribution and are not subject to any further or separate review. Accordingly, the tools are different for macOS than they are for alternative iOS app distribution to iOS devices.

###### Status Based Checks for App Developers

[998] Apple has already built mechanisms in the EU that use characteristics of the developer to implement differentiated user experiences with the developer’s app.

[999] In March 2024, Apple introduced “trader status,” a categorisation associated with an app developer that allows the developer’s app to be distributed in particular ways within certain geographic regions. Following the introduction of this attribute, Apple required all app developers to declare a trader status, but the category is only used to determine how apps are distributed within the EU. When an app developer is not considered a trader in the EU for a particular app, app users associated with the EU App Store receive a notification that certain consumer protection laws do not apply. If an app developer is considered a trader for an app distributed within the EU, certain contact information is published about that app developer.

[1000] When Apple first implemented trader status, it prevented app updates for all apps distributed within the EU for which developers had not submitted their trader status. Apple later removed all apps from the EU App Store for which trader status was not provided and verified. Apple did not remove those apps from other App Stores. So, Apple has the capability to introduce a new developer-based policy, verify that developers meet the requirements of that policy, and allow users to access the associated app based on the policy relevant to both the developer and the user.

[1001] Whilst trader status implemented in the EU is an example that may require Apple developers to provide additional information, Apple also collects certain developer attributes and information based on developer location. When a developer enrols in the Developer Program to build iOS apps, Apple collects developer information, including name, age, contact information, location information and preferred language. Based on the user’s App Store region, Apple may display some of the developer’s information, including contact information and business information. For example, app developers based in South Korea are required to provide additional information about their identity, including email address, business registration number, and, for companies, company name and phone number, that is displayed on app product pages.

[1002] The Apple Developer Enterprise Program also offers an example of developer-based policies that allow users to download apps irrespective of the user’s primary App Store based on developer attributes. The Developer Enterprise Program illustrates the presence of technical infrastructure that would allow for alternative app distribution without significant technical changes to iOS. The Developer Enterprise Program appears to be agnostic to the user’s primary App Store and instead requires valid enterprise certificates. The use of this certificate-based infrastructure allows Apple to alter app behaviour to remotely disable apps if the developer’s status changes, that is, if the developer is no longer a member of the Developer Enterprise Program.

[1003] So, Apple has mechanisms within its infrastructure to allow apps to function differently based upon geographic and other characteristics of their developers.

###### Consolidating IPAs and ADPs for distribution

[1004] Once an app passes the review mechanism which Apple elects to deploy, and provided that the developer meets any lawful criteria Apple chooses to impose, Apple’s processes prepare a deliverable asset for distribution of that app to iOS devices.

[1005] All applications must be signed before they can be installed and executed on a device, ensuring only Apple approved apps run on iOS devices. In order to sign an application, developers need to attach a provisioning profile to their app. Provisioning profiles are required for all developers and are issued by Apple, based on the criterion provided by developers for distributing their apps. These provisioning profiles also include entitlements. The embedded entitlements are validated by iOS as part of the app’s execution process.

[1006] iOS enforces app security through a series of validation checks. These include verifying the app’s code signature, which confirms the provisioning profile, ensures the developer is actively registered with Apple, and validates that the app’s entitlements are authorised for proper functionality. If these validation checks are not satisfied, iOS prevents the application from being installed or executed.

[1007] Access to special app capabilities, such as use of alternative payment sources, is present within the iOS SDK, but these capabilities are not automatically granted to developers. Instead, access is determined by Apple’s policy decisions, which are implemented through entitlements granted or withheld via its developer infrastructure. Once approved, these entitlements are incorporated into a developer’s provisioning profile. In this way, provisioning profiles operate as a mechanism through which Apple can selectively enable access to platform capabilities without modifying the underlying frameworks themselves. Moreover, the App Store Connect develops multiple versions of an app submitted for review by the developer, such that each version of the app is unique to different iOS versions and devices. As a result, two instances of the same app, built from the same binary using the same SDK, can exhibit different functionality depending solely on the provisioning profiles, iOS version, or device they are tied to.

[1008] Apple’s systems take the binary, that is, what the developer has developed in Xcode, and prepare a set of IPA files, which comprises everything which is needed for the device to install and run the app. Apple's processes must generate multiple versions of each app. Each IPA corresponds to a specific operating system and device. For example, the IPA generated for iOS 17 for an iPhone 15 Pro is different to that generated for iPadOS 17 iPad Mini, which is different in turn to that generated for iOS 26 for an iPhone 17, and so on. Apple also creates smaller delta packages which contain only the differences from the previous version, for streamlined updates. Creating the IPAs involves running proprietary tools to generate the binaries and digitally signing them.

[1009] IPA files are generated for apps distributed via the App Store. In order to allow alternative distribution, Apple created ADPs which I have already described. It is only in the form of an ADP that apps for alternative distribution can be downloaded onto a user’s iOS device.

[1010] Once Apple has created the ADP for an app, developers can download the ADP to their own servers, from App Store Connect. An API can also be used to automate this process. The developer then uses the ADP to distribute its app, whether via an alternative marketplace app or web distribution. Apple also built a mechanism to notify the marketplace that the ADP is available, if the app developer chooses to help streamline the process.

[1011] The iOS device and Apple’s systems take the ADP as an input to initiate the installation of an alternatively distributed app. All files within an IPA are cryptographically signed so that Apple’s systems can verify their authenticity and that they have not been altered.

[1012] In addition to provisioning and entitlement-based authorization, Apple provides mechanisms that allow developers to define conditions under which an application may be installed or executed on a device. For example, developers can specify installation requirements that check attributes such as the user’s age rating eligibility, minimum supported operating system version, and required device capabilities. These conditions are evaluated prior to installation and execution and can prevent an app from being installed or used if the specified criteria are not met. This demonstrates that application availability and functionality can be conditioned on defined criteria through system-level configuration.

[1013] These system-level controls extend beyond static installation requirements to include user-specific authorisation mechanisms that determine whether and how an app may execute at runtime. On iOS, user-specific authorisation is enforced through FairPlay via Apple’s App License Delivery mechanism. FairPlay uses cryptography to confirm that a user is entitled to run an app, and the app will only execute on the device if that entitlement is verified.
