The App License Delivery SDK extends this system to apps distributed outside the App Store by enabling developers to generate digital keys that represent a user’s authorisation to use an app obtained through an alternative marketplace or website. Apple’s App License Delivery SDK implements this model by enabling developers to operate licence servers that generate per-download licences that the operating system retrieves at install time. These licenses are verified by FairPlay at runtime and may be time limited, expiring after a defined period unless renewed, and can also be revoked dynamically.

[1014] Consistent with this model, during app installation a user-specific license is issued that authorizes execution of the app on a particular device. This license includes cryptographic keys used by the operating system to validate and enable the app’s execution, and is bound to the user’s account and device context, ensuring that only authorized users can run the app. In this way, Apple enforces user-specific and device-specific access and functionality through system-level verification, such that app behaviour is determined dynamically based on user-specific authorisations.

[1015] These same mechanisms would be deployed to support alternative distribution in Australia.

###### Installation of alternative distribution apps

[1016] When a user is browsing an alternative app marketplace or a developer’s website and viewing the information presented on a developer’s website or marketplace for a particular app, the user’s device is interacting with the app developer’s server, the marketplace, and, where applicable, the marketplace’s server. Apple’s servers are not involved.

[1017] When a marketplace app or a developer's website would like to initiate an app installation, Apple’s primary involvement is to assess whether the installation on the iOS device is safe for the user and that the user understands what they are installing, and to facilitate the install itself. This is the final opportunity for Apple to implement measures to protect both the user and the developer before the app is downloaded onto the user’s device.

[1018] When a user initiates an installation of an alternative distribution app from a website, the user's click calls on a set of APIs to start the installation process. In doing so, the browser, developer's server and devices’ operating system, iOS, communicate to pass the consent to install the relevant ADP file, and the cryptographic keys exchanged between the App License Delivery software tool and the FairPlay system.

[1019] If the app that is to be installed is a marketplace, its binary will include information that tells iOS that the app is a marketplace and that the developer has access to the applicable entitlement, such as the Alternative App Marketplace Entitlement for Europe and/or Japan. Apps that contain the necessary “entitlement” code are able to call the APIs that are necessary for a marketplace to function on an iOS device.

###### Installation or disclosure sheets

[1020] I have discussed this topic earlier and need say nothing more about it here.

###### Apple checks before installation

[1021] Apple would also implement a series of checks during the installation process, to ensure the authenticity of the app and that the developer's intent to distribute via the alternative channel is still in effect. The function and purpose of these checks is to ensure that the app's binary is what was reviewed by Apple, has not been altered since it was reviewed by Apple, and is not unauthorised by the developer or third party marketplace. These checks guard against the risk that an alternative app marketplace or website distributor replaced the app binary reviewed by Apple with a different, unreviewed binary that could contain malicious code, and that the app was not subsequently found to have malware after the ADP was generated and signed.

[1022] At the time the user’s device initiates the app installation, Apple’s servers check whether the app intended to be installed on an iOS device is still eligible for distribution. Apple checks whether the app has been taken down by the developer since the developer received the ADP, whether the developer remains a current member of the Developer Program, and for web distribution, that the website is owned by the developer initiating the install to prevent unauthorised distribution. This communication involves an interaction between the set of iOS APIs, Apple’s servers and the browser app to verify all these checks. For marketplace app installation, additional checks are made to verify the marketplace is in good standing and the relationship between the marketplace and developer is intact so that the developer can revoke a relationship and be guaranteed that Apple will respect that wish and stop distribution.

[1023] Where an app does not pass these checks, prior to installation of the app by the user, Apple is able to block installation on the user's device, consistent with Apple's purpose of protecting the user from harmful apps and protecting the developer from unauthorised distribution. Where the app has already been installed on an iOS device from a developers’ website or a marketplace app, it is not clear as to the means by which Apple could remove the app.

[1024] Finally, in so far as the technical solution to facilitate the installation of alternative apps in Australia is concerned, and even assuming there are no new requirements in Australia when compared to other jurisdictions where Apple has been required to facilitate alternative distribution, Apple would still need time to undertake technical work to extend the developer/marketplace onboarding flows to cover Australian-based alternative distribution and ensure the correct legal arrangements are in place for that territory. Further, it would need to extend the on-device eligibility for users in Australia with an Australian Apple Account to enable web and marketplace app installation, which would require an iOS update. Further, it would need to extend the mechanisms of calculating and billing for the selected business model for the Australian dollar and any local tax implications.

###### Updates for alternative distribution apps

[1025] In order to support alternative app distribution within iOS, it is also necessary to make provision for apps distributed other than via the App Store to be updated periodically.

[1026] There are two cases applicable to app updates, depending on whether the app is downloaded from a marketplace or, in Europe, directly via web distribution; web distribution of apps is not enabled in Japan.

[1027] The App Store’s update daemon, being a program that runs in the background without direct user control, runs periodically and updates apps automatically when certain conditions are met, that is, when an iOS device is internet–connected usually via Wi-Fi, connected to power and automatic updates have been enabled by the user. When those conditions are met, the daemon automatically downloads and installs any updates for apps on the device without the need for user involvement. By this means the user always has the most up to date version of the app made available by the developer.

[1028] In the case of apps distributed by alternative app marketplaces in Europe and Japan, Apple designed and made available to marketplace operators an equivalent but bespoke functionality to facilitate updates. This functionality, which is called MarketplaceAppExtension (formerly MarketplaceExtension), comprises a new set of APIs within the MarketplaceKit Framework. These APIs are made available to developers of apps for distribution via alternative app marketplaces in Europe and Japan through the alternative app marketplace entitlements, which, upon the developer meeting the relevant criteria, can be referenced in the code of the app’s binary. This functionality, where enabled by the marketplace operator, is invoked periodically by iOS, to allow the marketplace app to call the developer's server to check whether a newer version of the previously-installed app is available and then, where an update is available, to initiate that update to the user’s device. The MarketplaceAppExtension functionality can be used by developers to build their own bespoke update mechanisms for their marketplaces. This process, in contrast to the App Store daemon process, does not involve or require the marketplace app to make a call upon Apple's servers, nonetheless, when the update is triggered by the marketplace, Apple servers would be consulted, like they would be for an initial installation, to conduct various checks, for example, that the updated binary is not marked with malware.

[1029] In this way, in making these tools available, Apple gives marketplace developers greater control over updates of apps, including as to the terms on which it distributes updates from its marketplace.

[1030] In the case of direct web distribution, there is no marketplace app on the iOS device to manage the update. Generally, once a day iOS will find all web distributed apps. The iOS device will then check for a manifest file on each website to listen for app updates. The iOS device creates a list of apps and versions that can be installed from that domain. If the developer provides the required metadata for available updates, Apple actions the updates.

[1031] Given that my orders are to be confined to users in Australia, the tools described above would form the basis for an automatic update solution for marketplace apps made available in Australia, and Apple would adopt a similar process in respect of updates for direct web distribution.

###### Tracking, calculating and raising fees for alternative distribution

[1032] Because the terms of the business model in which alternative app distribution is permitted can only be selected once the specifics of my orders are known, the technical design and development of the platform infrastructure to support that new business model cannot be fully determined. For example, the rights and pricing engineering for alternative distribution and alternative payment solutions can only be designed and implemented once the approach to charging developers and alternative app marketplace operators is resolved.

[1033] Whatever commercial model is adopted, Apple’s commercial teams would need to develop a set of eligibility criteria and contractual terms. Mr Friedland’s team would then be responsible for building any technical workflows, to assess the chosen eligibility criteria and to record where the relevant eligibility criteria have been met.

[1034] The following considerations arise in determining the appropriate solution in Australia.

[1035] At install time, the device itself uses location services and other criteria, such as the Apple Account associated with the device, to determine whether the device is eligible for alternative distribution. If so, the device is permitted to attempt installation from the alternative app marketplace or directly from the developer's website. It is likely that Apple would use an analogous system for Australia.

[1036] After an installation has occurred via APIs on the device, the device makes a call to Apple servers to report that the installation has happened, the territory and what app was installed. Analytics tools process that report into aggregate account data including the storefront, number of unique installs, device and time period.

[1037] The system calculates whether the thresholds for the fee or commission have been met, which is turned into a balance. Those systems and the workflows to be used in enabling the calculation of the relevant fee or commission will be dependent on the monetisation approach adopted in Australia. There are likely to be modifications to the systems and workflows for calculations.

[1038] Finally, billing the developer the requisite fee and collecting the balance involves a further technical workflow across a number of systems, including handling of new currency and taxation considerations.

[1039] It is likely that Apple would re-use parts of the existing technical solution where the requirements match or are similar. So, Apple could re-use the workflow for calculating and sending a bill to the developer if the orders permit that form of monetisation by Apple. What else Apple is able to re-use and what it will be required to design afresh will depend on my orders.

###### Preparations for testing, roll-out and transition phase

[1040] Once the engineering solution has been established, and prior to the launch of the new model, there is a period of time required to test and qualify the new workflows and technology. Multiple teams within Apple would be involved in preparing the public facing documentation which describes the changes to the model and guided steps to implementation. Further, it would be necessary to staff support teams who must be trained with the expertise to address developer and user queries on the solution for a specific jurisdiction.

[1041] Changes to the technical model for app distribution and in-app digital transactions would require an iOS update. Apple would require time to implement that iOS update. Each periodic iOS update ordinarily comprises thousands of software bundles.

[1042] There are rigorous testing standards for OS updates, which are conducted internally by Apple, as well as part of what Apple refers to as a “seed release”, which is a pre-release phase or beta testing phase. During the pre-release phase, Apple releases the update to developers who wish to participate and who download the pre-release iOS update in order to test the new functionalities and APIs and provide feedback for Apple’s consideration of any modifications or further engineering builds which are required. The pre-release update is also then released for users who would like to beta test, and to cellphone carriers who are responsible for testing that the iPhone/iPad continues to operate with their network and approving the update for release to users. Apple’s release “cadence” for the final versions of an update to the OS has generally been approximately 3 to 4 months. The pre-release phase is generally undertaken during that window.

#### Alternative in–app payment solutions and steering

[1043] In relation to other jurisdictions like South Korea, the Netherlands, Europe, Japan and the United States, if Apple were ordered to facilitate alternative payment solutions in an app other than IAP, whereby a user can complete a transaction for digital goods and services within an app using an alternative payment provider (a payment service provider (PSP)) other than Apple, or steering users within an iOS app to alternative out-of-app payment methods, there are a number of technical processes which Apple would need to consider.

[1044] To enable in-app purchases of digital content and services that use Apple’s IAP, Apple makes available to developers a framework called StoreKit. StoreKit includes the ability to call APIs and look up a developer's product catalogue, which is reviewed and hosted by Apple, and to initiate and verify a transaction. When a developer opts in for alternative payments, then, to the extent that the developer and the user uses alternative payment solutions, that catalogue is no longer available. Apple effectively steps out of the process, leaving the developer to call their own backend server to determine what they wish to offer and the price of in-app purchases.

[1045] As I have touched on earlier, for alternative payment solutions other than IAP, Apple has created a set of APIs called External Purchase APIs, within the StoreKit framework, that can display disclosure sheets to the user, for transactions made through apps distributed via the App Store, and facilitate reporting of transactions for the purposes of Apple collecting its commission on transactions made through apps distributed via the App Store and alternative distribution channels.

[1046] This is different from the functionality offered by Apple’s IAP functionality, where Apple handles the end-to-end payment processing, including, for example, management of the in-app content and pricing schemes, purchase flows with many supported payment types, customer service and refunds, and Apple’s fraud monitoring. Apple does not process the third-party payments, and for apps distributed via alternative distribution channels, Apple’s IAP does not function for in-app transactions of digital content, for example, calls to StoreKit would not function where the app is installed outside of the App Store.

[1047] There are entitlements for facilitating alternative payment solutions, both for payments in the app and linking out. The concept of linking out means providing a hyperlink for users from within an iOS app to out–of–app payment methods for accepting and processing payments for purchases of digital content for use in–app.

[1048] In both the Netherlands and South Korea, the entitlements are called StoreKit External Purchase Entitlement granting developers the ability to implement third party payment systems and enabling such systems within an app, and StoreKit External Purchase Link Entitlement, enabling apps to include links that direct users to make and complete an external purchase for in–app digital goods. Although Apple has adopted the same name for these entitlements across multiple jurisdictions where Apple has been required to facilitate alternative payment solutions and linking out, the components of those entitlements, for example the eligibility criteria and the permissions to which they grant access, differ by jurisdiction and there is a storefront list inside the binary to indicate which storefronts the alternative payment option is enabled for. To be granted either of those entitlements, the developer must provide certain app information, payment processing information and the developer’s website URL for customer support and for linking out.

[1049] These entitlements are part of the StoreKit framework and, like the MarketplaceKit framework, is already included in the iOS SDK. While this functionality is present on iOS devices, Apple restricts access to it using entitlements. In this way, the StoreKit External Purchase Entitlement and the StoreKit External Purchase Link Entitlement operate as permissions enabling access to “private APIs” that allow developers to integrate this functionality within their apps.

[1050] The specific requirements governing the alternative purchase entitlements and their implementations within the StoreKit framework vary across jurisdictions. The variations in current implementation and available capabilities across jurisdictions for alternative payment mechanisms on iOS are illustrated in the table below.

Availability of Alternative Payment Mechanisms Across Jurisdictions

| Jurisdiction | Implement Alternative Payment Solutions | Implement Web Links for Processing & Promoting Payments | Offer IAP Along with Alternative Payment Methods | Requirement to Present Disclosure Sheets |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| European Union | ✓ | ✓ | ✕ | ✓ |
| Japan | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| United States | ✕ | ✓ | ✓ | ✕ |
| Netherlands | ✓ | ✓ (developer’s website only) | ✕ | ✓ |
| South Korea | ✓ | Not specified | ✕ | ✓ |
| Rest of the world | ✕ | ✕ | ✕ | ✕ |

[1051] These differences in functionality are largely driven by policy decisions implemented by Apple in response to regulations in various jurisdictions, rather than technical constraints. For example, in the United States, developers may offer alternative payment links and steering without requiring the StoreKit External Purchase Entitlement or StoreKit External Purchase Link Entitlement. Requiring the entitlements to access this functionality is a policy decision by Apple, and the implementation in the US highlights that the StoreKit framework can support alternative payment functionality without additional technical gating.

[1052] As an example, consider the workflow for facilitating external payment links and external purchases on iOS. For external purchases, Apple’s developer documentation outlines the “canPresent” property, which implements certain policy decisions from Apple. Specifically, developers are required to check the “canPresent” property before allowing external purchases. This check in turn uses data collected by Apple to verify that the App Store “storefront” is eligible to offer external purchases, the person making the transaction is eligible and the developer’s app configures the entitlement and country codes where alternative payment methods are allowed.

[1053] Apple’s infrastructure thus currently supports significant geographic variation in iOS in-app payments.

[1054] Similar entitlements would need to be developed for Australia, with the eligibility criteria, scope of information sought from the developer and access granted by means of the entitlement, determined in accordance with the legal order.

[1055] If a developer has a StoreKit External Purchase Entitlement or a StoreKit External Purchase Link Entitlement, the name of the entitlement is cryptographically embedded into the code signature of their app’s binary, which is then recognised by iOS and signals the permissions that are unlocked by those entitlements. The entitlements provide access to the External Purchase APIs which are necessary to facilitate external purchases within an app other than IAP and to offer external purchase links because those APIs, when called upon, do the following.

[1056] First, they verify for the running app whether the user is eligible.

[1057] Second, they present appropriate disclosures to the user, for transactions made through apps distributed via the App Store. The form of the disclosure sheets varies across jurisdictions, but they explain that purchases are made through a source other than Apple, for acceptance by the user. As with disclosure sheets for alternative app distribution, they are a means of educating users of a change in the standard process, that is, IAP so that the user is provided with the opportunity to assess their risk appetite in proceeding with providing their payment details to sources outside of Apple.

[1058] Third, they report to Apple external purchase tokens, which are unique strings that are generated by Apple and received by a developer’s app or website where a customer elects to view a developer’s external purchase offerings. This enables Apple to keep track of external purchase attempts but not the completed transaction, which can then be used to verify reporting of completed transactions by the developer to Apple, for Apple to then calculate the applicable commission or fee and any taxes Apple is obligated to pay.

[1059] Without access to these External Purchase APIs, developers cannot enable this functionality in their app in compliance with Apple's terms and conditions.

[1060] The solutions across the Netherlands, South Korea, Europe, Japan and the United States utilise APIs and entitlements that have slightly different behaviours and disclosure sheets depending on the requirements in each jurisdiction.

[1061] As is the case with the technical solution for facilitating alternative distribution in Australia, if Apple were required to facilitate alternative payment solutions or steering, depending on the legal requirements, and the commercial business model constructed by Apple within the bounds of that order, cross-functional teams would develop a bespoke solution that matched the requirements. That solution would include developing, inter–alia, entitlements based on certain eligibility criteria, geo-based unlocking of the desired functionality, functionality which allows tracking, reporting and calculation of commissions for transactions outside of the App Store and through payment solutions other than IAP, and designing and building disclosure sheets. Apple would seek to use prior work from other jurisdictions, but bespoke engineering for Australia is likely to be required.

[1062] Mr Friedland would expect the technical effort will be less resource intensive than for South Korea, the Netherlands and Europe, since his team would build on the prior work for those jurisdictions where possible. Given the steps described above for a technical engineering solution that involves alternative payment solutions and linking out in Australia, that is, limited to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account, and taking into account the work to be undertaken by cross-functional teams and the testing / roll-out phase of an operating system update, it is estimated that it would take several months to develop and prepare for commercial release.

#### General considerations and conclusions on timing

[1063] In Professor Somayaji’s opinion, the changes required to enable alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions would likely require limited technical effort. He would expect less than six months. In his view much of the infrastructure required to implement Epic’s proposed relief has been developed in the form of frameworks such as StoreKit and MarketplaceKit, and most if not all of the further implementation effort required for Epic’s proposed relief should involve changes to automatically enforced geographic policies, not changes to code. As a result, such changes should not require substantial technical effort.

[1064] Professor Somayaji said that whilst specific rules regarding alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions can be implemented by changing the source code of Apple’s iOS operating system and its App Store infrastructure systems, that is not how deployment-specific constraints are typically addressed in software systems. Instead, standard practice is to implement policy engines. Thinking of software infrastructure as plumbing, policy engines would be the valves that enforce certain policies based on their settings. When plumbing is placed into a structure, it is built to accommodate likely changes, with valves and other controls put into place to allow water flow to be regulated or diverted as necessary. Similarly, a policy engine allows software to adapt to changes in policy without requiring code changes. The engine is given a new policy in the form of changes to the underlying settings and the functionality of the system changes.

[1065] Professor Somayaji said that in this system the mechanism or code by which certain functionality is facilitated does not require changing. Specifically, mechanism and policy are separated. Source code is implemented so that it could enforce a variety of policies rather than assuming a single policy will always be used. Thus, when policy changes, the same code can be used under a different policy setting. In general, a separation between mechanism and policy improves software longevity because mechanism or code changes are more costly and more prone to errors than policy changes. For this reason, the separation of mechanism and policy and the subsequent use of policy engines has long been a design principle of operating systems.

[1066] Professor Somayaji gave the example of a basic user account system. If the system only has five user accounts, an administrator with appropriate access could, in principle, add the usernames and passwords for the five users directly to the operating system’s code. In this example, the operating system would recognize those five users and allow them to access the system. However, to add a sixth user or to remove a previously authorised user, the entire operating system would have to be rebuilt and re-installed after changing its code. In contrast, consider the same system with a policy engine. When deciding whether a user is authorised, the policy engine simply queries the relevant database to verify if the user is able to access the system or not. The contents of the database can be changed at any time without requiring any code changes.

[1067] Now it was said by Mr Friedland that developing entitlements that are specific to Australia requires significant technical work. But Professor Somayaji gave the following response.

[1068] In the context of iOS, entitlements are deployed by Apple to control access to aspects of relevant frameworks such as MarketplaceKit and StoreKit. In this way, entitlements are access control mechanisms that gatekeep access to functionality that only some apps are allowed to have. Adding a new entitlement or changing how an existing one works is thus a modification to the app-level access control mechanisms of iOS. In other words, new entitlements are a policy change that control access to already existing functionality.

[1069] If one assumes that the iOS entitlement system is a reasonably well-designed access control mechanism that was built with the expectation that entitlements would change over time, then basic changes to iOS entitlements, on their own, would require modest levels of engineering effort, for example, changing a small number of lines in a handful of files. The potential complexity of adding or modifying entitlements would instead lie in the implementation of mechanisms that facilitate actual functionality that is to be enabled, rather than the entitlement itself.

[1070] Apple has already implemented the key functionality behind the potential new entitlements that may be required in response to my orders. To give some apps special privileges would thus primarily entail Apple’s addition or modification of policies. These new or modified policies will gatekeep certain functionality and restrict that functionality to users with an Australian Apple Account.

[1071] Further, because much of this existing functionality is only available in certain jurisdictions, Apple has likely already created policy engines throughout their systems that could be used to enable alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions in response to my orders. Such policy engines are typically needed because otherwise legislative, administrative or other local changes in requirements anywhere in the world would require software updates across the iOS ecosystem, which would be impractical. Instead, policy engines would turn most such changes into updates to policy databases, which engineers can change with minimal effort.

[1072] Apple already manages significant variation in app distribution policies across jurisdictions. Apple handles significant geographic variation by operating the App Store in 175 jurisdictions. The App Store has over 40 languages and supports over 40 different currencies, each with unique tax regulations which require adjustments in pricing, and geo-specific local laws and regulations as regards distribution and transactions for digital goods and service. In turn, iOS devices communicate with the App Store to identify the apps available for distribution. For instance, Apple’s devices would not permit certain functionality based on where the user is located and the billing account details of the user. Further, iOS verifies device eligibility for apps from alternative distribution using on-device processing. In order to maintain eligibility, users must physically be located in the allowed jurisdiction. So, there are systems on iOS that already identify where the user is located and consequently which apps are available to the user based on rules across jurisdictions.

[1073] Another example of policy engines are the systems that ensure only licensed software can be installed on an iPhone. Apple uses an app installation process in which only custom app binaries, unique to each individual device, are permitted to be installed on that device. Apps must be given a user-specific license so the operating system knows the app is authorized for the user, and apps are “thinned” so only the media assets that are required for that specific device are included with each app. This licensing mechanism means that Apple already chooses which apps are authorized on a user’s device, and that authorization is determined by policies enforced collectively by policy engines on iOS devices and Apple servers.

[1074] There are various policy variations and entitlements that Apple has previously implemented, specifically with respect to frameworks required to enable alternative app distribution channels and alternative payment solutions.

[1075] Apple has implemented differentiated policies that result in varied functionality available to developers and users across multiple jurisdictions. Such differentiated policies also indicate that Apple has built parameters that can be tweaked within a reasonable timeframe to implement solutions in response to the relief ordered by me, using the same underlying technical components such as StoreKit and MarketplaceKit.

[1076] Further, according to Professor Somayaji, the introduction of specific policies or updates required for different policy engines would not be extensive across different teams and ultimately would not require a substantial amount of technical effort.

[1077] As part of enabling alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions on iOS devices, some changes would need to be introduced across the following four core areas, being developer onboarding processes, software development tools such as Xcode, automated app review systems and on–device implementations. Apple has already conducted extensive work with respect to solutions developed across other jurisdictions.

[1078] In order to allow alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions on iOS devices, Apple would likely set some criterion based on which developers are allowed to receive the requisite permissions and entitlements. Apple may require certain terms which a developer must meet in order to gain access to the requisite entitlement. But the complexity of enabling alternative app distribution or alternative payment solutions does not arise from policy implementation or eligibility criteria design but instead arises in the development of the mechanisms that facilitate these functions on iOS devices. Such mechanisms have already been deployed by Apple in other jurisdictions. So, changes to the criterion and specific workflows that allow developers to participate in alternative app distribution or offer alternative payment solutions would require minimal changes to include additional policies or change existing policies, which would then be enforced by the requisite policy engines.

[1079] After the developer has successfully enrolled and is determined by Apple to be eligible to participate in alternative app distribution or offer payment solutions other than IAP, the developers would need to be able to build these capabilities into their app. I have referred to Apple’s Xcode earlier in my reasons. Some changes to Xcode may be required before developers are able to integrate this functionality into their apps. Apple has already deployed versions of Xcode that allow developers to add the requisite entitlements within their apps. These updates would take minimal technical effort to implement in Australia.

[1080] Further, there would need to be some technical work for the app review system implemented as part of my orders in Australia. But given that notarization has already been implemented by Apple in other jurisdictions as part of the app review process for apps distributed through alternative distribution channels, the implementation of app review processes in compliance with my orders would not require a significant amount of technical effort. A notarized direct download model will not lead to a deterioration of security on iOS, assuming app sandboxing remains the same on iOS devices.

[1081] Finally, for iOS devices to recognise the entitlements introduced in response to my order to facilitate alternative app distribution and payment solutions other than IAP, Apple would likely need to introduce some on-device changes. Such on-device changes typically require an operating system update. Operating system updates require careful evaluation and testing as flawed updates could affect the functionality of billions of devices. Because of the risks associated with changing core operating system software, such changes would take more technical effort than the potential updates that would be required across other components. However, since the core functionalities required to implement Epic’s proposed remedies already exist in iOS, the on-device code changes should be minimal. Apple has already implemented alternative app distribution and alternative payment solutions on iOS in other jurisdictions. As a result, the requisite technical work would involve updating the entitlements that gatekeep the relevant functionality.

[1082] So, including the approximate three to four months stated by Mr Friedland for testing iOS updates, Professor Somayaji expects the total technical effort required for implementing solutions according to my orders in Australia to be less than six months.

[1083] Now Apple seeks a period of time to comply with whatever injunctive relief that I grant. And Apple says that it would require up to six months if the relief were limited to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account.

[1084] Professor Somayaji’s opinion is that Epic’s proposed orders, particularly if geographically limited, do not require significant engineering work and principally involve policy changes that can be developed and deployed by Apple in less than six months.

[1085] Now as I have indicated, Mr Friedland’s evidence explains why about six months will be required to implement alternative distribution and alternative payments in Australia. He describes seven discrete technical processes and workflows that must be formulated, designed and implemented in order to enable alternative app distribution in Australia.

[1086] Further, I accept that this will not be an exercise simply in adjusting valves in existing plumbing or simply making policy changes. As Mr Friedland’s evidence makes clear, the exercise required of Apple in order to implement my orders will involve both engineering changes and the formulation and implementation of new policies.

[1087] Further, before Apple knows what orders it must comply with, it cannot design or implement the engineering and policy changes required to enable it to conform to those orders. Whether and to what extent Apple will be able to redeploy existing tools, such as the mechanism it has designed to facilitate updates of apps downloaded by alternative distribution, will depend inter–alia on whether Apple’s deploying those existing mechanisms would comply with my orders.

[1088] Further, the technical solution for facilitating alternative payments or steering would depend on the requirements of my orders and the business model adopted within the bounds of the orders. It will likely involve bespoke engineering for Australia.

[1089] Based on those matters, Mr Friedland estimates that it will take some months to engineer and test a technical solution to provide alternative app distribution and alternative payments to iOS devices associated with an Australian Apple Account, and prepare it for commercial release, once the particular requirements of my orders are known. Particularly given the likely need for an iOS update, and the need to ensure that changes appropriately preserve security to the extent possible, Apple says that its proposal that it be given six months to implement its proposed injunctive relief is reasonable and appropriate.

###### The start date

[1090] In all the circumstances and given that I have limited the operation of the orders to the Australian App Store, it is appropriate that I fix a start date of some four months away being 1 March 2027. That should be sufficient for Apple working efficiently to deal with the necessary technical and commercial issues involved.

[1091] But I will provide in my orders for an additional two months flexibility if Apple can justify this as being needed on the evidence. I will provide in my orders that Apple can apply no later than 15 February 2027 but no earlier than 1 February 2027 for a further two months period which, if justified on the evidence then filed, may result in the start date being postponed until 3 May 2027.

#### Duration of injunctive relief

[1092] Apple says that any injunctions ordered should be in effect for a period of five years from the date by which compliance is required. It says that the facts that give rise to my orders are not a permanent state of affairs. It says that the supply of services of iOS app distribution and iOS in-app payment solutions occur in dynamic markets that will evolve with technological innovation, eventually beyond recognition.

[1093] Given the nature of these high–tech markets, Apple says that five years is an appropriate time period to ensure that the contraventions are remedied. Further, it says that such a fixed time frame will ensure that my orders do not endure when they are no longer justified or appropriate to prevailing technologies and market conditions.

[1094] Now in my view, to make orders that are only to operate for a fixed time is inapposite.

[1095] First, any such fixed term is arbitrary. Adopting Apple’s logic, why not three years? Or why not 10 years? I am not able to satisfactorily ascertain what such a fixed term should be on the current evidence.

[1096] Second, I do not need to set a fixed term given s 80(3) which provides:

> The Court may rescind or vary an injunction granted under subsection (1) or (2).

[1097] If, in the known world, market changes or technological advances have taken place which justify a rescission or variation of the orders that I have made, then that can be established on the evidence and factual matrix then available. In other words, if Apple’s predictions are correct then it can seek and justify any required rescission or variation at the later appropriate time.

[1098] Third, to require Apple to invoke s 80(3), if it so chooses, appropriately puts the onus on it to substantiate any material market or technological change that it says justifies any rescission or variation. In terms of knowledge of any such change or its significance, Apple is in a better relative position than Epic to have knowledge of and to identify such a change and its significance. It is appropriate that it bear the onus under any s 80(3) application.

#### Costs

[1099] Apple submits that the appropriate costs order in terms of the trial on liability is that it pay 70% of Epic’s costs of the proceeding on the standard basis.

[1100] Apple points out that I accepted Epic’s case that Apple’s prohibition on direct downloading (or sideloading) of native apps, and on alternative payment solutions for digital in-app purchases, contravened s 46. But I rejected Epic’s case that Apple’s refusal to distribute alternative app stores via the App Store contravened s 46. I also rejected Epic’s case concerning various terms of the DPLA and App Review Guidelines and rejected Epic’s claims under ss 45 and 47 and its unconscionable conduct claims.

[1101] Apple says that accordingly the proceeding is properly characterised as one in which there has been some degree of mixed success. So it says that it is appropriate that there be some apportionment of costs. Apple submits that an appropriate apportionment is that Apple pay 70% of Epic’s costs.

[1102] Now the focus of Epic’s case was its allegation that Apple’s conduct contravened s 46, which Epic established. Further, the aspects of the case on which Epic did not succeed factually overlapped with the areas of the case in which Epic did succeed. Nevertheless I agree with Apple that some reflection should be made in the costs order for the fact that Epic did not succeed on some of its causes of action.

[1103] In my view, Epic should have 85% of its costs of the liability trial. As to the parties’ costs concerning the hearing on relief, I will reserve the parties’ costs at this stage.

#### Conclusion

[1104] For the foregoing reasons, I propose to make orders in the terms set out at the outset.

I certify that the preceding one thousand one hundred and four (1104) numbered paragraphs are a true copy of the Reasons for Judgment of the Honourable Justice Beach.

Associate:

Dated: 22 October 2026
